===== ICLE-US-IND-0001.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear moral position and a strong emotional investment in the topic. The Exxon Valdez disaster is a powerful case study, and your concern for environmental damage and corporate accountability comes through vividly. To revise effectively, focus on three main areas: thesis and argument, organization and development, and style and correctness.

Clarify your thesis

Right now, the opening begins with “Money has been said to be the root of all evil,” but the essay quickly shifts to Exxon, corporate greed, and environmental negligence. That makes the main claim feel broad at first and then narrower later.

Try to state your argument more directly in the introduction. For example, your thesis could be something like:

“The Exxon Valdez disaster shows how corporate greed, poor preparation, and negligence can cause lasting environmental and human harm, and it demonstrates why large corporations must be held accountable.”

This would give the essay a sharper focus. Right now, your point of view is clear, but the exact argument needs to be more precise.

Strengthen the structure of the essay

Your essay has passionate ideas, but the paragraphs do not always build logically from one to the next. A stronger structure would help your argument feel more persuasive.

A possible organization:

Introduction: Introduce the issue and present your thesis.
Paragraph 1: Explain what happened in the Exxon Valdez spill.
Paragraph 2: Describe Exxon’s failures before and after the spill.
Paragraph 3: Discuss the effects on wildlife, Indigenous communities, and the fishing industry.
Paragraph 4: Argue what Exxon should have done and what broader reforms are needed.
Conclusion: Reinforce the lesson about corporate accountability.

Also, some transitions are abrupt. For example, you move from your personal reaction (“While I laid in my living room…”) to claims about Exxon’s responsibility without always showing the connection. Use transitions such as “More importantly,” “In addition,” “As a result,” or “This failure demonstrates…”

Develop your evidence more fully

You make several strong claims, but some need clearer evidence or explanation:

“The response by Exxon was slow, disorganized and unacceptable.”
“The publishing of Exxon's notes and court transcriptions, prove…”
“The effects… are still felt.”

These are potentially persuasive points, but they need support. What specifically was slow or disorganized? What did the notes or court records show? How were fishing communities or wildlife still affected?

Also, there is a placeholder in the essay: “.” This needs to be replaced with the actual quotation or paraphrased response from Exxon.

Be careful with claims like “biggest Public Relations blunder by any firm in the nation.” That is a dramatic statement, but unless you can prove it, it may weaken your credibility. You could revise to:  
“The spill became one of the most damaging public relations crises in corporate history.”

Balance emotion with analysis

Your emotional language gives the essay energy, but at times it becomes so forceful that it weakens the academic tone. Phrases like “catastrophe beyond belief,” “mind boggling,” and “pollute and rape our land” are intense, but they can sound more rhetorical than analytical.

Try replacing emotionally charged phrasing with precise language. For example:

“catastrophe beyond belief” → “a major environmental disaster”
“mind boggling” → “enormous”
“rape our land” → “severely damage the environment”

You can still express strong opinion, but in argumentative writing, evidence and careful phrasing are usually more persuasive than outrage alone.

Improve sentence-level correctness

There are several grammar and style issues to revise:

Capitalization: “Prince William sound” should be “Prince William Sound.”
Word choice: “first hand” should be “firsthand.”
Sentence construction: “I personally witnessed first hand” is inaccurate if you saw it on television rather than in person.
Awkward phrasing: “the Captain in charge the vessel” should be “the captain in charge of the vessel.”
Agreement: “The publishing of Exxon's notes and court transcriptions, prove” should be “proves.”
Redundancy: “I feel this statement is personally true” repeats the same idea.

You also sometimes overuse “I feel” and “I personally.” Since this is an argument, state claims confidently instead of repeatedly framing them as personal feelings.

Final suggestion

This essay has a strong foundation because you clearly care about the issue. Your revision should aim to turn that passion into a more focused and evidence-based argument. If you sharpen the thesis, organize the body more clearly, support claims with specifics, and adopt a more formal tone, the essay will become much more convincing.

Revision priorities:
Write a clearer thesis.
Replace the placeholder and add concrete evidence.
Reorganize paragraphs around specific points.
Reduce overly emotional wording.
Edit for grammar, punctuation, and clarity.

With revision, this could become a compelling argument about corporate responsibility and environmental ethics.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0002.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position—crime may bring short-term money, but it ultimately destroys lives—and that gives the paper a strong moral focus. The personal example also helps make the argument concrete and emotionally engaging. For revision, I’d suggest working on three main areas: thesis/focus, organization/development, and sentence-level correctness.

Strengthen the thesis
Right now, your main claim appears in several places (“crime does not pay,” “it destroys your life”), but it would be stronger if you stated it clearly and directly in the introduction.

For example, you could end the first paragraph with something like:
> Although crime may seem like a quick path to wealth, it ultimately leads to punishment, financial loss, and harm to innocent family members.

That gives the reader a roadmap for the essay. At the moment, the introduction is somewhat broad and repetitive, and it ends awkwardly with “as the title of this essay states ,” which looks unfinished. Replace that with a clear thesis sentence.

Improve organization
Your essay currently has a basic structure:
Paragraph 1: general comments about crime and money
Paragraph 2: example/story
Paragraph 3: conclusion

That structure works, but the first paragraph is too general, while the second paragraph is long and does too much at once. Try revising for clearer paragraphing:

Introduction: explain the temptation of “getting rich quick” and present your thesis.
Body paragraph 1: discuss why people turn to crime.
Body paragraph 2: present the real-estate example as evidence.
Body paragraph 3: explain the consequences—not only for the criminal, but also for the family and victims.
Conclusion: restate the argument and leave the reader with a final thought.

This would help the essay feel more purposeful rather than like a general reflection followed by one long story.

Develop the argument more analytically
The example is effective, but the essay would be stronger if you analyzed it more. Right now, much of paragraph 2 tells the story, but you can also explain how the story proves your claim.

For instance, after describing the arrest, you might discuss:
The criminal lost his freedom.
The family lost financial security and social reputation.
The elderly victims lost money and trust.
Short-term wealth led to long-term ruin.

That kind of explanation turns the story into evidence for an argument.

Tone and word choice
Some wording is informal or imprecise. Since this is an academic argumentative essay, aim for a more formal tone.

Examples:
“get rich quick attitude” → “desire to get rich quickly”
“fast track to riches and money” → “a shortcut to wealth”
“the bottom line is” → use sparingly; it becomes repetitive

Also be careful with phrases that sound judgmental without explanation. For example, saying people commit crimes because they are “fed up with society’s rules” may be too vague. Explain what you mean more specifically.

Sentence-level errors to correct
There are many grammar, spelling, and punctuation issues that distract from your ideas. Some common patterns:

Spelling
societies → society’s
expectable → acceptable
catigory → category
will → willing
beleive → believe

Word choice / grammar
“Most of the people that turn to crime are either fed up…”  
  This sentence is incomplete because either suggests two options, but the structure is unclear.
“see and easier way” → see an easier way
“There was no reason for this man to have chosen…”  
  Good idea, but could be more concise: This man had no real need to choose crime.

Punctuation / sentence structure
“Crime does not pay, is a very true statement.”  
  Better: “Crime does not pay” is a very true statement.
“But, he was greedy…”  
  No comma needed after But.
Some sentences are run-on or awkwardly joined. Read each sentence aloud to check whether it flows naturally.

A few sentence revisions as models
Original:
> Some people are not patient and see and easier way of earning money, either by stealing it or selling drugs or other illegal contraband.

Revised:
> Some people lack patience and see crime—such as theft or drug dealing—as an easier way to make money.

Original:
> The government took control of all the families money, including the house in which they lived.

Revised:
> The government seized the family’s assets, including the house they lived in.

Final revision priorities
When you revise, focus on this order:
Write a clear thesis
Reorganize the body into clearer points
Add analysis of consequences
Edit for grammar, spelling, and punctuation

You have a solid central message and a compelling example. With clearer organization and more careful editing, this could become a much stronger argumentative essay.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0003.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay raises an interesting and important claim: that pay reflects not just the difficulty of work, but society’s assumptions about what kinds of labor are truly “valuable.” The child-care example is a strong choice because it exposes a real tension in American values. The draft shows thoughtful engagement with the topic, but it would be much stronger with a clearer thesis, tighter organization, and more precise language.

What the essay does well

Strong central concern  
You move beyond the simple slogan “equal work deserves equal pay” and ask a deeper question about how societies assign value. That is a sophisticated argumentative move.

Effective example  
Child care is a compelling case study because it connects economics, gender, family life, and public values. This gives your essay relevance and complexity.

Thoughtful tone  
Your style is serious and reflective, which suits an academic argument.

Main areas to revise

Clarify the thesis
Right now, the essay circles around the main point, but it does not state it directly enough. Your reader should be able to identify your exact claim by the end of the introduction.

A possible thesis might be:

> Although Americans claim to support equality, wages often reflect cultural assumptions about social value rather than the actual importance of the work; child-care labor is essential to both families and the economy, yet it remains underpaid because society undervalues care work.

This gives you a clear argument that the rest of the essay can prove.

Strengthen organization
The essay currently reads as one long progression of ideas rather than a tightly structured argument. Try organizing it this way:

Introduction: present the issue and thesis  
Body paragraph 1: explain how pay reflects social values, not just effort or skill  
Body paragraph 2: show that Americans claim to value children  
Body paragraph 3: explain the contradiction—child-care workers are still underpaid and low status  
Body paragraph 4: show why child care is economically essential to the workforce  
Conclusion: explain why this matters and what change is needed

This structure will help your reader follow the logic more easily.

Develop the argument with evidence
At the moment, the essay relies mostly on reasoning, which is good, but it needs support. Add at least a few concrete examples or facts, such as:

average wages of child-care workers
comparison with other occupations
statistics on how child care enables parental participation in the labor force
evidence about public spending or debates on education and family policy

Without evidence, some claims sound persuasive but not fully convincing.

Make the conclusion more decisive
Your ending has an interesting idea—financial status shapes social status—but it feels hesitant. Rather than ending on irony alone, state the significance of your argument clearly.

For example:

> If Americans genuinely value children and equality, then they must recognize child-care labor as socially essential work and compensate it accordingly.

That gives the essay a stronger finish.

Sentence-level issues to fix

Awkward or unclear phrasing
Several sentences are overly abstract or difficult to follow. For example:

“The idea that  seems at first to be in harmony…”  
  This opening is incomplete because of the placeholder. You need to state the idea directly.

“would profess to the belief”  
  Better: “would profess the belief” or “would claim to believe”

“equality of work as defined by specific task…”  
  This phrase is hard to process. Simplify it.

Try to prefer direct, concrete wording over heavily layered abstract phrasing.

Grammar and word choice
A few corrections:

“a pereniel subject” → “a perennial subject”
“as well as man with family responsibilities” → “as well as men with family responsibilities”
“contribution to the society they live in” → more naturally, “contribution to society”

Also watch article use and sentence balance.

Sentence length
Some sentences are too long and packed with multiple ideas. Break them into shorter units so each sentence makes one clear point. For example, your third paragraph could be split into several sentences to improve readability.

Style suggestions

Avoid repeating “valuable” too often without defining it. Do you mean economically profitable, socially necessary, morally important, or publicly respected? Clarify this distinction.
Be careful with generalizations like “Americans profess…” These can work, but they are stronger when qualified: “Many Americans claim…” or “American public discourse often suggests…”
Use transitions to show logic: however, therefore, for example, by contrast, as a result.

Revision priorities

Replace the incomplete opening and write a clear thesis.
Reorganize the essay into clearer argumentative paragraphs.
Add evidence to support claims about child care, wages, and social value.
Simplify unclear sentences and correct grammar issues.
Write a stronger, more assertive conclusion.

Final thought

This draft has a thoughtful core argument and a strong example. What it needs most is sharper focus and clearer delivery. If you make the thesis explicit, support it with evidence, and simplify the prose, the essay will become much more persuasive.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0004.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay raises an important and still-relevant question: how society values unpaid domestic labor. You clearly care about the issue, and your central claim is understandable: homemaking and child-rearing are socially valuable and deserve recognition, perhaps even financial compensation. The paper has a strong moral concern and several effective contrasts between paid work and unpaid work. To revise successfully, focus on sharpening the thesis, strengthening the argument’s logic, and improving sentence-level clarity.

What’s working well

Clear topic and strong sense of purpose  
From the opening paragraph, the reader can identify the subject: the social and economic undervaluing of homemaking. That focus gives the essay coherence.

Effective use of comparison  
You compare marketplace work with domestic labor in a way that helps readers see the imbalance in social recognition. The neurosurgeon vs. raising four children example is especially useful because it dramatizes the problem of comparing different kinds of value.

Engaged, persuasive voice  
Your tone is confident and invested. This helps the essay feel like an argument rather than a summary.

Main revision priorities

Make the thesis more precise
Right now, the essay moves between two possible claims:

homemakers should receive direct financial compensation, and/or
society should rethink how it defines “worth” and contribution.

These are related, but they are not the same argument. By the end, you also suggest a third option: paying the family unit rather than the individual. For revision, choose one main position and state it clearly.

For example, you might argue:
Society should financially compensate unpaid caregivers, or
Society should not necessarily pay homemakers directly, but it must stop equating monetary income with human worth, or
Family income should be understood and structured as shared compensation for both paid and unpaid labor within a household.

Once you decide, make sure every paragraph supports that exact claim.

Develop the argument with stronger evidence
The essay relies heavily on assertion. Many claims are plausible, but they need support. For instance:

“many of the ills of society… have been traced by study after study”  
“the media makes her feel behind the times”  
homemaking contributes services “equally valuable” to marketplace jobs

These points would be more persuasive if supported by examples, data, or specific research. Even in an argumentative essay, readers need more than strong opinion. Consider adding:

economic estimates of unpaid domestic labor
research on child development and caregiver presence
discussion of public policy in countries that subsidize caregiving
examples of tax credits, parental allowances, or shared household income models

Address counterarguments
A strong argumentative essay acknowledges objections. For example, if homemakers were paid:

Who would pay them—the state, employers, or spouses?
How would society measure homemaking fairly?
Would compensation reinforce traditional gender roles?
Should childless homemakers be treated the same as caregivers of children?

You mention “mothers, or even childless homemakers,” which suggests complexity, but you do not fully explore it. Addressing counterarguments will make your essay more intellectually rigorous.

Organization and structure

Your essay generally moves logically, but some paragraphs drift. The final paragraph introduces new solutions rather than concluding the argument developed earlier. Revise by giving each paragraph a focused purpose:

Introduction: define the problem and present a precise thesis  
Body paragraph 1: explain how paid work is socially valued  
Body paragraph 2: show the social/economic value of homemaking  
Body paragraph 3: explain consequences of undervaluing domestic labor  
Body paragraph 4: respond to objections and propose a realistic solution  
Conclusion: reinforce the significance of your claim

Style and language

Reduce outdated or informal phrasing
Some expressions weaken your credibility or sound dated, for example:

“first-world cultures”
“doctors, lawyers and Indian chiefs”
“workworld”
“with a capital C”

Aim for more formal and precise language.

Watch word choice and grammar
There are a few noticeable errors and awkward phrases:

“an neurosurgeon” → a neurosurgeon
“this contribution to society in so immense” → is so immense
“whether they be male or female” → whether they are male or female
“for he or she who works outside the home” is awkward; revise to for those who work outside the home
“if, of course, the worker is married,” not “it”

Also, some sentences are very long. Break them into shorter units so your ideas are easier to follow.

Be careful with generalizations
Statements about “women,” “the media,” or “our culture” can sound too broad unless qualified. Try using wording such as “many people,” “some cultural messages,” or “in many industrialized societies.”

Final advice
Your essay has a compelling subject and a solid starting argument. The next step is to make it more focused, evidence-based, and analytically precise. If you clarify exactly what policy or social change you are arguing for, support your claims with concrete evidence, and address possible objections, this will become a much stronger argumentative essay.

Revision goals:
Write a one-sentence thesis that states one clear position.  
Add at least two specific pieces of supporting evidence.  
Include one counterargument and rebuttal.  
Edit for concision, grammar, and more formal wording.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0005.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a strong philosophical ambition: you question a familiar proverb by testing its logic, definitions, and implications. That intellectual seriousness is a real strength. You are clearly trying to do more than react emotionally; you want to expose weaknesses in the saying “money is the root of all evil.” The main areas to revise are clarity of thesis, organization, precision of terms, and sentence control.

Clarify your main claim early
At present, your argument moves through several possibilities—money, desire, happiness, virtue, class, power—before the reader can clearly identify your central position.

I think your thesis is something like:
the proverb is too vague to be meaningful, because neither “desire” nor “evil” can be measured clearly; and/or
money itself is not evil, but wealth and power can enable oppression.

Choose one main claim and state it directly in the first paragraph. For example:

> The proverb “money is the root of all evil” is misleading because it confuses money itself with human desire and with the misuse of wealth and power.

That gives the essay a stable center.

Define key terms consistently
A major issue is that your essay depends on abstract words—evil, virtue, desire, happiness, money, wealth, power—but their meanings keep shifting.

For instance:
You first discuss money as if it causes evil.
Then you move to desire for money.
Then to happiness as contentment.
Then to virtue as the counterpart of happiness.
In the final paragraph, the focus becomes power and historical oppression.

These may be connected, but the essay needs to show exactly how. Define the important terms once and stick to those definitions. Ask yourself:
Is the issue money, greed, or power?
Is virtue the same as happiness, or are those different?
Is your argument logical, moral, psychological, or historical?

Right now, the essay risks sounding profound but imprecise.

Improve paragraph structure and progression
Your paragraphs contain interesting ideas, but they do not always build logically.

A clearer structure might be:

Introduction: introduce the proverb and state your thesis.
Paragraph 1: explain why money itself cannot be the cause of evil.
Paragraph 2: distinguish money from the desire for money; explain why desire is hard to measure.
Paragraph 3: discuss happiness/contentment and how this complicates the proverb.
Conclusion: argue that power and human motives, not money alone, better explain wrongdoing.

This will help the reader follow your reasoning instead of having to reconstruct it.

Watch for unsupported leaps in logic
Some claims need more explanation. For example:

> “there is reason to suspect a general trend towards virtue in affluent households”

This is a large claim and not obviously true. Why would financial stability produce virtue? You mention happiness and contentment, but the connection is not fully developed. Likewise, the claim that only “serendipitously rich” heirs could be virtuous in such a world is intriguing but too compressed. Slow down and explain the reasoning step by step.

Sentence style: simplify for force
You have an elevated style, but many sentences are so dense that meaning becomes hard to follow. Often, shorter sentences would make your ideas sound stronger, not weaker.

For example, this sentence is overloaded:

> “I contend that the saying is meaningless-or a least impractical - for summing up the woes and corruption of humanity since desire-much less evil- cannot be appropriated objectively and wheighed against financial aptitude.”

You could revise it to:

> I contend that the saying is at least impractical as an explanation of human corruption. Neither desire nor evil can be measured objectively against wealth.

That keeps your idea while improving readability.

Tone and diction
Your vocabulary is ambitious, but sometimes word choice feels inaccurate or overly ornate. Examples:
“appropriated objectively” — probably not the word you want
“financial aptitude” — this means skill, not amount of wealth
“awesome mystery as evil” — rhetorically interesting, but vague in an argumentative essay

Aim for precision over grandeur.

Mechanical and grammatical issues
There are several spelling and editing problems that weaken credibility. A few examples:
“If money was” → “If money were”
“privelaged” → “privileged”
“acruement” → likely “accrual” or “accumulation”
“wheighed” → “weighed”
“serrendipitously” → “serendipitously”
“where in which” → remove one phrase
“or a least” → “or at least”

Also watch punctuation:
Hyphens and dashes are used inconsistently.
Quotation marks around terms like “happiness” and “desire” are fine if you are defining them, but overuse can make the prose feel uncertain.
The symbol  should be removed and replaced with a proper transition.

What to do in revision
As you revise, focus on these priorities:
Write a clear one-sentence thesis.
Decide whether the essay is mainly about money, greed, or power.
Define virtue and happiness clearly, or cut one if it distracts.
Reorganize paragraphs so each develops one idea.
Shorten and simplify sentences.
Proofread carefully for spelling and grammar.

Final thought
This essay shows real intellectual promise. You are trying to interrogate a cliché rather than accept it, which is exactly what strong argumentative writing does. Your next step is to make the argument more disciplined: clearer terms, cleaner structure, and more direct prose. Once that happens, your philosophical insight will come through much more powerfully.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0006.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay raises an interesting question about whether Marx’s principle can operate in modern Western society, and it shows clear engagement with family, religion, individual freedom, and moral relativism. The paper has a thoughtful tone and some strong abstract thinking. To revise effectively, focus on three main areas: clarifying your thesis, strengthening your argument structure, and improving precision in language and evidence.

Clarify the thesis

At present, your central claim is not fully clear. You seem to argue that Marx’s ideal of egalitarian contribution is admirable but ultimately not workable in Western culture because of individual freedom, conflicting values, and moral relativism. That idea appears only near the end.

What to revise:
State your main argument clearly in the introduction.
Identify exactly which Marxist principle you are discussing. Because the quotations are replaced by , readers cannot tell whether you mean “from each according to his ability, to each according to his needs” or another statement. You need to name it directly and explain it.
Make sure every paragraph connects back to that thesis.

Possible revised thesis:
“Although Marx’s principle of contributing according to ability and receiving according to need reflects admirable moral aspirations, it cannot be fully realized in modern Western society because individual freedom, disagreements about moral obligation, and social relativism prevent a shared standard of contribution and reward.”

Strengthen paragraph organization and logical development

Several paragraphs contain good ideas, but the essay often moves too quickly from one broad claim to another. The result is that the reader can follow the general topic but not always the logic.

Introduction
The opening is too vague because the quotation is missing and because phrases like “that principle” and “varying degrees” are not explained. Also, “If one were to break the community structure into small parts…” is abstract and unclear.

Revise by:
Introducing Marx’s idea directly.
Defining key terms such as “egalitarianism,” “contribution,” or “reward.”
Ending with a clear thesis.

Family paragraph
This paragraph is one of your strongest because it uses a concrete example. However, you should explain more explicitly how the family supports your argument. Does the family show that the principle works naturally in small communities but not in large societies? If so, say that.

Christianity/social institutions paragraph
This section suggests that Western ethics are shaped by Christian teaching, but it needs evidence and nuance.
Not all Western moral thought comes primarily from Christianity.
If you make this claim, support it with examples or qualify it carefully.

Also, “The New Testament teaches ” needs a full quotation or paraphrase.

Monetary reward and questions paragraph
This is potentially your best argumentative section because it raises the practical problem of measuring social contribution. However, the rhetorical questions pile up without being fully answered.

Revise by:
Turning those questions into a direct claim: society lacks an objective way to measure whose contribution deserves what reward.
Then illustrate with one example, such as parenting versus social work or public service.

Freedom and individualism paragraph
This paragraph identifies an important tension, but it needs closer explanation. Why does individual freedom undermine Marx’s principle? Show the causal connection more explicitly.

Conclusion
Your conclusion is reasonable, but it arrives abruptly. It should briefly summarize your main reasons and restate the argument with confidence.

Use evidence rather than broad assertion

Right now, many claims are sweeping:
“In Western society, the primary guide is Christianity.”
“The individual is paramount in the post-modern world.”
“The modern person lives in a world where relativism reigns supreme.”

These statements may be arguable, but they are too general without support.

To improve:
Add at least a few specific references: Marx, Christian ethics, political philosophy, or social examples.
Even one or two concrete examples per body paragraph would make the essay much more persuasive.
Avoid presenting debatable claims as universal facts.

Style and language issues

Your style is appropriately formal, but there are grammar and wording problems that weaken the essay’s authority.

Errors to correct
“on e” → “one”
“who it to define” → “who is to define”
“form the family” → “from the family”
“will unlikely be achieved” → “is unlikely to be achieved”

Word choice and phrasing
Replace “&” with “and” in formal academic writing.
“Absent a deviation from our general nature…” is awkward and overly abstract. Simplify.
“The religious would rest knowing…” sounds unnatural. Consider: “Religious individuals may believe that ultimate rewards come from God.”
Be careful with terms like “post-modern world” and “moral relativism.” If you use them, define them.

Revision priorities

If you revise in stages, do this first:
Restore and explain the missing quotations.
Write a precise thesis in the introduction.
Make each paragraph begin with a clear claim tied to the thesis.
Replace rhetorical questions with analytical statements.
Add specific support and reduce overgeneralization.
Proofread for sentence-level errors and formal style.

Final comment

This essay has a strong foundation: you are trying to grapple with a serious philosophical issue rather than merely summarizing a slogan. Your next step is to make the argument more explicit, better supported, and more carefully organized. If you clarify your thesis and develop each paragraph with concrete reasoning, the paper will become much more persuasive.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0007.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear controlling idea: whether crime “pays” depends on how we define “pay,” and you argue that the true calculation must include financial, physical, mental, and legal costs. That is a strong foundation for an argumentative essay because it moves beyond a simplistic yes/no answer. The essay is also easy to follow overall, and the “small business” analogy gives the piece a consistent frame.

What is working well

Strong central approach
You do a good job redefining the key term in the prompt. Instead of treating “pay” as only immediate profit, you broaden it to include long-term losses. This gives your argument depth.

Effective organization
The essay progresses logically:
define “pay”
consider financial costs
consider opportunity cost
consider health costs
consider legal costs
conclude

That structure helps the reader understand your reasoning.

Concrete examples
Your opening jewelry store example is vivid and helps readers see your point quickly. Similar examples later—medical bills, legal fees, lost work capacity—also support the argument well.

Main areas to revise

Make your thesis more explicit
Right now, your position is implied more than directly stated. A stronger thesis at the end of the introduction would make the essay more persuasive.

For example, you could write something like:
“Although crime may produce short-term financial gain, it rarely pays when one considers the full costs of time, risk, injury, stress, and legal consequences.”

This would give the essay a sharper argumentative focus.

Strengthen the conclusion
Your conclusion starts well, but the final sentence—“no taxes”—introduces a joking or casual note that weakens the seriousness of the argument. It also risks sounding as though the essay ends by granting criminals an advantage rather than reaffirming your main claim.

Instead, end by reinforcing your argument:
“When all financial, physical, and psychological costs are considered, crime is usually not a profitable or sustainable way of life.”

Clarify and refine tone
Some parts of the essay sound informal or overly conversational for academic argument. Phrases like:
“small businessman criminal”
“does better the legitimate businessman”
“in the professions”

can sound awkward or imprecise.

Try for a more formal style. For instance:
“the individual criminal”
“has one apparent financial advantage over legitimate businesses”
“in a professional occupation”

Develop analysis, not just examples
Several paragraphs list possible costs, but they could go further in analyzing why those costs matter. For example, in the paragraph on opportunity cost, you begin an interesting point: crime “pays” differently depending on what legal alternatives a person has. That idea is sophisticated, but it needs more explanation. Why does opportunity cost matter? How does it affect the claim that crime pays?

Where possible, move from example to interpretation:
What does this cost show?
How does it support your thesis?
Why does it make crime less “profitable” than it appears?

Improve precision and correctness at the sentence level
There are several language issues that should be revised:

“equiptment” → “equipment”
“all manners of loss” → “all manner of losses” or “all types of loss”
“get-away cars” → usually “getaway cars”
“monies earned” → “money earned” is usually better
“health related loss” → “health-related loss”
“he does better the legitimate businessman” → “he does better than the legitimate businessman”

Also watch pronoun consistency. You repeatedly use “he” and “his” to refer to criminals in general. In a revised academic essay, consider using “a criminal… they” or simply plural nouns such as “criminals… they.”

Suggestions for revision

When revising, focus on these priorities:

Add a clear thesis to the introduction.
Revise the conclusion so it firmly supports your argument.
Make the tone more academic and less conversational.
Expand analysis in body paragraphs rather than only listing costs.
Proofread carefully for spelling, grammar, and phrasing.

A possible revision plan

Introduction: Define “pay” and end with a direct thesis.
Body Paragraph 1: Financial cost of crime, including start-up costs and reduced net gain.
Body Paragraph 2: Opportunity cost—time spent on crime versus legal work.
Body Paragraph 3: Physical and psychological harm.
Body Paragraph 4: Legal consequences, including attorney fees and imprisonment.
Conclusion: Reassert that crime may offer immediate gains but rarely pays overall.

Overall, this is a thoughtful essay with a strong underlying idea. The next step is to make the argument more explicit, polished, and analytical so that the essay reads as a confident academic argument rather than a series of observations.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a strong sense of conviction. You argue consistently that money itself is not evil, but the love of money is. That distinction gives the essay a solid central idea, and your examples about families, companies, and individuals show that you are trying to connect the argument to real life. For revision, the main areas to improve are thesis precision, organization, development of evidence, and sentence-level accuracy.

Strengthen the thesis and focus
Your opening begins strongly, but “This idea is completely erroneous” is too vague because the reader has to infer what “this idea” refers to. State the claim directly and clearly in the first sentence.

For example:
The claim that money is the root of all evil is mistaken; rather, it is the love of money and the greed it encourages that lead to harmful behavior.

This gives you a sharper thesis and sets up the whole essay more effectively.

Also, your essay sometimes shifts between two claims:
money is necessary for daily life, and  
the love of money causes harm.  

Both points are useful, but make sure the second is clearly the main argument, while the first serves as support.

Improve organization
At the moment, the essay reads more like a series of reflections than a structured argument. Try revising into a clearer progression:

Possible structure:
Introduction: present the issue and thesis.
Body paragraph 1: explain why money itself is necessary and morally neutral.
Body paragraph 2: explain how greed or excessive desire causes harm.
Body paragraph 3: give specific examples of consequences for individuals, families, or society.
Conclusion: restate the distinction and reinforce the main point.

You already have material for these sections, but you need clearer paragraphing. Right now, some paragraphs are only one sentence long, while others contain several different ideas. Group related ideas together.

Use more specific evidence
Your argument would be much more persuasive if it included concrete examples rather than rhetorical generalizations.

For instance, this sentence is effective in tone but too broad:
“How many times have we seen on the evening news a family being broken apart, a company going under, or even a nation crumbling...”

Instead of asking a rhetorical question, you could give one or two specific examples:
corporate fraud,
political corruption,
financial crimes,
family conflict over inheritance or debt.

Even if you do not cite formal sources, more specific examples will make the essay feel more convincing and less repetitive.

Develop ideas more fully
Several good points are introduced but not fully explained. For example, you say money can be “used for good” and can “bring relief to those in need.” That is an important counterpoint, but it needs development. How exactly? Through charity? Medical care? Education? Community support?

Similarly, the paragraph about children is interesting, but the connection to your thesis needs clarification. Are you arguing that the desire to possess begins early and must be guided morally? If so, make that explicit.

Adjust tone for academic argument
Some phrases are too absolute or emotional for an academic essay:
“completely erroneous”
“root of all evil”
“wreaks havoc”
“love affair with money”

These are vivid, but they can sound preachy rather than analytical. You do not need to remove strong language entirely, but balance it with explanation and reasoned analysis.

Revise sentence-level errors
There are quite a few grammar, punctuation, capitalization, spelling, and word-choice problems. Below are some important examples:

“Money perhaps, is” → remove comma: “Money, perhaps, is” or better “Money is perhaps”
“all together” → “altogether”
“capitivates” → “captivates”
“he begins” → capitalize: “He begins”
“4-5 year-old” → “4- or 5-year-old”
“is money the root of all evil?” → capitalize: “Is money the root of all evil?”
“sued for good” → “used for good”
“it’s needs met” → “its needs met”
“However, the love it” → “However, the love of it”

Also, avoid using “&” in formal writing. Write “and.”

Work on sentence clarity
Some sentences are overly long and could be made clearer by splitting them. For example:

Original:
“This love of money urges them on, causes them to neglect their families and at times to commit crimes for which they are imprisoned.”

Revised:
The love of money can drive people to neglect their families. In extreme cases, it can even lead them to commit crimes.

Shorter sentences can increase clarity and force.

Final advice
Your core idea is strong: money is a tool, while greed is the real danger. To improve the essay, focus on:
stating the thesis more directly,
organizing paragraphs more logically,
adding specific examples,
developing each point more fully,
correcting grammar and mechanics.

With revision, this could become a much clearer and more persuasive argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear and provocative central claim: the cliché “crime does not pay” is not universally true and functions more as moral reassurance than factual description. That is an interesting argument, and your essay succeeds in challenging a familiar assumption. The piece is also organized logically, moving from the phrase’s social function to counterexamples and then to the broader economic system around crime.

What is working well

Clear thesis and argumentative purpose  
Your final sentence gives the essay a definite position: the phrase is “wishful thinking rather than a truism.” This is a strong argumentative move because it reframes the issue rather than simply rejecting the saying.

Effective use of examples  
You use a range of examples—individual criminals, sentencing, and the prison industry—to show that “payoff” can take different forms. This broadens the discussion beyond simple financial reward.

Good paragraph progression  
Each paragraph develops a related part of the argument. The essay reads coherently and builds toward the conclusion.

---

Main areas to revise

Sharpen the thesis earlier and define key terms  
The essay begins with “The phrase ,” which seems to be a placeholder. Presumably this is “crime does not pay.” State that directly in the opening sentence. Also, define what you mean by “pay.” Do you mean:
financial gain,
social power,
personal satisfaction,
reduced punishment compared with the benefit gained,
or economic benefit for institutions?

Right now, the essay moves among these meanings. That flexibility helps your argument, but it can also make it feel slippery. A stronger thesis might say that crime can “pay” when rewards outweigh punishments for offenders or institutions.

Strengthen the logic in paragraph 3  
Your question—“If, however, crime does not pay, then why does it persist?”—is compelling, but the next sentence is too absolute: “Behavior that is not reinforced in some way will not persist.” Human behavior is more complicated. Crime may persist for many reasons: desperation, addiction, coercion, ideology, poor judgment, or weak deterrence. Consider softening this claim so your argument sounds more credible and less overgeneralized.

Use more precise and reliable evidence  
Several examples feel dated, unclear, or insufficiently supported:
“Michael Milliken” appears to refer to Michael Milken.
Calling Richard Nixon “certainly a criminal” may be rhetorically forceful, but it needs more precision if used as evidence.
The statistic about convicted murderers averaging 15 years in prison needs a source, and without one it is hard to evaluate.
The prison-industry paragraph makes broad claims about states competing for prisoners; again, this needs evidence.

Since this is an argumentative essay, your strongest revision would be to add specific, sourced evidence and explain how each example supports your claim.

Clarify the prison-industry paragraph  
This paragraph introduces an important idea: crime may “pay” not only for criminals but also for institutions and local economies. However, the connection to your thesis could be made more explicit. Right now, it feels like a shift from individual offenders to public systems. Add a transition such as: “Even when crime does not benefit the offender, it may still generate economic benefits for others.” That would unify the essay.

Improve sentence-level clarity and correctness  
There are a few awkward or incorrect phrases:
“stock broker scams” → perhaps “securities fraud” or “stock market fraud”
“managed to continue his activities” is vague—what activities?
“which, after, not only saves money” → likely “which, after all, not only saves money”
“cost effective” should be cost-effective
Some sentences are long and could be tightened for clarity

Also watch tone: phrases like “more cold-blooded statistician than I am” add personality, but they also slightly distract from the analytical tone.

---

Suggestions for revision

Revise the introduction so the phrase and thesis are explicit from the start.  
Define “pay” in the first or second paragraph.  
Replace or update examples with clearer, well-documented cases.  
Add source-based evidence for statistics and claims about incarceration economics.  
Strengthen transitions between individual and institutional forms of “payoff.”  
Refine word choice and grammar for a more formal academic tone.

---

Possible revised thesis

You might try something like:

> Although the phrase “crime does not pay” serves as a useful moral warning, it is not always true in practice, because some offenders retain significant benefits from their crimes and institutions built around punishment may also profit from criminal activity.

This version keeps your core idea while making the scope of the argument clearer.

Overall, this is a thoughtful essay with a strong underlying argument. Your next step is to make it more precise, better supported, and more academically rigorous.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0010.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that people should be financially rewarded for individual effort. The writing shows confidence, and you use a historical comparison to support your view. The conclusion also raises an interesting idea—that money is not just money, but a symbol of reward, motivation, and opportunity. That is a strong foundation. To revise effectively, focus on making your argument more precise, logically developed, and grammatically polished.

Clarify and sharpen your thesis
Your main claim appears in the first paragraph:

> “I feel it is vital that in today’s society men and women should be financially reward for their individual efforts.”

This is the central idea, but it needs clearer wording and stronger focus. First, “reward” should be “rewarded.” More importantly, the thesis should state not only what you believe but why. Right now, the reasons are spread across the essay.

A stronger thesis might be:
In modern society, people should be financially rewarded for individual effort because rewards motivate productivity, encourage people to develop their abilities, and contribute to social progress.

This gives your essay a clear roadmap.

Improve organization and paragraph development
Your essay generally moves from past to present to hypothetical society to conclusion, which is a useful structure. However, some paragraphs feel broad and underdeveloped. Try to make each paragraph focus on one main reason.

For example:
Paragraph 1: Introduction and thesis
Paragraph 2: In both past and present, effort produces results
Paragraph 3: Financial rewards motivate innovation and specialization
Paragraph 4: If rewards are disconnected from effort, motivation and productivity may decline
Paragraph 5: Conclusion

This would make the essay easier to follow.

Also, some claims need more explanation. For example, in paragraph 4 you suggest that equal treatment regardless of effort would lead to jealousy, conflict, and even destruction. That is a dramatic claim, so it needs evidence or more careful reasoning. Instead of jumping to extreme outcomes, explain the likely chain of effects more logically: reduced motivation, lower productivity, resentment among harder workers, and slower progress.

Make your reasoning more precise
You often rely on general statements such as:
“Life is about rewards.”
“Each of us is measured daily by some scale of achievement.”
“The harder you work, the greater the reward.”

These ideas are understandable, but they are too absolute. In argumentative writing, avoid claims that sound universally true unless you can prove them. In reality, hard work does not always lead to greater reward, and success depends on other factors too, such as opportunity, education, and social conditions.

To strengthen the essay, acknowledge complexity:
People should generally be rewarded for effort and contribution.
Financial reward is one important motivator, though not the only one.
A fair society should reward effort while also ensuring basic human needs are met.

This makes your argument more mature and persuasive.

Address possible counterarguments
A strong argumentative essay usually considers an opposing view. Your essay assumes that rewarding everyone equally would remove motivation, but you do not consider why some people support greater equality. For example, some might argue that basic needs such as healthcare, food, or housing should not depend entirely on income.

You do not need to agree, but briefly addressing this point would improve the essay. You could argue:
basic needs should be protected,
but extra effort, skill, and contribution should still bring greater financial reward.

This would make your position more balanced and credible.

Revise sentence-level issues
There are several grammar and word-choice errors that distract from your ideas. Some examples:

“financially reward” → financially rewarded
“took up of majority” → took up the majority
“held us meet” → help us meet
“treated equal” → treated equally or treated the same
“jealously” → jealousy
“selfish and hoarding” → selfish and prone to hoarding
“lazy and stagnate people” → lazy and stagnant people

Also watch out for run-on sentences. For example:

> “We have many machines to held us meet our food, clothing and shelter needs with little or no effort on our part, it would appear.”

This should be divided or restructured:
We now have machines that help us meet our food, clothing, and shelter needs with far less physical effort than in the past.

Strengthen tone and word choice
Some phrases are vague or informal, such as “I would venture to guess” and “Worst case scenario.” In academic argument, use a more direct and formal style. For instance:
“It is likely that…”
“A possible consequence is…”

Also, avoid overusing “I feel” and “I don’t think so.” Since this is an argumentative essay, your position is already understood. Stronger phrasing would be:
“It is vital that…”
“Financial reward is not only about money; it also reflects opportunity and personal advancement.”

Final advice
For revision, focus on three priorities:
Rewrite the thesis so it clearly states your reasons.
Develop each body paragraph around one reason with clearer explanation.
Edit carefully for grammar, word choice, and sentence structure.

You have a solid central argument and a clear sense of purpose. With more precise reasoning and cleaner language, this essay could become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay raises an interesting question about whether people can or should be paid according to their “contribution to society.” You show awareness that pay is shaped by social values, wealth, opportunity, and inequality. The essay also has a clear argumentative tone. To revise effectively, focus on clarifying your thesis, strengthening logic, organizing ideas more tightly, and correcting language errors.

Clarify your main argument
At the moment, your position is somewhat hard to pin down. You seem to argue that:
contribution to society is subjective,
unequal pay is often unfair,
but equal pay according to contribution is impossible.

These ideas can work together, but you need to state them more directly and consistently. Your opening paragraph should present a clear thesis such as:

Possible thesis:  
*Although many jobs are clearly underpaid or overpaid, compensation cannot be perfectly matched to “contribution to society” because social value is difficult to measure and economic systems reward market demand, inheritance, and privilege as much as effort.*

This kind of thesis would help unify the rest of the essay.

Improve paragraph structure and focus
Several paragraphs contain interesting points, but they do not always connect clearly to the thesis.

Paragraph 1
This paragraph introduces the issue, but it stays general. End it with a precise claim rather than broad statements about disappointment and employers’ different opinions.

Paragraphs 2–3

Paragraph 4

Paragraph 5
This paragraph introduces public perceptions of overpayment, especially doctors. However, the example is too sweeping (“everyone seems to think”) and may weaken your credibility. Avoid overgeneralizations and support claims carefully.

Your essay raises moral questions, but sometimes it shifts too quickly from observation to conclusion.

For instance, the final sentence argues that because we do not want to interfere with the wealthy or give money to those society does not think “deserve” it, equal pay by contribution is impossible. This conclusion feels abrupt. Why does it follow? You need to explain more fully:

who would decide what counts as valuable,
why market systems reward some activities more than others,
and why reform is difficult even if current outcomes are unfair.

In other words, show the steps in your reasoning, not just the conclusion.

Use a more precise academic style
The essay has strong voice, but some phrasing is too informal, vague, or rhetorical for academic argument.

Examples:
“some sort of excitement that money cannot buy” — stylistically interesting, but not analytical.
“live as parasites” — vivid, but overly loaded unless carefully framed as the view of critics.
“everyone seems to think” — too broad.

Try to replace emotional or sweeping language with precise claims:
*Critics argued that investment income allowed some people to benefit economically without making a corresponding social contribution.*
*Public opinion often reflects resentment toward professions perceived as highly paid.*

Address grammar and word-choice errors
There are a number of language issues that need editing. Some examples:

“In the 1920s in American” → “In the 1920s in America”
“interest of their investments” → “interest on their investments”
“A migrant work” → “A migrant worker”
“The answer this question” → “The answer to this question”
“Democracry” → “democracy”
“responsibly” → “responsibility”
“much too money” → “too much money”
quotation mark error: `equally'` → `"equally"` or no quotation marks
comma usage and sentence structure need review in several places

Also watch out for run-on sentences and awkward phrasing. Reading the essay aloud may help you hear where sentences become unclear.

Suggested revision strategy
When revising, try this structure:

Introduction  
   Define the issue and present a clear thesis.

Body Paragraph 1  
   Explain why “contribution to society” is difficult to measure.

Body Paragraph 2  
   Show how inherited wealth and investment income separate earnings from labor and social usefulness.

Body Paragraph 3  
   Discuss unequal opportunity and challenge the idea that pay always reflects merit.

Body Paragraph 4  
   Consider objections: market economies reward scarcity, training, and demand, not just effort.

Conclusion  
   Conclude that while perfect equality based on contribution is unrealistic, the gap between social value and financial reward raises important ethical concerns.

Overall
This essay has a thoughtful core idea and good potential. Your next step is to make the argument more focused, better organized, and more explicitly reasoned. If you revise with a clearer thesis and stronger paragraph connections, the essay will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear argumentative purpose and a strong, confident voice. You take a definite position—crime can be profitable in America—and you organize the essay around three main lines of support: history, money, and consequences. That gives the paper a recognizable structure and helps the reader follow your claim. For revision, focus on making the argument more precise, logical, and polished at the sentence level.

Clarify and sharpen the thesis
Your thesis is clear in direction, but it is still somewhat broad: “historical context, the current material gain, and even the consequences of crime in America” can all prove the point. Try to make the claim more specific and arguable.

For example, you might revise toward something like:
Although people often claim that crime never pays, in the United States certain crimes can bring financial profit, public notoriety, and even institutional benefits that reduce the deterrent effect of punishment.

This version is more focused and signals the three body points more directly.

Strengthen logic and avoid weak examples
The biggest issue in the essay is that some examples do not fully support your argument, or they blur the difference between crime, celebrity, and political conflict.

Examples that need reconsideration:
Boston Tea Party: This is historically interesting, but using revolutionary protest as proof that “crime pays” may weaken your argument because readers may see it as a special political case rather than ordinary crime.
Benedict Arnold: This example is confusing. His actions were considered treason, not just “criminal only because of the date.” The point needs much more explanation or should be replaced.
O.J. Simpson: This example relies on a very specific cultural moment. Also, “Whether he is found guilty or innocent” makes the argument feel less controlled because the essay is about crime, not just fame from accusation.

Choose examples that more directly prove profit from crime, such as:
organized crime during Prohibition
financial fraud
people profiting from tell-all books, interviews, or media rights after crimes
systems that fail to deter repeat offenses

Improve paragraph development
Each body paragraph has a topic, but the evidence often appears as a list rather than a developed explanation. Try this pattern in each paragraph:

Topic sentence  
Specific example  
Explanation of how the example proves the claim  
Link back to the thesis

For instance, in the paragraph about money, instead of moving quickly through several points, explain how criminals, publishers, and media industries profit, and why that undermines the saying “crime does not pay.”

Address overstatement and tone
Your voice is energetic, but some sentences rely on sweeping claims that may sound exaggerated or unfair. Academic argument becomes stronger when it sounds controlled rather than sarcastic.

Examples:
“No one can argue…” → Too absolute
“crime becomes more profitable” → Needs evidence
“statistics rise continuously” → No source is given
“free room and board, medical care, higher education…” → This sounds more rhetorical than analytical

Try replacing overstatement with measured language:
“Many cases suggest…”
“Some offenders may benefit…”
“In certain circumstances…”

Edit for grammar, diction, and correctness
There are several sentence-level issues that revision should fix.

Word choice / correctness:
“vents shaping America’s early history” → events
“speak-eases” → speakeasies
“Los Vegas” → Las Vegas
“monetary rewards gained” → repetitive; use profits or financial gains
“if it were not why would people commit them” → needs punctuation and agreement

Sentence problems:
“In fact one needs only to consider…”  
  → Add a comma after “In fact.”
“No one can argue that crime is not a means of making money, if it were not why would people commit them.”  
  → This is a comma splice and has pronoun/agreement problems.  
  Better: No one can deny that crime can be a means of making money; if it were not, why would people commit such acts?

“Even if they do have to wait until after they are released from prison”

The ending includes “tes.” Remove this.

Replace or better explain weak examples.

Feedback Report

Your essay takes a clear position: you acknowledge historical unfairness toward women, but argue that feminism and modernization have also created new pressures and problems. That gives you a workable argumentative focus. To improve the essay, concentrate on clarifying your thesis, strengthening your evidence, and revising language that sounds too general or unsupported.

Main argument and thesis
Right now, your essay seems to argue several things at once:
women faced discrimination in the past,
feminism helped women,
feminism pressures women unfairly,
men and women are naturally different,
traditional roles were better in some ways.

Because these ideas are competing for attention, the essay lacks one clear controlling thesis. Try to state your central claim in one sentence at the end of the introduction. For example:

> Although feminism has helped expand opportunities for women, society should not assume that all women want the same roles, and true equality should include freedom of choice rather than pressure to reject traditional paths.

This kind of thesis is more precise and gives the essay a direction.

Organization
Your paragraphs need stronger topic sentences and clearer connections to the thesis. At the moment, some paragraphs move too quickly between different points. For revision, try this structure:

Introduction: historical inequality + clear thesis  
Paragraph 1: ways feminism has improved women’s opportunities  
Paragraph 2: your concern that some women feel pressured to reject traditional roles  
Paragraph 3: workplace issues, using careful evidence rather than broad claims  
Conclusion: restate argument and emphasize freedom of choice

This will make the essay easier to follow.

Evidence and reasoning
The biggest weakness is that many claims are stated as fact without support. In academic argument, you need evidence, examples, or sources. For instance:

“Women just can't fight in a war very well.”
“They are not strong enough…”
“many employers are the victims.”
“Divorce is at fifty percent and rising… kids are being more neglected.”

These are sweeping claims, and some are controversial or factually debatable. Without evidence, they weaken your credibility. Instead of making absolute statements, use more careful language:
“Some argue that average physical differences may affect performance in certain combat roles.”
“Some employers may fear false accusations, although harassment remains a serious issue.”
“Some critics of changing gender roles argue that these changes may contribute to family stress.”

Notice how these versions sound more analytical and less unsupported.

Tone and fairness
A strong argumentative essay should show that you understand opposing views fairly. Phrases like “these people were crazy” make the tone sound emotional rather than reasoned. Academic writing is stronger when it avoids insults and extreme language. Replace such phrasing with something like:

> I disagree with this position because I believe combat roles raise serious physical and safety concerns.

Also, be careful not to treat “women,” “men,” or “feminists” as if all members of those groups think the same way. Acknowledge variety within groups.

Problematic assumptions
Some parts of the essay rely on assumptions that need much more support, especially statements about what “nature” or “God” intended. You can include moral or religious beliefs if relevant, but in an academic essay they should not substitute for evidence. If you want to argue that men and women often make different choices, it would be better to discuss social expectations, biology, or statistics rather than simply asserting divine intention.

Similarly, your conclusion says, “Women should be created equal,” but the common phrase is “created equal” or “treated equally.” You seem to mean that women should have equal rights and respect, even if men and women are not identical.

Style and sentence-level issues
There are frequent grammar and word-choice errors. Some common ones:

societies role → society’s role
work place → workplace
verses → versus
piers → peers
The are not → They are not
the woman → women
being more stressful → experiencing more stress
they to do → them to do

Also, avoid repetitive openings like “A lot of…” and “These people…” because they sound vague. Be more specific.

What to do in revision
When you revise, focus on these priorities:
Write a clear thesis.
Remove or rewrite sweeping generalizations.
Replace emotional judgments with academic tone.
Add specific evidence or examples.
Proofread carefully for grammar, spelling, and word choice.

Final thought
Your essay raises an important and debatable idea: equality should not mean forcing everyone into the same life choices. That is a strong foundation for an argument. To make it persuasive, you need to present the claim more carefully, support it with evidence, and avoid overgeneralizations about men, women, and feminists.

Feedback Report

You have a clear position and a strong sense of moral urgency. The essay argues consistently that crime may seem rewarding at first, but it leads to escalating behavior and harsher consequences. Your use of a detailed hypothetical example shows that you understand argumentative writing needs evidence or illustration, not just opinion. Clarify and strengthen the thesis
Right now, your central claim is present, but it is buried in personal reflection and repeated in different ways. Try stating it more directly at the end of the introduction.

For example:
“Although crime may seem profitable in the short term, it ultimately leads to escalating behavior, harm to innocent people, and severe punishment.”

This gives the reader a roadmap for the essay.

Improve organization

A stronger structure might be:

Body Paragraph 1: Explain how getting away with small crimes can encourage bigger crimes.
Body Paragraph 2: Explain how punishment becomes more serious when crimes involve danger to others.
Body Paragraph 3: Use the convenience-store example to show how a “simple” robbery can spiral into much more serious consequences.
Conclusion: Reinforce the lesson that crime brings destruction, not success.

At the moment, some paragraphs are very long and contain multiple ideas. Break them up so each paragraph develops one main point.

Make the argument more formal
Your voice is vivid and engaging, but parts of the essay sound conversational rather than academic. Phrases such as “a cool million,” “run like hell,” “guess what happens,” and “sounds like trouble to me” weaken the formal tone.

Try replacing casual language with more precise wording:
“a cool million” → “a large sum of money”
“run like hell” → “run as fast as possible”
“guess what happens” → “as a result”
“sounds like trouble to me” → “this results in serious legal and moral consequences”

You can still keep your strong voice, but the diction should fit an academic argumentative essay.

Control sentence length and punctuation
Several sentences are run-ons or become difficult to follow because too many ideas are packed together. This is the biggest issue affecting clarity.

For instance, your bank robbery sentence tries to do too much at once. Break long thoughts into two or three shorter sentences. Shorter sentences will make your argument sound more controlled and persuasive.

Also watch for comma splices and missing apostrophes. Examples:
“Its's” should be “It’s”
“committs” should be “commits”
“indivudual” should be “individual”
“deterent” should be “deterrent”
“whrn” should be “when”
“strats” should be “starts”
“ths” should be “this”
“persistant” should be “persistent”
“simply robbery” should be “simple robbery”

Careful proofreading will help a lot here.

Be precise with reasoning
Your main idea about escalation is good, but sometimes the reasoning becomes overstated. For example, saying that someone who starts committing small crimes can already “consider yourself a habitual offender” is too absolute. Not every first-time offender becomes a habitual criminal. Instead, you could say:

This sounds more balanced and credible.

robbery occurs
one robber uses a gun during escape
shots are fired in a public area
a child is injured
the charges become much more serious

After describing the scenario, analyze it clearly. Explain why it proves your thesis: crime creates unintended consequences, endangers innocent people, and leads to harsher legal penalties.

Use a more formal academic tone.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central idea: money itself is neutral, but the human desire for what money can buy can lead to harmful behavior. That is a strong and arguable position, and you already show good awareness that the issue is more complex than the saying “money is the root of all evil” suggests. To revise effectively, focus on sharpening your thesis, improving organization, and correcting recurring sentence-level problems.

Strengthen the thesis
Right now, the essay begins with the proverb and then gradually moves toward your actual claim. Your real argument seems to be:

money is a form of power,
money itself is not evil,
greed and unhealthy attitudes toward money are what produce evil.

That is a more precise and interesting thesis than the title idea alone. State it clearly near the end of the introduction. For example:

> Although money gives people power, money itself is morally neutral; it becomes a force for evil only when greed and obsession control the person who seeks it.

A clear thesis will help the reader understand the direction of the essay.

Improve paragraph structure and development
Each body paragraph introduces a useful idea, but the essay feels somewhat compressed. At the moment, your points are stated rather than fully argued. Try to give each paragraph one clear focus:

Paragraph 1: Define money as power and explain why people value it.
Paragraph 2: Explain that money is neutral, but attitudes toward it can become destructive.
Paragraph 3: Show how greed leads people to ignore ethics and use money wrongly.
Conclusion: Return to the proverb and clarify your final position.

To strengthen development, add specific examples. For instance, instead of listing “drug use, gambling and prostitution,” explain *how* the desire for money or what it buys can lead someone into exploitation, corruption, or moral compromise. You could also include examples of socially acceptable forms of greed, such as pursuing status, luxury, or influence at the expense of others.

Refine the logic of the argument
There is a slight tension between your opening sentence and your later claim. The essay starts by treating the saying “Money is the root of all evil” as true, but later you argue that money is not good or bad in itself. These ideas can fit together, but you need to explain the distinction more carefully.

For example, you might argue that:
the proverb is partly true because the pursuit of money often motivates wrongdoing,
but money itself is not evil; the real source of evil is greed or moral corruption.

That distinction will make your essay more logically consistent.

Work on sentence clarity and grammar
Several sentences contain grammar, punctuation, or wording problems that weaken your authority. Here are some important patterns to revise:

a) Possessives and word forms
“Ones's” → “One’s”
“greedy persons life” → “a greedy person’s life”

b) Capitalization
“when the need for money” should begin with a capital: “When”

c) Sentence structure / comma splices
This sentence is a run-on:
> Enough money gives you the power to get whatever you need to survive, lack of money may mean you can't survive.

You could revise it as:
> Enough money gives people the power to obtain what they need to survive, while a lack of money may threaten survival.

d) Missing articles or awkward phrasing
“in the definition of term money” → “in the definition of the term ‘money’”
“Values and ethics play very little importance in structure of a greedy persons life”  
  Better:
  > Values and ethics play very little role in the structure of a greedy person’s life.

e) Incomplete or weak concluding sentence
“Money does good or evil--depending on who has the money” is too abrupt and slightly awkward.
  Try:
  > Therefore, money can be used for either good or evil, depending on the character and values of the person who possesses it.

Improve style and academic tone
Some phrases are effective, such as “money represents power,” but others sound overly broad or informal. For example:
“The list of addictions people develop are endless” should be “is endless”, but also consider a more formal phrasing:
  > The range of destructive desires associated with money is extensive.

Also, avoid unsupported absolute statements like “Greedy people usually have one objective.” A more measured academic tone would be:
> Greed often causes people to prioritize personal gain over ethical responsibility.

Add a concluding paragraph
The essay currently ends suddenly. A conclusion should not simply stop after the final claim. Instead, briefly restate the argument and show its significance. For example, you might conclude that society should not blame money itself, but should be more concerned with the values that shape how people pursue and use wealth.

Final advice
Your essay has a solid core idea and good potential. In revision, concentrate on:
stating a precise thesis early,
organizing paragraphs around one main point each,
adding specific examples,
fixing grammar and punctuation,
ending with a fuller conclusion.

With those changes, your argument will become clearer, more persuasive, and more polished.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear personal voice and a definite position: you support some feminist goals but believe traditional dating customs and chivalry have declined. That strong perspective gives the piece energy. For revision, focus on making your argument more precise, balanced, and academically persuasive.

Clarify your main claim
Right now, your thesis shifts a little. At times, you seem to argue that feminism has harmed dating; at other times, you argue that men are simply confused about modern expectations. Those are related, but not identical claims.

This kind of thesis is clearer, more arguable, and less sweeping than saying “feminists” caused the problem.

Avoid overgeneralizations
A major weakness is the repeated use of broad claims about “women,” “men,” and “feminists.” For instance:
“women in general are offended”
“feminists in this country”
“Men were gentlemen in the 60’s”

These statements are too absolute and can weaken your credibility because readers will immediately think of exceptions. Academic argument usually works better when it acknowledges complexity.

Instead, qualify your claims:
“Some men may feel uncertain about dating expectations today.”
“Some women prefer independence in dating, while others still appreciate traditional gestures.”
“In my experience, dating norms in the 1960s felt more clearly defined.”

This makes your argument sound more thoughtful and less biased.

Replace emotionally charged language
Phrases like “this is bunk!” and references to being “slapped with ‘date rape’ or ‘sexual harassment’” create a confrontational tone. That may express frustration, but it risks alienating readers and oversimplifying serious issues.

In revision, aim for a more measured tone:
Instead of “this is bunk,” try “I disagree with the assumption that chivalry is inherently insulting.”
Instead of suggesting accusations happen simply because men are “too forward,” distinguish between respectful gestures and genuinely inappropriate behavior.

A balanced tone will make your essay stronger and more credible.

Strengthen your evidence
Much of the essay depends on personal experience and anecdotes from friends. Personal experience can be a useful starting point, but in an argumentative essay, you need broader support.

Consider adding:
evidence about changing dating norms over time
research on attitudes toward chivalry or gender roles
statistics or studies on household labor, gender expectations, or workplace equality

This would help move the essay from opinion to argument.

Address counterarguments more fully
One of the strongest parts of your essay is that you acknowledge positive outcomes of feminism, such as equal pay, career opportunity, and freedom from rigid domestic roles. Keep that. It shows fairness.

However, you should also engage more directly with the opposing view: some people may see traditional chivalry as tied to unequal assumptions about women needing protection or financial support. Even if you disagree, explain why you think respectful gestures can still exist without inequality.

That would deepen your argument:
Can chivalry be redefined as mutual courtesy rather than male dominance?
Could modern dating work better through communication rather than fixed gender roles?

These questions could help you develop a more nuanced position.

Improve organization
Your essay currently reads more like a personal reflection than a structured argument. To revise, try this structure:

Introduction  
   Present the topic and your thesis clearly.

Paragraph 1: Positive achievements of feminism  
   Acknowledge gains in equality and opportunity.

Paragraph 2: How dating expectations have changed  
   Explain the shift from traditional roles to less defined expectations.

Paragraph 3: Why you value chivalry

Paragraph 4: Counterargument and response  
   Address why some reject traditional gestures and explain your response.

Conclusion  
   Restate your position in a balanced way.

Sentence-level issues

“stigmatism” should be “stigma.”

Shorter, cleaner sentences will make your points easier to follow.

Final advice

To make the essay more persuasive, revise toward precision, balance, and evidence. Your essay has a clear central idea: crime has serious consequences, not just for victims but also for offenders and society. You also show awareness that “crime does not pay” can be understood in financial, moral, and social terms. That gives you a strong foundation. To revise effectively, focus on three main areas: clarity of thesis, organization, and sentence-level accuracy.

Clarify your thesis and purpose
