This helps in two ways:
It tells the reader exactly what your argument is.
It previews the main body points.

Improve paragraph structure and progression
Your body paragraphs each raise a relevant point, but the essay would be stronger if each paragraph had:
a clear topic sentence,

a specific example,
a link back to the thesis.

Paragraph 2: emotional and moral consequences for the offender
Paragraph 3: damage to neighborhoods and property values

Similarly, in the paragraph about criminal records, explain how background checks affect employment and housing opportunities.

The essay ends abruptly. A conclusion should not simply stop after the last point. Add a final paragraph that:
restates the thesis in new words,
summarizes the main reasons,
leaves the reader with a final thought.

For example, you might conclude that crime may seem profitable in the short term, but its lasting legal, emotional, and financial consequences prove otherwise.

“so to will the tax money” → “so too will the tax money”

“acquantances” → acquaintances
“oppurtunity” → opportunity

d) Capitalization

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear interest and strong personal engagement with the topic. You sound confident, and your energy gives the piece a distinctive voice. You also try to distinguish between “organized” and “unorganized” crime and make a debatable claim about why one is more profitable than the other. That said, the essay needs substantial revision to become a strong academic argument.

Clarify your thesis

Right now, your central claim is not fully clear at the beginning. The essay seems to argue that organized crime is more profitable and more protected by corruption than unorganized crime, but this point emerges gradually rather than being stated directly.

Try to present a thesis early, for example:

> While both organized and unorganized crime harm society, organized crime is more enduring and profitable because it relies on networks, money, and corruption.

A clear thesis will help readers follow your argument.

Strengthen organization

The essay has a basic two-part structure—first unorganized crime, then organized crime—but the introduction and conclusion do not frame the argument effectively.

Problems:
The opening is vague: “I have heard this statement…” does not tell the reader what statement you mean.
The ending shifts suddenly into personal biography, which does not belong in the conclusion unless the assignment specifically asks for it.
Some transitions are informal or unclear (“save the best for last”).

Revision advice:
Use a more traditional argumentative structure:
Introduction: define the issue and state your thesis.
Body paragraph 1: explain unorganized crime and its limits.
Body paragraph 2: explain organized crime and its structure.
Body paragraph 3: discuss corruption and profit.
Conclusion: restate the argument and explain its significance.

Remove the final paragraph about your name, age, and major unless required by the assignment.

Your essay often sounds conversational, which weakens its authority in an academic setting.

Examples:
“save the best for last”
“MONEY!”
“BIG sum of money”
“whomever coined this term is on point exactly”

These phrases create emphasis, but they are too informal for an argumentative essay. Also, first-person references such as “I know this” and “To me” are overused. First person is not always wrong, but here it often substitutes for evidence.

Better approach:
Instead of saying:
> “I know this… because of the class that I am currently taking”

write:
> “Organized crime can be defined as an ongoing collaboration between two or more individuals for profit through illegal activity.”

That shifts the focus from you to the argument.

Be careful with unsupported claims

This is the biggest issue. Many of your statements are very broad and need evidence.

Examples of claims needing support:
robbery, assault, and rape are “petty offenses”
unorganized criminals are “somewhat less educated”
police “only harass and arrest the petty criminals”
government officials are “willing to overlook illegal acts because of the payoff”

These are sweeping assertions. In an academic essay, you need credible evidence: statistics, course readings, scholarly sources, or specific examples.

Also, some claims are logically or ethically problematic. For instance, calling rape or assault “petty offenses” is inaccurate and seriously undermines your credibility. “Petty” usually refers to minor crimes; violent crimes are not minor, even if they do not involve large amounts of money.

Improve definitions and precision

Your categories need more careful treatment. “Organized” and “unorganized” crime are not simply divided by intelligence, education, or profitability. You should define each term more precisely.

For example:
Unorganized crime: spontaneous or individual criminal acts, often without long-term planning or group structure.
Organized crime: coordinated, ongoing criminal activity carried out by networks for profit.

This will make your comparison more analytical and less judgmental.

There are several awkward or incorrect phrases:
“both sides of the track” → usually “both sides of the tracks”
“will to deal with others” → “willing to deal with others”
“that is ongoing” → “that are ongoing” if referring to activities, or revise sentence structure
“through the form of payoffs” → “in the form of payoffs”

Also, some sentences are wordy and repetitive. For example:
> “There is one thing that makes this all possible: MONEY!”

could become:
> “The primary force sustaining organized crime is profit.”

That is more concise and academic.

Focus on analysis, not personal impression

You have strong opinions, but the essay will be more persuasive if you analyze causes and systems rather than presenting personal beliefs. For instance, your strongest material appears when you discuss how money, corruption, and institutional protection allow organized crime to continue. Expand that section with evidence and examples.

Revision priorities

Remove the autobiographical ending.
Replace informal language with academic wording.
Correct inaccurate or exaggerated claims.
Add evidence for every major assertion.
Define “organized” and “unorganized” crime more carefully.

You have a strong topic and a clear interest in it. With revision, this could become a focused argument about how structure, profit, and corruption make organized crime more durable than individual criminal acts.

Your essay has a clear position and a strong personal voice. What is working well

Awareness of complexity

This will make the essay feel more logical and less circular.

This is the biggest issue in the essay. Phrases such as:

“big chunky lesbian who hates all men”
“a bunch of whining women”
“all men are evil”
“Feminists are becoming anarchists”

weaken your credibility because they rely on stereotype and exaggeration rather than analysis. Even if you are describing a public caricature, the language is inflammatory and imprecise. In academic argument, you should describe perceptions more carefully, for example: *“Some critics stereotype feminists as anti-male, which oversimplifies the movement.”*

Susan B. Anthony is mentioned, but the broader history of feminism is not explained.
Claims about equal pay, sex crimes, and domestic violence need evidence or at least more specific explanation.
Statements about modern feminism being “misused” should be illustrated with examples, not just asserted.

To strengthen the essay, use at least a few concrete references: historical facts, named movements, legal changes, or specific debates.

Clarify key terms
You use “feminism,” “feminist cause,” “radical women,” and “modern day feminism,” but these are never clearly defined. Since your whole argument depends on distinguishing between “true” feminism and distorted versions of it, you need to define your terms more carefully.

For instance, what do you mean by feminism? Equality of legal rights? Social equality? Economic equality? And what exactly counts as “extreme”?

“A women” → A woman
“woman is no such an absurd notion” → women’s equality is no such absurd notion
“cases to an extremes” → cases to extremes
“lay the foundation” may need laid the foundation depending on the sentence structure

Also watch comma use, capitalization, and agreement.

Final advice

stating the thesis more clearly,

removing stereotypes and exaggeration,

and supporting claims with evidence.

This is the biggest area for revision. Your argument depends on historical examples, but several are either underdeveloped or questionable.

Susan B. Anthony, Elizabeth Cady Stanton, or Sojourner Truth for first-wave feminism
Betty Friedan, Gloria Steinem, or more radical groups from the 1960s–70s for second-wave feminism
Public myths such as “bra-burning” as an example of media distortion

Most importantly, explain your examples. Don’t just name people; show how they support your point.

“From the very beginning the Women’s Movement in America has been fighting a losing battle.”

These are sweeping claims and are historically hard to defend, especially since feminist movements achieved major successes: suffrage, workplace reforms, legal protections, and broader educational access. Your argument would be stronger if you said the movement faced public resistance and misrepresentation, not inevitable failure.

“wild, disgruntled zealots”
“So what am I getting at?”
“good of their cause”

“People normally associated with politics, religion, or art are quickly lumped into categories regardless of individual differences.”
“the public it was being aimed was not so jaded.”

Revise for clarity and precision.

Edit grammar, spelling, and mechanics
There are a number of sentence-level errors that distract from the argument. A few examples:
“Whenever a movement is began” → “Whenever a movement begins”
“oppostion” → “opposition”
“therfore” → “therefore”
“readiliy” → “readily”
“have promote” → “have promoted”
“in lieu of” is used incorrectly here; you likely mean “in favor of” or “because of attention to”

Also watch for comma errors:
“This ignorance of other less aggressive feminists, made it seem…” → remove the comma
“adamant, and often obstinate opposition” → no comma needed

What to do in revision
As you revise, focus on these priorities:
Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Reorganize body paragraphs so each one supports that thesis.
Replace weak or inaccurate examples with better historical evidence.
Moderate exaggerated claims.
Edit carefully for grammar and tone.

Final thought
Your essay has a promising central insight: social movements are often judged unfairly through stereotypes. That is a worthwhile argument. If you support it with more accurate examples, clearer structure, and a more polished academic style, the essay will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear topic and a confident argumentative voice. You establish a broad context by referring to the Beijing conference, then narrow the discussion to the United States. The piece also shows awareness of important historical milestones such as women’s suffrage and second-wave feminism. To revise effectively, focus on strengthening your argument, clarifying your thesis, and improving accuracy and style.

Clarify and deepen your main argument
Right now, the essay suggests that feminism has had a positive impact in the U.S. and that America is “an example for women around the world.” However, this claim remains quite general. A stronger argumentative essay needs a specific thesis that clearly states your position and hints at your reasons.

For example, your thesis could be something like:
*Feminism has transformed American society by expanding women’s political rights, educational access, and professional opportunities, although full equality has not yet been achieved.*

This would give the essay a clearer direction and help organize your body paragraphs.

Develop your evidence
Much of the essay relies on broad statements rather than concrete support. For instance, you say feminism opened doors in law schools, medical schools, and boardrooms, but you do not provide examples, evidence, or explanation. To make your argument more persuasive, add:
specific historical examples,
brief references to laws or social changes,
facts or trends where possible.

For example, you might mention:
the 19th Amendment and its significance,
Title IX and educational opportunities,
changes in women’s participation in higher education or the workforce.

You do not need a long list of statistics, but your points should be supported enough to move beyond assertion.

Address complexity
Your conclusion that “America continues to set an example for women around the world” is a strong claim, but it currently sounds too absolute. A stronger argument often acknowledges limitations or counterarguments. For example:
Women gained voting rights, but political equality did not automatically produce social or economic equality.
Access has improved, but pay gaps and underrepresentation in leadership still exist.

Including this complexity will make your essay more credible and intellectually mature.

Improve organization and paragraph focus
The essay generally follows a logical sequence, but the third paragraph tries to do too much at once: it discusses whether the “battle” continues, introduces major feminist figures, and then moves into women’s opportunities today. Consider dividing this into two paragraphs:
one on the continuation of feminist struggle,
one on the concrete achievements of modern feminism.

This will improve readability and help each paragraph develop one central idea.

Revise for tone and academic style
Your voice is energetic, but some phrases are too dramatic or repetitive for an academic argument. For example:
“battle cries”
“same sermon, from the same pulpit”
repeated phrases like “women of America” / “men of America”

These create a rhetorical style, but they can weaken precision. Aim for a more formal and analytical tone. Also, “I myself am American” makes the introduction personal, but the essay would sound more academic if you focused less on yourself and more on the topic.

Correct word choice and mechanical issues
There are several errors in spelling, grammar, and usage that need attention:

“World Conference Women” → likely *World Conference on Women*
“suffragates” → *suffragists* or *suffragettes*
“Betty Friedan and Gloria Steinam” → *Gloria Steinem*
“Women's access to law schools, medical schools, college campuses, and corporate board rooms have expanded”  
  Since the subject is *access*, use has expanded
“board rooms” → usually *boardrooms*
There is also a formatting/punctuation problem here:  
  “They have always preached the same sermon, from the same pulpit: .Throughout America…”  
  The placeholder needs to be replaced or removed, and there should be a space after the period.

Be careful with apostrophes, spelling of names, and subject-verb agreement, since these errors affect credibility.

Strengthen the conclusion
Your conclusion is too brief and does not fully reflect the discussion. Rather than ending with a single opinion sentence, try to:
restate your thesis,
summarize your main points,
leave the reader with a more thoughtful final idea.

For example, you might conclude that feminism has significantly expanded women’s rights in the U.S., but the movement remains necessary because legal gains do not automatically ensure full equality.

Revision priorities
Write a more specific thesis.
Add concrete supporting examples.
Separate large ideas into clearer paragraphs.
Acknowledge that progress is real but incomplete.
Edit for academic tone and sentence-level correctness.

Overall, the essay has a solid foundation and a clear sense of purpose. With more precise argumentation and stronger evidence, it could become a much more persuasive and polished piece.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and stays focused on the prompt: you argue that money itself is not the root of all evil, but that human choice and the love of money can lead to evil actions. This gives your paper a solid central claim. You also use several kinds of support—examples of honest ways to gain money, examples of crimes committed for money, and the point that charitable organizations depend on money. These are good starting points for an argumentative essay.

Strengths

Clear stance
   - From the introduction, readers understand that you disagree with the statement “Money is the root of all evil.”
   - Your conclusion returns to your main point that individual moral choice matters most.

Logical contrast
   - You effectively distinguish between money and the love of money/greed.
   - This distinction is the strongest idea in your essay and should remain the center of your revision.

Relevant examples
   - You mention both positive uses of money (employment, donations) and negative pursuits of money (lying, stealing, murder).
   - This balance helps show that money itself is neutral.

Main areas to revise

Make your thesis sharper and more direct
Right now, the introduction takes a while to reach your main argument, and the sentence about the phrase being “incorrect” is a little confusing. Also, the symbol “” suggests missing information.

Try revising the introduction so it clearly states:
what claim you are responding to,
your position,
and your main reason.

For example:
> Although people often say that money is the root of all evil, money itself is not evil. Rather, greed and immoral choices are what lead people to commit evil acts.

This kind of thesis is more precise and easier to defend.

Strengthen paragraph development
Several paragraphs make a good point, but they could be developed more fully. Often, you state an idea and then move on quickly.

For example:
In paragraph 2, you say that honest work and inheritance are legitimate ways of gaining money. Good point—but explain why this proves money is not evil.
In paragraph 3, you say money can motivate evil acts. Here you should clarify that this does not make money evil; it shows that desire, greed, or desperation can drive behavior.
In paragraph 5, your charity example is useful, but the paragraph becomes wordy and slightly repetitive. Tighten it and connect it more clearly to your thesis.

A helpful structure for each body paragraph is:
Topic sentence
Example
Explanation of how the example supports your argument

Improve organization and transitions
Your ideas are generally logical, but the essay would be stronger if each paragraph had a clearer role.

A possible structure:
Introduction: present the saying and your thesis
Body 1: money gained and used honestly is not evil
Body 2: people may commit evil acts to get money, but greed is the real cause
Body 3: money can also be used for good, such as charity
Conclusion: evil comes from human choices, not money itself

Use transitions such as:
“However,”
“In contrast,”
“For example,”
“Therefore”

These will help the essay flow more smoothly.

Refine wording for an academic tone
Some sentences sound conversational or imprecise. For an argumentative essay, aim for more formal and exact language.

Examples:
“wold” → would
“If money was the root” → If money were the root
“worth-while” → worthwhile
“will he choose” could become whether an individual chooses

Also, avoid broad claims that may be hard to prove, such as:
> “most if not all funds donated are used for the purposes intended”

That statement is too absolute. A more careful version:
> “many reputable organizations use donations to support important public needs.”

Watch grammar and sentence style
There are a few grammar and mechanics issues that you should correct in revision:
spelling: would, not *wold*
punctuation around quotations
parallel structure in lists
sentence clarity in long sentences

For instance, this sentence is awkward:
> “If one is attaining money honestly, how can honestly be associated with evil?”

A clearer version:
> “If a person earns money honestly, it is difficult to argue that money itself is evil.”

Final revision advice

As you revise, focus on making your essay argue one precise claim: money is a tool, and evil comes from the motives and choices of people. That idea is already present in your draft; now you need to express it more clearly, organize the support more carefully, and tighten your language.

Suggested next steps
Rewrite the introduction with a clear thesis.
Give each body paragraph one main point.
Add explanation after each example.
Revise for formal tone and grammar.
Make the conclusion echo the thesis more directly.

You have a strong core argument here. With clearer phrasing and stronger development, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

You have a clear central idea: money is necessary in modern life, but it becomes dangerous when it controls people rather than serving them. That is a strong basis for an argumentative essay because it presents a tension rather than a simple opinion. Your voice is confident, and several sentences are memorable, especially the final line: “It is for us to use, not for it to use us.” That gives the essay a clear moral conclusion.

What’s working well

Clear position
Your essay argues that money is indispensable, but that obsession with money and power corrupts people. This is a thoughtful stance because it avoids extremes.

Effective contrast
You use contrasts well:
money as a tool vs. money as a master
living with money vs. living for money
earned money vs. easily obtained money

These contrasts help organize your ideas and make your argument more persuasive.

Strong ending
The conclusion is concise and reinforces your main point. It leaves the reader with a clear takeaway.

Main areas to revise

Strengthen the thesis and overall focus
Right now, the essay begins broadly and then shifts among money, power, work ethic, and success. These ideas are related, but the essay needs a more precise thesis to unify them.

A stronger thesis might be something like:
Although money is essential in modern society, it should remain a tool for meeting needs and achieving worthwhile goals rather than becoming a source of power, status, or personal control.

This would help you connect all body paragraphs back to one argument.

Improve organization
The essay has good ideas, but the progression is somewhat loose. Try organizing it more deliberately:

Possible structure:
Introduction: explain why money is necessary today; present thesis.
Body paragraph 1: money is essential because modern life depends on it.
Body paragraph 2: once needs are met, people may seek power and status through money.
Body paragraph 3: people value money differently; earned money may be used more responsibly.
Body paragraph 4: true success should not be measured only in financial terms.
Conclusion: money should serve human values, not rule them.

Add topic sentences so each paragraph clearly advances the argument.

Develop ideas with examples
Many claims are interesting, but they remain general. To strengthen the argument, add specific examples or brief illustrations. For instance:
Why does wealth sometimes lead to a desire for power?
Can you mention a type of person who measures success only by income?
What would count as a “substantive accomplishment” beyond money?

Even one concrete example per paragraph would make the essay more convincing.

Clarify wording and sentence structure
Some sentences are hard to follow because of awkward phrasing or incomplete ideas. For example:

“Without money nothing works.”  
  Clear, but you could make it more formal:  
  “In modern society, it is difficult to function without money.”

“Once ones basic and comfort needs are met, what’s next, power or the power to influence.”  
  This needs correction and clearer structure:  
  “Once one’s basic needs and comforts are met, the next desire may be power or influence.”

“This begats the will for more power and the cicle goes on.”  
  Better:  
  “This can create a desire for even more power, and the cycle continues.”

Try reading each sentence aloud to see whether it sounds complete and natural.

Edit grammar, spelling, and punctuation carefully
There are frequent language errors that weaken the essay’s authority. Since your ideas are strong, editing will make a big difference.

Some corrections:
currupts → corrupts
ones → one’s
begats → likely begets
cicle → cycle
sence → sense
sucess → success
heir → likely for or on their
indespencable → indispensable
some where → somewhere

Also watch quotation use.  
You wrote: “Absolute power currupts absolute”  
The common phrase is: “Absolute power corrupts absolutely.”

Aim for a more formal academic tone
Your meaning is clear, but some phrasing sounds conversational:
“the almighty dollar”
“what’s next”
“went off the barter system years ago”

These expressions are vivid, but in an academic essay you may want slightly more formal wording unless you are deliberately using a rhetorical style.

Final advice for revision
When revising, focus on these priorities:
Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one develops one main point.
Add specific examples to support your claims.
Edit carefully for spelling, grammar, and punctuation.
Make wording more precise and formal.

You already have a strong core message and several effective contrasts. With clearer structure and careful editing, this essay could become much more persuasive and polished.

You have a clear central position: money itself is not evil; people’s attitudes and actions toward money can become harmful. That is a strong, arguable claim, and your essay stays focused on that issue throughout. You also use a helpful question-and-answer structure in the introduction and conclusion, which gives the piece a clear sense of purpose.

What’s working well

Clear stance
Your conclusion is consistent with the rest of the essay. The reader understands your answer to the prompt.

Awareness of complexity
You do not simply say “money is good” or “money is bad.” Instead, you distinguish between money as an object and human misuse of money. That distinction is the strongest idea in the essay.

Moral reasoning
You try to explain *why* people connect money with evil: greed, power, neglect of family, and harmful choices. This gives your argument a moral and social dimension.

---

Main areas to revise

Clarify the opening and thesis
The placeholder “You hear people say ” makes the introduction feel unfinished. Presumably, the phrase is “money is the root of all evil.” You should state that directly and then present your thesis in one clear sentence.

Current issue: the introduction asks several questions, but your own position comes a bit late.

Try this approach:
Introduce the saying.
Explain what people usually mean by it.
State your thesis clearly.

Possible revised thesis:

This would give the essay a sharper argumentative focus.

---

Strengthen organization and paragraph development
Right now, each paragraph contains a relevant idea, but some points are underdeveloped or repetitive. For example, paragraphs 2–4 all discuss desire, greed, and moving away from a Christian life. These could be organized more clearly.

A stronger structure might be:

Introduction + thesis
Why people say money leads to evil (greed, power, selfishness)
Why money itself is not evil (it is an object, morally neutral)
How money can be used for good (charity, education, shelter)
Conclusion

This structure would make your reasoning easier to follow.

---

Some sentences make absolute claims that may weaken your argument because they sound too broad.

For example:
“Your family and friends will become unimportant…”
“You will do almost anything for it.”
“The christian way of life is often thought of as being subservant, living without material things…”

These statements may not be true for everyone. In academic argument, it is better to qualify claims.

Instead of: “You will do almost anything for it.”  
Try: “Some people may become so consumed by wealth that they neglect their relationships or make unethical choices.”

This sounds more precise and credible.

---

Use examples or evidence
The essay is almost entirely based on personal opinion. For an argumentative essay, your ideas would be much stronger if you included:
a biblical reference, if you want to keep the Christian framework (for example, the difference between money and the *love of money*)
a real-world example of wealth being used destructively
a real-world example of wealth being used positively through charity, education, or community support

Even one or two specific examples would make the argument more persuasive.

---

Improve style and tone
Your meaning is usually clear, but some wording is awkward or informal. Try to use more precise language.

Examples:
“you always want more that you have” → “people often want more than they have”
“the life associated with gambling” → be more specific about debt, addiction, or crime
“how you allow it to use you” → interesting phrase, but explain it more carefully

Also, if you choose a Christian framework, make sure it is presented thoughtfully and consistently. Right now, the essay assumes a Christian perspective rather than arguing from it. Common issues:
than/that: “more that you have” should be “more than you have”
Capitalization: “christian” should be “Christian”; “God” is correctly capitalized
Spelling:
  - “subservant” → “subservient”
  - “mear” → “mere”
  - “can not” → usually “cannot”
  - “educated the uneducated” → “educate the uneducated”

  - “be lead” → “be led”

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a consistent argument: you argue that “crime does pay” by comparing criminal activity to conventional work in terms of job security, income, flexibility, and retirement. The piece is easy to follow because it is organized around these points. For revision, though, you need to strengthen the logic, improve the academic tone, and correct recurring grammar/mechanics issues.

Strengths
Clear thesis: Your opening immediately states your position.
Logical structure: Each body paragraph focuses on one basis of comparison.
Consistent focus: You stay on the main claim throughout the essay.

Main issue: argument and logic
The biggest weakness is that the essay treats crime as if it were simply another career option, while ignoring its major costs and consequences. Because of this, the argument feels one-sided and not fully credible.

What to revise
Acknowledge counterarguments. For example: arrest, imprisonment, violence, addiction, fines, restitution, injury, and loss of freedom all strongly challenge the claim that crime “pays.”
Question your assumptions. Statements like “A criminal will always be employed” and “The average criminal can work one hour a day and make $300” are sweeping generalizations. Who is the “average criminal”? What type of crime are you discussing?
Use realistic evidence. The essay relies on invented numbers rather than support from research. In an academic argument, you need facts, statistics, or examples from reliable sources.
Consider the difference between short-term gain and long-term outcome. Even if crime brings quick money, does it “pay” overall when legal punishment and personal risk are included?

A stronger version of your argument might be: although crime can produce short-term financial rewards, it rarely pays in the long run because the risks outweigh the benefits. Even if you want to keep your original position, you must address these objections.

Organization
Your essay has an introduction, body paragraphs, and a conclusion, which is good. However, the development within paragraphs is limited.

Suggestions
Begin each body paragraph with a clear topic sentence.
Follow with evidence or examples, not just assertions.
End each paragraph by explaining how the example supports the thesis.

For example, your second paragraph could focus specifically on “job security,” but then it should also address whether illegal work is actually secure when prison or police intervention can end it at any time.

Tone and style
The essay’s tone is too casual and sometimes sounds as though it endorses crime rather than analyzing the idea. In academic writing, aim for a more measured and analytical voice.

Examples
“Let’s take time to compare…” → more formal: “A comparison between criminal activity and legal employment reveals…”
“I’d definitely say…” → more academic: “Overall, this comparison suggests…”

Also avoid emotionally loaded or simplistic phrasing when more precise language would help.

Grammar, word choice, and mechanics
There are several sentence-level errors that need attention.

Common issues in your draft
its/it’s:  
  “Being a criminal has it’s advantages” → its advantages  
  (“it’s” = “it is”)
whether/rather:  
  “concerned with rather or not” → whether or not
Sentence error:  
  “There is never a lack of work are a means…” → probably or a means
Plural/singular agreement:  
  “a specific numbers of hours” → number
Verb form:  
  “insurance will costs them” → cost
Pronoun agreement:  
  “If he/she invest half this money…” → invests
Punctuation:  
  Several semicolons are misused. A semicolon should join two closely related independent clauses, not replace commas casually.
Hyphenation:  
  “hard working person” → hard-working person
Numbers/money formatting:  
  Be consistent and only use exact figures when supported by evidence.

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are wordy or awkward. For example:

“In our changing economy any individual who holds down a job must be concerned with whether or not he or she will continue to have that job.”  
  This repeats “job” too often. You could write:  
  “In a changing economy, employed people may worry about whether they will remain employed.”

“If a criminal starts at age 15 and works only 5 hours per week until age 30…”  
  Since “works” here refers to crime, you should be careful with wording so the meaning is precise and not unintentionally casual.

Revision priorities
Rethink the thesis so it is more nuanced and defensible.
Add evidence from credible sources rather than made-up averages.
Address counterarguments directly.
Revise for academic tone and remove informal phrasing.
Proofread carefully for grammar, punctuation, and word-choice errors.

Final note
You have a solid basic structure and a clear sense of argument, which is a good starting point. The next step is to make the essay more credible, balanced, and academically rigorous. Right now, the paper reads more like a provocative opinion piece than a strong academic argument. With better evidence, more nuance, and careful editing, it can become much more persuasive.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0028.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear argumentative direction and a strong sense of voice. The central claim is easy to identify: you argue that, for some urban youth, crime appears financially rewarding because poverty, limited opportunity, and weak deterrence make it attractive. That is a debatable claim, which is a good starting point for an argumentative essay. To revise effectively, focus on strengthening your reasoning, improving fairness and precision, and supporting your claims with evidence.

Clarify and narrow your thesis
Right now, the essay’s argument is broad and sometimes sounds absolute: “crime pays” and “the proof exists on any street in urban America.” These sweeping claims weaken credibility because readers will immediately think of exceptions.

Try revising the thesis so it is more precise and arguable. For example:

*In some low-income urban communities, crime can appear more rewarding than legal work because poverty, visible wealth from illegal activity, and weak enforcement create powerful incentives.*

This version keeps your main idea but avoids overgeneralization.

Avoid unsupported generalizations and stereotypes
A major issue is that many claims rely on assumption rather than evidence. For example:

“In many cases the answer is simple: crime.”
“The only danger that exists for a criminal come from other criminals.”
“Police are no threat as they are far outnumbered.”
“The proof exists on any street in urban America.”

These are very broad statements about “urban America,” “inner-city youth,” and crime. Without statistics or credible sources, they can come across as stereotyping rather than analysis. Also, linking luxury clothing and cars directly to criminal activity is a leap in logic.

To improve:
Replace absolute language with qualified language: *some, in certain cases, may, can appear*.
Distinguish between appearance and proof.
Add evidence: crime rates, incarceration rates, youth unemployment data, or studies on economic incentives and crime.

Build a stronger argumentative structure
At the moment, the essay reads more like a sequence of assertions than a developed argument. Consider organizing it around clear reasons that support your thesis. For instance:

Economic pressure and limited legal opportunities
The visible rewards of criminal activity
Perceived low risk of punishment
Why this creates a cycle

Then give each paragraph:
a topic sentence,
explanation,
evidence,
and analysis of how it supports your thesis.

This will make the essay feel more logical and persuasive.

Address counterarguments
A strong argumentative essay should consider opposing views. Your current draft assumes that legal work is always too unrewarding and that crime is rational. But a reader may object that:
crime also brings serious risks: prison, violence, addiction, trauma, instability;
many low-income youths do not choose crime;
structural inequality does not excuse harming others.

You do not need to abandon your argument, but you should acknowledge these points and respond to them. For example, you might argue that crime is not truly beneficial in the long term, but it can still appear attractive in the short term. That would make the essay more nuanced.

Improve tone and audience awareness
Some phrases sound provocative, sarcastic, or dismissive, which may alienate readers:
“a dog chasing its tail”
“killing babies”
“Why work at McDonalds...?”

These lines create emotional force, but they reduce the essay’s academic credibility. Aim for a more analytical tone. Instead of mocking legal work or exaggerating public attitudes, explain the economic comparison carefully.

For example, rather than “Why work at McDonalds...?”, you could say:
*For some youths, low-wage work may seem less attractive than illegal activities that promise faster financial gain.*

Revise for logic
There are places where the reasoning needs more care. For example, if crime “pays,” does that mean it is profitable overall, or only that it appears profitable to observers? Expensive cars and clothes may be signs of status, but not necessarily proof of stable wealth. You could strengthen your argument by discussing the difference between short-term visible gain and long-term consequences.

That distinction would make your position much more sophisticated.

Sentence-level corrections
There are several grammar and mechanics errors to fix:

“The only danger that exists for a criminal come from other criminals”  
  → comes
“as to wether one gets caught”  
  → whether
“The public calls for more police but refuse”  
  → refuses
There is an extra punctuation mark: “needed to hire them. .”
“no en in sight”  
  → likely end
Brand punctuation is inconsistent: “Guess? clothing”
Be careful with repeated phrasing and wordiness.

Also, some sentences are long and could be tightened for clarity.

A possible revision direction
You have the basis for an interesting argument if you shift from a sensational claim (“crime pays”) to a more analytical one about why crime may seem economically rational in certain environments. That would let you discuss poverty, policing, incarceration, and labor conditions in a more credible way.

Final advice
For your next draft:
Rewrite the thesis more narrowly.
Replace sweeping claims with qualified ones.
Add concrete evidence from reliable sources.
Organize body paragraphs around clear reasons.
Address at least one counterargument.
Adopt a more academic, less inflammatory tone.
Edit carefully for grammar and mechanics.

Your essay has energy and a clear point of view. The next step is to turn that strong opinion into a balanced, evidence-based argument.

===== ICLE-US-MICH-0001.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central concern: computers have brought major benefits while also creating intellectual and social problems. The piece shows ambition because it tries to weigh both sides rather than simply praise technology. For revision, focus on sharpening the thesis, improving logic and evidence, and adopting a more precise academic tone.

Main strength: a debatable topic with a clear tension
The essay’s most interesting idea is the “catch-22”: computers make life easier physically but harder mentally. That contrast gives you the basis for an argument. You also attempt to move beyond obvious benefits by discussing overreliance, human error, and job loss.

However, the essay currently reads more like a set of observations than a tightly argued position. Your revision should make the central claim more specific and then organize each body paragraph around proving it.

Thesis and argument
Your thesis now is:

> “The computer has made life easier from a physical standpoint and harder from a mental standpoint.”

This is clear, but it is still broad. What exactly does “harder from a mental standpoint” mean? Dependence? Reduced understanding? Anxiety? Poorer decision-making?

A stronger thesis might be:

> While computers have increased efficiency in workplaces and daily life, they have also encouraged overdependence on automated calculation and intensified social insecurity through job displacement.

This version is more precise and gives you two clear body sections:
efficiency and convenience  
dependence/error and social consequences

Organization
Your overall structure is logical: introduction, benefits, harms, conclusion. But some paragraphs mix ideas that should be separated.

Suggested structure
Introduction: context + precise thesis
Body 1: physical/practical benefits of computers
Body 2: intellectual dependence and misunderstanding of computer output
Body 3: automation, unemployment, and emotional/social consequences
Conclusion: restate argument and propose realistic responses

Right now, factory automation appears in both positive and negative ways without enough explanation. If job loss is one of your harms, develop it in a separate paragraph rather than mentioning it briefly in multiple places.

Evidence and development
The biggest weakness is lack of support. Many claims are asserted but not demonstrated. For example:
“This has created a surplus of ‘needless’ computations”
“Mathematical models, theories, etc are absolutely riddled with errors”
“This supposed reliability has left many workers out of work”

These could be persuasive, but you need examples, explanation, or evidence. In an argumentative essay, readers need to see *how* and *why* these claims are true.

How to improve
Give a concrete example of office efficiency: payroll, document editing, inventory systems.
Explain a case of overreliance: using spreadsheets without understanding formulas, or accepting a calculator result without checking whether it is reasonable.
Clarify job displacement: assembly lines, bank tellers, or administrative roles replaced by software or machines.

Even one brief example per paragraph would make the essay much stronger.

“catch-22” — vivid, but informal; use “paradox” or “double-edged development”
“etc” — avoid this in formal essays
“everyone already knows” — weakens your credibility; explain instead of assuming
“2nd & 3rd world countries” — outdated and inappropriate terminology; if relevant, use “lower-income countries,” though this point may be unnecessary
“he” for a general person — use gender-inclusive wording such as “they”
“absolutely riddled with errors” — too sweeping unless you can prove it

Try to use more careful language: “can lead to,” “may encourage,” “in some cases,” “often,” rather than universal statements.

There are several sentence-level issues to fix.

Common problems
Apostrophes: “1940's” should be 1940s
Spacing/punctuation: extra spaces appear throughout
Parallel structure:

Also watch for sentence boundaries. Some long sentences would be clearer if divided.

Revision priorities

Edit carefully for grammar, punctuation, and clarity.

Feedback Report

Your essay presents a clear position: computers have improved life in many ways, but they also make society more impersonal and overdependent on machines. This is a strong topic for an argumentative essay because it allows you to weigh benefits and drawbacks. You also show genuine engagement with the issue, which gives the essay energy. For revision, the main goals are to sharpen your thesis, improve organization, and make your claims more precise and formal.

What is working well

Balanced argument: You do not treat computers as purely good or bad; instead, you acknowledge both sides.
Clear topic: The essay consistently stays focused on the impact of computers on people’s lives.
Concrete examples: ATMs, banking, cars, and medical technology help ground your ideas in real-world situations.
Strong personal voice: Your concern about losing human interaction comes through clearly.

Main areas to improve

Strengthen the thesis
Your introduction gives your general opinion, but the thesis is somewhat vague and repetitive. For example, phrases like “I feel” appear often, which weakens the authority of your claim.

Instead of:
“These changes resulting from the invention of the computer, have been, I feel both bad and good.”

Try a more direct thesis:
“Although computers have made daily life faster and more efficient, they have also reduced human interaction and increased society’s dependence on technology.”

This version is clearer, more formal, and sets up the structure of the essay.

Improve paragraph organization
Your essay has a basic structure—introduction, benefits, drawbacks, conclusion—but each body paragraph would be stronger if it had:
a clear topic sentence,
supporting examples,
explanation of how those examples prove your point.

For instance, in the “benefits” paragraph, you mention writing papers, affordable personal computers, and ATMs. These are useful examples, but they need to be tied more explicitly to one central idea, such as efficiency and convenience.

Similarly, in the “drawbacks” paragraph, you discuss job loss, reduced interaction, danger, overreliance, and emotion. That is a lot for one paragraph. Consider splitting this into two body paragraphs:
one on loss of human interaction and jobs
one on overdependence and risk

This would make your reasoning easier to follow.

Use a more formal academic tone
Because this is an argumentative essay, try to reduce conversational phrasing, exaggeration, and repeated first-person expressions.

Examples to revise:
“I feel the invention of the computer…”
“Not to mention…”
“It seems these days EVERYTHING is run by computers!”
“a bunch of wires”

These phrases sound informal. A more academic style would be calmer and more precise. You can still express your opinion, but do so with confidence and control.

Be more precise with claims
Some statements are too broad or unsupported:
“Basically every facet of life has been affected by computers…”
“Interraction with humans in some places is extinct.”
“People soon stop thinking…”

These are interesting points, but they need either qualification or evidence. Instead of making absolute claims, use more careful language:
“Computers affect many areas of daily life.”
“In some settings, computers have reduced face-to-face interaction.”
“Overreliance on technology may weaken people’s problem-solving habits.”

This makes your argument more credible.

Revise sentence clarity and mechanics
There are several grammar, spelling, and punctuation issues that distract from your ideas. A few examples:

“people's life styles” → “people’s lifestyles”
“live transpire much quicker” → likely “life progress much more quickly”
“ATM” → “ATMs”
“proceed with the same transactions” → “perform the same transactions”
“Interraction” → “Interaction”
“obstical” → “obstacle”
“on-coming” → “oncoming”
“incapable, in some instances to function” → “incapable, in some instances, of functioning”

Also watch comma placement. For example:

The comma after “computer” is unnecessary.

Revision priorities
Write a clearer, more direct thesis.
Reorganize the body so each paragraph focuses on one main point.
Replace informal phrases with more academic language.
Qualify broad claims and explain examples more fully.
Proofread carefully for spelling, grammar, and punctuation.

Final thought

Feedback Report

Your essay identifies an important issue: the gap between the American ideal of equal opportunity and the reality of inequality in a fast-paced, media-driven society. You have a strong critical perspective, and your central concern is clear. To revise effectively, focus on clarifying your thesis, organizing your ideas, and improving sentence-level correctness.

Main strengths
Interesting topic and argument: You challenge common assumptions about success, equality, and individual responsibility in the U.S.
Thoughtful social critique: Your comments about mass media, technology, and the tendency to blame individuals for structural problems suggest strong analytical potential.
Ambitious scope: You are trying to connect ideology, history, media, and modern life, which gives the essay intellectual depth.

Main areas to revise

A. Clarify the thesis
Right now, your opening paragraph raises several claims at once:
success requires following social rules
everyone is said to have equal opportunity
failure is blamed on the individual
American founding values have changed
equality was never real

These ideas are related, but they need to be shaped into one focused thesis. A thesis should make a clear, arguable claim that the rest of the essay can support.

For example, a stronger thesis might be:
> In modern America, media and social expectations promote the belief that everyone has an equal chance to succeed, but this ideology hides social inequality and encourages people to blame individuals for failures caused by larger structural problems.

That version combines your main ideas into one argument.

B. Improve organization
At the moment, the essay reads more like a set of connected reflections than a structured argument. To revise, give each paragraph a distinct purpose.

A possible structure:
Introduction: explain the ideology of success and present your thesis.
Body paragraph 1: show how media promotes narrow models of success and behavior.
Body paragraph 2: explain how American ideals of equality conflict with lived reality.
Body paragraph 3: discuss how technology, speed, and consumer culture make inequality easier to ignore.
Conclusion: restate the main argument and explain why it matters.

Each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that connects back to the thesis.

C. Develop ideas with evidence and explanation
Many of your points are compelling, but they remain general. For example, you mention TV commercials and programs, but you do not explain how they promote these values. Try to add:
specific examples from media
explanation of how those examples shape public attitudes
clearer links between technology, individualism, and inequality

For instance, instead of saying:
> T.V. comercials & T.V. programs project models of how one should be.

You could write:
> Television commercials and entertainment programs often present success as the result of personal choice, discipline, and consumption, encouraging viewers to believe that those who fail simply have not worked hard enough.

That gives more interpretation and makes the argument more persuasive.

A. Run-on sentences and punctuation
Several sentences combine too many ideas without proper punctuation. For example:
> In the 20th cen, the values of/that the United States was founded on have changed in meaning and sense when applied to modern day America, nothing was ever equal.

This is a run-on sentence. You need to separate or restructure the ideas:
> In the twentieth century, the values on which the United States was founded changed in meaning when applied to modern America. In reality, equality was never fully achieved.

B. Word choice and phrasing
Some phrases are unclear or awkward:
“realitive humanness” → perhaps shared humanity
“attribute thier inadequacy to achieve” → attribute their lack of success
“The parts of the new machine that need oiled and regulated” is interesting as an image, but incomplete and unclear. If you want to use this metaphor, explain it more fully.

C. Grammar and spelling

comercials → commercials
everyday → every day
americans → Americans
substitued → substituted
nolonger → no longer
realitive → relative
unsuccessfull → unsuccessful
thier → their

Also avoid informal abbreviations like T.V. and 20th cen. in a formal academic essay. Write television and twentieth century.

Avoid repeating “one” too often; it sounds stiff and repetitive. You can often use people, individuals, or Americans.
Break long sentences into shorter ones to make your meaning easier to follow.

Fix run-on sentences.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a strong voice and clear passion. You are trying to make a nuanced argument: technology itself is not inherently harmful, but the way societies use it can produce isolation, ignorance, and cultural narrowness. That is a promising and arguable position. To revise effectively, focus on clarifying your thesis, organizing your ideas more logically, and improving sentence-level precision.

Clarify the main argument

Right now, the essay begins broadly with “technology as a whole,” then moves into personal benefits, then into criticism of American education and cultural attitudes. These ideas are related, but the exact claim is not fully clear.

It seems your real argument is something like:

> Technology has brought major benefits, but in the United States it has also contributed to cultural isolation, especially among young people, because it is used in ways that discourage global awareness and language learning.

If that is your point, state it directly in the introduction. A stronger thesis will help the reader understand why the examples about geography, schools, and foreign languages matter.

Strengthen organization

The essay would benefit from a clearer structure. At the moment, some paragraphs combine too many ideas. Consider this progression:

Introduction: present the balanced claim that technology is both beneficial and dangerous depending on how people use it.
Paragraph 1: explain the benefits of technology, using your family and travel examples.
Paragraph 2: introduce the problem—technology can foster isolation and complacency.
Paragraph 3: show this problem in the U.S. educational/cultural context (geography knowledge, limited language study, lack of global awareness).
Conclusion: explain what should change.

This would help the essay feel more focused and persuasive.

Develop evidence more carefully

Your essay relies mostly on personal experience, which gives it sincerity, but for an argumentative essay, you should also explain how each example proves the broader claim.

For example:
The anecdote about children asking whether Spain is in Michigan is vivid, but you need to connect it explicitly to your argument. Is the problem technology itself? Or poor education? Or a culture made inward-looking by convenience and media?
Your point about foreign language instruction is compelling, but again, explain how this relates to technology. Does technology make Americans feel they do not need other languages? Does it encourage a false sense of self-sufficiency?

Right now, some evidence supports a claim about American education and culture more than a claim about technology. Make sure those links are explicit.

Improve precision and tone

You have some memorable phrasing, but a few expressions are overly dramatic or unclear for academic writing.

Examples:
“the greatest Nemesis of our world” feels too grand and vague.
“a great majority of Americans live in their own personal closets” is striking, but it may sound more rhetorical than analytical.
“The effort of the United States has caused our minds to be heard” is unclear in meaning.

Try to use language that is forceful but more precise. Academic argument is usually stronger when it explains rather than dramatizes.

Revise sentence structure and grammar

Several sentences are run-ons or contain comma splices. For example:

“Science and technology have allowed me to travel to this part of the world, they have provided a living for my family…”  
  This should be broken into smaller sentences or joined properly with conjunctions/semicolons.

“Since I was twelve, I had an intense desire to learn Spanish, and because of these circumstances, that I mentioned before, I had no choice…”  
  This sentence is too long and awkward. Simplify it.

Also watch for:
agreement errors: “technology has become a large detriment” is acceptable, but later “Science and technology have had effects… and I feel that its people…” should be “their people” if the subject is plural.
hyphenation/punctuation: “self-sufficient- I don't need any help” should be punctuated more clearly.
word choice: “wherein the greatest problems lie” sounds formal but not natural in this context.

End with a stronger conclusion

The last sentence is difficult to understand and does not clearly conclude the argument. Your conclusion should restate the main claim and suggest the broader importance. For example, you might argue that unless technology is paired with education, curiosity, and cultural openness, its benefits will be undermined by social ignorance.

What to do in revision

As you revise, try these steps:
Write a one-sentence thesis that clearly connects technology to cultural isolation.
Make sure each paragraph has one main idea.
Add topic sentences that show how each paragraph supports the thesis.
Explain how each example proves the argument.
Edit for shorter, clearer sentences.

Final comment

This essay has strong potential because it comes from genuine experience and raises an interesting contradiction: technology can connect the world, yet people may still become narrower in outlook. Your next step is to make that insight more focused, better supported, and more clearly expressed.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear topic and a consistent position: computers are an important invention that people now depend on in everyday life. The writing is easy to follow overall, and your introduction and conclusion connect well through the repeated question about life without computers. That gives the essay a sense of unity.

What is working well

Clear central idea  
Your main claim is obvious from the beginning: computers have changed people’s lives and become essential. This is good for an argumentative essay because the reader always knows what you are trying to prove.

Logical overall structure  
The essay moves in a sensible order:
computers are part of daily life,
people rely on them,
they have changed society,
therefore they are one of the greatest inventions.

This progression helps the reader follow your reasoning.

Relevant examples  
You mention homes, schools, offices, shopping, businesses, and job skills. These examples show that computers affect many parts of life, which supports your main argument.

Main areas to revise

Strengthen the thesis
Right now, the essay is more explanatory than argumentative. You clearly say computers are important, but the thesis should make a sharper claim.

For example, instead of:
> “Computers have significantly changed people's lives.”

you could write something like:
> “Computers are one of the most important inventions of modern life because they increase efficiency, expand access to information, and shape work, education, and daily routines.”

This version gives the reader a roadmap for the body paragraphs.

Develop each body paragraph more fully
Many of your points are repeated rather than expanded. For example, you say several times that computers are useful, efficient, and part of daily life. Instead of repeating the same idea, try giving specific support.

For instance:
In education: explain how students use computers for research, online learning, writing, and communication.
In business: explain how computers manage data, speed communication, and automate routine tasks.
In personal life: explain banking, navigation, entertainment, shopping, and social connection.

A strong body paragraph should include:
a topic sentence,
explanation,
one or two specific examples,
a sentence linking back to your argument.

Address the “drawbacks” point more clearly
Near the end, you briefly mention drawbacks and what happens when computers break down. This is an interesting idea, but it appears very late and is not developed. Since this is an argumentative essay, you could improve it by including a short counterargument.

For example:
> “Although computers make life easier, dependence on them can create problems when systems fail. However, this dependence also shows how deeply computers are integrated into modern society.”

This would make your essay feel more thoughtful and balanced.

Sentence-level issues to correct

Grammar and word choice
There are several sentences with awkward or incorrect phrasing. A few examples:

“For many people computers have become a part of their everyday life.”  
  Add a comma after “people.”  
  Corrected: “For many people, computers have become a part of everyday life.”

“In people's homes calculators as usually found…”  
  “as” should be “are.” Also, calculators are not usually considered the same as computers in this context.  
  Possible revision: “In many homes, personal computers are common household tools.”

“speed-up many laboreous tasks”  
  “speed up” should not be hyphenated here, and “laboreous” should be laborious.  
  Corrected: “speed up many laborious tasks.”

“Computers speed-up tasks that used to take up a lot of time.”  
  Better: “Computers speed up tasks that once took a great deal of time.”

“People have come to depend on the function of a computer to help everyday life run smoothly.”  
  This sounds awkward.  
  Possible revision: “People have come to depend on computers to keep everyday life running smoothly.”

Repetition
You repeat words and ideas too often, especially “computers,” “everyday life,” and “rely/depend.” Repetition can make the essay sound basic. Try using variation where appropriate:
technology
digital tools
these machines
such devices

Also vary sentence structure so each paragraph does not sound the same.

Accuracy
You write:
> “the invention of the calculator (the first computer)”

This is historically inaccurate or at least oversimplified. If you want to mention the history of computers, use a more accurate statement, such as:
> “since the invention of early computers”
or
> “since the development of modern computing technology”

Organization suggestions
A clearer structure for revision might be:

Introduction
Hook
background on how common computers are
strong thesis with 2–3 reasons

Body Paragraph 1: Daily life
home use, communication, shopping, entertainment

Body Paragraph 2: Education and work
schools, offices, productivity, job skills

Body Paragraph 3: Dependence and possible drawbacks
what happens when systems fail
brief counterargument
explain why this still proves their importance

Conclusion
restate thesis
emphasize how deeply computers shape modern life

Final advice
Your essay has a solid foundation, but it needs more specific evidence, sharper organization, and careful editing. Focus especially on making your argument more precise and reducing repetition. If you revise with clearer topic sentences and more developed examples, this could become a much stronger argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has an ambitious and interesting goal: you try to connect Einstein’s theory of relativity to broader philosophical and political consequences, especially the development of atomic weapons and a global culture of fear. That is a strong starting point for an argumentative essay because it reaches beyond summary and tries to make a claim about significance. To revise effectively, focus on clarifying your thesis, improving organization, and strengthening accuracy and style.

Main strength: ambitious thinking
The most promising part of your draft is that you are not simply explaining relativity; you are arguing that Einstein’s ideas changed how humanity thinks about knowledge, time, and global politics. This gives your essay intellectual ambition. Lines such as your claim that relativity challenged “closure” and that fear has shaped the global community show that you are aiming for a philosophical argument, not just a report.

Clarify the thesis
Right now, the central argument is hard to identify because the introduction moves quickly across several ideas: Einstein’s genius, relativity, quantum mechanics, philosophy, time, and atomic bombs. A reader needs one clear thesis near the end of the introduction.

For example, your thesis could become something like:

“Einstein’s theory of relativity transformed modern thought by challenging fixed ideas of time and knowledge; however, its most visible impact in the twentieth century came through humanity’s destructive political use of scientific discovery, especially atomic weapons.”

This kind of sentence would give the essay a clear direction and help unify the body paragraphs.

Improve organization
At present, each paragraph raises a different issue, but the connections are not always clear. Try organizing the essay into a logical progression:

Introduction: introduce Einstein and state your thesis.
Paragraph 1: explain what relativity changed in human thought (time, perspective, certainty).
Paragraph 2: discuss the philosophical impact—why relativity challenged older assumptions about truth or closure.
Paragraph 3: connect scientific change to twentieth-century politics, especially atomic weapons.
Conclusion: explain why this matters now and what humanity should learn from it.

This structure would help the reader follow your reasoning.

Develop ideas with evidence
Many of your claims are intriguing but underdeveloped. For instance:

“What Einstein did to humanity’s philosophy was change the notion that closure could be obtained.”
“Fear has been the main point of departure for the global community construction.”

These statements need explanation and support. Ask yourself:
How did relativity change philosophy?
Which philosophers or ideas are you referring to?
How exactly did atomic weapons create a global system based on fear?
What historical evidence supports the USA-USSR claim?

Even a few concrete examples would make your argument more persuasive.

Be careful with factual accuracy
Some statements are inaccurate or misleading, which weakens credibility:

Einstein is spelled Einstein, not “Einstien.”
“his and his wife’s” is controversial and would need careful support if included; otherwise, avoid it.
Relativity did not simply “come with” quantum mechanics; they are distinct areas of physics.
Einstein did not “invent” the atomic bomb. His work contributed to modern physics, but the bomb was developed by many scientists and political actors.
Stephen Hawking, not “Hawkings.”

Because this is an academic essay, accuracy matters a great deal.

Style and tone
Your style is often passionate, which is good, but some phrases are too vague or poetic for an academic argument. For example:

“volumes, volumes a literature”
“utopic and nostalgic dust”
“indwell the topic”

These expressions are interesting, but they make your meaning unclear. Aim for language that is precise rather than impressionistic. Academic writing does not need to sound flat, but it should be easy to follow.

Also avoid overusing semicolons and sentence fragments. For example:
“The Socratic idea that all was known. And that it was only a matter of time...”
This should be revised into a complete sentence.

Sentence-level corrections
There are frequent problems with spelling, punctuation, capitalization, and grammar. A few patterns to review:

Spelling: Einstein, histories → history’s or in recorded history, nostalgic, shortcomings, Hawking
Capitalization: Socratic, Einstein
Punctuation: avoid unnecessary semicolons; don’t capitalize words after semicolons unless needed
Sentence fragments: several sentences are incomplete
Word choice: “setup” should be “set up” as a verb

You should proofread carefully or read the essay aloud to catch awkward sentences.

Revision priorities
If you revise, I recommend this order:

Write a clear one-sentence thesis.
Reorganize the essay around 3 main points.
Replace vague claims with explanations and examples.
Correct factual inaccuracies.
Edit for grammar, spelling, and punctuation.

Final thought
This draft shows originality and intellectual risk-taking, which is valuable. Your next step is to make the essay more focused, more accurate, and easier for the reader to follow. You already have a strong idea; now you need to shape it into a clear academic argument.

Feedback Report

You have a clear position and a strong sense of investment in your topic. Your essay argues that shopping malls have harmed downtown Kalamazoo, especially poor and working-class residents, and that central-city decline is tied to suburbanization and class separation. This is a thoughtful argument with real social awareness. For revision, focus on sharpening your thesis, organizing your ideas more logically, and improving sentence-level correctness and precision.

What’s working

Clear stance
You make your opinion known early: malls are “more of a detriment than a blessing.” That gives the essay direction.

Strong local focus
Using Kalamazoo makes the essay concrete rather than abstract. Readers can see that you are writing from experience and observation.

Important social argument
Your strongest idea is that malls do not just change where people shop; they change who has access to business, investment, and opportunity. That is a meaningful claim.

Main revision priorities

Strengthen the thesis
Right now, your thesis states your opinion, but it does not fully preview your reasons. A stronger thesis should name the main harms you will discuss.

For example:
“Although shopping malls offer convenience, they have harmed Kalamazoo by drawing businesses and wealth away from downtown, increasing class division, and weakening neighborhoods that depend on local investment.”

This gives your essay a clearer roadmap.

Improve organization
The essay has good ideas, but they sometimes appear in a rushed or uneven order. Try organizing by point, not by stream of thought.

A possible structure:
Introduction: background on Kalamazoo and thesis
Body paragraph 1: how downtown originally functioned as a shared center
Body paragraph 2: how cars, suburbs, and malls pulled wealth and businesses outward
Body paragraph 3: effects on poor and working-class residents
Body paragraph 4: attempts to revive downtown (colleges, festivals) and why they are limited
Conclusion: restate argument and emphasize consequences

This will help each paragraph develop one main idea.

Use more formal academic tone
At times, the essay shifts into conversational language, such as:
“Why should we…”
“and still could be!”
“where the more poorer people are”

These moments show emotion, but they weaken the academic tone. Instead of rhetorical questions and exclamations, use direct analytical statements.

For example:
Less formal: “Why should we… go downtown… when I can go to a nice indoor mall?”
More formal: “For suburban shoppers with cars, indoor malls offer convenience, comfort, and concentration of services that downtown shopping districts often cannot match.”

Clarify cause and effect
Your central argument depends on a chain of causes:
cars/suburbs -> malls and strip malls -> businesses leave downtown -> poor residents lose access to investment.

That logic is strong, but you need to explain it more carefully and precisely. Sometimes the essay jumps too quickly from one historical point to another. Add transitions such as:
“As a result,”
“This shift led to…”
“Consequently,”
“In turn…”

Sentence-level issues to correct

There are frequent grammar, word choice, and proofreading problems. These make your ideas harder to follow.

Common issues:
Word choice
“clientel” -> clientele
“the more poorer people” -> poorer people or low-income residents
“flu from the inner cities” -> likely flight from the inner cities
“There neighborhoods” -> Their neighborhoods
“many business try” -> many businesses try

Agreement and form
“1 large university, 1 private college and two…”  
  Better in formal writing: “one large university, one private college, and two community colleges.”
“office building still remain” -> office buildings still remain

Sentence boundaries and punctuation
Some sentences are run-ons or use awkward punctuation. For example:
“Everyone was near the central city - so everyone could use the central city.”
This could become:
“Because most residents lived near the city center, people from different social classes could use the same businesses and public spaces.”

Capitalization
Avoid beginning a sentence with lowercase:
“and still could be!”

Development and evidence
Your argument would be much stronger with specific examples. Right now, many claims are broad but unsupported. Consider adding:
names of malls or shopping areas in Kalamazoo
examples of downtown stores or industries that closed or moved
details about festivals or college-related businesses
if appropriate, brief facts about suburban growth or downtown decline

Even a few concrete examples would make the essay more persuasive.

Final advice
As you revise, focus less on saying everything at once and more on guiding the reader through your argument step by step. You already have a compelling central idea: malls reshape cities in ways that hurt those with the fewest choices. Your next draft should make that idea clearer, better organized, and more polished.

A possible revised claim for your conclusion
You might end with something like:
“In Kalamazoo, shopping malls have not simply provided a new place to shop; they have accelerated the movement of wealth, businesses, and opportunity away from downtown. As a result, the residents least able to leave the city center are the ones who bear the greatest costs.”

That would give your essay a strong, focused ending.
