Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: television has great potential, but in practice it has harmed society by encouraging escapism, laziness, and weaker family relationships. The paper is readable and focused, and you do a good job of balancing television’s benefits against its negative effects before arguing your main claim. That structure gives the essay a thoughtful tone rather than a one-sided one.

What is working well

Clear argumentative direction  
Your thesis is consistent throughout the essay. You argue that television has changed society significantly, and you emphasize that its actual influence has been more harmful than beneficial.

Effective contrast
You repeatedly use the contrast between television’s *potential* and its *real effect*. This is your strongest idea, and it gives the essay coherence.

Strong, confident voice  
Phrases such as “escape route of the masses” and “one has only to turn it on to tune out reality” are vivid and persuasive. They show that you are trying to do more than summarize; you are making an argument.

Main areas to revise

Make the thesis more precise
Right now, your main claim is spread across several sentences. Try to state it in one clear sentence at the end of the introduction.

For example:  
Although television has expanded access to information and culture, its dominant effect on society has been negative because it encourages passive entertainment, weakens family interaction, and discourages active engagement with real life.

This version gives the reader a roadmap for the body paragraphs.

Strengthen paragraph development
Your body paragraphs make claims, but they need more explanation and support. Often you move quickly from one general statement to another.

For instance, in paragraph three, you argue that television:
is seen as entertainment rather than education,
dominates households,
reduces family togetherness,
increases laziness.

These are important points, but they are crowded together. Consider separating them more clearly:
one paragraph on television as passive entertainment,
one paragraph on effects on family life,
one paragraph on motivation/laziness.

Then, for each paragraph, add specific examples or reasoning. Ask:
*Why* does television reduce family interaction?
*How* does it encourage passivity?
Are there examples of the kinds of programs or habits you mean?

Avoid overgeneralization
Some claims are too absolute, which can weaken the argument because readers may question them.

Examples:
“In the minds of most Americans…”
“Millions of Americans can be found to be staring at the T.V. at any one time…”
“T.V. is a major cause of lack of togetherness…”

These statements may be rhetorically strong, but they need evidence or more careful wording. You could revise to:
“For many Americans…”
“Television often functions as…”
“Television can contribute to a lack of togetherness…”

Academic argument is often stronger when it sounds measured rather than sweeping.

Improve logical consistency
At times, the essay suggests television both brings families together and causes a lack of togetherness. That is an interesting tension, but you need to explain it more clearly. For example, you might argue that families may be physically in the same room while not truly interacting. That would resolve the apparent contradiction.

Edit sentence-level grammar and word choice
There are several language issues that you should correct in revision:

“has potential” → has the potential
“than cannot be given by newspaper” → than can be given by newspapers
“T.V. is a major cause of lack of togetherness” → T.V. is a major cause of the lack of togetherness
“could hardly have been dreamed of 00 years ago” → could hardly have been dreamed of 100 years ago
“integrety” → integrity
“dificulties” → difficulties
“has becomed” → has become

Also watch article use (“the newspaper” / “newspapers”), plural forms, and spacing around punctuation.

Style suggestions

Be careful with repetition.  
You repeat “society” very often, especially in the introduction and conclusion. Try substituting terms like people, viewers, families, American culture, public life where appropriate.

Use consistent form for television.  
You switch between “television,” “TV,” and “T.V.” Choose one form and use it consistently. In a formal essay, television or TV is best.

Sharpen the conclusion.  
Your conclusion mostly repeats earlier ideas. Instead, restate the thesis more forcefully and leave the reader with a final insight. For example, you might argue that the problem is not the technology itself, but the way society chooses to use it.

Revision priorities
Write a sharper one-sentence thesis.
Reorganize body paragraphs so each has one main focus.
Add specific explanation/examples to support claims.
Qualify broad statements to sound more credible.
Proofread carefully for spelling, grammar, and consistency.

Overall, this essay has a solid central argument and a strong sense of purpose. With more precise organization, better support, and careful editing, it could become a much more convincing academic argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central claim: the polio vaccine was one of the most important inventions of the twentieth century because it saved lives and helped shape later medical progress. That is a strong and interesting argument. You also show awareness of audience by contrasting how younger people think about polio versus how earlier generations feared it. This gives the topic relevance.

To revise effectively, focus on clarity of argument, organization, evidence, and sentence-level correctness.

Strengthen the thesis

Your introduction has energy, but the thesis is currently too broad and sometimes overstated. For example, “the greatest invention and discovery” and “inequivalent to any other invention” are difficult to prove. In an argumentative essay, a more precise and defensible claim is stronger.

Possible revision of the thesis:
> The development of the polio vaccine was one of the most significant medical achievements of the twentieth century because it dramatically reduced childhood death and disability and paved the way for later advances in disease prevention.

This version is arguable, specific, and easier to support.

Improve organization

Right now, the essay has a basic beginning-middle-end structure, but the body paragraphs need clearer focus.

A stronger structure might be:

Introduction: background on disease and thesis
Body paragraph 1: how polio affected people before the vaccine
Body paragraph 2: how the vaccine reduced death/disability and changed childhood life
Body paragraph 3: how the success of the vaccine influenced medical research and public confidence in vaccination
Conclusion: restate the significance of the vaccine

At present, your second paragraph moves between several ideas: young people today, AIDS, parents’ fears, the Baby Boom, population control, and disease generally. That makes the argument feel scattered. Try to keep one main idea per paragraph.

Use stronger evidence and avoid speculation

Your essay would be much more persuasive with specific evidence. For example:

How common was polio before the vaccine?
What kinds of disability did it cause?
How much did cases decline after Salk’s vaccine was introduced?
How did the vaccine influence later medical developments?

Some claims need either support or removal. For example:

“whether it had anything to do with the Baby Boom is for each individual to decide”
“Every few years a new one comes along to normalize an overpopulated world”
“The polio vaccine stopped death in its path”

These statements are either speculative, too absolute, or not academically grounded. In formal argument, avoid unsupported generalizations. Instead, make careful claims that you can defend.

For example, instead of linking polio vaccination to the Baby Boom, you could say:
> By reducing childhood illness and disability, the vaccine helped more children survive and live healthy lives.

That is clearer and easier to support.

Maintain an academic tone

Some phrases sound conversational or emotional rather than formal:

“For sure”
“I'm sure”
“Things weren't impossible any more”
“The polio vaccine stopped death in its path”

Academic writing works better when it is measured and precise. You can still sound passionate, but use evidence-based language.

For example:
Replace “For sure there was an effect” with “The vaccine had a significant social impact.”
Replace “The polio vaccine stopped death in its path” with “The vaccine sharply reduced the death and disability caused by polio.”

Also, avoid using “&” in formal writing; write “and.”

Revise sentence clarity and grammar

There are many sentence-level issues that make your ideas harder to follow. Main concerns:

Spelling
“mamed” → maimed

Word choice
“future’s children” → future generations or children of future generations
“inequivalent” is not the right word here; try unmatched or unequaled
“utilized” can usually be simplified to used

Grammar and punctuation
Add commas after introductory phrases:  
  “Unfortunately, this still occurs...”
Avoid repeated words:  
  “never never”
Be careful with fragments and run-on sentences.
Avoid random capitalization:  
  “CAUSE” should not be capitalized for emphasis in an academic essay.

Example revision
Original:
> For 20 year olds polio has never never been an issue, it is one of the many vaccines we received as a child and for us the new dreaded ailment is AIDS, which we have no cure for...

Possible revision:
> For many twenty-year-olds, polio is not a personal fear because they received the vaccine in childhood. For their parents and grandparents, however, polio was a serious and frightening disease.

This breaks one long sentence into clearer parts.

Clarify your conclusion

Your final paragraph introduces an important point: the success of vaccination changed what medicine believed was possible. That idea could become a strong conclusion if developed more clearly.

For instance:
> Beyond saving lives, the polio vaccine demonstrated that scientific research could prevent devastating disease on a large scale. Its success strengthened public trust in vaccination and encouraged future medical breakthroughs.

Revision priorities
Write a more precise thesis.
Reorganize body paragraphs around distinct reasons.
Remove speculation and add factual support.
Use a more formal academic tone.
Edit carefully for grammar, punctuation, and word choice.

You have a promising topic and a meaningful argument. With clearer structure and more evidence, this essay could become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear main idea: the airplane has transformed modern life in several major areas. You also do a good job organizing the body into categories—travel, business, entertainment, and international relations—which gives the paper a logical structure. The essay shows that you have a strong sense of topic development. For revision, focus mainly on sharpening your thesis, improving sentence-level correctness, and making your evidence more precise.

Strengthen the thesis
Right now, your introduction states that airplanes changed people’s lives and then lists several affected areas. That is a good start, but your thesis could be more specific and argumentative. Instead of simply saying airplanes changed life, make a claim about how and to what extent they changed it.

For example:
*The invention of the airplane revolutionized modern life by shrinking travel time, expanding international business and entertainment, and reshaping political relations between nations.*

This version is clearer, more formal, and previews your body paragraphs.

Improve paragraph focus and development
Each body paragraph has a clear topic, which is a strength. However, some points need more explanation and stronger evidence.

Travel paragraph
This is one of your strongest paragraphs because you include a concrete comparison (driving from Michigan to California vs. flying). Keep that kind of example. However, make sure your claim is accurate and consistent: flying that distance in “about 3 hours” may be too short depending on the route, so be careful with specifics.

Intercontinental relations paragraph
This is an important idea because it introduces both positive and negative effects. That makes your argument more thoughtful. However, the paragraph becomes a little unclear at the end. You should explain more explicitly that airplanes have made diplomacy faster and more frequent, but they have also made warfare more destructive.

Use a more formal academic tone
Your essay sometimes sounds conversational. Phrases like:
*“As I’ve already said”*
*“I’m going to touch on”*
*“definitely flourished”*

make the essay sound informal. In academic writing, aim for a more neutral tone.

For example:
*“As previously noted”* instead of *“As I’ve already said”*
*“The final area to consider is…”* instead of *“The last area I’m going to touch on is…”*

Also, avoid using “&” in formal writing. Write out and.

Correct grammar, spelling, and word choice
There are many sentence-level errors that you should revise carefully. Some common patterns:

Spelling / word form
*efficeintly* → efficiently
*effected* (when you mean influenced) → affected
*shorten* → shortened
*choosen* → chosen
*possibly* → possible
*musicans* → musicians
*Pearl Habor* → Pearl Harbor

Word choice
*“solely (vacation) travel”* is awkward. Just say leisure travel or personal travel.
*“in their perspective countries”* should be in their respective countries.

Grammar

*“it was brought about some negative changes”* is incorrect. Try:

*“The invention of the airplane has affected more than leisure travel; business, entertainment, and international politics have also felt its impact.”*

Write a stronger conclusion
Your conclusion currently repeats the idea that airplanes had both positive and negative effects, but the wording is awkward. A stronger conclusion should briefly restate your argument and leave the reader with a final thought.

For example:

Revision priorities

Replace informal phrases with more academic language.

You already have a solid organizational framework. With careful editing and more formal development, this essay could become much stronger.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central idea: AIDS changed not only public health but also social attitudes and behavior. You also use concrete examples, such as Ryan White, which helps support your argument. For revision, focus on three main areas: accuracy and nuance of your claims, organization, and sentence-level correctness.

Strengthen the argument with more precise claims

Your thesis is understandable, but several claims are too absolute or generalized. For example:

“it always leads to death”
“there is no cure”
“casual sex…rarely occurs”

These statements sound forceful, but they weaken your credibility because they are oversimplified. In argumentative writing, it is usually stronger to qualify claims. For example:

Instead of “it always leads to death,” say “at the time, AIDS was widely viewed as a fatal disease.”
Instead of “casual sex…rarely occurs,” say “the epidemic led many people to reconsider attitudes toward casual sex and sexual safety.”

Also be careful with wording like “homosexual male…they probably have AIDS too.” I understand that you are criticizing prejudice, not endorsing it, but the phrasing could be clearer. You might write:

“Many homosexual men were unfairly stigmatized because people ignorantly assumed they were infected.”

That version makes your own position unmistakable.

Clarify the main focus of the essay

Right now, the essay seems to make two related arguments:

AIDS caused fear, stigma, and discrimination.
AIDS changed people’s behavior and made them more cautious.

Both are good points, but the essay moves between them somewhat abruptly. Try revising your introduction so your thesis names both ideas clearly. For example:

Then organize the body so each paragraph develops one part of that claim.

Improve paragraph structure and development

Your body paragraphs contain useful ideas, but they need stronger topic sentences and fuller explanation.

Possible structure:
Paragraph 1: AIDS created fear and prejudice.
  - Discuss stigma toward gay men.
  - Explain why this prejudice was harmful.
Paragraph 2: Public reactions were often unjust.
  - Use Ryan White as a focused example.
  - Explain what this case shows about social panic.
Paragraph 3: AIDS changed behavior and attitudes.
  - Discuss fear of infection, safer sex, and increased awareness.

Right now, some points are asserted but not fully explained. For instance, “This kind of ignorant thinking is breaking us apart” is emotionally strong, but you should explain how it “breaks us apart” (through discrimination, exclusion, fear, misinformation, etc).

Use a more formal academic tone

There are places where the essay sounds conversational:

“I feel this has really made people think about their actions.”
“I’m sure more changes will come about.”

In an academic argument, try to rely less on personal opinion and more on analysis. For example:

“This fear prompted many people to reflect more carefully on sexual behavior and health risks.”
“As public education increased, social attitudes and health practices continued to change.”

Revise for grammar, spelling, and punctuation

There are several sentence-level errors that you should correct in the next draft. Some common ones include:

many peoples lives → many people’s lives
awhile → a while
it’s presence → its presence
untill → until
it’s consequences → its consequences
devistating → devastating
definately → definitely
more of stigma → more of a stigma
wouldn’t allow him to attend school is fine, but the sentence would benefit from commas:

Also watch for unnecessary extra spaces and inconsistent indentation.

Suggested revision priorities

When you revise, do these in order:

Rewrite the thesis so it clearly states your main argument.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one has one clear purpose.
Replace absolute claims with more precise, supportable language.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central claim: nuclear energy and nuclear weapons have been among the most significant forces shaping modern life. You also make an important moral argument—that the Cold War redirected attention and resources away from humanitarian and environmental concerns. The essay has energy, conviction, and a strong sense of historical importance. To revise effectively, focus on historical precision, clearer argument structure, and sentence-level control.

Clarify and narrow the thesis
Your current thesis is powerful but broad:

> “the discovery and harness of atom and its energy and the corresponding invention of nuclear weapons have been the most significant factors of change in our lifetime…”

This gives your topic, but it does not fully specify how nuclear technology changed the world. A stronger argumentative thesis would name the main effects you will prove. For example:

nuclear weapons reshaped international politics through fear and deterrence,
changed daily life through Cold War culture,
and diverted resources from social needs.

That would give the essay a more focused roadmap.

Improve organization
Right now, the essay reads more like a reflective response than a fully structured argument. Each paragraph has relevant ideas, but the progression is not always clear.

A possible revision structure:

Introduction
Introduce the 20th century as an age of global warfare and shrinking distance.
Present a specific thesis about the effects of nuclear technology.

Body paragraph 1: Political/global impact
Explain how nuclear weapons changed international relations.
Discuss deterrence, arms races, fear, and division between superpowers.

Body paragraph 2: Social/cultural impact
Develop your personal examples of bomb shelters, anti-communist thinking, and everyday fear.
Show how nuclear anxiety shaped ordinary life.

Body paragraph 3: Economic/moral consequences
Argue that military spending diverted attention from environmental and humanitarian issues.

Conclusion
Reflect on post-Cold War change and the possibility of using atomic energy for constructive purposes.

This structure would help readers follow your reasoning more easily.

Support claims with evidence
Many of your points are compelling, but they need more concrete support. For instance:

“my country has spent uncountable trillions of dollars” is too vague and likely inaccurate.
“more change than our planet has seen since its genesis” is dramatic but exaggerated.
“several thousand troops” seems too small to support the scale of your claim.

In argumentative writing, credibility matters. Use specific and verifiable evidence: examples, dates, historical events, or approximate statistics from reliable sources. Even one or two concrete references per paragraph would strengthen the essay greatly.

Watch historical and factual accuracy
There are places where wording weakens your authority:

“the twentieth century has seen more wars than any other previous bloc of time” is a sweeping claim that would need proof.
“communism with nuclear threat, Soviet citizens with anti-Christ” captures Cold War fear, but be careful not to overgeneralize or blur ideology, religion, and nationality.
“the dismantling of the Berlin Wall and hundreds of other smaller symbols of freedom” is unclear; be more precise about what those “symbols” are.

Try to distinguish between personal memory, public perception, and historical fact.

Strengthen style by simplifying some sentences
You often aim for elevated language, which gives the essay force, but sometimes the sentences become awkward or imprecise. Examples:

“Through advances in communication…” not “Though advances…”
“the discovery and harness of atom” should be “the discovery of the atom and the harnessing of its energy”
“has virtually arrived a standstill” should be “has virtually come to a standstill”

In general, prefer clear, direct sentences over heavily dramatic phrasing. For example, instead of:
> “our frigid planet and cold neighbors may once again be a warm and friendly people”

you might write:
> “the end of Cold War hostility created the possibility of more cooperative international relationships.”

That revision is less poetic, but more precise.

Edit grammar, spelling, and punctuation carefully
There are a number of sentence-level errors that you should correct in revision. Some examples:

Though → Through
harness of atom → harnessing of the atom
In the second of the world wars → In the Second World War
cheif → chief
bombshelters → bomb shelters
comma use is often excessive or misplaced
some phrases are unidiomatic, such as “secret cities for underground”

Read each sentence aloud and ask:
Is the grammar correct?
Is the phrasing natural?
Is the meaning precise?

Keep your strongest material
One of the best features of the essay is the connection between world events and lived experience. Your reference to growing up with bomb shelters and nuclear fear gives the topic immediacy. Keep that material, but frame it as evidence of a broader social effect, not just a personal memory.

Revision priorities
Write a more specific thesis.
Reorganize body paragraphs around distinct reasons.
Add concrete historical evidence.
Reduce exaggeration and improve precision.
Edit for grammar and sentence clarity.

You have the basis of a meaningful argument here. With stronger structure and more precise language, this essay could become much more persuasive and academically effective.

Your essay has a clear historical focus and a readable structure. It traces a sequence: discovery of penicillin, expansion of antibiotic research, and growth of the pharmaceutical industry. Develop each paragraph around one main point
Your body paragraphs contain several ideas at once. To improve clarity, give each paragraph a distinct role:

Paragraph 1: Introduce penicillin and explain why it was historically important.
Paragraph 2: Show how penicillin led to research into other antibiotics and synthetic variation.
Paragraph 3: Explain the larger consequences: industry growth, research institutions, and changes in medicine use.

This will make the essay feel more controlled and purposeful.

Use more precise and accurate wording
Several phrases are vague or scientifically imprecise.

Examples:
“medicinal chemistry” — acceptable, but “modern medicine” or “pharmaceutical science” may fit better depending on your focus.
“stopping and preventing the spread of infections” — antibiotics treat bacterial infections; they do not broadly “prevent the spread” in all cases.
“simple illnesses, such as the common cold” — this is a major accuracy problem. Antibiotics do not treat the common cold because it is caused by viruses, not bacteria. This sentence should be removed or reframed, perhaps as a warning about overuse or misunderstanding.
“the work of a small bacteria” — should be “a small bacterium” if singular. However, penicillin is actually produced by a mold (Penicillium), not a bacterium. This is an important factual correction.

Because the essay discusses scientific history, factual precision matters a great deal.

Add specific evidence or examples
Your claims would be stronger with a few concrete details. For instance:
Mention Alexander Fleming and the year 1928.
Briefly refer to later scientists such as Howard Florey and Ernst Chain, who helped develop penicillin for medical use.
Give an example of another antibiotic discovered after penicillin.
If you mention companies like Upjohn, explain why that example matters.

Even one or two specific references would make your essay more convincing and less general.

Reduce passive voice and repetitive sentence patterns
Many sentences begin with “It was discovered,” “It was attempted,” or similar passive constructions. Passive voice is not always wrong, but overusing it makes the prose feel indirect and repetitive.

Example:
Original:  
“It was discovered that the older drugs could be synthesized efficiently while still remaining biologically active.”

Possible revision:  
“Chemists discovered that they could synthesize older drugs efficiently without destroying their biological activity.”

This version is clearer and more energetic.

Fix grammar, usage, and mechanics
A few sentence-level issues need attention:

“It was also attemped” → “It was also attempted”
“research of their uses” → “research on their uses”
“led to the increase of health” → better as “improved the health of millions of people”
“in vitro” does not need quotation marks unless you want to discuss the term itself.
The dash in “small bacteria -- the producer of penicillin” is awkward; use an em dash or rewrite the sentence.

Also watch spacing and paragraph indentation consistency.

Improve the conclusion
Your conclusion currently restates the general topic but could do more to reinforce your argument. Try ending by emphasizing the broader significance of penicillin’s discovery.

For example:
“Ultimately, penicillin did more than cure infections: it reshaped medical research, expanded the pharmaceutical industry, and established antibiotics as one of the defining achievements of modern medicine.”

Revision priorities
Write a clear argumentative thesis.
Correct scientific inaccuracies, especially about the common cold and the organism that produces penicillin.
Add a few specific historical examples.
Revise passive, repetitive sentences into clearer active ones.
Strengthen paragraph unity and the conclusion.

You already have a solid foundation and a logical historical progression. With a sharper thesis and more precise evidence, this could become a much stronger argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central idea: modern conveniences like the washing machine make life easier, but they may also contribute to a less active lifestyle. That is a debatable and interesting claim, and your tone is lively and engaging. You use humor and exaggeration effectively in places, especially when describing people “clutching the machine tightly to their chests” or panicking at the idea of washing clothes by hand. These details give the essay personality.

That said, to revise this into a stronger argumentative essay, you should focus on clarifying your thesis, improving logical development, and adjusting style for academic writing.

Clarify the main argument

Right now, the essay begins by praising invention and the washing machine, then shifts toward the argument that labor-saving devices reduce exercise and may contribute to weight problems. This shift is interesting, but the thesis is not stated clearly enough early on.

Try to make your position explicit in the introduction. For example:

*Although the washing machine has greatly reduced household labor, it also symbolizes a broader dependence on technology that encourages physical inactivity.*

A thesis like this tells the reader exactly what the essay will argue.

Strengthen organization

At the moment, each paragraph touches on a related idea, but the essay does not build a clear line of reasoning. Consider this structure:

Introduction: Introduce inventions and present your thesis.
Benefits of washing machines: Explain how they save time and effort.
Dependence on convenience: Show how people rely on such devices.
Negative consequence: Argue that labor-saving technology reduces physical activity.
Conclusion: Weigh both sides and restate your argument.

This would help the essay move from description to argument more smoothly.

Develop ideas with evidence, not just exaggeration

Your examples are vivid, but many are deliberately exaggerated for comic effect. That makes the essay entertaining, but it weakens the argument if overused. For instance, the image of people running from a fire carrying a washing machine is funny, but not persuasive evidence.

To make the essay stronger, balance humor with more realistic support:
Explain how household machines reduce time spent on physical chores.
Discuss how modern lifestyles are more sedentary than in the past.
If appropriate, mention obesity, convenience culture, or reduced daily physical activity more directly.

Even without formal research, you can use more grounded reasoning.

Use a more formal academic style

Several features of the essay sound conversational rather than argumentative:
rhetorical questions: “What ever would a college student do...?”
multiple ellipses: “But is that all that bad?....”
ampersands: “&”
phrases like “millions and millions” or “furiously running about”

These choices create voice, but for an academic essay, they can make the argument sound less controlled. Replace them with direct statements. For example:

Instead of *“What ever would a college student do without a washing machine?”*  
  write *“Many students have become so dependent on washing machines that hand-washing seems impractical.”*

Also, avoid overusing quotation marks around words like “wonders” and “modern,” unless you are making a very specific point.

Watch word choice and outdated language

The repeated use of “man” and “housewives” sounds dated and excludes many people. Use more inclusive language such as:
*human beings*, *people*, or *society*
*homeowners*, *families*, or *household workers*

This will make your writing more appropriate for a modern academic audience.

Edit carefully for grammar and mechanics

There are a number of small errors that distract from your ideas. Some examples:

continously → *continuously*
necesity → *necessity*
with out → *without*
What ever → *Whatever*
handclothes washing → awkward; try *washing clothes by hand*
them referring to *houses* is incorrect in “those houses that own them”; better: *in households that own one*
avoid &; write *and*

Also, be careful with agreement and pronouns. For example:
*“The washing machine, in those houses that own them...”*  
  should be revised for singular/plural consistency.

Reconsider the conclusion

Your final sentence is memorable, but it oversimplifies the issue: *“Yes, the old days were hard, but was weight watchers ever an issue?”* This creates a sharp contrast, but it may not be historically accurate or nuanced enough. A stronger conclusion would acknowledge both sides:

washing machines save time and improve daily life;
however, dependence on labor-saving devices may reduce physical activity and encourage unhealthy habits.

Final advice

For revision, focus on turning a humorous reflection into a more convincing argument. Keep your strong voice, but support it with clearer reasoning and a more formal style. Your essay has a promising central insight; now it needs sharper structure, a clearer thesis, and more precise language to make that insight persuasive.

end with a thoughtful final comment.

Your essay has a clear sense of urgency and moral purpose. You argue that AIDS changed public attitudes toward sex, risk, and social responsibility, and you also emphasize how fear and prejudice shaped early responses. Those are strong ideas for an argumentative essay. To revise effectively, focus on clarifying your thesis, improving organization, and strengthening your evidence and tone.

Clarify the main argument

People initially ignored AIDS because of prejudice.

These ideas are important, but they need to be brought together into one clear thesis. You seem to mean that the emergence/recognition of AIDS was one of the most important developments of the 20th century.

A stronger thesis might be something like:

That would give your paper a more focused direction.

Improve paragraph structure and progression
Your essay generally follows a chronological pattern, but some paragraphs jump between ideas. For example, the paragraph about Ryan White moves from public fear, to media campaigns, to condoms in schools, to abstinence, to children’s sexual behavior. Education and prevention: argue for realistic sex education and explain why awareness matters.
Conclusion: emphasize acceptance, dignity, and the need to continue educating the public.

This structure would make your argument easier to follow.

“t.v.” should be “TV” or “television”

historical facts about the early AIDS epidemic
examples of public discrimination
Ryan White’s role in changing public attitudes
statistics or expert support for sex education and safe-sex practices

If you argue that education is necessary because abstinence-only messages are unrealistic, support that point with evidence rather than rhetorical questions alone.

Feedback Report

You have a clear personal voice and a strong central position: most twentieth-century inventions have harmed human life, while medical inventions are the main exception because they save lives rather than dominate nature or other people. This is an ambitious and philosophically interesting argument. Your revision should now focus on making that argument more precise, better supported, and more persuasive to a skeptical reader.

Strengthen the thesis

Your thesis is bold, but it is currently too absolute and somewhat unclear in scope. For example:

> “the only inventions of the twentieth century that have significantly changed people's lives for the better are those that have occured in the medical field”

This is a very difficult claim to prove because it excludes all non-medical inventions. A reader will immediately think of sanitation systems, communication technologies, transportation safety, or agricultural advances. If you want to keep the strong position, you need much stronger evidence. Otherwise, consider revising the thesis to something more defensible, such as:

“Among twentieth-century inventions, medical advances have had the most clearly positive effect on human life.”
“While many twentieth-century inventions have increased humanity’s power to harm nature and one another, medical inventions stand out for their life-preserving benefits.”

These versions still preserve your idea but make it easier to argue convincingly.

Add specific evidence

At the moment, the essay relies mostly on general claims and moral reflection. That gives it passion, but argumentative essays also need concrete support.

For example, when you praise medical inventions, name some:
antibiotics such as penicillin
vaccines
insulin treatment
medical imaging
organ transplantation

Then explain how they improved people’s lives.

Likewise, when you criticize other inventions, be specific:
industrial machinery contributing to pollution
nuclear weapons
chemical weapons
technologies that accelerate deforestation or extraction

Without examples, your argument sounds more like a personal meditation than a developed academic claim.

Develop the logic of your argument

There are a few places where the reasoning needs clarification.

A. “Technology stems from fear of death and insecurity”
This is an interesting idea, but you currently state it as if it were self-evident. A reader may ask:
Is all invention driven by fear?
What about curiosity, compassion, convenience, or creativity?

If this idea is central, explain it more carefully and acknowledge complexity.

B. “Medical inventions do not dominate nature”
This claim is harder to defend than it seems. Medicine can be understood as a form of control over nature, especially disease and the body. You may mean that medical inventions are ethical uses of knowledge because they preserve life rather than exploit or destroy. If so, say that more directly.

C. “Cannot be used to dominate other people”
This is also too absolute. Medical knowledge can sometimes be unequally distributed or misused. You do not need to abandon your point, but you should avoid sweeping claims that are easy to challenge.

Improve organization

Your essay has the beginnings of a clear structure, but each paragraph needs a stronger purpose.

A possible structure:
Introduction: introduce the debate and present the thesis clearly.
Body paragraph 1: explain why many inventions harm nature, with examples.
Body paragraph 2: explain how inventions are used to dominate other people, with examples.
Body paragraph 3: explain why medical inventions are different, with examples.
Conclusion: restate the argument and reflect on its significance.

Right now, the essay ends quite abruptly. Add a conclusion that gives the piece a sense of completion.

Adjust tone for academic writing

Your voice is thoughtful, but some phrasing is too conversational or emotionally charged for an academic argumentative essay.

Examples:
“Interestingly enough” is informal and unnecessary.
“our petty insecurities” sounds dismissive.
“truely ‘kind’ expressions” is emotionally appealing, but not analytically precise.

Aim for a tone that is still engaged but more formal and measured.

Sentence-level corrections

There are several language errors that should be fixed in revision:

occured → occurred
affect (noun needed here) → effect
truely → truly
0the century → 20th century or twentieth century
Remove the extra space in “For these reasons ,”
peacefully coexistent with nature would sound more natural as “peacefully coexisting with nature”

Also watch for spacing and punctuation consistency.

Most important revision priorities

If you only have time for a few changes, focus on these:
Revise the thesis so it is strong but defensible.
Add concrete examples of both harmful and beneficial inventions.
Clarify your reasoning about why medical inventions are morally different.
Write a fuller conclusion.
Correct spelling, word choice, and proofreading errors.

Final comment

This essay shows strong independent thinking and a willingness to make a challenging argument. That is a real strength. The next step is to turn that thoughtful stance into a more rigorous academic argument by narrowing claims, providing evidence, and refining the logic. With revision, this could become a much more persuasive essay.

How to revise

The satellite dish revolutionized global communication by reshaping news, business, and entertainment.

Then organize the essay around 2–3 main supporting points rather than many scattered ones.

Thesis needs to be specific

Stronger thesis example
> The satellite dish transformed late twentieth-century society by making global news immediate, expanding international business communication, and reshaping entertainment culture.

This gives the essay a clear direction and helps each body paragraph stay focused.

Body paragraphs need evidence and explanation
Many claims are asserted without support. For example:

“40% of U.S. millionares are entertainers.”
“The intelligence of an average teenager is lowering itself due to cable viewing.”

These are either unsupported, exaggerated, or too vague. In academic argument, readers need evidence and analysis. If you make a factual claim, provide a credible source or explain it carefully. If you cannot support a statement, remove or soften it.

Revision strategy
For each body paragraph:
Start with a topic sentence.
Give one concrete example.
Explain how the example proves your thesis.

Example structure:
Topic sentence: Satellite technology changed politics by allowing world events to be broadcast instantly.
Evidence/example: CNN’s live international coverage gave political leaders and ordinary citizens access to the same breaking news.
Analysis: This reduced delays in communication and increased the global influence of television journalism.

Tone and style
The essay sometimes uses dramatic language (“as large as a small FIAT,” “Presidents and dictators alike”) that sounds more journalistic than academic. That style can be engaging, but for an argumentative essay, aim for a more precise and formal tone.

Also, avoid phrases like “one must not forget” or “it is up to the average person to decide.” These sound conversational and do not advance your argument.

Organization
Your current structure is introduction + several short paragraphs + conclusion, but the paragraphs are uneven and underdeveloped. Some are only two sentences long and do not fully explore the point.

Suggested outline
Introduction
Introduce satellite dish technology
Explain its significance
End with thesis

Body Paragraph 1: News and politics
CNN and instant global communication
Effect on diplomacy/public awareness

International communication, finance, markets
Explain practical impact

Body Paragraph 3: Entertainment and culture
MTV/cable television/global media culture
Discuss positive or negative effects

Conclusion
Restate thesis
Summarize key effects
End with a thoughtful final point

Sentence-level errors to correct

“it’s center” → “its center”
“recieve” → “receive”
“that nights news” → “that night’s news”
“daily bases” → “daily basis”
“affect on the world economy” → “effect on the world economy”
“millionares” → “millionaires”
“pro’s and con’s” → “pros and cons”
“people lives” → “people’s lives”

Also watch:

inconsistent capitalization (“panasonic,” “CORE,” “Satellite”)
vague or incorrect dates (“the last 0 years,” “past 20 years”)

“The creation of the satellite dish in the past 20 years”
“space programs such a STARWARS”

Be careful with historical facts and terminology. If you are uncertain, research and revise. Accuracy is essential in academic writing.

Narrow your claim, build fuller paragraphs, and edit carefully for correctness and tone. With revision, this could become a much more persuasive essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear strength: it captures the emotional significance of the fall of the Berlin Wall and argues that the event helped bring about the end of the Cold War. The opening is vivid and engaging, and your personal connection gives the piece energy and sincerity. To revise effectively, focus on sharpening your argument, improving organization, and correcting style and mechanics.

Clarify your thesis
Right now, your main claim appears near the end: that the fall of the Berlin Wall was “the catalyst force that led to the end of the Cold War.” This is a strong argumentative position, but it should appear much earlier, ideally in the introduction.

At present, the first paragraph is atmospheric but does not clearly tell the reader what you will argue. Try ending the introduction with a precise thesis such as:

“The fall of the Berlin Wall was a decisive catalyst in the collapse of communist regimes across Eastern Europe and thus played a major role in ending the Cold War.”

This would help readers understand the direction of the essay from the start.

Strengthen the essay’s structure
The essay currently moves from personal memory, to background on communism, to examples from Eastern Europe, and finally to the conclusion. The ideas are relevant, but the progression could be more logical.

A clearer structure might be:

Introduction: historical moment + thesis  
Paragraph 1: why the Wall mattered symbolically  
Paragraph 2: how it inspired other Eastern Bloc countries  
Paragraph 3: why this makes it a catalyst for the end of the Cold War  
Conclusion: restate argument and significance

Your second paragraph mixes personal anecdote, historical background, and political argument. Consider separating these functions. The anecdote about your classmate’s father is interesting, but right now it interrupts the flow. Either connect it more directly to your argument or reduce it.

Develop analysis more fully
You make an important claim: the Wall’s fall gave hope to people in other communist countries. However, the essay needs more explanation of how and why this happened.

For example, when you mention Hungary, Czechoslovakia, Poland, and Romania, explain briefly what happened in each case or why Berlin influenced them. Even one sentence of explanation would make your reasoning stronger. Right now, some claims feel asserted rather than demonstrated.

Also, be careful with overstatement. Saying the Wall was the cause of the end of the Cold War may be too absolute unless you acknowledge other factors such as Soviet reforms, internal economic weakness, and years of political resistance. You could strengthen the argument by showing nuance:

“Although other forces had already weakened communist rule, the fall of the Berlin Wall became the most visible and powerful symbol of its collapse.”

That sounds more historically credible.

Tone and style
The essay has a passionate voice, which is a positive quality. However, some sentences are overly dramatic or too long, which makes them hard to follow. Try to balance emotion with precision.

For example, the opening contains many dashes and several sentence fragments. This creates excitement, but too much punctuation weakens clarity. Revise for smoother sentence control.

Also, phrases like “our winning of the Cold War” need clarification. Who is “our”? If you mean the West or the United States, say so directly. Be careful not to assume a shared political perspective without defining it.

Grammar, word choice, and mechanics
There are frequent errors in spelling, punctuation, agreement, and usage. These distract from your ideas. Some examples:

“mesmirized” → *mesmerized*  
“historic moment” → *historic moments* or *a historic moment*  
“our now as-President Bush” → awkward phrasing; perhaps *“President Bush now likes to refer to as…”*  
“Tchekoslovaquia” → *Czechoslovakia*  
“Roumania” → *Romania*  
“events of Berlin Wall” → *events surrounding the Berlin Wall* or *the fall of the Berlin Wall*  
“would not have taken a very different course” → this is confusing; perhaps *“might have taken a different course”*  
“catalyst force” → *catalyst* is enough

You also rely heavily on dashes. Use periods and commas more often to create clearer sentence boundaries.

Conclusion
Your final sentence is effective because it returns to the memorable image of “the day the wall fell.” Still, the conclusion would be stronger if it restated the argument more clearly and concisely instead of introducing uncertainty just before the end.

Revision priorities
State your thesis clearly in the introduction.  
Reorganize body paragraphs around distinct argumentative points.  
Add brief historical explanation to support your claims.  
Reduce overly emotional phrasing and long dash-heavy sentences.  
Proofread carefully for spelling, punctuation, and factual naming.

You have the foundation of a compelling historical argument. With clearer structure and stronger support, this essay could become both persuasive and memorable.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that television was the twentieth century’s most significant invention because it changed how people receive information, entertainment, and connection to the wider world. The paper shows energy and ambition, and you do a good job of comparing television to radio and newspapers to establish historical context. The next step is to make the argument more precise, better organized, and more polished at the sentence level.

Strengthen the thesis
Your introduction builds toward the claim that television “stand[s] out” among inventions, but the thesis is still somewhat vague. A stronger thesis should state exactly why television matters most.

For example, you might revise the thesis into something like:
> Among twentieth-century inventions, television had the greatest impact because it transformed how people received news, experienced entertainment, and connected to events and people across the world.

This gives your essay a clearer structure and tells the reader what to expect in the body paragraphs.

Improve organization and paragraph focus
Right now, the essay generally moves from “before television” to “after television,” which is a good foundation. However, some paragraphs contain several ideas at once. Try organizing each body paragraph around one main reason that television was so influential.

A possible structure:
Introduction: background on inventions; thesis
Body paragraph 1: life before television—radio and newspapers
Body paragraph 2: television improved access to news by adding images
Body paragraph 3: television created new kinds of shared experience and global connection
Body paragraph 4: television’s power can influence and persuade, so it must be used responsibly
Conclusion: restate argument and significance

This would help the essay feel more deliberate and less repetitive.

Develop ideas with more specific evidence
Many of your points are interesting, but they stay general. For an argumentative essay, you should support claims with specific examples. For instance:
major historical events watched on television
political speeches or debates that reached mass audiences
live sports broadcasts
educational or medical programming

You do mention examples such as a president speaking to another country and a mother watching her son’s Olympic race. These are good starting points, but they would be stronger if connected more directly to your argument. Explain how these examples prove television’s unique impact.

Refine your tone and wording
Some phrases are vivid, but a few are too informal or exaggerated for academic writing. For example:
“literal explosion of inventions” is dramatic but imprecise
“peak” should be peek
“keep the vision in television” is memorable, but it sounds more like a slogan than a formal conclusion

Aim for a more consistent academic tone. You can still be engaging, but try to prefer clarity over dramatic phrasing.

Work on sentence clarity
Several sentences are long and difficult to follow. Shortening them or dividing them into two sentences will improve readability.

For example:
> “Along with the news and information the radio brought to the homes of the people of the world, the television was able to add images- pictures that enhanced the entire absorbtion of what was being broadcast.”

This could become:
> In addition to the news and information radio provided, television added images. These pictures made broadcasts more vivid and easier for audiences to understand.

This version is clearer and more direct.

Correct grammar, spelling, and punctuation
There are frequent sentence-level errors that distract from your argument. Some patterns to revise:

Possessives/plurals: “one lives” should be one’s life
Spelling: “vertually” → virtually; “boarders” → borders; “absorbtion” → absorption; “spening” → spending; “necesary” → necessary
Word choice: “peak” → peek
Capitalization: “olympic” should be Olympic
Apostrophes: “1940’s” is usually 1940s
Symbols in formal writing: use and instead of &
Hyphenation/punctuation: “images- pictures” should be punctuated more cleanly, likely with a dash or colon

Also watch for sentence fragments, such as:
> “A system that seemed to work...”
This is not a complete sentence as written.

Reorganize body paragraphs so each one develops one main point
Edit carefully for grammar, spelling, and sentence clarity

Overall, your essay has a strong central idea and a clear sense of purpose. With better organization, more specific support, and cleaner prose, it could become a much more convincing argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay presents a clear overall position: the computer is one of the most important inventions of the twentieth century because it changed how people and businesses handle information. The topic is strong, and your focus on the computer’s effect on everyday life gives the essay a clear direction. To revise effectively, work on thesis precision, paragraph development, style, and sentence-level correctness.

Main strengths

Clear topic and stance: You consistently argue that computers transformed society.
Logical broad structure: The essay moves from early computers, to the personal computer, to wider social effects.
Relevant examples: You mention writing papers, taxes, databases, portable computers, and communication technologies, which help show practical impact.

Thesis and argument

Your introduction suggests the main claim, but the thesis is still somewhat broad and awkwardly phrased:

> “The computer however in this, the information age, has had a great impact on how society and especially business gathers, creates, responds to and, understands everyday information.”

This could be made more precise and arguable. Right now, the essay mostly explains that computers are useful, but it could argue how and why they were transformative in a more focused way.

Revision suggestion
Try a thesis like:

“Among twentieth-century inventions, the personal computer had one of the greatest effects on society because it made information easier to create, store, analyze, and share in both homes and workplaces.”

This version is clearer, more concise, and gives you four main points that can structure body paragraphs.

Organization and development

Your essay has a basic progression, but the body paragraphs are very long and sometimes repetitive. Much of the third paragraph repeats the idea that computers help people “process information.” Instead of repeating, develop separate points.

Possible improved structure
Paragraph 1: Early computers and their limited use
Paragraph 2: The invention of the personal computer and affordability/access
Paragraph 3: Specific changes in work and daily life
Paragraph 4: Broader social impact and future importance

Each paragraph should begin with a clear topic sentence. For example:

“Early computers were important, but their use was mostly limited to governments and large corporations.”
“The personal computer revolutionized society by making computing available in ordinary homes.”
“As computers became more advanced, they transformed communication, work, and access to information.”

Also, be careful with historical claims. For example, “IBM Corporation started a revolution… the invention of the personal computer” oversimplifies the history. IBM popularized the PC, but did not literally invent all personal computing. If you are making historical claims, make them more accurate.

Style and clarity

A major issue is wordiness. Many sentences are much longer than necessary and lose force because they repeat the same idea in different forms.

For example:

> “It was a way to organize your work and the information that you had to process in an easier fashion and much more quickly.”

This could become:

“It allowed users to organize and process information more easily and quickly.”

Aim for:
fewer filler phrases (“just about,” “in turn,” “you now had”)
more direct verbs
less repetition of “information,” “process,” and “tool”

Also, the essay shifts between formal academic language and second person (“you can sit in your home…”). In an argumentative essay, it is usually better to maintain a more formal tone. Instead of “you,” write “users,” “people,” or “individuals.”

Grammar and mechanics

There are several sentence-level issues that need correction:

Subject-verb agreement
“There has been many inventions” → “There have been many inventions”

Punctuation
You often use commas incorrectly or place them where they interrupt meaning:
“The computer however in this, the information age…”
“used though, by corporations…”

Try simpler punctuation:
“However, in this information age, the computer has had a great impact…”
“It was used mainly by corporations and governments…”

Capitalization and spelling
“Modem” → “modem”
“recieve” → “receive”

Pronoun consistency
You shift between “one,” “you,” “we,” and “society.” Choose one perspective and keep it consistent. Academic essays usually prefer third person.

Conclusion

Your conclusion restates the main idea, but it could be stronger by doing more than repeating that computers are important. Try ending with a broader implication:

“Because computers changed not only how information is processed but also how people work, communicate, and learn, they remain one of the most influential inventions of the twentieth century.”

Priorities for revision

Rewrite the thesis so it is concise and specific.
Break up and reorganize body paragraphs around distinct points.
Cut repetition and use more precise language.
Maintain a formal academic tone instead of addressing the reader as “you.”
Edit carefully for grammar, punctuation, and spelling.

Overall, your essay has a solid central idea and a good foundation. With clearer structure and tighter language, your argument will become much more convincing and polished.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central claim—that the 20th century can be called “The Century of Democracy”—and it shows genuine engagement with global political change. The personal voice also gives the piece energy: linking your argument to the U.S. elections, the fall of the Berlin Wall, and the Soviet Union’s transition makes the essay feel immediate and sincere.

That said, to strengthen the essay as an argumentative piece, you need a clearer thesis, more precise historical reasoning, and more careful organization.

Clarify and narrow the thesis
Right now, the essay builds toward the claim that the 20th century was “The Century of Democracy,” but the argument remains broad and somewhat celebratory. A stronger thesis would do more than name the century; it would explain why democracy is the defining feature and how your examples prove it.

For example, instead of:
“I think it is only fair to call the 20th Century, ‘The Century of Democracy.’”

You might write something like:
“Despite war and dictatorship, the 20th century is best understood as the ‘Century of Democracy’ because it saw the collapse of major authoritarian regimes, the spread of democratic ideals across Europe, and a growing belief that governments should be accountable to their people.”

This gives your reader a roadmap.

Improve organization and paragraph focus
Several paragraphs mix personal reflection, historical background, and claims without a clear topic sentence. Try to make each body paragraph do one job.

A possible structure:
Introduction: historical change + thesis  
Paragraph 1: the fall of the Berlin Wall and German reunification as a symbol of democratic change  
Paragraph 2: the Soviet Union’s decline and movement away from communism  
Paragraph 3: broader global effects, such as the easing of Cold War tensions  
Conclusion: restate argument and reflect on significance

This would make the essay feel more controlled and persuasive.

Use evidence more carefully and accurately
Your examples are relevant, but some claims are too sweeping or imprecise.

Historical accuracy / phrasing issues:
“the invention of the automobile” is not accurate for the 1920s; automobiles became widespread then, but they were invented earlier.
“East and West Germany to unite as one democratic superpower” is overstated. Reunified Germany became an important democratic nation, but “superpower” is not the best term.
“the former superpower of the Soviet Union is now making its transition into a democracy” simplifies a much more unstable and contested political process.
“The idea of a nuclear war is practically non-existent today” is too absolute.

In argumentative writing, avoid exaggeration. More cautious wording will make you sound more credible:
“greatly reduced”
“symbolized”
“suggested a broader shift”
“moved toward”
“raised hopes for”

Distinguish emotion from argument
Your personal perspective is one of the essay’s strengths, but at times the piece relies more on feeling than on analysis. For example, “I’m feeling very patriotic these days” and “I feel lucky” are vivid, but they do not by themselves prove the argument.

Keep some of that voice, but connect it to a larger claim:
Why does the Berlin Wall matter politically?
How does the collapse of Soviet communism support your thesis?
Why does democracy, rather than technology or war, define the century?

Adding even a sentence of explanation after each example would help.

Address complexity
A strong argumentative essay usually acknowledges that the issue is not simple. Your essay presents democracy as an almost universal bond, but the 20th century also included fascism, colonialism, genocide, military dictatorships, and democratic failures. Briefly recognizing this would make your argument more mature.

For instance:
“Although the century also witnessed some of history’s most destructive dictatorships, its later decades were marked by powerful democratic movements that reshaped global politics.”

This kind of concession strengthens rather than weakens your claim.

Style and sentence-level revision
Your writing is fluent, but some sentences are wordy or awkward. Aim for greater precision.

A few edits to consider:
“Each decade in itself has introduced” → “Each decade introduced”
“How could we ever forget” → avoid rhetorical questions unless they clearly serve your argument
“could follow its happening” → “could witness its fall”
“The Soviet Union, as well is going through” → “The Soviet Union as well is going through” or better, “The Soviet Union is also undergoing”
“what was once the Berlin Wall- the Wall” → punctuation should be cleaner: “what was once the Berlin Wall—the wall”
“marxism” should be capitalized: “Marxism”

Also, be careful with comma use; you sometimes insert commas where they are not needed.

Final revision priorities
When revising, focus on these three goals:
Write a more specific thesis
Give each paragraph one clear argumentative purpose
Replace broad, emotional, or absolute statements with precise historical analysis

Overall, this essay has a strong idea and an engaging voice. With sharper structure and more careful reasoning, it could become a much more convincing argument.

Your essay relies heavily on emotional language, but an argumentative essay also needs specific evidence. Many claims are very broad:
“blowing holes in our sky”
“rendering our seas useless”

These phrases are dramatic, but they are not explained or supported. Also, some points are scientifically inaccurate or misleading. For example, the “holes in our ozone layer” are not primarily caused by nuclear energy. That weakens your credibility.

Give examples such as radioactive waste, accidents at nuclear plants, or the human cost of atomic bombs.

Nuclear accidents can contaminate land and water for long periods.

Topic sentence with one clear point
Explanation of that point
Specific example or evidence
Link back to the thesis

Introduction: introduce atomic technology and present your thesis
Body paragraph 1: dangers of atomic weapons
Body paragraph 2: risks of nuclear power production
Body paragraph 3: why governments still use it / counterargument
Conclusion: restate your position and call for action

This would help your ideas progress more logically.

“The lack of sight from our leaders is almost appalling.”
“what is to become of our children.”
“It starts with you.”

These lines sound persuasive, but in an academic essay you should balance emotion with analysis. You can still express concern, but with more formal phrasing:

  - “The discovery of atomic energy is undoubtedly the most awe-inspiring as well as the most terrible invention…”

  - Correct spelling: “irreversible.”

Write one clear thesis.

Main revision priorities

Make the thesis more specific
Right now, your introduction suggests several ideas: speed of communication, live visual information, shrinking the world, and negative effects. Because the thesis is broad, the essay feels somewhat general.

Try to state exactly what you will argue in the body. For example:

> Television was one of the most significant inventions of the twentieth century because it allowed people to witness events visually and immediately, shaped public opinion during major political crises, and transformed how ordinary people understood the wider world.

This kind of thesis gives you a roadmap for the essay.

Strengthen paragraph structure
Each body paragraph should begin with a clear topic sentence and then develop one main point with explanation and evidence.

For example:
Paragraph 1: Television changed public understanding of war and politics.
Paragraph 2: Television made distant places and events feel immediate and familiar.
Paragraph 3: Despite these benefits, television can also mislead or desensitize viewers.

At the moment, some ideas are repeated across paragraphs, and the final paragraph combines benefits and harms without fully developing either.

Use evidence more carefully and precisely
Your examples are promising, but they need more explanation. For instance, when you mention the Vietnam War, explain more directly how televised images influenced public protest. With Somalia, clarify what event or type of coverage you mean and how it led to “public concern and effort.”

Also avoid overstatements such as:
“Gone are the days of unknown and unheard of areas.”
“Television is able to bring any part of the world into your very living room within a matter of seconds.”

These statements are rhetorically strong, but they are too absolute. Academic argument is usually stronger when it is more precise and qualified.

Clarify your discussion of the negative side
Your essay says television can be “harmful” and can “desensitize” viewers, but this point is underdeveloped. If you want to include a counterargument, explain it fully:
How does television desensitize viewers?
Is the problem violence, repetition, manipulation, or passivity?
Does this weaken your main claim about its importance, or simply show that important inventions can be used badly?

Right now, the essay gestures toward this issue without integrating it into the argument.

Style and language notes

You write in a formal tone, which suits the assignment, but some sentences are wordy or awkward. Aim for clearer, more direct phrasing.

Examples of sentences to revise
Original: “One of the most significant inventions of the 20th century has to do with the speed at which information is now communicated throughout the globe.”  
  Clearer: “One of the most significant twentieth-century inventions is television because it dramatically increased the speed and reach of communication.”

Original: “This simple fact has changed in many ways how we live...”  
  Clearer: “This ability has changed how people experience world events.”

Original: “There are, however, several downsides to the television, and, like all inventions can be used in a useless way.”  
  Clearer: “However, television also has drawbacks and, like any invention, can be used poorly.”

Grammar and mechanics to fix

A few recurring issues distract from the argument:

Capitalization: “vietnam war” should be Vietnam War.
Verb tense: “television had changed people’s lives” should probably be has changed people’s lives.
Word choice: “made smaller the dimensions of the planet” is awkward; try made the world feel smaller.
Sentence control: Some sentences are long and loosely connected. Break them into shorter units when needed.
Commas: Be careful with unnecessary commas, especially in the introduction.

Suggested revision plan

Rewrite the thesis so it lists 2–3 main reasons television is significant.
Reorganize the body so each paragraph focuses on one reason.
Expand your examples, especially Vietnam and Somalia, with clearer explanation.
Decide whether the negative effects deserve their own paragraph or a shorter counterargument near the end.
Edit for concision, capitalization, and sentence clarity.

Overall, this essay has a solid central idea and good potential. Your next step is to turn a broad reflection into a more focused academic argument with clearer structure and stronger support.

Your essay has a clear position and a strong overall idea: the airplane changed human life by transforming travel, cultural understanding, and international relations. Good choice of supporting points

improved international relations and business

These are strong categories for an argument.

Effective use of personal experience

“The invention of the airplane dramatically changed people’s lives by making long-distance travel practical, expanding cultural understanding, and strengthening global political and economic connections.”

This version is more specific and previews your body paragraphs.

Improve organization

A possible structure:

Body paragraph 1: travel became faster, safer, and more accessible
Body paragraph 2: exposure to other cultures broadened people’s perspectives
Body paragraph 3: international relations, trade, and communication improved
Conclusion: restate importance and explain why the invention still matters today

Right now, the second paragraph mixes travel, cultural understanding, and personal experience. The third paragraph also combines politics, trade, and postal communication somewhat quickly. Try to develop each paragraph more fully.

Develop evidence more carefully
Your claims are interesting, but some need more explanation or support.

For example:

When discussing international relations, give a clearer example of how airplanes help diplomacy or trade.
“Postal communication system” is mentioned briefly, but the connection is not fully developed. Do you mean airmail? Delivery of documents? If so, explain.

Since this is an argumentative essay, each point should follow a pattern:
claim -> explanation -> example -> connection to thesis

Some phrases sound conversational or exaggerated:
“astronomical”
“opened many doors”
“basically speeded up”
“the whole postal communication system also”

Try to replace these with more precise language. Academic writing is usually strongest when it is specific rather than dramatic.

For example:
instead of “astronomical”, say “enormous” or “far-reaching”
instead of “opened many doors”, say “created new opportunities”

There are several sentence-level errors that distract from the argument. Here are some important ones:

“The extend to which” -> “The extent to which”
“what seemed a completely impossible concept” -> “what once seemed an impossible concept”
“long distance traveling” -> “long-distance travel”
“I still did not have the opportunity to witness it first hand” -> “I still did not have the opportunity to witness it firsthand”

“The frequency that which foreign countries are able to communicate” -> incorrect phrasing; perhaps “The frequency with which countries can communicate and negotiate face to face”
“has improved drastically the establishment of international trade” -> better as “has drastically improved the development of international trade”
“speeded up” -> “sped up” is more standard

Revision priorities

Final thought

Feedback Report

You have chosen an important public-health topic and your essay shows a clear concern for education and prevention. Your central idea—that knowledge and public awareness can help prevent AIDS when medicine is limited—is meaningful and arguable. To revise successfully, focus on three main areas: thesis and argument, organization, and style/accuracy.

Clarify your main argument
Right now, the essay suggests several ideas at once:
medicine has not cured AIDS,
education is necessary,
people behave irresponsibly,
society fears AIDS,
prevention is possible through knowledge.

These ideas are related, but your essay needs one clear thesis that states exactly what you are arguing. For example:

Possible thesis:  
*Because AIDS cannot be fully controlled by medicine alone, public education about transmission, prevention, and risk behavior is the most effective way to reduce its spread.*

This thesis is specific and gives your paper direction. Then each paragraph should support that claim.

Improve organization
At the moment, the essay reads more like a series of observations than a structured argument. Try using a clearer paragraph plan:

Introduction
Introduce the issue.
Give brief context.
End with your thesis.

Body Paragraph 1: Limits of medicine
Explain why medical treatment alone was not enough, especially in the period you are discussing.

Body Paragraph 2: The role of education
Discuss schools, public programs, support centers, and health campaigns.

Body Paragraph 3: Misconceptions and risky behavior
Explain how ignorance about transmission contributes to the spread of AIDS.

Conclusion
Restate the argument and end with a strong final thought about prevention and public responsibility.

This structure will make your essay easier to follow and more persuasive.

Use stronger evidence and more precise reasoning
Many of your points are general. Argumentative writing becomes stronger when you include specific evidence, such as:
examples of public health campaigns,
facts about how HIV/AIDS is transmitted,
statistics about infection rates,
references to how education changed behavior.

For example, instead of saying people “choose to ignore that it exists,” explain *how* fear and stigma prevent open discussion and lead to poor prevention.

Also be careful with claims that may be inaccurate or oversimplified. For instance, mentioning “dentists who forget to put on their plastic gloves” is too casual and may distract from your point. If you want to discuss medical transmission, do so carefully and factually.

Revise tone for academic writing
Your tone is often passionate, which is good, but some wording is too informal or emotionally charged for an academic essay. Examples:
“w/” should be written as with
“&” should be written as and
“it’s killing our population” is dramatic and vague
“selling their lives short” is informal
“possible suicide” is unclear and too extreme

Aim for a formal, precise tone. For example:
Instead of “it’s killing our population,” write “it has had devastating effects on many communities.”
Instead of “selling their lives short,” write “putting themselves at serious risk.”

Be careful with word choice and sensitivity
This topic requires respectful language. For example:
Instead of “victims of AIDS,” use “people living with AIDS” if that is what you mean.
Instead of defining groups in a stigmatizing way, focus on behaviors and risk factors.
Avoid suggesting that certain groups alone are responsible for disease spread.

Also note that AIDS is the disease caused by HIV; if relevant, use the terms accurately.

Fix grammar and sentence problems
There are many sentence-level issues that make the essay harder to read. Focus especially on:

Run-on or awkward sentences
Example:
> “Upon realizing that homosexuals are not the only possible carriers of the disease, but drug abusers, people who interact w/ many unknown sexual partners, as well as dentists who ‘forget to put on their plastic gloves;’ people may begin to think before acting.”

This sentence is overloaded and confusing. Break it into simpler ideas.

Punctuation
You often misuse semicolons. A semicolon should connect two closely related independent clauses, not replace commas randomly.

Word forms and agreement
“The AIDS viruses was discovered” → “The AIDS virus was discovered”
“many peoples lives” → “many people’s lives”
“interact sexual” → “interact sexually”
“drugs effect” → “drug’s effects” or better “the effects of drugs”

Contractions
Avoid contractions like “it’s” in formal essays unless quoting speech. Use “it is.”

Sample revision of your opening
Here is one possible revision of your first paragraph:

Original idea: medicine has advanced, but AIDS remains a challenge.  
Revised version:  
*Although twentieth-century medicine made major advances in treating disease, AIDS remained one of the most serious public-health challenges of the era. Because no simple medical cure was available, prevention depended heavily on public education, awareness, and responsible behavior.*

This version is clearer, more formal, and directly introduces your argument.

Final advice
For your next draft:
Write a clear thesis.
Organize the essay into logical body paragraphs.
Replace general statements with specific evidence.
Remove informal abbreviations and emotional phrasing.
Edit carefully for grammar, punctuation, and accuracy.

You already have a strong topic and a clear sense of urgency. With better structure, evidence, and academic tone, this essay can become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

You have a clear, engaging personal voice, and your essay makes a strong case for why contact lenses matter in everyday life. The piece is easy to follow, and your examples from sports, swimming, and daily routines help readers understand your position. Your enthusiasm also gives the essay energy. For revision, the main areas to strengthen are argument focus, organization, tone, and sentence-level correctness.

Strengthen the argument

Your opening claim is very large: “one of the most important inventions of the 20th century.” To support that, you need more than personal convenience. Ask yourself:
Important for whom?
Important in what ways?
Compared with what other inventions?

You could strengthen the essay by arguing that contact lenses are important because they:
improve quality of life,
allow greater freedom in sports and physical activity,
offer convenience and independence compared with glasses.

That would give your essay a clearer structure and make the argument feel less like a personal reflection and more like a defended position.

Improve organization
The essay generally follows a timeline, but it would be stronger with clearer paragraph purpose. Right now, some paragraphs mix history, personal experience, and opinion.

Consider revising into this structure:
