Body Paragraph 1: Explain how they improve daily life and mobility.
Body Paragraph 2: Discuss benefits for sports and active people, using your basketball/swimming examples.
Body Paragraph 3: Explain advances from hard lenses to soft/disposable lenses and why that matters.
Conclusion: Reinforce why contact lenses are significant.

This will help the essay sound more deliberate and persuasive.

Adjust tone for an academic audience
Your voice is lively, which is a strength, but some phrases are too casual for formal academic writing. Examples:
“&” should be replaced with and
“lense craze” is informal and also misspelled
“I can’t even begin to explain” is conversational
“which I must admit is a bit ridiculous!” sounds judgmental and off-topic
“glasses are a thing of the past!” is too absolute

Try to keep the engaging voice while using more formal wording. For example:
Instead of “I’d be blind most of the time,” write “I would be significantly limited in daily activities.”
Instead of “a pain to clean,” write “difficult to maintain.”

Be careful with overstatement
Some claims are exaggerated:
“glasses are a thing of the past”
contact lenses as one of the most important inventions of the century

These statements can weaken your credibility because readers may see them as too extreme. A more nuanced claim would be more convincing:
“For people with active lifestyles, contact lenses have been one of the most practical vision-related inventions of the 20th century.”

This still expresses a strong opinion but is easier to defend.

Fix grammar, spelling, and punctuation
This draft contains many sentence-level issues that distract from your ideas. Focus especially on:

Spelling
lense / lenses / contact lense case → lens / lenses / contact lens case

Subject-verb agreement
“a style called ‘hard lenses’ were the only kind made”  
  → was the only kind made

Punctuation and spacing
There are many extra spaces before punctuation and inconsistent use of semicolons, dashes, and quotation marks. Keep punctuation simple and clean. For example:
“At the time, I played basketball; if I wore my glasses, they would end up on the floor, and if I didn’t wear them, I would be more or less blind.”

Sentence boundaries
Some sentences are too long and loosely connected. Break them into clearer units.

Stay focused
The paragraph about colored contact lenses feels unnecessary because it does not clearly support your main argument. If you keep it, explain how cosmetic options increased the popularity or cultural significance of contact lenses. Otherwise, cut it and use the space to develop your stronger points.

Suggested revision of thesis
Here is one possible revised thesis:

Final thoughts
Your essay already has two important strengths: clear personal investment and specific examples. Revision should focus on making the piece sound more academic and giving the argument a stronger structure. If you tighten the thesis, organize the body around clear reasons, and correct grammar and punctuation, this could become a much more convincing argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central idea: feminism can be understood as one of the most important “discoveries” of the twentieth century because it changed how society thinks about women’s roles, work, and identity. That is an interesting and debatable claim, and your conclusion returns effectively to the idea of feminism as a “discovery in progress.” The paper already has a recognizable structure and a strong sense of purpose. For revision, the main priorities are historical accuracy, precision of argument, and development of evidence.

What’s working well

Original framing: Calling feminism a “discovery” is a fresh way to approach the topic.
Clear thesis direction: The essay argues that feminism has had major effects on modern life and is still unfinished.
Logical progression: You move from broad context, to women’s rights, to representation/image, and then to the unfinished nature of feminism.

Main revision priorities

Strengthen historical accuracy
Several claims are too general or factually shaky, which weakens your credibility.

Examples:
“the 0th century” appears to be a typo; it should be 20th century.
You say women won “the right to vote in the 1800s,” but in the U.S. women gained national suffrage with the 19th Amendment in 1920.
“The first significant equal rights movement in the U.S. was in the 70s with the Equal Rights Amendment” is inaccurate. There were major women’s rights movements long before the 1970s, including suffrage movements in the 19th and early 20th centuries.
“60 cents for every dollar” may have reflected a commonly cited figure at one time, but it needs either a source or more careful phrasing.

Revision advice: Replace broad or questionable statements with precise ones. If you cannot support a statistic, either remove it or qualify it more carefully.

Clarify and sharpen the thesis
Right now, the essay suggests that feminism is a discovery because society has only recently recognized women’s inequality and begun to challenge it. That is promising, but the thesis needs to be stated more explicitly.

A stronger thesis might say something like:
> Feminism can be considered one of the twentieth century’s most important social “discoveries” because it exposed long-accepted inequalities, reshaped women’s roles in work and family life, and challenged damaging cultural images of women.

This gives you three clear body points to develop.

Develop each paragraph with examples and explanation
At present, the body paragraphs rely mostly on general claims. To persuade readers, you need specific examples.

For instance:
In the paragraph about equality, mention voting rights, access to education, workplace discrimination laws, reproductive rights, or the women’s liberation movement.
In the paragraph about image, you could discuss media representation, sexist language, beauty standards, or the objectification of women.
In the final paragraph, explain why feminism remains incomplete and what inequalities still exist.

Try to use the pattern:
claim → example → explanation → link to thesis

Improve wording and tone
Some phrasing is awkward or imprecise.

Examples:
“modern day life” → modern life
“it was still the most typical as well as the ideal” → it was still considered both normal and ideal
“the woman is still only earning” → women still earn less than men on average
“things as varying as pornography or the language we use helps” → things as varied as pornography and everyday language help

Also, quotation marks around “discovery” and “discovered” are understandable, but overusing them can make the prose feel uncertain. If this is your key metaphor, define it once and then use it confidently.

Edit sentence-level issues
There are several grammar and punctuation problems that should be cleaned up in revision.

Examples:
“made towards” → made toward
“housekeeping and tending the children” → keeping house and tending to children
“Only when women have achieved equality with men, will there cease…”  
  Better: Only when women have achieved equality with men will there cease to be a need for feminism.
Be careful with spacing and comma use.

Suggested organizational revision
You could revise into this structure:

Introduction
   - Broader context of important twentieth-century changes
   - Introduce feminism as a social “discovery”
   - Clear thesis with 2–3 main reasons

Body Paragraph 1: Legal and political change
   - Suffrage, legal rights, equal opportunity movements

Body Paragraph 2: Social and economic change
   - Changing expectations about work, family, and pay equity

Body Paragraph 3: Cultural change
   - Representation, language, pornography/media, stereotypes

Conclusion
   - Return to “discovery in progress”
   - Emphasize feminism’s continuing relevance

Final thought
Your essay has a strong core idea, but it needs more precision and support to become convincing. Focus first on correcting the historical claims, then build out each paragraph with concrete evidence and clearer analysis. If you do that, your central metaphor of feminism as a major modern “discovery” will become much more powerful.

The essay is easy to follow because each paragraph focuses on a different aspect of the issue.

Logical paragraphing

Make the argument more precise and analytical

Topic sentence
Specific example
Explanation of why it matters
Link back to thesis

“countries that which have many weapons” → “countries that have many weapons” or “countries with many weapons”

“it also is beneficial” → “it is also beneficial”

“it is not beneficial to our environment but it is also saves money”

“hopeful by the 21st century” → “hopefully by the twenty-first century”

& → and
w/o → without

2 → two

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central position: computers have significantly improved daily life by making information storage, calculation, editing, and travel arrangements more efficient. The topic is relevant, and your use of personal examples gives the paper sincerity and concreteness. To revise effectively, focus on organization, clarity, formality, grammar, and development of your argument.

Main strengths
Clear overall claim: you argue that computers have had an important effect on people’s lives.
Relevant personal examples: storing school records, balancing a checkbook, using a word processor, and arranging study abroad all help show real-world impact.
Sense of scope: you move from personal experience to a broader social claim, which is a good strategy in an argumentative essay.

Main issues to revise

A. Strengthen the thesis
Right now, the essay begins generally (“Computers have definitely effected peoples lives”) but does not present a strong, precise thesis. Your argument will be more convincing if you clearly state how computers affect people.

For example, you might revise the thesis to something like:
> Computers have transformed people’s lives by making information easier to store, everyday tasks faster to complete, and large systems such as travel and education more efficient.

This gives your essay a roadmap.

B. Improve organization
The first paragraph contains many ideas at once: storage, editing, finances, communication, and your father’s online ticket purchases. Because so many points appear in one long paragraph, the essay feels rushed.

Try organizing it into clearer body paragraphs:
Computers help organize and store information
Computers save time and reduce error in daily tasks
Computers make large institutions and travel systems more efficient

This will make the argument easier to follow.

C. Use more formal academic style
Since this is an argumentative essay, avoid informal shorthand such as:
“w/” → with
“w/out” → without
“&” → and
“info” → information
“on line” → online

Also avoid conversational phrasing like “Well” and overuse of “of course.” A more formal tone will make your argument stronger.

D. Fix grammar and word choice
There are frequent language errors that interfere with credibility. Some important ones:

effected → affected  
  (“Effected” usually means “brought about”; here you mean “influenced.”)
peoples lives → people’s lives
my life has change → my life has changed
had to this → had to do this
form the US → from the US
the this program → this program
would inevitably inundated → would inevitably be inundated

Also watch verb consistency and sentence structure. For example:
> “I no longer need to keep these records but only to transfer them to my computer.”

This is understandable, but more precise would be:
> “I no longer need to keep paper records because I can store them on my computer.”

E. Avoid overly long sentences
Many of your sentences are run-on or loosely connected with commas. This makes the essay harder to read. Break long sentences into shorter, clearer ones.

For example, this sentence:
> “Also, w/ the use of the Word Processor I'm able to edit & rearrange documents w/out altering the entire document, & furthermore, though I have yet to use the Communications program in my computer, this program seems to be extraordinarily helpful.”

Could become:
> “A word processor also allows me to edit and rearrange documents quickly without rewriting the entire text. In addition, communication programs seem extraordinarily useful, even though I have not yet used them myself.”

Develop the argument further
At times, the essay assumes rather than proves. For example, when discussing the International Programs Office, you say computers probably processed your information. That may be true, but the argument becomes stronger if you explain why computers matter in such settings:
They reduce paperwork
They speed record-keeping
They make scheduling and booking more accurate
They allow large institutions to manage many students efficiently

In other words, move from personal impression to clear reasoning.

Improve the conclusion
Your final sentence is broad, but the conclusion could do more than restate the topic. Try summarizing your key reasons and reinforcing the significance of the argument.

For example:
> In both personal and institutional contexts, computers make work faster, more organized, and more accurate. For these reasons, they have become one of the most influential technologies of modern life.

Priorities for revision
Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Divide the essay into separate body paragraphs by main idea.
Replace informal abbreviations with formal academic language.
Correct major grammar and spelling errors.
Shorten long sentences and improve punctuation.
End with a stronger concluding paragraph.

Final note
You already have the raw material for a solid essay because your examples are specific and relevant. The next step is to present those ideas in a clearer academic structure. If you revise for formality, organization, and sentence clarity, your argument will become much more persuasive.

Is it because it enables instant global communication?
Because it makes governments more accountable?
Because it changes how people respond to world events?

A stronger thesis might be:
“The communications satellite is one of the most important inventions of the century because it allows instant global transmission of images and information, making distant political events immediate and harder to ignore.”

This gives your essay a clear direction.

Improve organization
At the moment, the essay reads as one long paragraph with several sentence fragments and abrupt transitions. Readers need a clearer structure:

Suggested structure
Introduction: Present the claim about satellites.
Body paragraph 1: Explain what satellites do that earlier inventions (radio, TV, videotape) could not do as effectively.
Body paragraph 2: Use Tiananmen Square as evidence.
Conclusion: Explain the broader significance and acknowledge limits.

Also, some ideas appear suddenly, such as “Truth before censorship” and “who wouldn’t want to help his neighbor?” These could work better if prepared more carefully and connected to your argument.

Develop your evidence more carefully
Your main example is potentially effective, but it needs more explanation. You mention the “Chinese student who stood firm… against Communism,” referring to the famous “Tank Man” image. This is a strong image, but you should explain how it supports your claim:

The event was broadcast internationally.
The broadcast made viewers feel immediate connection.
This visibility increased international pressure on the Chinese government.

Be careful, however, not to overstate what you can prove. For example, saying that without satellites “the movement for democracy… would have been lost” is too absolute. It would be more accurate to say that satellites helped the world witness the event and made it harder for authorities to hide it.

Similarly, your claim that people and governments “will be less tempted to do wrong” is interesting, but speculative. If you keep it, qualify it:
“Satellite coverage can discourage wrongdoing by increasing the likelihood that abuses will be seen by the world.”

Focus on formal academic style
Your argument is passionate, which is good, but some phrasing is too conversational or unclear for an academic essay.

Examples:
“Plus when China is placed down the block…” sounds informal and weakens your ending.
“the satellite keeps a watchful eye on all of us” is vivid, but also personifies technology in a way that may sound exaggerated.

Aim for a more formal tone:
Replace “Plus” with “Moreover” or remove it.
Replace rhetorical questions with direct statements.

Correct grammar, spelling, and word choice
There are many sentence-level errors that distract from your ideas. Some important ones:

geo-centric satellite → likely communications satellite or geostationary satellite
evention → inventions
preceed → precede
invention possess → inventions possess
openned → opened
litterally → literally
Teniman Square → Tiananmen Square
sensorship → censorship
a like → alike
mor → more

There are also punctuation problems:
“As it happens- the most profound byproduct of the satellite.” is a fragment.
“Freedom US. the tank of injustice…” is unclear and likely a sentence error.
Avoid overusing commas after introductory phrases like “And it is certain, that…”

Revise unclear sentences
Some sentences are difficult to understand and need to be rewritten completely. For example:

Original:
“Freedom US. the tank of injustice made its way into the lives of most of the world's people.”

This is not grammatically clear. You may mean something like:
“Through satellite transmission, the image of a lone protester confronting a tank brought the struggle for freedom in China into homes around the world.”

Try to make each sentence express one clear idea.

End with a stronger conclusion
Your conclusion should restate the argument and show its significance. Right now, the final sentence is too casual. A stronger ending might be:

“By making distant events immediate and visible, satellites have transformed global awareness and increased the power of truth against censorship.”

Revision priorities
Rewrite the thesis more precisely.
Break the essay into paragraphs.
Clarify and expand the Tiananmen example.
Remove exaggerated or unsupported claims.
Correct grammar, spelling, and punctuation.
Replace informal wording with a more academic tone.

You have a compelling core idea here. With clearer structure and cleaner prose, this could become a much stronger argumentative essay.

Also, the conclusion should do more than say “who knows where these machines will take us.” Try to restate your main argument and leave the reader with a stronger final thought.

“god send”

“definantly” → “definitely”
“god send” → “godsend”
“accesible” → “accessible”
“previosly” → “previously”
“The extend” → “The extent”
“they is clearly imp” → “they are clearly important”

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: television is a major 20th-century invention because it changed how people receive information and understand the world. The paper also shows strong engagement with the topic and offers more than one reason for your claim. You discuss both news and entertainment, which gives the essay range. For revision, the main areas to strengthen are organization, sentence control, precision, and development of argument.

Strengths

Clear central idea: You argue that television has significantly changed people’s lives.
Relevant supporting points: You explain that TV makes news immediate, connects cultures, and provides entertainment that can teach lessons.
Awareness of complexity: You do not present television as entirely positive; you also mention distorted portrayals of reality and violence. This makes the argument more thoughtful.

Main revision priorities

A. Improve the thesis
Your opening identifies the invention and suggests why it matters, but the thesis would be stronger if it clearly listed the main reasons your essay will develop.

For example:
> Television has significantly changed people’s lives by making news immediate, connecting people across cultures, and shaping how audiences learn about the world through entertainment.

This gives the essay a clearer roadmap.

B. Strengthen paragraph structure
Right now, the first paragraph contains several different ideas: instant news, political reaction, shared information, and intercultural understanding. Try separating these more clearly so each paragraph focuses on one main point.

A possible structure:
Introduction + thesis
Television and instant access to news
Television and global/cultural connection
Television as entertainment and informal education
Conclusion

This will make the argument easier to follow.

C. Control long sentences
Many of your sentences are run-ons or comma splices. This is the biggest language issue in the essay. For example:

> “Now they can see it happening, for themselves, as it is happening, this allows them to make their own, informed judgment...”

This combines multiple complete ideas incorrectly. Break long sentences into shorter ones.

Example revision:
> Now people can see events for themselves as they happen. This allows them to make more informed judgments about those events.

Shorter sentences will make your ideas sound more confident and academic.

D. Use a more formal academic style
Some wording is too conversational or imprecise for an argumentative essay.

Examples:
“every person in the world” → too absolute
“well, at least everybody who watches” → too casual
“thru” → use through
“&” → write and
“good guy” → hero or protagonist

Also avoid overgeneralizing. Instead of saying television gives *everybody* the same information, say:
> television allows large audiences to receive similar information at the same time.

Development of ideas

A. Add specific examples
Your argument would be more convincing with concrete examples. You mention laws, international events, and cultural exchange, but these points stay general. Consider adding one specific illustration:
live war coverage
election results
disaster reporting
international sports events
educational or documentary programming

Specific examples help prove your claims.

B. Explain your reasoning more fully
Some ideas are interesting but need clearer explanation. For instance, you say television helps people from different cultures “come together and discuss ideas.” Explain how this happens. Does seeing other cultures reduce misunderstanding? Does it create shared reference points? Add 1–2 sentences of analysis.

C. Clarify the counterargument
Your discussion of violence and unrealistic portrayals is useful, but the paragraph becomes unclear in places:
> “have a tendency to glorify violence, as well as making life seem the good guy always wins...”

Revise this to make the point more precise:
> Many television programs present unrealistic versions of life by glorifying violence and suggesting that moral conflicts are simple and that heroes always survive.

Then explain why, despite this problem, television still has value.

Grammar and mechanics

Focus especially on:

Run-on sentences / comma splices
Hyphenation: “second- or third-hand accounts”
Prepositions and wording: “making life seem [as if] the good guy always wins”
Pronoun agreement and precision
Punctuation spacing: avoid extra spaces before/after punctuation
Dashes: use them carefully and consistently

A possible revision strategy

Rewrite the thesis to name your main reasons.
Divide the essay into clearer body paragraphs.
Go sentence by sentence and split any sentence that contains more than one full idea.
Replace informal expressions with formal academic wording.
Add at least one specific example in each body paragraph.
End with a brief conclusion that reinforces your argument.

Final thought

This essay has a solid foundation and a thoughtful argument. Your main challenge is not lack of ideas; it is presenting those ideas in a more organized, precise, and controlled way. If you revise for structure and sentence clarity, your argument will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear argumentative purpose: you want to show that the microwave was a revolutionary invention because it broke with traditional cooking methods and changed modern life. The piece has energy and a strong sense of enthusiasm, which helps make your argument engaging. The main areas to revise are clarity, accuracy, organization, tone, and sentence-level correctness.

What is working well

Strong central claim: You consistently argue that the microwave transformed cooking.
Ambitious style: The ocean/storm metaphor gives the opening a dramatic quality.
Relevant supporting ideas: You include useful points about speed, safety, and convenience.
Sense of historical contrast: Comparing fire-based cooking with microwave technology is a solid argumentative strategy.

Main revision priorities

Sharpen the thesis
Right now, the essay strongly praises the microwave, but the thesis could be more precise. Instead of broadly saying it “revolutionized” cooking, specify how and why.

For example:
> The microwave revolutionized food preparation by replacing traditional flame-based cooking with a faster, safer, and more convenient method suited to modern life.

This gives your essay a clearer roadmap.

Improve organization and paragraph unity
Your body paragraphs contain good ideas, but they need clearer topic sentences and smoother progression.

A stronger structure might be:

Introduction: present the historical shift and thesis.
Body paragraph 1: microwaves broke from the long tradition of fire-based cooking.
Body paragraph 2: microwaves save time and fit modern schedules.
Body paragraph 3: microwaves improve convenience and safety.
Conclusion: restate the significance.

At the moment, some ideas are repeated (“there is no fire,” “time is conserved”) and some sentences feel loosely connected.

Use a more formal academic tone
Your essay is vivid, but some phrases weaken the credibility of the argument because they sound exaggerated or conversational.

Examples:
“they would have thought you crazy, or bewitched, or under the influence of heavy drugs”
“slaving in the kitchen”
“This marvel of technology”

These phrases may entertain, but in an argumentative essay they can make the writing seem less serious. Replace them with more measured wording.

For example:
> People living a century ago would likely have found such a method difficult to imagine.

Be careful with historical and scientific accuracy
A few claims are overstated or inaccurate.

“Never before has such an invention revolutionized a science like this” is too sweeping.
Cooking has changed through many major inventions, so avoid absolute statements unless you can prove them.
The explanation of microwave heating is a bit imprecise. You do not need much science here, but be careful.

You could say:
> Unlike traditional methods that rely on external heat from flame or heated surfaces, microwaves heat food using electromagnetic energy.

Also, “At the dawn of time” and “Throughout History” are exaggerated. Use more precise phrasing such as “for most of human history.”

Edit grammar, spelling, and word choice carefully
There are many sentence-level errors that distract from your ideas. Some examples:

culunary → *culinary*
consistant → *consistent*
Progress has move slow → *Progress has moved slowly*
rythm → *rhythm*
showed little changed → *shown little change*
twentyth → *twentieth*
comodity → *commodity*
dizzing → *dizzying*
differents perspectives → *different perspectives*
fool of mankind → likely *food of mankind*

Watch capitalization too:
“History” should usually be lowercase unless part of a title.
“Children” and “As” are unnecessarily capitalized mid-sentence.

Fix sentence construction and punctuation
Some sentences are awkward, run-on, or fragmented.

For example:
“Modern times saw gas and electric ranges as to staple.”  
  This is unclear. You might write:  
  > In modern times, gas and electric ranges became standard kitchen appliances.

“The microwave is excellent for re-heating making it easier for people to make excess food to be eaten later.”  
  Better:  
  > The microwave is especially useful for reheating, which allows people to prepare extra food and eat it later.

“A mother who works, can come home can whip up something...”  
  Revise for grammar and style:  
  > A working parent can come home and prepare a meal in minutes.

Style note on the introduction
The extended sea metaphor is creative, but it goes on for too long and delays your main point. In an argumentative essay, it is usually better to get to the thesis sooner. You could keep one or two metaphorical sentences, but simplify the opening.

Final advice
As you revise, focus on this order:

Rewrite the thesis clearly.
Reorganize body paragraphs by reason.
Replace exaggerated or informal language with precise academic phrasing.
Correct grammar, spelling, and punctuation.
Read the essay aloud to catch awkward sentences.

You have a strong core idea here. With a clearer structure and more polished language, this could become a persuasive and effective argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central subject—the impact of radio—and it shows an effort to connect invention, politics, information, and entertainment. You also use concrete examples from everyday life, which helps make the topic relatable. For revision, the main areas to improve are focus, argument, organization, and language accuracy.

Clarify your thesis
Right now, the essay begins with an interesting idea about repressive government, but most of the essay shifts to the broader effects of radio and television on daily life. Because of that, the introduction promises one argument, while the body delivers another.

Ask yourself: What exactly are you trying to argue?
Possible directions:
Argument A: Radio is one of the most influential inventions of the 20th century because it transformed politics, information, and entertainment.
Argument B: Radio made modern mass influence possible, both positively (information, entertainment) and negatively (propaganda, social passivity).

Choose one main claim and make sure every paragraph supports it.

Example of a stronger thesis:
> Radio was one of the most important inventions of the twentieth century because it transformed how governments influence citizens, how people receive information, and how families experience entertainment.

That thesis matches the content of your essay much more closely.

Strengthen paragraph organization
Your essay would be stronger if each paragraph had one clear main idea. At the moment, some paragraphs mix several ideas together.

A clearer structure might be:

Introduction
   - Introduce the importance of radio
   - Present your thesis

Radio and political power
   - Explain how radio can spread propaganda
   - Connect back to your teacher’s comment about repressive governments

Radio as information
   - Weather, news, immediacy, reaching millions
   - Compare briefly with newspapers

Radio/television as entertainment
   - Explain how broadcast media changed leisure habits
   - Use your examples of children, TV, and your elderly relative

Conclusion
   - Restate the main argument
   - End with a broader reflection on its impact

This would help the essay feel more deliberate and argumentative, rather than a series of observations.

Develop analysis, not just examples
You include useful examples, but sometimes you stop at description. Try to explain why each example matters.

For instance:
“I listen for the weather forecast” is a good example, but then explain that radio changed decision-making by giving people immediate access to practical information.
Your point about people becoming “passive observers” is interesting and could become a strong analytical point. Expand it: Does radio encourage convenience at the cost of participation? Does mass media shape public opinion?

This kind of explanation will make the essay feel more thoughtful and academic.

Be careful with historical and logical claims
Some claims are too broad or inaccurate:
“The machine gun has not changed all that many lives” is hard to defend and sounds insensitive, especially given warfare in the 20th century.
“Nowadays everybody watches T.V.” is too absolute.
“It was not until the invention of radio that the power of the media became evident” ignores the historical influence of print media.

Try using more precise language:
“Radio expanded the reach and speed of mass communication in ways newspapers could not.”
“Television became a central form of entertainment in many households.”

Improve tone and word choice
Some wording is informal or imprecise for an academic essay:
“greatest thing in the world”
“everybody”
“sit by the radio”
“news making process”

Aim for a more formal style. For example:
“Television is a major source of comfort and stimulation for her.”
“Many people rely on broadcast media for news and entertainment.”

Edit grammar, spelling, and mechanics
There are several sentence-level issues that need correction. Some important ones:

Use numerals carefully in formal writing: write “two” instead of “2.”
“Marshall Law” should be “martial law.”
“it' s” should be “it’s.”
“forcast” should be “forecast.”
“T.V.” is usually written “TV” or “television.”
Avoid using “&” in formal essays; write “and.”
“My señora” includes a special character issue in your draft; make sure encoding is correct.
Watch spacing around apostrophes and punctuation.

Your essay has a promising topic and some engaging examples, but it needs a more focused claim and clearer structure. For your revision, prioritize these steps:

Decide on one main argument about radio.
Rewrite the introduction so the thesis matches the body.
Organize body paragraphs by topic.
Expand your analysis of political influence, information, and entertainment.

You already have the raw material for a stronger essay; the next step is to shape it into a more unified argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear everyday focus and a relatable example: the microwave as a useful modern invention. The family examples help make your argument concrete, and the contrast with your great-uncle’s opinion shows that you understand there are different perspectives. That is a good starting point for an argumentative essay.

What is working well

Clear topic
You stay focused on one invention rather than talking too generally about “science and modern technology.” This helps the reader follow your main idea.

Use of personal examples
Your description of your family’s cooking routine is effective because it shows *how* microwaves save time in real life.

Acknowledgement of another viewpoint
Including your uncle’s opinion strengthens the essay because argument writing is usually better when it considers disagreement.

---

Main areas to revise

Strengthen the thesis

Right now, the opening begins broadly and then shifts into examples, but the essay does not state a strong, direct argument. A thesis should clearly express your position.

For example, you could write something like:

“Among the many technological advances of the twentieth century, the microwave has had a significant positive impact because it saves time, makes meal preparation more efficient, and suits the needs of busy modern families.”

This gives the essay a clear claim and also suggests the points you will develop.

---

Improve organization

At the moment, the essay reads more like a series of thoughts than a structured argument. Try using a clearer paragraph plan:

Introduction: introduce the topic and state your thesis
Body paragraph 1: explain how microwaves save time for working families
Body paragraph 2: give your family example as evidence
Body paragraph 3: present the opposing view and respond to it
Conclusion: restate your main point and end clearly

Your great-uncle anecdote is interesting, but it takes up a lot of space. Make sure it supports the argument rather than distracting from it.

---

Develop the argument more fully

You make one main point—that microwaves are efficient—but the essay would be stronger if you expanded this idea. Ask yourself:

Why is speed important in modern life?
How does the microwave help families manage work, school, and meals?
Is convenience the only benefit, or are there others, such as reducing effort or making reheating safer/easier?

Also, when you mention that a microwave “isn’t right for everyone,” explain that point more carefully. Does this weaken your argument, or are you saying that although some people prefer traditional cooking, microwaves are still valuable for many households? Make that relationship clear.

---

Use a more formal academic tone

Some phrases are too conversational for an academic essay, for example:

“a little story to tell”
“pop them into the microwave”
“the long and short of the story”

These expressions sound natural in speech, but in formal writing you should aim for a more controlled style. For instance:

“An example of this difference in opinion can be seen in my extended family.”
“place them in the microwave”
“Ultimately, my uncle gave the microwave to my parents”

You can still use personal examples, but present them more formally.

---

Edit grammar, punctuation, and word choice

There are several sentence-level errors that you should correct in revision. Here are some important ones:

“peoples lives” → “people’s lives”
“For instance a microwave” → “For instance, a microwave”
“left overs” → “leftovers”
“we each take turns cook” → “we each take turns cooking”
“they’ re” → “they’re”
“recieved” → “received”
“micro-wave” → “microwave”
“the point is, is that” → “the point is that”

Also watch spacing and formatting. There are some inconsistent indents and spaces in the draft.

---

Make sentences more precise

Some sentences are awkward or incomplete. For example:

“In today’s society it is common, in the ‘average’ family for both parents to work.”

This would be clearer as:

“In many contemporary families, both parents work outside the home.”

That version is more concise and formal.

Another example:

You could revise this to:

“This method speeds up meal preparation, as the vegetables can then be cooked in the microwave within minutes.”

Organize the essay into distinct argument paragraphs.
Expand your reasons and explain them more fully.
Keep the anecdote, but shorten it and connect it clearly to your argument.
Edit carefully for grammar and punctuation.
Replace conversational phrases with more formal language.

You already have the basis of a solid topic and some useful examples. With stronger structure and a more formal style, this could become a much more convincing argumentative essay.

===== ICLE-US-MICH-0044.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a practical, relatable topic. You argue that ATMs have significantly improved everyday life by making money easier to access and saving time. The personal examples also help make your point concrete. For revision, focus on three main areas: strengthening the thesis, improving organization, and polishing sentence-level accuracy and style.

Argument and Thesis

Your main claim is understandable, but it could be sharper and more specific. Right now, the essay says ATMs changed people’s lives because they provide convenience, emergency access to cash, and time savings. These are good points, but they should be clearly previewed in the introduction.

For example, instead of beginning with a general statement and then moving into personal reaction, you could write a thesis like:

“One of the most important inventions of the twentieth century is the ATM because it gives people immediate access to cash, saves time by reducing dependence on bank hours, and increases personal safety by reducing the need to carry or store large amounts of money.”

This kind of thesis gives your essay a stronger structure and makes your argument sound more confident.

Organization and Development

Your essay has the basic parts of an argument, but the paragraphs could be organized more logically. Right now, some ideas are repeated, and some transitions are abrupt.

A stronger structure might be:

Introduction: introduce ATMs and state your thesis
Body paragraph 1: emergency access and convenience
Body paragraph 2: time-saving and independence from bank hours
Body paragraph 3: safety benefits and brief response to the counterargument about jobs
Conclusion: restate the significance of ATMs and end with a broader comment about technology

You do include a counterargument—loss of bank jobs—but it is too brief. Instead of saying only that we should “focus on the positive aspects,” try responding more directly. For example:

“Although some people argue that ATMs reduced the number of bank employees needed for routine transactions, the machines also allowed banks to serve customers more efficiently and gave people access to banking services at any time.”

That makes your argument more balanced and persuasive.

Use of Evidence

The personal examples help, especially the grocery store situation. However, the essay relies heavily on personal opinion (“I feel,” “I really appreciate,” “I do not know what I would do”). In an argumentative essay, try to balance personal experience with broader reasoning.

For example, instead of only saying you appreciate your ATM card, explain why this matters generally:

“ATMs are especially useful in daily situations such as shopping, travel, or emergencies, when a person may not have enough cash available.”

This shifts the essay from individual experience to a more universal argument.

Style and Tone

Your tone is sincere, but some phrasing sounds informal or repetitive. Try to reduce repeated use of expressions like “I feel” and “convenience.” Academic argument sounds stronger when claims are stated directly.

For example:
Weak: “I feel ATM cards are an excellent invention.”
Stronger: “ATM cards are an excellent invention.”

Also, avoid unnecessary wording:
“without this convenience at access to money” → “without convenient access to money”
“cases when money is needed” → “when money is needed urgently”

Grammar, Mechanics, and Word Choice

There are several sentence-level issues to correct:

Word choice / phrasing
“Bank-cash machines” is awkward; use “automated teller machines” or “ATMs.”
“working men and women of the late 1900’s” sounds odd and dated; simply say “working people.”
“appear in all areas of the World” → “are found throughout the world.”
“promote danger” → “increase the risk of theft” or “create danger.”
“our every day life” → “our everyday lives.”

Grammar
“I can not” → “I cannot”
Avoid shifting between ATM machines and ATM cards unless you mean different things. Be precise: the machine and the card are not the same.
“ATM machines” is redundant because the “M” already stands for “machine.” Use “ATMs.”

Punctuation and formatting
There are some spacing issues and an unnecessary dash near the end.
Be careful with apostrophes: “1900's” should be “1900s.”
Capitalization: “World” usually should not be capitalized here.

A Possible Revision Goal

As you revise, aim to make each paragraph focus on one main reason that ATMs changed people’s lives. If you do that and tighten the grammar, your essay will become much more convincing.

Summary of Priorities
Write a clearer, more specific thesis.
Organize body paragraphs by separate reasons.
Develop the counterargument more fully.
Reduce repetition of personal phrases like “I feel.”
Correct grammar, word choice, and punctuation issues.

You have a solid topic and a clear opinion. With better organization and more precise language, this essay can become a strong and effective argument.

The piece is easy to follow at the sentence level, but it would become much stronger if you clarified your central argument and organized each paragraph around that argument more consistently.

Main strength: clear engagement and concrete examples
One of the best features of this essay is that it sounds authentic. You are clearly interested in the subject, and that helps the reader stay engaged. The Madrid example is especially effective because it shows, rather than just tells, why cellular technology seemed so astonishing. The discussion of emergencies and car accidents also gives the essay practical relevance.

Main issue: the thesis is unclear or inconsistent
Your opening claims that cellular phones are “a complete luxury and not a necessity.” That is a clear position, but the rest of the essay does not fully argue it. Instead, the body mostly explains:
how cellular phones changed people’s lives,
how they can help in emergencies,
how they can be dangerous while driving,
how expensive they are,
how amazing the technology is.

These are interesting points, but they do not all directly support the claim that cellular phones are a luxury rather than a necessity. By the end, the essay sounds more celebratory than argumentative.

How to revise
Decide exactly what you want to argue. For example:
Option 1: Cellular phones are impressive inventions, but they remain luxuries rather than necessities.
Option 2: Cellular phones have transformed communication, but their convenience comes with social and safety costs.
Option 3: Cellular phones are one of the most remarkable inventions of the 20th century because they increase accessibility and connectivity.

Any of these could work, but you should choose one and make every paragraph support it.

Organization: improve paragraph focus
Right now, some paragraphs contain several different ideas at once. For example, the third paragraph discusses accidents, expense, and affordability. That is too much for one paragraph.

A clearer structure might be:
Introduction: present the invention and your thesis.
Body paragraph 1: convenience and accessibility.
Body paragraph 2: emergencies and practical uses.
Body paragraph 3: drawbacks such as distracted driving and cost.
Body paragraph 4: personal example showing the technology’s reach.
Conclusion: restate your argument and reflect on the broader meaning of technological change.

This would help the essay feel more purposeful and polished.

Development: explain your reasoning more fully
Some ideas are introduced but not fully developed. For example, if your argument is that phones are luxuries, explain why emergency use still does not make them necessities. Is it because public phones or home phones could serve the same function? Is it because only certain professions truly need constant access? Adding that reasoning would make the essay more persuasive.

Similarly, the sentence “To me, it is a bit ridiculous” introduces a strong personal judgment, but you do not explain it. Why is it ridiculous? Because people become too dependent? Because they expect constant availability? Expand your point.

Tone and style
Your voice is lively, but some expressions are too informal for an academic argumentative essay. For example:
“a bit ridiculous”
“lots of people”
“The scary thing is that it’s just around the corner!”

These phrases are not wrong, but they make the tone sound conversational rather than analytical. Try replacing them with more formal wording:
“unnecessary”
“many people”
“This rapid technological change may also be unsettling.”

Also, avoid overusing exclamation marks. They weaken the academic tone.

Sentence-level issues
There are several grammar, punctuation, and style problems to fix in revision:

Avoid &; write and.
“friends houses” should be friends’ houses.
“their car phone” is awkward; car phone or cell phone while driving would be smoother.
“Although most people who can afford to buy a cellular phone, can afford to pay the bills!” is incorrect and awkward. You could revise to:  
  Most people who can afford to buy a cellular phone can also afford its monthly costs.
“it' s” should be it’s.
Be careful with semicolons. For example, “if they are stranded or broken down” should not follow a semicolon there.

Conclusion
Your conclusion shifts into robots and futuristic technology, which is interesting but somewhat disconnected from your main claim. Try ending by returning directly to your thesis. For example, if your argument is about luxury versus necessity, conclude by explaining what cellular phones reveal about modern society’s desire for constant connection.

Revision priorities
Rewrite the thesis so it clearly matches the essay you want to write.
Reorganize body paragraphs so each has one main idea.
Add explanation showing how each point supports your argument.
Make the tone more formal and reduce conversational phrasing.
Edit for punctuation, possessives, and sentence clarity.

You already have strong material here; the next step is to turn it into a more focused argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear overall purpose: to argue that a “two-model approach” to origins should be taught in schools. You also show ambition by trying to evaluate the opposing side and by organizing the paper around different kinds of support: reasoning, authorities, evidence, and counterarguments. That gives the essay a recognizable argumentative structure. For revision, the main work is to make your argument more precise, more logically consistent, and much better supported with actual evidence from sources.

Clarify your thesis and key terms
Your central claim appears to be that teaching both evolution and creation in classrooms is more effective than teaching evolution alone. However, the essay shifts between several claims:
both should be “discussed”
both should be “taught”
both should be taught in “science class”
both should be presented “nondogmatic, yet forceful and aggressive”

These are not the same claim. Decide exactly what you are arguing. For example:
Should creationism be taught in science classes?
Should it be discussed in humanities/religion/philosophy classes?
Should schools present multiple views on origins, but not as equal scientific theories?

Also define terms carefully. “Creation,” “creationism,” “creation science,” and “two-model approach” are treated as interchangeable, but they are not necessarily identical. A stronger essay would explain what each term means and why that distinction matters.

Strengthen logic and avoid overstatement
At times, the essay relies on assertions rather than reasoning. For instance, you call the two-model argument “strong,” “effective,” and “excellent” many times, but you do not always demonstrate *why*. Replace evaluative language with analysis.

Example:
Instead of “their reasoning is excellent,” explain specifically how a piece of evidence supports the claim.
Instead of “the opposition is increasingly weak,” show exactly where the opposing argument fails.

Be especially careful with analogies. The Newton/Einstein example is interesting, but it does not automatically prove that creationism should be taught alongside evolution in science classes. Questioning accepted ideas is important in science, but science still relies on evidence, testing, and methodological standards. Your revision should explain how that principle applies to this specific debate.

Use credible evidence, not placeholders or vague references
The biggest weakness is lack of usable evidence. The essay contains many placeholders (“”), anonymous quotations, and broad claims such as “in a large number of various sources” or “one of the best studies mentioned in Bergman’s book.” These do not allow the reader to assess credibility.

For revision:
Replace every placeholder with an actual quotation or paraphrase.
Identify sources fully: author, title, context, and relevance.
Avoid anonymous quotes unless there is a compelling reason.
Explain why a source is credible and how it supports your point.

Also, some evidence is one-sided. For example, citing Richard Bliss as “a strong advocate of creation science” may weaken your ethos unless you acknowledge possible bias and evaluate the study critically. If you use research, include details: sample size, method, results, limitations.

Address the strongest counterarguments fairly
You do include counterarguments, which is good, but many are presented vaguely (“some people feel ”). Strong argumentative writing represents opposing views accurately and specifically.

You should address major objections such as:
creationism is religious rather than scientific
courts have ruled on what can be taught in public school science classrooms
“teaching both sides” may create a false equivalence if one view does not meet scientific standards

When you answer these objections, do more than dismiss them. Engage them carefully with evidence.

Improve organization and paragraph development
Most body paragraphs begin with a broad topic sentence, but they often drift into multiple ideas. Try this structure for each paragraph:
clear claim
evidence
explanation of evidence
link back to thesis

For example, your paragraph on Newton and Einstein becomes repetitive and could be reduced to one focused point about the value of questioning accepted theories.

Also, your introduction is too long and rhetorical. The opening uses large, philosophical questions, but your essay is really about curriculum policy. Start closer to the issue.

Revise style and sentence-level correctness
Your writing is fluent, but there are recurring problems:
wordiness/redundancy: “strong, effective argument,” “excellent reasoning,” “increasingly weak”
informal phrasing: “(surprise) evolution”
awkward wording: “pondered at some point in their lives this”
grammar/punctuation: “where as” should be “whereas”; “the two model approach” should be “two-model approach”; “fro ma” should be “from a”; capitalize sentence openings (“The opposition asks…”)
tone: avoid loaded language like “aggressive,” “incredible claims,” or “true answers”

Aim for a more neutral, academic style.

A possible revision strategy
Rewrite the thesis so it makes one precise claim.
Remove placeholders and add real citations.
Reorganize body paragraphs around 3–4 main reasons.
Add one paragraph that seriously engages legal and scientific objections.
Revise for concision and formal tone.

Overall, you have the beginnings of a structured argument, but the essay needs sharper focus, stronger evidence, and more careful reasoning to be persuasive in an academic context.

Feedback Report

Your essay takes a clear position in favor of allowing euthanasia in some circumstances, and it shows strong engagement with moral, religious, and legal dimensions of the issue. You also attempt to respond to opposing views, which is essential in argumentative writing. For revision, your main priorities are: clarifying your thesis, organizing your argument more logically, and improving sentence-level accuracy and completeness.

Clarify your central argument

At present, your position shifts. In the introduction, you argue that euthanasia should be a personal choice. Later, you say “it is not society’s choice to decide,” which supports individual freedom, but you also emphasize that life is God’s gift and that euthanasia conflicts with moral values. In the final paragraph, you support euthanasia only if the patient is suffering and has no realistic hope of recovery. That is a more limited claim.

Try revising your thesis into one clear sentence, for example:

> Although euthanasia raises serious moral and religious concerns, a mentally competent, terminally ill patient should have the right to choose a painless death when suffering becomes unbearable and recovery is impossible.

This gives you a stable claim to defend throughout the essay.

Improve organization

Right now, the essay reads more like a series of reflections than a carefully structured argument. Some paragraphs repeat the same idea (values, morality, suffering) without adding new reasoning.

A stronger structure might be:

Introduction  
   Define euthanasia briefly and present your thesis.

Opposing argument: moral/religious objections  
   Explain why some people oppose euthanasia: sanctity of life, “playing God,” medical ethics.

Your response  
   Argue that personal autonomy and relief from unbearable suffering can justify euthanasia in limited cases.

Legal/medical example  
   Use Dr. Kevorkian or the Cruzan case as evidence, but explain clearly how the case supports your point.

Conclusion  
   Restate your argument with nuance: euthanasia should not be casual or unrestricted, but it may be ethical in carefully defined situations.

This structure will help readers follow your reasoning.

Develop evidence more clearly

You refer to “my articles,” “The Last Goodbye,” and “Prolifers Say Cruzan Death a Signal of Things to Come,” but you do not explain enough about them. Also, several places contain missing material marked with , which makes your argument incomplete.

For revision:
Identify each source clearly.
Summarize what the article argues.
Explain how it supports or challenges your thesis.
Avoid vague phrases like “many advocates feel” unless you show where that claim comes from.

For example, instead of saying:
> Many advocates feel that the person will die anyway...

You could write:
> Supporters of euthanasia argue that when a terminally ill patient is certain to die, choosing a painless death may be more humane than forcing prolonged suffering.

Strengthen analysis

You often summarize beliefs, but you need more analysis of why they matter. For instance, when discussing religious values, ask:
How should religious beliefs influence law in a pluralistic society?
Should doctors follow religious norms or patient autonomy?
If freedom is God’s gift, does that support or weaken your case?

These deeper questions will make your essay more persuasive and more intellectually mature.

Revise tone and style

Some parts sound informal or conversational (“Of course this is not true”; “People will start making this a habit”). In academic argument, use more precise and measured language.

Examples:
“spark up the issue” → “intensify the debate”
“useless to society” → be careful; this phrasing is ethically troubling and needs critical framing if used at all
“an complex issue” → “a complex issue”

Also avoid overusing broad claims such as “society always...” or “never at once...,” unless you can prove them.

Correct grammar and mechanics

There are frequent grammar, spelling, and wording problems that interfere with clarity. A few important examples:

“an complex issue” → “a complex issue”
“ethic and human rights” → “ethics and human rights”
“someone else life” → “someone else’s life”
“Opposers” → “Opponents”
“than that should be” → “then that should be”
“patients personal decision” → “patient’s personal decision”
“en a painful life” → likely “end a painful life”
“Kervokian” → “Kevorkian”

Also watch pronoun consistency and sentence boundaries. Some sentences are awkward or run-on. Reading aloud can help you hear where a sentence needs revision.

Address the missing/incomplete parts

The sections marked  must be completed before this can function as a finished essay. Right now, those gaps weaken your credibility and make parts of the reasoning impossible to follow.

Final recommendation

Your essay has a meaningful topic, a sincere voice, and the basis for a strong argument. For the next draft, focus on:
writing one precise thesis,
organizing paragraphs around clear argumentative points,
explaining your evidence in more detail,
and editing carefully for grammar and completeness.

With those changes, your paper will become much clearer and more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position, a recognizable argumentative structure, and a strong sense of moral engagement. You consistently argue that the anti–death penalty position is stronger, and you organize the essay around major pro and con claims, especially retribution, deterrence, and the value of human life. That gives you a solid foundation. For revision, the main priorities are: (1) strengthen your use of evidence, (2) sharpen your thesis and reasoning, and (3) improve style and correctness.

Biggest issue: missing and weak evidence
The most serious problem is that many of your key claims are unsupported or incomplete. Several quotations are marked as , which makes the essay feel unfinished. Since this is an argumentative essay, you need specific evidence and properly integrated sources.

For example:
“Robert Hoyler supports this claim by stating, .”
“Richard Nygaard supports this concept by saying, .”
“David Conway writes, .”

These must be replaced with real quotations or paraphrases, and each source should be introduced and explained. Do not just insert a quote and move on. After each source, explain how it supports your point.

Also, some claims are too broad to stand without evidence:
“the majority of Americans support capital punishment”
“the biggest fear in life is the fear of death”
“the number of violent crimes has not dropped”

These need either citation or more cautious phrasing. If you cannot prove them, revise to something like “many Americans have historically supported capital punishment” or “supporters often argue that fear of death deters crime.”

Thesis and argumentation
Your thesis is present, but it is somewhat repetitive and broad. In the introduction, you say several times that the anti-death-penalty argument is “stronger,” “more sensible,” and “more powerful.” Try combining these into one precise thesis.

For example, you could revise toward:
> Although supporters of capital punishment argue that it delivers justice and deters crime, the death penalty is ultimately unjustifiable because it diminishes the value of human life, rests too heavily on revenge, and lacks convincing evidence of superior deterrence.

This version tells the reader exactly why your side is stronger.

Also, some reasoning needs to be more precise. For instance, you often equate execution with society “lowering itself to the status of the criminal.” That is a strong moral claim, but it needs fuller explanation. Why are state punishment and murder morally equivalent? Are they both premeditated killings? If so, develop that logic carefully rather than assuming the reader already agrees.

Balance and counterargument
You do a good job presenting the pro-death-penalty side before rebutting it. However, the pro side is sometimes described in a way that sounds oversimplified:
“The arguments in favor of capital punishment revolve around the ancient biblical concept of ‘an eye for an eye.’”

This is too narrow. Many supporters argue from justice, public safety, cost, or closure for victims’ families—not only religion. Representing the opposing side fairly will make your rebuttal stronger.

Similarly, be careful with claims like:
“Anyone who is placed in their shoes would wish for the death penalty...”

Not everyone would. Avoid absolutes unless you can prove them.

Tone and style
Your voice is passionate, which helps the essay feel engaged. But at times the tone becomes too emotionally loaded for academic argument. Phrases such as:
“heinous act”
“society simply craves violence”
“wake up”
“effective destruction of civilization”

make the conclusion sound more like a speech than a reasoned academic argument. Strong argument usually works better when it sounds controlled and evidence-based.

Try to replace emotional generalizations with measured claims. For example:
Instead of “society simply craves violence,” write “capital punishment may reflect a cultural desire for retribution.”
Instead of “wake up,” write “reconsider.”

Organization and paragraph development
Your overall structure is logical, but some body paragraphs are underdeveloped. A stronger paragraph pattern would be:

topic sentence → explanation of claim → evidence/source → analysis → link back to thesis

Right now, some paragraphs offer a claim and then repeat it in different words. Push each paragraph further by adding evidence and analysis.

Your conclusion restates your position clearly, but it would be stronger if it briefly summarized the main reasons from the essay rather than ending mostly with emotional appeal.

Sentence-level issues
There are several grammar and usage issues to revise:
Word choice: “highest sentence” should be harshest penalty or most severe punishment.
Agreement/pronouns: “When society takes the life of a criminal, they set...” → it sets or revise to avoid singular/plural mismatch.
Spelling/formatting: “indivi dual” should be individual.
Awkward phrasing: “The fact still remains” is wordy; use However or Nevertheless.
Overuse of repetition: “value of human life” appears often; that is your theme, but vary sentence structure so it does not sound repetitive.

Revision priorities
Replace all  placeholders with real source material.
Revise the thesis to be more specific.
Add evidence for major factual claims.
Reduce overgeneralizations and emotional language.
Strengthen analysis after each quote.
Edit for grammar, precision, and concision.

You have the core of a persuasive essay here. The next step is to make it sound less like a moral reaction and more like a fully supported academic argument.

Feedback Report

You have a clear position: affirmative action remains necessary, and advocates should make their case more carefully. The essay shows engagement with controversy and attempts to consider both sides, which is a strong foundation for an argumentative paper. To revise effectively, focus on clarifying your thesis, improving organization, using evidence precisely, and strengthening style and correctness.

Clarify and sharpen the thesis

Your introduction spends a lot of time on background and on telling the reader what you “plan” to do. This makes the argument feel tentative. In academic argument, it is usually stronger to state your claim directly.

For example, instead of:
“I plan first to define affirmative action…”
you could argue something like:
“Although critics argue that affirmative action creates dependency and reverse discrimination, it remains necessary because racial inequality persists, legal remedies are permitted when narrowly applied, and access to institutions cannot improve without active intervention.”

This would give the paper a stronger sense of purpose. Right now, your essay sometimes shifts between:
defending affirmative action,
explaining the debate,
critiquing the way advocates argue.

Those can fit together, but you need a clearer central claim and topic sentences that show how each paragraph supports it.

Improve organization and paragraph focus

Several paragraphs contain useful ideas but need tighter structure.

Main issue:
The paper moves abruptly from legality, to necessity, to advice for advocates. That shift is not always clearly connected.

Revision strategy:
Try organizing around 3 major claims:
Affirmative action is often misunderstood and should be defined carefully.
It is legally defensible when implemented properly.
It remains necessary because discrimination continues and passive equality is not enough.

Then, if you want, add a final section:
Advocates should strengthen their case by addressing opponents more directly.

This would make the paper feel more unified.

Also, some paragraphs begin with broad claims but then drift. For example, the paragraph beginning “Opponents argue that blacks will never experience true equality…” mixes opponents’ claims, your rebuttal, and social commentary. Break this into clearer parts:
what opponents argue,
why that reasoning is flawed,
your evidence or explanation.

Use evidence more effectively

A major weakness is that many quotations and references are incomplete: “” appears several times, and some sources are mentioned without full explanation. In a finished essay, every quotation needs to be complete, introduced, and analyzed.

What to revise:
Replace all placeholders with actual quotations or paraphrases.
Identify who each source is and why they are credible.
After quoting, explain how the evidence supports your point.

For example, instead of:
“Another author states that, .”
you should write something like:
“Gillespie argues that race and sex had always shaped hiring practices, but affirmative action merely made those hidden preferences visible. This supports the idea that affirmative action did not introduce bias into hiring; it exposed bias that already existed.”

That final sentence of analysis is essential.

Also, be careful with broad claims such as “the only way to guarantee they get into the system is through affirmative action programs.” That is a strong claim and needs strong support.

Address the opposition more precisely

You do try to engage the other side, which is good. However, at times you describe opponents too generally or unfairly. For instance, “They demand blacks to get out of jail, stope using and selling drugs…” sounds sweeping and risks oversimplifying the opposing view. If you characterize opponents too broadly, readers may feel you are creating a straw man.

Revision strategy:
Attribute claims carefully.
Distinguish between serious legal objections, moral objections, and racist stereotypes.
Refute the strongest version of the opposing argument.

This will make your essay more persuasive and intellectually fair.

Strengthen style and tone

Your essay has thoughtful moments, but the style is sometimes wordy, repetitive, or imprecise.

Examples:
“Both sides present strong cases for their argument” → repetitive.
“Now that we have established…” sounds conversational and assumes agreement too quickly.
“nearly infallible” is too absolute for academic writing.

Aim for a more confident and formal tone. Also avoid phrases like “I think” too often unless the assignment specifically invites a personal stance; your evidence should carry the argument.

Correct grammar, diction, and mechanics

There are frequent sentence-level errors that distract from your ideas. Some examples:
“1960's” → 1960s
“tents of affirmative action” → likely tenets
“achie ved” → achieved
“in regards to” → with regard to
“stope” → stop
“Beside the fact” → Besides the fact
“writer's need” → writers need
“opponents statement” → opponents’ statement

Also check:
subject-verb agreement,
apostrophes,
comma placement,
spelling of names (for example, “Gillepsie” may be misspelled).

Final revision priorities

Write a direct, arguable thesis.
Reorganize paragraphs around clear claims.
Replace all placeholders with complete evidence.
Analyze quotations instead of just inserting them.
Present opposing arguments more fairly and precisely.
Proofread carefully for grammar and usage.

You have the basis of a strong argumentative essay here: a clear concern with justice, an effort to engage multiple viewpoints, and a willingness to evaluate rhetoric as well as policy. With clearer structure and stronger use of evidence, your argument will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

You have a clear position and a recognizable argumentative purpose: you want to show that the case against teaching New Age beliefs is stronger than the case for it. The essay also shows effort in research and in comparing different kinds of sources. For revision, the main priorities are: (1) sharpen the thesis, (2) improve organization, (3) use stronger evidence and analysis, and (4) adopt a more academic tone.

Thesis and focus
Your thesis appears near the end of the introduction, but it is long and hard to follow. It also tries to prove too many things at once (“values,” “consequence,” “first hand accounts,” “mainstream sources,” “hard facts”). A stronger thesis would be more precise and arguable.

For example:
> The argument against teaching New Age beliefs is more persuasive than the argument in favor because its sources provide clearer ethical claims, more concrete consequences, and more consistent evidence.

This version gives you three clear body points. It also avoids overstating your case.

You should also define your central issue more carefully. Right now, the essay shifts between yoga, New Age beliefs, Eastern religions, and teaching New Age ideas in schools. These are not identical topics. Decide exactly what you are arguing:
teaching New Age ideas in public schools?
promoting New Age spirituality generally?
the credibility of pro–New Age sources?

Once you decide, keep the essay focused on that one claim.

Organization and coherence
The paper often moves from one idea to another without clear transitions. The introduction begins with a joke about “Yoda/yoga,” then moves into the New Age movement, then into political correctness, then into your thesis. This creates a weak opening for an academic essay.

Consider revising the structure like this:

Introduction
   - Brief background on New Age beliefs
   - Clear definition of the debate
   - Concise thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Problems with the pro–New Age case
   - unclear definitions
   - reliance on opinion
   - weak or questionable evidence

Body Paragraph 2: Why the anti–New Age case seems stronger
   - argument from consequences
   - use of testimonies
   - ethical concerns

Body Paragraph 3: Evaluation of sources
   - what makes a source credible?
   - are “mainstream” and “Christian” the same thing?
   - what kind of evidence is most persuasive?

Conclusion
   - restate thesis
   - explain why this matters

Also, several places contain missing quotations marked as “.” Those gaps make it impossible for readers to evaluate your evidence. In revision, either include the full quotation and citation or remove the reference.

Evidence and analysis
This is the area that most needs work. Many of your claims rely on personal reaction rather than evidence. For example, saying you “ran out of [your] room” to ask neighbors about tobacco is vivid, but it is not strong academic support. Instead, verify the claim using reliable evidence.

A few important points:

Do not assume popularity equals truth. You argue that anti–New Age sources are stronger partly because they are “mainstream” and reach more people. A source is not more credible simply because it is widely read.
Be careful with religious authority. You treat the Bible as a “very true and established book of values.” That may be convincing for some audiences, but in an academic argument you need to recognize that not all readers will accept it as proof. If you use the Bible, frame it as evidence within a Christian value system rather than universal proof.
Evaluate sources fairly. You criticize pro–New Age authors for opinion-based writing, but many of your anti–New Age sources come from explicitly Christian magazines with their own clear bias. To strengthen your essay, acknowledge that both sides have perspectives and then explain why one side still seems more persuasive.

Try to move from reaction to analysis:
What exactly does the source claim?
What evidence does it provide?
Is that evidence verifiable?
Why is it persuasive or unpersuasive?

Tone and style
Your voice is energetic, but parts of the essay sound conversational rather than academic. Phrases such as “that little green guy,” “stupendous job,” or “for all I know” weaken your authority.

Aim for a more formal tone:
Replace casual wording with precise terms.
Avoid exaggeration (“can not possibly be considered,” “very true”).
Capitalization is inconsistent: “new age,” “New Age,” “Teaching.” Choose one standard form.
Watch grammar and mechanics: sentence boundaries, apostrophes (“author’s” vs. “authors”), and spelling (“cannot,” “teaching,” “its” vs. “it’s”).

Most important revision goals
Narrow the topic to one clear issue.
Rewrite the thesis so it is concise and specific.
Replace personal reactions with documented evidence.
Assess source credibility more carefully and fairly.
Adopt a more formal academic style.

Final thought
The essay has a strong sense of purpose, and you clearly care about the topic. Your revision will improve most if you focus less on declaring one side “obviously” right and more on demonstrating why its evidence is stronger. If you do that, your argument will become much more convincing.
