Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central aim: to compare how opponents and proponents of nuclear power argue, and to show that proponents rely more on factual evidence while opponents appeal more to public fears and values. That is a strong rhetorical focus, and the essay shows clear effort to organize the debate into major categories such as cost, safety, cleanliness, and waste.

What is working well

Clear overall position  
Your introduction establishes the issue, identifies both sides, and presents a thesis. Readers can tell what you want to argue.

Logical paragraph structure  
Most body paragraphs stay focused on one main topic: cost, safety, environmental impact, and waste. This gives the essay a workable structure.

Awareness of audience and persuasion  
You are not only discussing nuclear power itself; you are analyzing how each side persuades the public. That rhetorical angle is interesting and gives the essay purpose.

Main areas to revise

Clarify the thesis and keep it consistent
Your thesis suggests a rhetorical analysis: opponents appeal to values; proponents use “facts and figures.” However, much of the essay shifts into *supporting nuclear power itself* rather than analyzing the arguments. Decide which essay you are writing:

Option A: a rhetorical analysis of both sides’ persuasive strategies  
Option B: an argumentative essay defending nuclear power

Right now, it does both at once, which weakens the focus. If you keep the current thesis, each body paragraph should explain how supporters persuade, not simply repeat their claims.

Strengthen evidence and credibility
Many claims are presented as facts, but there are no sources. For example, statements about cost differences, radiation levels, deaths from other energy sources, and waste volumes need evidence. Without sources, the essay sounds confident but not academically reliable.

Also, some claims are too absolute, such as:
“there is nothing to worry about”
“run completely environmentally safe”

These are overstatements and make the argument less persuasive. Use more precise language: “supporters argue that risks can be minimized through current storage methods,” etc

Improve fairness and avoid bias
You often describe opponents as “closed minded,” “uninformed,” or driven mainly by fear. That makes the essay sound one-sided and dismissive. In academic argument, even if you disagree, you should represent the other side fairly. Many concerns about nuclear power are based on legitimate issues such as long-term waste storage, plant accidents, and cost overruns.

A more effective approach would be:
explain opponents’ concerns accurately
acknowledge where those concerns have merit
then show how proponents respond

That will make your analysis more credible.

Revise for sentence control and grammar
There are many sentence-level issues that interfere with clarity. Some common patterns:

Comma splices / run-on sentences  
Example: “Thus, a large group of opponents to nuclear power are formed, they fear this new entity…”  
These should be split or joined correctly.

Word choice errors
“do to” → due to
“counter act” → counteract
“then” in comparisons → than
“took” → taken
“radio active” → radioactive
“rail road” → railroad
“meggawatts” → megawatts

Awkward phrasing
“Through careful analyzation” → Through careful analysis
“the facts around nuclear power” → the facts about nuclear power
“safe operation of nuclear power facilities as compared to conventional power facilities” could be made much more concise.

Use a more formal academic tone
Some phrases are too informal or exaggerated:
“beat the nuclear waste issue to death”
“like nuclear power flat out”
“shot down from the start”

Replace these with more formal alternatives. Also avoid emotionally loaded language unless you are analyzing it deliberately.

Strengthen paragraph development

Topic sentence naming the persuasive strategy

Explanation of how that claim persuades the public  
Brief evaluation of whether it effectively answers opponents

That would better match your thesis.

Suggested revision priorities

Rewrite the thesis so the essay has one clear purpose.  
Add credible sources for statistics and factual claims.  
Revise body paragraphs to focus on rhetorical analysis rather than unsupported advocacy.  
Edit for grammar and sentence clarity.  
Adopt a more balanced, formal tone.

Example of a stronger thesis
You might revise your thesis to something like:

> In debates about nuclear power, opponents often persuade by emphasizing public fears about safety, waste, and long-term harm, while proponents rely more heavily on economic and environmental data to present nuclear energy as a practical alternative to fossil fuels.

This version is clearer, more balanced, and easier to support.

Final thought
You have the foundation of a strong essay: a clear issue, a debatable claim, and a workable organizational plan. Your biggest task now is to improve focus, fairness, and academic credibility. If you revise with those goals in mind, this essay can become much more persuasive and polished.

Feedback Report

Your essay takes on a complex issue and shows clear engagement with it. A major strength is that you do not treat school integration as a simple “good” or “bad” policy; instead, you weigh both educational outcomes and race relations. Your use of personal experience with Chapter 220 also gives the paper a distinctive voice and helps ground the argument in lived reality.

To revise effectively, focus on three main areas: thesis and argument, evidence and support, and style/tone.

Strengthen the thesis and overall claim
Right now, the introduction provides background, but your central argument is still fairly broad:

> “The ethical dispute over school integration has based itself on questions over value and consequence…”

This sounds more like a description of the topic than a debatable thesis. Try to make your position clearer. For example, you seem to argue that school integration can improve education and race relations, but only when it is implemented early, carefully supported by schools, and backed by the community. That is a stronger, more specific claim.

Also, each body paragraph should connect explicitly to that thesis. At times, the essay reads like a list of pros and cons rather than a controlled argument. Consider organizing it around a clearer line of reasoning:
Integration can improve academic outcomes.
Those benefits depend on age of entry and school climate.
Integration can improve race relations, but poor implementation can intensify tension.
Community attitudes are crucial to success.

Improve the use of evidence
You include several references to research, but some evidence is incomplete or too vague. For example, there are places marked with , and some sources are introduced generally (“a study done by two researchers from New York”) without enough detail. In revision:
Identify authors or titles whenever possible.
Explain what the evidence actually shows.
Connect each source directly to your point.

For instance, after presenting a study, add analysis: Why does this matter? How does it support your argument about implementation, age, or environment?

Your personal example is valuable, but it should be handled carefully. Right now, the Chapter 220 discussion is vivid, but it risks becoming anecdotal evidence that stands in for broader proof. Use it as an illustration, not the sole basis for major claims about integration programs in general.

Reconsider tone and generalizations
This is the area that most needs attention. Some sentences make broad claims about racial groups that sound unsupported or unfairly sweeping. For example:
“many of them were rebellious towards students and teachers”
“Many black students took advantage of the white communities reluctance…”

These statements may reflect your experience, but in an academic argument they need to be qualified carefully and supported with evidence. Otherwise, they can weaken your credibility and make readers question your fairness. Focus on structures, conditions, and implementation problems rather than attributing motives or behaviors to large groups.

For example, instead of saying students “took advantage,” you might say that inconsistent discipline and racial anxiety among administrators created resentment and conflict. That keeps the focus analytical rather than accusatory.

Clarify paragraph development
Some paragraphs contain multiple ideas that need clearer transitions. For example, in the paragraph on self-esteem, you move from classroom climate to minority status to parental and teacher attitudes. These are all relevant, but the links between them should be clearer. Use topic sentences that state the paragraph’s main point, then develop one idea at a time.

Similarly, the last paragraph makes an important argument about community attitudes, but it would be stronger if you tied it back more directly to your thesis: community support is not just helpful; it is necessary for integration to succeed.

Edit for sentence-level clarity and correctness
There are many strong ideas here, but grammatical and stylistic issues sometimes obscure them. Pay attention to:
Word choice: “conductive” should be conducive; “hinderance” should be hindrance.
Sentence structure: some sentences are long and run together. Break them into shorter units for clarity.
Missing words/errors: “As can seen” should be As can be seen.
Formal style: avoid phrasing like “I can verify” and replace it with something more academic, such as my experience suggests or my experience illustrates.
Consistency: if you use terms like *desegregation*, *integration*, and *busing*, make sure you distinguish them clearly if needed.

Revision priorities
When you revise, I recommend this order:
Rewrite the thesis so your position is precise.
Reorganize body paragraphs so each one advances that thesis.
Add/repair evidence and citations, especially where information is incomplete.
Revise tone to avoid overgeneralization and strengthen fairness.
Proofread for grammar and clarity.

Final thought
This essay has a strong foundation because it tackles a difficult issue honestly and from multiple angles. Your next step is to make the argument more precise, better supported, and more carefully framed. If you do that, the paper will become much more persuasive and academically effective.

Feedback Report

You have a clear position: you argue that surrogacy is unethical because it creates emotional harm and complicated parental claims. That gives the essay a definite argumentative direction, which is a strong starting point. To revise effectively, focus on four areas: completeness, organization, use of sources, and style/mechanics.

Complete the missing parts

Right now, the essay sometimes sounds like notes for a paper rather than a finished academic argument.

Strengthen the thesis and avoid repetition

> “Surrogate motherhood is not an ethical way for infertile couples to have a child because of the emotional strains and ties present among the surrogate mother and the other two parents.”

This is a workable thesis, but it could be more precise. You return repeatedly to “emotional strain,” but the essay would be stronger if you identified specific reasons. For example:

the surrogate may form an attachment to the baby;
the intended mother may feel excluded;
the arrangement may create conflict over parental rights.

A more focused thesis would help the body paragraphs develop distinct points rather than circling the same general claim.

Improve paragraph organization
At the moment, many paragraphs mix several ideas together. For instance, one paragraph discusses bodily autonomy, infertile couples’ emotional needs, and the dehumanization of the surrogate mother all at once. Try this structure:

Body Paragraph 2: conflict between surrogate and intended parents.
Body Paragraph 3: impact on the intended mother/family dynamics.
Counterargument Paragraph: explain why some support surrogacy, then respond.
Conclusion: restate argument and significance.

Use clear topic sentences so each paragraph advances one main reason.

You refer to outside voices (“Health services,” “Annas,” “the article above”), but these references are vague. A reader needs to know:

Who is being cited?
What exactly are they claiming?
How does that support your argument?

Also, avoid summarizing opposing views in a way that seems unfair or unsupported. For example:

> “They do not see the surrogate mother as a person who has feelings, but rather as just a walking womb...”

This is emotionally forceful, but it risks sounding exaggerated unless you support it with evidence. In academic argument, strong claims need proof.

Develop analysis, not just assertion
Many sentences state an opinion without fully explaining it. For example:

Address logical problems
A few claims need more careful thinking:

You say “biologically there are only two parents, excluding the adoptive mother,” but later you argue “equal rights should be shared among the surrogate mother and the other two parents.” These ideas create confusion about how many parents you think are involved and why.
You suggest the child is “just as much the surrogate mothers as it is the fathers,” but if your point is ethical rather than biological, explain that more carefully.
Be careful with generalizations such as “many people” unless you show who those people are.

Try to make your reasoning consistent throughout.

The paper contains a number of sentence-level errors. Some common ones:

Word choice/usage: “mother hood” should be motherhood; “bore” should be borne; “who's” should be whose; “substancial” should be substantial.
Subject-verb agreement: “Health services, however, argues” should be argue if plural.
Apostrophes: “couples lives” should be couples’ lives; “surrogate mothers” may need surrogate mother’s depending on meaning.
Sentence clarity: some sentences are awkward or repetitive.

Read the essay aloud during revision; this will help you catch repetition and unclear phrasing.

Final revision priorities
Fill in all missing material.
Rewrite the thesis to name 2–3 specific reasons.
Reorganize paragraphs so each has one clear main point.
Support claims with accurate source use and concrete evidence.
Edit carefully for grammar and wording.

You have a strong opinion and an important topic. With fuller development, clearer organization, and more precise use of evidence, this essay could become much more convincing.

   - The conclusion effectively restates your position and summarizes the main points.

Awareness of counterargument
   - You do not ignore the pro-death-penalty side; you acknowledge and respond to it.
   - This is important in argumentative writing and gives your essay credibility.

Main revision priorities

Replace placeholders and fully support claims
The biggest issue is that many quotations or statistics are missing, marked by . As a result, several paragraphs make claims without actual evidence on the page. In revision:

Insert the missing evidence.

After each quotation or statistic, explain its significance instead of moving on immediately.

For example, instead of:

a clear topic sentence,
evidence directly related to that topic,
analysis tying the evidence back to the thesis.

This will improve clarity and emphasis.

This is a claim, but it needs explanation. Why are they less persuasive? Because life imprisonment protects society without state killing? Because retribution conflicts with human rights? Spell out the reasoning more fully.

A useful revision strategy: after every piece of evidence, ask yourself, “So what?” Then answer it.

Improve balance and fairness in the counterargument

deterrence,
retribution,
closure for victims’ families,
constitutional legality.

Then respond point by point with evidence. This will make your paper sound more intellectually rigorous.

Style and language issues

Tone and word choice
Some sentences are too informal or overly broad for academic argument:
“fair is fair”
“scapegoat”
“one would think”

Examples:

“scapegoat” → “a convenient suspect” or “a defendant selected too hastily”

   - Your introduction states a debatable claim and previews the direction of the paper.

This will make your paper sound more credible and less biased.

Topic sentence: clear claim
Evidence: quote, statistic, or example
Analysis: explain how the evidence supports your point
Link back to thesis

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position, compares two authors, and tries to persuade the reader by evaluating which argument is more convincing. That gives you a workable argumentative structure. To revise effectively, focus on three main areas: accuracy of evidence, strength of reasoning, and clarity/style.

Biggest issue: your essay relies on unsupported claims

For example, you accept claims that gay service members would disrupt barracks life, reduce trust, and weaken cohesion, but you do not provide evidence beyond Pattullo’s opinion. In argumentative writing, especially when comparing sources, you need to do more than say one author “makes a sound point.” You must explain why the point is logical and whether it is supported.

Also, the essay includes several places marked , which suggests missing quotations. Without the actual quoted material, your analysis cannot be verified. Be sure to include the quotations you are discussing and cite them accurately.

Be careful with logic and assumptions
A number of your claims are based on generalizations rather than reasoning. For example:

“It is very natural to be uncomfortable…”
“This would offend many heterosexuals…”
“If homosexuals are allowed to serve then homosexuality will be virtually accepted…”

These statements assume that discomfort or prejudice should determine policy. That is a weak argumentative basis unless you justify why such reactions are valid grounds for exclusion. Right now, the essay often treats fear or discomfort as proof.

You should also avoid false analogies and double standards. You argue that gay soldiers and straight soldiers cannot share barracks because of possible sexual attraction. But by that logic, straight men and straight women could never work together either. If you want to keep this line of argument, you need to address that inconsistency directly.

Improve your treatment of Wall’s argument
Your discussion of Wall is mostly dismissive. You say he is “lighthearted” or unrealistic, but you do not fully explain his civil-rights reasoning. In a strong comparative argument, you should represent the opposing view fairly before challenging it. Otherwise, the essay sounds biased rather than persuasive.

Try this revision strategy:
Summarize Wall’s main claim accurately.
Identify his strongest evidence or principle.
Then explain specifically where you think his reasoning is incomplete.

This will make your critique stronger and more credible.

Strengthen the thesis
Your thesis is too wordy and grammatically tangled. It currently takes too long to reach the main claim. Try making it direct:

Possible revision:  
“Although James M. Wall argues that excluding homosexuals from the military is a civil-rights violation, E. L. Pattullo presents the more persuasive case because he focuses on military cohesion, living conditions, and discipline.”

This version is clearer and previews your main reasons.

Organization: make each body paragraph do one job
Your body paragraphs sometimes mix summary, opinion, and comparison in a confusing way. A cleaner structure would be:

Paragraph 1: Pattullo’s argument about barracks intimacy and privacy  
Paragraph 2: Wall’s response and why you find it weak  
Paragraph 3: Pattullo’s argument about troop cohesion  
Paragraph 4: Civil rights vs. military effectiveness  
Conclusion: explain why your comparison matters

Use topic sentences that clearly connect each paragraph to your thesis.

Style and tone
Some wording is awkward, informal, or imprecise. A few examples:

“There should be a ban gays serving in the military” → “there should be a ban on gays serving in the military”
“will make on change their mind” → “will change one’s mind”
“for whomever” → “for anyone”
“there was not reason” → “there was no reason”
“has had an affect” → “has had an effect”

Also, avoid phrases like “the obvious answer may be…” unless you are prepared to show why it seems obvious. Academic arguments usually sound stronger when they are precise rather than dramatic.

Grammar and sentence-level concerns
There are frequent grammar issues that affect credibility:
Subject-verb agreement: “Wall’s articles does” → “Wall’s article does”
Plural/possessive errors: “homosexuals interacting sexually with each other” is fine, but elsewhere noun forms are inconsistent
Comma use and sentence structure: some sentences are run-ons or overly long
Word choice: “affect” vs. “effect”; “there” vs. “their” in some constructions

Read the paper aloud during revision; this will help you hear unclear phrasing.

Conclusion needs to do more than repeat
Your final paragraph introduces your personal reaction (“Before reading Pattullo’s article…”), but that shifts attention away from the essay’s analysis. In formal argument, the conclusion should synthesize your reasoning, not mainly discuss your change of mind.

Instead, end by explaining why the comparison between civil rights and military cohesion matters, and what standard should guide policy decisions.

Revision priorities
Insert the missing quotations and cite them properly.  
Revise the thesis for clarity and directness.  
Test Pattullo’s claims instead of simply accepting them.  
Present Wall’s argument more fairly before rebutting it.  
Edit for grammar, diction, and sentence clarity.

You have the foundation of a comparative argument here, but the next draft needs more critical distance from your source and more precise writing.

Paragraph 2: Drug testing protects athletes from serious physical and psychological harm.
Paragraph 3: Drug testing discourages drug use by student and professional athletes who act as role models.

Right now, some paragraphs overlap, and the “Borlange” and “Test Case” paragraphs feel more like extra source summaries than steps in your argument. Make sure every paragraph directly advances your thesis.

This will make your essay more persuasive.

“strickter” -> stricter

Edit carefully for grammar, spelling, and punctuation.

Analysis: explain how that evidence is effective or ineffective
Connection: show how it supports your thesis

For example, instead of writing that an article “uses fallacy,” explain the exact problem:

A false cause?
An unsupported assertion?

Be specific.

Integrate quotations correctly
The many  gaps make it impossible to evaluate your evidence. In revision, each quotation should be:
introduced with context,
copied accurately,
explained afterward.

Example structure:
*Shriver argues that “….” This matters because…*

Do not let quotes stand alone. Every quote needs interpretation.

> Although arguments for and against school prayer both raise important concerns, the most practical and constitutional solution is a moment of silence, because it protects religious freedom without imposing religion on students.

This version is clearer and easier to defend throughout the essay.

Some paragraphs contain several ideas at once. Try to give each body paragraph one main point. A good paragraph structure is:

topic sentence
evidence
analysis
concluding sentence

For instance, in the section on the Conservative Religious Group, you could make one paragraph about religion in the curriculum, one about freedom of speech, and one about the claim that prayer reduces social problems.

Avoid unsupported judgments
You often call one side “biased” and the other “unbiased.” Be careful with these labels. In argument essays, it is better to show bias through evidence than just name it. For example:

Explain the features that make the writing seem biased.

Word choice: “o.k.” is too informal for academic writing

Titles: be consistent in how you format article titles

Suggestions for revision

Rewrite the introduction so the background is shorter and the thesis is clearer.
Insert the missing quotations and analyze each one carefully.

Feedback Report

You have a clear position and a recognizable argumentative structure: introduction, three main reasons, and conclusion. Your essay shows strong engagement with the issue, and your voice is confident. The next step is to make the argument more academically credible, logically precise, and stylistically controlled.

Main strengths
Clear thesis: Your position is easy to identify: the death penalty is unnecessary because it does not reduce crime, is immoral, and harms society.
Logical organization: Each body paragraph focuses on a separate reason.
Strong sense of purpose: You are trying to persuade, not just describe, which is appropriate for an argumentative essay.

Most important revision priorities

A. Replace unsupported placeholders and missing evidence
Several places contain , which leaves key parts of your argument incomplete. These missing quotations or facts are especially damaging because you often introduce sources (“Forst,” “Solomon,” “Jim Gittings”) but do not actually provide the evidence. Before revising style, you must complete these gaps.

Also, some claims are presented as fact without support:
crime has increased over the years
the death penalty does not reduce homicide
wealthy defendants get better outcomes
innocent people have been executed

These may be arguable points, but in an academic essay they need specific evidence, not just assertion. Add verifiable data, studies, or documented cases.

B. Strengthen academic tone
Your essay often sounds conversational:
“Well, I believe…”
“Well it has been proven…”
“And to society what does a serious murder case do…”
“give people something to talk about”

For an academic argument, use a more formal style. For example:
Instead of “Well, I believe…”, write “This essay argues…” or “I argue…”
Instead of “Well it has been proven…”, write “Several studies suggest…”

Also avoid emotionally loaded wording like “murdering someone because they made a huge mistake.” If you want readers to trust your reasoning, aim for precise, measured language.

C. Clarify and refine your logic
At times your reasoning is too broad or oversimplified. For example:
“the only reason we are debating… is because of the increase in crime” — this is too absolute.
“the state… does not have the death penalty, a debate occurs over its necessity” — not always true.
“many politicians, especially Liberal Democrats” support the death penalty — this is historically and politically questionable, so be careful.

Try to avoid absolute statements unless you can prove them. Use language such as:
“one reason”
“many advocates argue”
“some politicians”
“critics contend”

D. Improve paragraph development
Your body paragraphs have good topics, but they often move too quickly from one point to another. A stronger paragraph pattern would be:

Topic sentence  
Explanation of the claim  
Evidence  
Analysis of the evidence  
Link back to thesis  

For example, in the paragraph about deterrence, you mention studies, then shift to your personal opinion, then to Solomon’s theory. Try to make the paragraph more unified: begin with deterrence, present evidence, explain why that evidence matters, then conclude.

Style and sentence-level issues

Grammar and wording
Here are some recurring problems to correct:

“increases crime” → “increased crime” or “the increase in crime”
“over it necessity” → “over its necessity”
“Another reason in which society does not need” → “Another reason society does not need”
“full-proof” → “foolproof”
“so ciety” → “society”
“If somebody makes a horrible mistake of murdering someone else, then is it moral…”  
  Better: “If someone commits murder, is it moral for the state to execute that person?”

Punctuation and sentence structure
You frequently use commas where stronger punctuation or sentence division is needed. Example:

> “Well, I believe that no matter what the circumstances, there is no need for a death penalty because it will not stop our criminal problems, it is immoral, and may even hinder the development of our society.”

This is overloaded. A cleaner version:
> “I argue that the death penalty is unnecessary because it does not reduce crime, is immoral, and may hinder social progress.”

Also, avoid starting too many sentences with “Well,” “Basically,” or rhetorical questions.

Work on counterargument
Your essay would become more persuasive if you engaged more directly with the strongest opposing view. For instance, supporters of the death penalty may argue:
it delivers justice
it protects society permanently
it may deter at least some offenders

Address these points fairly, then rebut them with evidence. Doing so will make your argument seem more balanced and mature.

Suggested revision plan
Fill in all missing quotations and citations.
Verify all factual claims with reliable sources.
Rewrite the thesis in a more formal, concise way.
Revise each body paragraph for unity and evidence-analysis structure.
Remove conversational phrasing and overstatement.
Edit grammar, punctuation, and word choice carefully.

Final thought
This essay has a solid foundation because your stance is clear and your reasons are relevant. The biggest issue is not lack of ideas, but lack of complete evidence and academic polish. If you revise for precision, support, and tone, this could become a much stronger argumentative essay.

That said, the essay needs substantial revision to become convincing and polished.

Similarly, in the moral-degeneracy paragraph, your point about “correlation is not causation” is strong. Build on it by explaining what kinds of evidence would actually be needed to prove that school prayer reduces violence or improves citizenship.

Show how your source or reasoning refutes it

That point is interesting, but it currently takes up disproportionate space compared with your more central arguments about freedom of religion and coercion. “religious intrest” → religious interest
“at the sake of another group” → at the expense of another group
“oppotunity” → opportunity

There are also places where wording is too absolute, such as “it is obvious.” In academic argument, it is usually better to say “the evidence suggests” or “this indicates.” That sounds more measured and persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay takes a clear position against government-mandated prayer in public schools and shows strong engagement with a controversial public issue. You also attempt to consider opposing views, which is an important feature of argumentative writing. For revision, focus on sharpening your thesis, improving organization, strengthening evidence, and clarifying your reasoning.

Main strengths

Clear stance: Your position is consistent throughout: school prayer mandated by government should not be instituted.
Awareness of counterargument: You acknowledge what supporters might say and try to respond.
Serious tone: The essay has an appropriately formal tone for an academic argument.

Thesis and focus

Your thesis appears at the end of the introduction: public-school prayer should not be instituted because religion would be imposed on children who would recite prayer without genuine interest. This is a workable claim, but the essay later introduces several additional arguments:
parents/community should teach morals
school prayer would not reduce crime
after-school religious activities are preferable
government-written prayer would be meaningless
school prayer violates freedom of religion

These are all potentially useful, but right now they compete with one another. Revise your thesis so it previews your main reasons more clearly. For example, you might argue that government-sponsored school prayer is wrong because it violates religious freedom, does not produce genuine moral reflection, and distracts public schools from their educational purpose.

That will help unify the paper.

Organization

At present, several paragraphs wander or contain internal contradictions. For example, you argue against prayer during school hours, but then propose that public schools should institute after-school religious activities. That suggestion may weaken your position because it still involves the public school in religion. If you keep that point, you will need to explain carefully why voluntary after-school activity is different from government-sponsored prayer.

Try this structure:
Introduction with historical context and focused thesis
Body paragraph 1: Public schools are educational, not religious, institutions
Body paragraph 2: Government-written prayer is artificial and cannot create sincere faith or morality
Body paragraph 3: Mandated prayer excludes or pressures students of different beliefs
Body paragraph 4: Response to the “school prayer improves morals/crime” argument
Conclusion

Make sure each paragraph begins with a clear topic sentence and sticks to one main idea.

Use of evidence

A major issue is that many quotations are missing and replaced by . As a result, your sources currently do not support your claims. You will need to:
insert the actual quotations or paraphrases
explain how each source supports your point
avoid relying on references to publication dates as if the date itself adds authority

Also, some claims are too broad or unsupported, such as:
“The majority of communities would, indeed, find agreement…”
“students will not pray since there will be no reason…”
“there is some connection between the rise in crimes and the lack of prayer…”

These statements need evidence or should be softened. Avoid speculation unless you can support it.

Reasoning and argument

Some arguments need tighter logic:
You say religion is imposed on children, but also claim they will recite the prayer with little interest. Explain more directly why compelled participation is harmful even if some students are indifferent.
You argue that parents teach morals, which is reasonable, but this does not fully prove schools should not allow prayer. Clarify the distinction between moral education and state-sponsored religion.
Be careful with overstatement. For instance, “There can never be freedom of expression…” is too absolute. A more precise claim would be stronger.

Style and sentence-level issues

Your writing often sounds formal, but many sentences are wordy or awkward. Aim for directness. A few examples:

“Supposedly, this would promote moral values i children”  
  → correct typo (“in”) and simplify.
“if they are of a religion”  
  → awkward phrasing; try “if they belong to a religion” or rethink the sentence.
“Does this means”  
  → should be “Does this mean”
“Even is there is”  
  → should be “Even if there is”

Watch also for:
subject-verb agreement: “The majority would simply receive…”
pronoun agreement: “the student’s ability to freely express their faith” should be made consistent
repetition: “moral values,” “public schools,” and “prayer” appear very frequently without variation
inflated diction: phrases like “already decadent public school system” sound exaggerated and weaken academic credibility

Conclusion

Your conclusion restates your position effectively, but it would be stronger if it briefly summarized your best reasons rather than repeating general objections.

Revision priorities

Insert and integrate actual evidence in place of .
Rewrite the thesis to present 2–3 clear reasons.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one develops one distinct claim.
Resolve contradictions, especially the after-school religion proposal.
Edit for clarity, grammar, and concision.

You have the basis of a strong argument here. The next step is to make it more focused, better supported, and more logically consistent.

Feedback Report

Your essay takes a clear position in favor of animal testing and tries to address both sides of the debate. You also attempt to ground the argument in ethics rather than only opinion, which is a strong goal for an argumentative essay. For revision, the biggest priorities are: clarifying your thesis, improving organization, using evidence more carefully, and strengthening the academic tone.

Clarify and sharpen the thesis
Your essay’s main claim appears to be that animal testing is ethically justified when it is necessary to protect human life, though some forms of testing are not justified. That is a nuanced position, but it needs to be stated directly and early.

Right now, the introduction is very broad and spends too much time on “dilemmas” in general. Try ending the introduction with a precise thesis such as:

> Although some animal experiments are cruel and unnecessary, animal testing is ethically justified when it is essential for saving human lives, advancing medicine, and protecting public safety.

This thesis gives your paper a clear focus and helps unify later paragraphs.

Improve organization and paragraph focus
The paper currently repeats ideas and moves back and forth between claims, examples, and emotional appeals. A stronger structure would help your argument feel more controlled.

Consider organizing the essay like this:
Introduction: ethical dilemma + thesis  
Opposing view: why animal-rights activists object  
Your response: why medical benefits can justify some testing  
Evidence/examples: medical cases, safety testing, training  
Qualification: not all testing is justified  
Conclusion: restate position clearly

Also make sure each paragraph has one main idea. For example, the paragraph about animal-rights activists should focus only on their ethical argument, while the paragraph about Dr. Traystman should focus on medical benefit.

Use evidence more carefully
You include statistics, quotations, biblical references, and case examples, but many are incomplete or unsupported. Several places contain , which suggests missing quotations or citations. These gaps seriously weaken credibility. Before submitting a final draft, you need to:
complete all missing quotations,
identify sources clearly,
make sure names, cases, and statistics are accurate.

Also, some examples need more explanation. The “Timmy Baker” story is emotionally powerful, but it reads more like an anecdote than evidence unless you explain where it comes from and how representative it is. The same is true for the thalidomide example, which is also factually confusing as written. Be careful: if an example is inaccurate, it can damage the whole argument.

Strengthen your treatment of ethics
You are trying to argue morally, which is good, but some reasoning is too simplified. For instance, statements like:
“Animals were put on earth for humans to do with as they please”
“A human’s life is more important than an animal’s life”

are presented as self-evident rather than argued. In an academic essay, you need to explain why this hierarchy is ethically justified. You might distinguish between:
testing to save lives,
testing to prevent serious harm,
testing for convenience or cosmetic products.

This distinction would make your argument more persuasive because it shows you are not defending all animal testing blindly.

Maintain an academic tone
At times the tone becomes emotional, rhetorical, or informal:
“Thank God for little Timmy”
“After all, who’s life is more sacred…?”
“What better of an authority figure than God…”

These phrases may work in speech, but in formal academic writing they can sound biased or overly emotional. Replace them with measured analysis. For example:
Instead of “Thank God for little Timmy,” explain that the case illustrates a potential life-saving benefit.
Instead of ending with a rhetorical question, state your conclusion directly.

Also, be cautious with religious appeals. If you use the Bible, explain how that supports one ethical framework, but remember not all readers will share that authority.

Sentence-level issues to revise
There are frequent grammar, wording, and mechanics problems. Common issues include:
sentence fragments: “Some of them easily justified, and some are not.”
apostrophes: “friends side” → “friend’s side”; “human’s” → “humans”
word choice: “insure” should often be “ensure”; “aides” should be “aids”
agreement/pronouns: “One’s values and beliefs tend to stem from their…”  
awkward phrasing: “trying to save people’s lives, one way or the other”
spelling/typing: “contro versy,” “pain less,” “my not”
commas: many are inserted where they are not needed

Read the essay aloud slowly, or revise sentence by sentence for clarity. Shorter sentences will often help.

Final revision priorities
When you revise, focus on these in order:
Write a clear, arguable thesis.
Reorganize paragraphs around distinct points.
Replace missing quotations/citations and verify facts.
Use a more formal, less emotional tone.
Edit carefully for grammar and style.

Your essay has a strong central concern and shows genuine engagement with an important ethical issue. With clearer structure and more reliable evidence, it could become much more persuasive.

Introduction with thesis
Media inequality: TV coverage, newspaper attention, cultural visibility
Sexism and stereotypes: femininity, objectification, respect
Resources and funding: facilities, uniforms, scholarships, travel, recruiting
Counterargument: “women’s sports don’t make money” or “equal treatment creates pressure”
Response to counterargument
Conclusion

This structure would make the argument easier to follow and prevent repetition.

Develop counterarguments more carefully
You do include opposing views, which is a strength. However, some counterarguments are paraphrased vaguely or dismissed too quickly. Try to present them more fairly and then respond with evidence.

For example:
If colleges say women’s basketball is not a major revenue sport, acknowledge that reality.
Then argue that educational institutions are not supposed to fund sports only based on profit; they also have legal and ethical obligations under Title IX.

That kind of response will sound more mature and persuasive.

Watch tone and wording
Your passion is a strength, but a few lines sound too informal or sarcastic for an academic argumentative essay. Examples include:
“at least we don’t belong in the kitchen anymore”
“the men screwed things up first”
“lower end of the totem pole”

These phrases may distract readers or weaken your authority. Aim for a firm but more controlled academic tone.

Sentence-level issues to revise
There are many grammar and usage problems that affect clarity. Some common patterns:

Word choice/spelling:  
  - “supposeively” → “supposedly”  
  - “otherhand” → “other hand”  
  - “Their lucky” → “They’re lucky”  
  - “fell” → “feel”

Agreement and phrasing:  
  - “a sense of respect as a women” → “respect as women” or “respect for women”
  - “they tend not to take competition as serious as men” → “they tend not to take competition as seriously as men do”

Sentence clarity:  
  Some sentences are awkward or run on. For example:  
  “But so far the only improvement has been in the players themselves...”  
  This needs a more precise completion and explanation.

Consistency:  
  Stay consistent with tense and with terms like “women’s teams,” “female athletes,” and “women athletes.”

Reading the essay aloud will help you catch unclear phrasing.

Strengths to keep
The opening scenario is memorable.
The essay addresses an important issue with conviction.
You understand that inequality involves more than just money; it also involves respect and representation.
You attempt to engage opposing viewpoints, which is essential in argumentative writing.

Final advice
For your next draft, I would recommend this order of revision:
Fill in every missing source and fact.
Rewrite the thesis to name your main points.
Reorganize paragraphs around single claims.
Revise tone for a more academic voice.
Proofread carefully for grammar and usage.

You already have a strong foundation and clear commitment to your subject. With stronger evidence and cleaner organization, this essay could become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a recognizable argumentative structure. You introduce the issue, present opposing views, and then respond to them. That organization gives the paper a strong base. The topic is also important, and your conclusion returns effectively to the central claim that schools should protect students’ health. For revision, the main priorities are: completing missing evidence, strengthening reasoning, improving accuracy and tone, and polishing sentence-level style.

Biggest issue: missing evidence
The most serious problem is the repeated use of “” where quotations, statistics, or sources should appear. Because so much of your argument depends on evidence, these gaps make the paper feel unfinished and weaken your credibility.

For revision:
Fill in every missing quotation or statistic.
Identify each source clearly.
Make sure the evidence is explained after you present it.

For example, instead of:
> “According to Time, .”

write something like:
> “According to *Time*, a significant percentage of teenagers are already sexually active, which suggests that abstinence-only messages do not reflect students’ actual behavior.”

Then explain why that evidence matters.

Strengths in argument structure
You do several things well:
You state a clear thesis in the first paragraph.
You acknowledge counterarguments rather than ignoring them.
You organize body paragraphs around major objections: promiscuity, abstinence, and condom reliability.

That said, some paragraphs need clearer topic sentences and sharper links back to the thesis. Try to make the first sentence of each body paragraph state the objection, and the rest of the paragraph clearly refute it.

Improve the logic of your claims
At times, your argument becomes too absolute. For example:
“This shows that the idea of abstinence is a mere fantasy.”
“Condoms are the only means to prevent AIDS from spreading among sexually active people.”

These statements are too broad and can make readers skeptical. A stronger academic style uses qualified claims:
“This suggests that abstinence-only messaging is unrealistic for many teenagers.”
“For sexually active students, condoms are one important and practical method of reducing the risk of HIV transmission.”

Be careful not to overstate your case. Argument is strongest when it sounds measured and evidence-based.

Use evidence more critically
You include research, but often you drop in evidence without analyzing it enough. After each statistic or study, ask:
What exactly does this prove?
How does it answer the objection?
Are there limits to the evidence?

For instance, when you mention the Johns Hopkins study, explain:
who was studied,
what was measured,
and how the results support school condom distribution.

This will help the essay sound more academic and less like a list of sources.

Tone and audience awareness
Your tone is passionate, which is good, but some phrases are emotionally loaded:
“this horrible epidemic”
“abstinence is a mere fantasy”
“should be reason enough”

A more persuasive academic tone would be calmer and more precise. You are trying to persuade readers who may disagree with you, so avoid language that sounds dismissive of parents, religious leaders, or abstinence advocates. You can disagree respectfully.

Style, grammar, and word choice
There are a number of sentence-level issues. Some examples:

“step which needs to be taken” → “step that needs to be taken”
“steer away form” → “steer away from”
“John Hopkins Medical School” → likely “Johns Hopkins Medical School”
“than anyone” → “then”/actually here: “then” is still wrong; it should be “then” only for time, so use “then”? No: the sentence is “If condoms are our only means… than anyone…” so it should be “then anyone”
“the rate in which AIDS is spreading” → “the rate at which AIDS is spreading”
“refutals” → “refutations”

Also watch for awkward phrasing:
“The results they received consisted of…” is clumsy. Try: “The study found that…”
“students would be more apt to practice safer sex” could become “students would be more likely to practice safer sex.”

Strengthen the introduction and conclusion
Your introduction sets up the issue well, but it would be stronger if it included:
a complete statistic,
a more specific sense of why New York schools are debating the issue,
and a thesis that previews your main reasons.

Your conclusion currently repeats the thesis but does not fully synthesize the argument. Rather than simply restating that condoms help, briefly summarize your three refutations:
condom distribution does not necessarily increase promiscuity,
abstinence-only approaches do not address current student behavior,
condoms, while not perfect, significantly reduce risk.

Revision priorities
If you revise in order, I suggest:
Fill in all missing quotations/statistics.
Rework each body paragraph so evidence is followed by explanation.
Replace exaggerated claims with more precise ones.
Edit for grammar, spelling, and wording.
Strengthen transitions and conclusion.

Overall, this essay has a solid argumentative framework and a clear stance. With complete evidence, more careful reasoning, and cleaner style, it could become a much more persuasive academic argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay takes a clear position and shows strong awareness of audience and stakes. The central claim is consistent: arguments for school prayer rely on weak reasoning, while arguments against it better protect fairness and religious freedom. The essay also shows good rhetorical energy and a willingness to challenge opposing views directly. For revision, the biggest priorities are clarity of claims, stronger evidence, precision of language, and tighter organization.

Strengths

Clear argumentative stance.  
From the introduction onward, readers know what side you are on and why. Your thesis sets up a comparison between the two sides of the debate, which gives the essay direction.

Attention to counterargument.  
You do not simply assert your own view; you address what supporters of school prayer say and attempt to refute it. That is an important feature of effective argument.

Moral and constitutional focus.  
Your best moments come when you emphasize pluralism, fairness, and the rights of students with different beliefs. This is a strong line of argument because it connects directly to public education.

Main issues to revise

A. Missing key content
Several sentences contain placeholders such as “”. These gaps make parts of the argument impossible to evaluate. Before anything else, replace each placeholder with the exact claim, quotation, or legal principle you intend to use. Right now, the essay sometimes reads as if crucial evidence has been removed.

B. Overreliance on assertion rather than evidence
Many paragraphs make strong claims, but they are not supported with specific evidence. For example, you argue that prayer does not reduce violence or teen pregnancy, and that supporters confuse correlation with causation. That is a potentially strong point, but it needs proof: studies, historical evidence, legal cases, or expert testimony.

Similarly, references to Mike Gunn and constitutional law should be documented and explained. If you mention the law, name the relevant court decisions or constitutional principles.

C. Organization and paragraph focus
Most paragraphs begin with a promising claim, but some drift or repeat earlier points. Try giving each body paragraph one clear job:
present one supporting claim,
provide evidence,
explain how the evidence supports your thesis,
connect back to the broader argument.

Right now, several paragraphs move between morality, effectiveness, constitutionality, and fairness all at once. That weakens emphasis.

D. Tone and word choice
Some wording is too absolute or emotionally loaded for an academic argument. Terms like “fanaticism,” “evaporates,” “digest the religious philosophy of another,” or “constant threat to their morals” sound polemical. Strong arguments are usually more persuasive when they sound measured and precise.

Likewise, some claims are overstated:
“The only logical and fair answer...”  
“This view holds no validity.”

These phrases may alienate readers who are undecided. Instead, aim for language such as “This argument is unconvincing because...” or “A stronger interpretation is...”

Style and sentence-level concerns

A. Grammar and usage
There are several errors in grammar, agreement, and diction. A few examples:

“prayer lead to moral virtue” → “prayer leads to moral virtue”
“The concept of God is not discourage” → “The concept of God is not discouraged”
“determent” → probably “deterrent” or better, “obstacle”
“na�vet�” → fix the character error: “naivete” or “naïveté”

B. Word choice and precision
Some phrases are awkward or inaccurate:
“moral tarnishes” → “moral failings” or “injustices”
“sacrificing morals for education” seems exaggerated unless you explain exactly how
“public prayer contradicts constitutional law” is too broad; private student prayer is protected, while school-sponsored prayer is restricted

That last distinction is especially important. Your essay will become much stronger if you clearly separate:
private, voluntary student prayer  
from
school-sponsored or coercive public prayer

C. Sentence clarity
Some sentences are hard to follow because they are long or abstract. For example:

> “Because America's vast religious diversity and the lack of apparent good resulting from discouraging religion diversity, public schools need to remain free of public prayer.”

This sentence is grammatically incomplete and conceptually muddy. Consider revising to something like:

> “Because the United States is religiously diverse, and because school-sponsored prayer has not been shown to produce clear social benefits, public schools should remain free of organized public prayer.”

Suggestions for revision

Revise the thesis so it is more specific and less sweeping.  
   Example:  
   *“School-sponsored prayer should not return to public schools because it lacks proven social benefits, risks marginalizing students of minority faiths or no faith, and conflicts with the principle of religious neutrality in public education.”*

Fill all placeholders with concrete claims, quotations, or evidence.

Use evidence in every body paragraph. Include legal cases, historical examples, or reputable research.

Clarify the legal distinction between protected individual religious expression and unconstitutional school-sponsored prayer.

Moderate the tone to sound more academic and less confrontational.

Edit carefully for grammar and precision.

Final thought

This essay has a strong core argument and clear conviction. Your revision should focus on making that conviction more credible through specific evidence, legal precision, and calmer academic style. If you do that, the paper will become substantially more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear moral purpose and a strong central claim: discrimination against people with HIV/AIDS is unjust and often based on misinformation. The paper also shows real engagement with the issue and attempts to use examples, legal context, and rebuttal. That gives you a solid foundation. For revision, focus on accuracy, structure, evidence, and tone.

Clarify and strengthen your thesis
Your main argument appears in the first paragraph, but it is buried in a long introduction and mixed with emotionally charged claims about the “opposition.” Try to state your thesis more directly and precisely.

For example:
> People with HIV/AIDS should not be discriminated against by employers or insurers because HIV is not spread through casual workplace contact, such discrimination violates legal protections, and it denies people basic dignity.

This version is clearer, more specific, and easier to support.

Improve organization
Right now, the essay moves from general claims to examples, then to rebuttal, then back to emotional appeal and law. A clearer structure would help:

Introduction + thesis
Background on HIV/AIDS transmission
Evidence of workplace/insurance discrimination
Rebuttal of employers’ and insurers’ arguments
Legal argument (ADA, rights protections)
Conclusion

Also, some paragraphs are too long and try to do too many things. Give each paragraph one main idea.

Replace placeholders with real evidence
A major issue is that several quotations are missing and replaced with “”. In its current form, the essay cannot fully persuade because key evidence is absent. You need to either:
insert the actual quotations with proper citation, or
paraphrase the source material accurately.

Also make sure every example is fully explained. For instance, the Mark Woodley case is introduced dramatically, but because the quoted material is missing, the reader cannot evaluate what happened.

Be careful with factual accuracy
Some claims are too broad, outdated, or incorrect. This is especially important in an argumentative essay.

Examples:
“Aids is the most common disease of our world today.” — This is inaccurate and too sweeping.
“when someone becomes infected with HIV, it is going to develop into AIDS, and they are going to die.” — This is no longer medically accurate and was too absolute even historically.
“The government has recognized that a person with AIDS has a disability… This is not true” — This is confusing. Legally, HIV/AIDS may qualify as a disability under anti-discrimination law; do not dismiss that without explanation.

Revise with precise, supportable language. Avoid absolute words such as all, none, inevitable, absolutely unless you can prove them.

Use a more academic tone
Your passion is clear, but some phrasing weakens your credibility because it becomes overly emotional or biased.

Examples:
“martyr to the AIDS community”
“ousted by corporate America”
“bury someone with AIDS alive”
“They have nothing to stand on.”

These phrases sound rhetorical rather than analytical. In academic argument, let the facts produce the emotional effect. Aim for controlled, evidence-based language.

Address the opposition more fairly
You do try to rebut the opposing side, which is good. However, you characterize it as “weak” and “a fallacy” before fully explaining it. A stronger strategy is to present the opposing argument fairly, then answer it with evidence.

For example:
> Some employers argue that HIV-positive employees may pose a risk in health-care settings. However, medical evidence shows that HIV is not transmitted through casual contact, and courts have often ruled that blanket exclusions are unjustified.

That approach sounds more credible and persuasive.

Revise sentence-level issues
There are many grammar and style problems that distract from your argument. Watch for:

Capitalization: “AIDS,” not “Aids”
Subject-verb agreement: “instances… have been reduced,” not “has been reduced”
Word choice: “spokesperson,” not “spokes person”
Typos: “hospice care,” “final days,” “morally,” “societies”
Comma overuse: You often place commas between subjects and verbs or around essential phrases
Awkward phrasing: “who and who not to hire” should be “whom to hire” or better, “whether to hire someone”

Read the essay aloud during revision; many awkward sentences will become obvious.

Avoid unsupported comparisons
Statements like “If the issue had been race, or sex oriented, it would have reached the Supreme Court” are speculative unless you provide evidence. If you want to compare AIDS discrimination to other forms of discrimination, do so carefully and with support.

Conclusion
Your conclusion has strong intent, but it would be more effective if it summarized your strongest points instead of repeating emotional claims. End by reinforcing:
HIV is not spread by casual contact,
discrimination causes serious harm,
legal protections exist for a reason.

Revision priorities
Insert or replace all missing quotations.
Correct factual inaccuracies and overstatements.
Rewrite the introduction with a focused thesis.
Reorganize paragraphs around clear claims.
Shift to a more academic, less inflammatory tone.
Proofread carefully for grammar and mechanics.

You have a compelling topic and a strong sense of justice. With better evidence, cleaner structure, and more precise language, this essay could become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central position: business should value ethical relationships, not just profit. That is a strong and debatable claim, and you return to it consistently. You also show awareness that business ethics affects multiple relationships—producer/consumer, employer/employee, and corporation/society—which gives your argument useful scope.

To revise effectively, focus on four areas: thesis and structure, use of evidence, style and tone, and sentence-level correctness.

Clarify and sharpen the thesis
Right now, the essay circles around several related ideas: ethics, trust, relationships, advertising, greed, and long-term profit. Try to state your main claim in one precise sentence near the end of the introduction.

For example:
“Although profit is necessary in business, ethical treatment of consumers, employees, and communities is what creates trust, long-term success, and a positive role for business in society.”

This version is more specific than “business is not just making a profit.” It also gives you a structure for the body paragraphs.

Improve organization
At present, some paragraphs repeat the same point (“relationships matter more than profit”) without clearly advancing the argument. Build each body paragraph around one reason that supports your thesis.

A possible structure:
Introduction: define business ethics and present thesis.
Body 1: ethical producer-consumer relationships build trust and customer loyalty.
Body 2: unethical practices such as lying, fraud, or misleading advertising damage business credibility.
Body 3: ethical treatment of employees and communities strengthens long-term business success.
Body 4: counterargument—some believe profit should come first—then rebut it.
Conclusion: restate argument and explain why it matters.

Also, use stronger topic sentences. For instance, instead of beginning broadly with “Business is an economic institution,” you could write:
“Because business depends on trust, dishonest practices such as fraud and deception ultimately weaken both profit and reputation.”

Replace placeholders with real evidence and explain it
The biggest weakness in the draft is that many quotations and references are missing: “According to Fortune, ” and similar placeholders. Until those are filled in, the paper cannot fully support its claims.

When you revise:
Add the actual quotation or paraphrase.
Identify the source clearly.
Explain how the evidence proves your point.

A useful pattern is:
Introduce source → present evidence → analyze significance.

Example:
“As Business Ethics and Common Sense argues, honesty and fairness are essential to market exchange. This matters because consumers cannot make informed choices if companies rely on deception. In the long run, dishonesty may increase short-term sales, but it damages trust and customer loyalty.”

Right now, the paper often stops at summary. Push further into analysis.

Use a more academic tone
Some wording is too informal or emotionally charged for an argumentative essay. For example:
“This is what irritates business”
“just out for themselves”
“greedy”
“worked great”

Instead, aim for precise academic language:
“This view reduces business to profit alone.”
“Some firms prioritize self-interest over ethical responsibility.”
“Such practices reflect short-term profit seeking.”
“This approach proved effective.”

Also avoid sweeping generalizations such as “many people in business are just out for themselves.” That sounds overstated unless you provide strong evidence.

Strengthen the counterargument
You attempt a contrast with the B.F. Goodrich example, but this section is confusing because it actually seems to support your own argument. A true counterargument would be something like:

“Some argue that the primary responsibility of a business is to maximize profit, and that ethical concerns are secondary.”

Then rebut it:
“However, this view ignores the fact that long-term profitability depends on trust, reputation, repeat customers, and stable partnerships.”

That would make your argument more balanced and persuasive.

Edit for grammar, punctuation, and word choice
There are frequent run-on sentences and punctuation problems. A few examples:

“When someone thinks of business what do they think of?”  
  Add a comma: “When someone thinks of business, what do they think of?”

“It is often said that business and ethics don't mix which is why...”  
  Add punctuation and tighten: “It is often said that business and ethics do not mix, which is why...”

“businesses are suppose to follow”  
  Should be “supposed to follow.”

“Money can not buy you friends, health of happiness”  
  Should be “Money cannot buy friends, health, or happiness.”

Also watch repetition. The word “relationship” appears very often. Vary with terms like trust, credibility, loyalty, cooperation, or ethical responsibility.

Final advice
Your essay has a worthwhile argument and a clear moral purpose. The next step is to make it more persuasive by:
stating a sharper thesis,
organizing body paragraphs by reason,
adding the missing evidence,
analyzing sources instead of only summarizing them,
and revising for a more formal academic style.

Once you replace the placeholders and tighten the structure, this essay will become much stronger.

Feedback Report

You have a clear overall purpose: to compare Shriver’s and Methvin’s arguments and explain why Shriver is more persuasive. Your essay shows a good understanding of key criteria for evaluating arguments—clear claims, relevant evidence, attention to opposing views, and logical fallacies. The paper is organized in a recognizable way, moving from introduction, to Methvin, to Shriver, to conclusion. That structure gives you a solid foundation for revision.

What is working well

Clear thesis
Your thesis is identifiable: Shriver’s argument is stronger because he uses facts and avoids fallacies, while Methvin relies too much on opinion. This gives the paper direction.

Comparative structure
Discussing Methvin first and then Shriver helps the reader follow your evaluation. The contrast between the two essays is one of your strongest organizational choices.

Attention to argumentative methods
You do more than say one author is “better”; you explain why in terms of claim, support, authority, and opposition. That is strong analytical thinking.

Main areas to revise

Replace placeholders with actual quotations and analyze them
The biggest problem is that the paper contains multiple “” placeholders instead of quoted material. Since your assignment appears to be a rhetorical or argumentative analysis, your essay must include the actual language from both authors. Without it, your claims are not fully supported.

When revising:
Insert the relevant quotations.
Introduce each quote clearly.
Explain specifically how the quote supports your point.

For example, instead of saying “He states, ,” you should quote the sentence and then explain exactly why it is weak, vague, or persuasive.

Make your analysis more precise
Sometimes your judgments are too general. For instance, you repeatedly say Shriver uses “facts” and Methvin uses “opinions,” but you do not always explain what counts as a fact, how the evidence functions, or why it is convincing for a particular audience.

Try to move from broad evaluation to specific reasoning:
What kind of evidence is being used—historical example, constitutional interpretation, religious authority, survey data?
Is the evidence relevant, sufficient, and credible?
Why does one author’s use of evidence make his reasoning stronger?

Also be careful when identifying fallacies. You say Methvin commits an “appeal to unqualified authority,” but you need to prove that the authority is actually unqualified, not just unfamiliar to the reader.

Clarify your own standards for evaluation
You begin well by saying a strong argument needs an arguable claim and supporting evidence. Build on that. You might establish 2–3 clear criteria early in the essay, such as:
a clear and arguable thesis,
relevant and explained evidence,
engagement with opposing views.

Then apply those same criteria consistently to both authors. This will make your comparison feel more balanced and academic.

Improve balance and fairness
Your essay is strongest when it explains *why* Shriver succeeds. It is weaker when it dismisses Methvin too quickly. For example, you note that his First Amendment evidence is strong, but you do not fully explore it. If you acknowledge the strongest part of Methvin’s case and then explain why it still falls short, your argument will be more convincing.

Style and sentence-level issues

There are a number of grammar and proofreading problems that reduce clarity. Some recurring issues:

Missing articles: “an influential factor,” “the society of the past”
Verb form/agreement: “This ruling ha become” → “has become”
Word choice: “A research for his argument” → “In researching his argument”
Fragments/run-ons: several sentences are too loosely connected
Capitalization/punctuation: “although” should be capitalized at the start of the sentence in the conclusion
Spelling: “any where” → “anywhere”; “momen tum” → “momentum”
Parallelism: “people and learned through war and consequence” is ungrammatical

A few sentences in the introduction are especially awkward and should be rewritten for clarity. For example, your opening could be simplified and made more direct. Right now it is broad and somewhat repetitive before reaching the main point.

Organization suggestions

Consider revising your structure like this:

Introduction
Brief context on school prayer debate
Introduce both authors
Present thesis and evaluation criteria

Body Paragraph 1: Methvin
State his claim
Quote and analyze his evidence
Evaluate weaknesses in support and response to opposition

Body Paragraph 2: Shriver
State his claim
Show how his evidence is organized
Analyze his use of religious reasoning and constitutional/historical concerns

Body Paragraph 3: Direct comparison
Compare how each author uses evidence, authority, and counterargument

Conclusion
Restate why Shriver is more persuasive
Emphasize the importance of sound reasoning in public debates

Final advice

Your essay has a strong central idea and a workable structure, but it needs fuller textual evidence, sharper analysis, and careful editing. The most important revision is to add the missing quotations and analyze them in detail. Once you do that, your argument will become much more credible and persuasive.

You make your position known immediately and return to it in the conclusion. That helps the reader understand your purpose.

Focusing on the Harris execution gives the essay evidence rather than relying only on abstract claims. This is a strong strategy.

Acknowledgment of the opposing side
You do not ignore counterarguments. You note concerns about sensationalism, ratings, and prison security, which is important in persuasive writing.

Main revision priorities

Strengthen the thesis
Your opening claim is too broad: “the public has a right to be informed about anything and everything.” That is hard to defend and invites obvious objections. Narrow the thesis to fit your actual argument.

A stronger thesis might be:
> The media should be allowed to witness and report on executions because the public has a legitimate interest in how the state exercises its power, although prisons may restrict cameras and other equipment for security reasons.

This version is more precise, arguable, and easier to support.

Improve organization and paragraph focus
Right now, the essay sometimes reads like a summary of events rather than a sustained argument. Consider a structure like this:

Introduction: issue, context, thesis  
Body paragraph 1: Harris case and legal conflict  
Body paragraph 2: why public access/reporting matters  
Body paragraph 3: counterargument about sensationalism and your response  
Body paragraph 4: security reasons to restrict cameras but not reporters  
Conclusion: restate claim and explain why it matters

Several paragraphs now mostly list names of organizations or details of the lawsuit. Those details may be useful, but they should always support a clear paragraph point.

Replace placeholders and integrate evidence
The essay currently contains several “” gaps where quotations or evidence are missing. These must be completed. As it stands, the paper cannot fully support its claims.

When you add quotations, introduce and explain them. Don’t just drop them in. For example:
introduce the speaker,
give the quote,
explain how it proves your point.

Also, avoid relying too heavily on quotation. Your own analysis should remain the focus.

Deepen your analysis
At times, the essay states conclusions without fully explaining them. For example, you say that opponents’ concerns “do not override the First Amendment,” but you do not explain why. Be careful here: the First Amendment does not automatically guarantee unrestricted media access to all government activity. Your argument will be stronger if you acknowledge that rights can be balanced against security concerns, then explain why eyewitness reporting should still be permitted.

Similarly, your conclusion “If you find the event grisly and gruesome, turn it off” sounds forceful, but it oversimplifies the issue. Opponents are not only concerned with personal taste; they are also concerned with dignity, ethics, and sensationalism. Address those concerns more thoughtfully.

Style and sentence-level issues

Revise for grammar and wording
There are several errors that weaken your credibility. Some examples:

“there access should be restricted” → their access should be restricted
“One such case which receive national attention” → One such case that received national attention
“had made to effort” → had made no effort
“inmate are more tense” → inmates are more tense
“they are not reason” → they are not reasons

Also watch for awkward phrasing:
“the death penalty is actually put into effect” could be more direct: an execution is scheduled or carried out
“persons undergoing the death penalty” → people being executed

Maintain a more formal academic tone
Phrases like “it is that simple” sound dismissive. In academic argument, aim for confident but measured language. You can be persuasive without sounding abrupt.

Check punctuation and sentence boundaries
Some sentences are long and crowded with information. Break them up when necessary. For example, the sentence beginning “The reason Schnacke made the decision…” is confusing and seems to combine multiple ideas without proper punctuation.

Final advice for revision

As you revise, focus on precision, evidence, and analysis. Narrow your thesis, fill in the missing evidence, and make sure each paragraph advances one clear argumentative point. The essay’s core idea is promising: the public should be informed about executions, but media access can still be limited in practical ways. If you sharpen that claim and support it more carefully, your argument will become much more convincing.

If you want, I can also help you by:
rewriting your thesis and outline, or  
marking up the essay paragraph by paragraph.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear sense of personal investment, and that is its biggest strength. The opening anecdote is memorable and gives your argument emotional force. Readers can see why the topic matters to you, and your conclusion returns effectively to that personal experience. That said, the essay needs revision in thesis focus, organization, use of evidence, and tone to become a stronger argumentative paper.

Clarify your main claim

Right now, the essay argues several things at once:
parents fail to teach sex education,
schools should teach sex education,
schools should teach morals and values,
casual sex is wrong,
marriage is sacred.

These are related, but not identical claims. Your strongest arguable thesis seems to be:

Because many parents and churches do not adequately discuss sexual relationships with teenagers, schools should provide comprehensive sex education that includes safety, responsibility, and discussion of healthy relationships.

This is more precise than saying schools should teach “morals and values,” which can sound vague or overly broad. Schools can teach responsibility, consent, consequences, and respect more convincingly than they can teach one single moral code.

Strengthen organization and paragraph logic

Several paragraphs repeat the same point—that parents and churches are not doing enough—without adding much new development. Try restructuring around clear reasons:

Introduction: personal anecdote + thesis  
Reason 1: many teens do not receive enough guidance at home  
Reason 2: lack of education increases risks such as pregnancy and STDs  
Reason 3: schools are a practical place to provide accurate, consistent information  
Counterargument: some believe schools should not teach sexual values; respond to this  
Conclusion: return to your personal experience and explain what should change

This would give the essay a more logical progression.

Replace unsupported claims with evidence

A major weakness is the lack of usable evidence. You refer to “one article researched” and to studies, but the essay contains placeholders () instead of actual facts, statistics, or quotations. In an argumentative essay, these gaps seriously weaken credibility.

You need:
specific statistics on teen pregnancy or STDs,
evidence about whether sex education reduces risk,
a credible source about parent-child communication on sex,
perhaps a source comparing abstinence-only and comprehensive sex education.

Also, explain your evidence. Don’t just insert a statistic; show how it proves your point.
