Because this is an argument, the essay should persuade readers, not alienate them. Some word choices sound overly judgmental:
“degrading”
“animalistic”
“youngsters”
“hiding behind their bedroom doors”

These phrases make the paper sound emotional rather than reasoned. Try using more neutral, precise language. For example:
instead of “casual sex is unacceptable, its degrading, animalistic,” say  
  “casual sexual relationships can involve emotional and physical risks when they occur without maturity, communication, or protection.”

This keeps your position strong while sounding more credible.

Develop the counterargument more fairly

You do include opposition—people who think teaching sexual morals in schools is unethical—but you dismiss them too quickly. A stronger paper would acknowledge their concern more fairly:

Some parents believe moral instruction belongs in the home.
Some worry schools may promote beliefs that conflict with family values.

Then respond:
schools are not replacing parents,
schools can provide factual, health-based, and relationship-based education,
parents can still teach their own values at home.

This makes your argument more persuasive.

Revise sentence-level issues

There are frequent grammar, punctuation, capitalization, and word-choice errors. A few patterns to fix:

Apostrophes/plurals: “bird's and the bee's” → birds and the bees
Comma splice/run-on: “Casual sex is unacceptable, its degrading…”  
  → Casual sex is unacceptable; it is degrading…
Word choice: “class mate” → classmate
Capitalization: “Father,” “Him” should not usually be capitalized mid-sentence
Agreement: “Educations of sex in school have…” → Sex education in schools has…
Spelling: “Aids” → AIDS
Awkward phrasing: “The lack of parental guidance regarding sexual relationships for youths' emphasizes…”

Also, some sentences are unclear or fragmented, such as:

This is not a complete sentence.

Revision priorities

Write a clearer, narrower thesis.  
Reorganize body paragraphs around distinct reasons.  
Add real sources and remove placeholders.  
Use more formal, less judgmental language.  
Edit carefully for grammar and clarity.

You have the basis for a meaningful essay here. Your experience gives the paper authenticity; now your task is to shape that experience into a focused, evidence-based academic argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay raises an important ethical question and keeps a clear overall position: the decision to refuse life support should belong to the individual. The paper shows genuine engagement with the topic, and your use of direct questions helps convey the seriousness and emotional weight of end-of-life choices. For revision, the main areas to strengthen are thesis clarity, paragraph development, organization, and sentence-level correctness.

Strengths

Clear stance: Your main claim is consistent throughout: decisions about life support should be personal.
Thoughtful tone: The essay takes the issue seriously and acknowledges complexity rather than oversimplifying it.
Personal engagement: The paragraph beginning “I personally consider…” is one of the strongest because it gives concrete insight into your values and connects them to the argument.

Main revision priorities

A. Make the thesis more precise
Your introduction ends with a position, but it remains broad. “The right to refuse life-support is an individual one” is a good start, but you should sharpen it by indicating why. For example, your essay suggests three reasons:
people define life differently,
people have different limits regarding suffering and dignity,
physicians can advise but should not decide for patients.

A clearer thesis would help unify the body paragraphs.

B. Develop each paragraph around one clear point
Several paragraphs contain interesting ideas, but they move quickly from one thought to another without fully developing any one point. Try using a stronger topic sentence and then explaining it with examples or reasoning.

For instance:
Paragraph 2 discusses what life means, but it becomes repetitive (“memories,” “personality,” “events”) without clearly connecting back to life-support decisions.
Paragraph 3 begins defining death, but it is very brief and needs either evidence, explanation, or a stronger connection to your argument.
The physician paragraph raises good questions, but it becomes speculative (“I think… must have…”) rather than argumentative.

C. Improve transitions and logical flow
Right now the essay often moves by asking questions rather than answering them. Questions can be effective, but too many in a row can make the paper feel exploratory instead of argumentative.

A stronger structure might be:
Introduction and thesis  
Why definitions of life and death vary by individual  
Why suffering, dignity, and quality of life matter  
The role of doctors and family in advising, not controlling, the decision  
Your personal example as illustration  
Conclusion

Areas needing clearer support

Your essay would be stronger if you included specific examples rather than general statements. For example:
When discussing people who want every possible treatment, briefly explain why: religious beliefs, hope for future cures, family obligations, etc
When discussing refusal of life support, explain concerns such as pain, loss of communication, or permanent unconsciousness.
In the physician paragraph, explain the ethical importance of informed consent or patient autonomy instead of only saying doctors are sensitive.

Even one or two concrete examples would make your argument more persuasive.

Style and sentence-level issues

A. Repetition
You repeat key words such as “life,” “our lives,” “decision,” and “considered” very often. Some repetition is useful for emphasis, but too much weakens the prose. Combine related sentences and vary wording.

B. Word choice and phrasing
A few phrases are awkward or unclear:
“what makes our life important to use” → likely “important to us”
“people o want” → typo: “people who want”
“They do not want life to be ” → incomplete thought
“Would we want someone else to make this decision without regarding our wish(es)” → should be revised for grammar and clarity

C. Sentence boundaries and punctuation
There are several comma splices, fragments, and awkwardly joined sentences. Examples:
“But, it is our choice to make: this is our life, our death that we are talking about.”
“Since it is our life, our death we are considering here, the answer must be our own decision.”

Read the draft aloud and check whether each sentence has a clear subject, verb, and complete thought.

D. Missing/incomplete sections
The symbols  indicate places where material is missing. Be sure to complete these before revising at the sentence level, since some paragraphs currently feel unfinished.

Most important next steps

Rewrite the thesis so it clearly states your argument and reasons.
Revise each body paragraph to focus on one main idea connected to the thesis.
Replace some rhetorical questions with direct claims and explanations.
Add specific examples to support your points.
Edit for grammar, wording, and incomplete sentences.

Final note

This essay has a strong ethical focus and a sincere voice. Your central idea is compelling, but the paper currently reads more like a reflection than a fully developed argument. If you clarify the thesis, organize the body more tightly, and support your claims with fuller explanations, you can turn this into a much more persuasive essay.

“substance abuse”

These are related, but the essay needs one central claim. For example:

This gives you a clear line of argument. Then each body section should support that claim.

The essay currently moves abruptly from the commons project to buses, then property taxes, then Marquette identity, then existing greenspace, then football and rifle teams. This makes the paper feel scattered.

“grass, trees, and a fountain”
“so-called psychological differences”
“hoopla”
“Nothing”
“I hate to rain on many a person’s parade”

These make the argument feel less reasoned. Likewise, the comments about racial-history courses and the multicultural center seem unrelated to your main point and may alienate readers. If a point does not directly help your case, cut it.

Many paragraphs begin with a point but do not fully develop it. For each paragraph, try this pattern:

Evidence: facts, statistics, examples
Explanation: show how the evidence supports your claim
Link: connect back to the thesis

spelling: “psychological,” “affected,” “money,” “off” → “of”
capitalization: “That is where it should stay. that is where...”

avoid relying only on speculation

*“ripping him away”*
*“Think back to when you were four years old”*
*“All I see is a little boy…”*

Feedback Report

Your essay takes a clear position against euthanasia and tries to organize that position around three main objections: public opposition, ethical uncertainty, and legal vagueness. That is a solid starting structure for an argumentative essay. To revise effectively, focus on clarifying your claim, improving logic, and strengthening evidence.

Strengths
Clear stance: Your position is consistently against broad euthanasia practices.
Attempt at organization: The introduction previews three reasons, and the body paragraphs generally follow that plan.
Engagement with controversy: You recognize that euthanasia raises moral, legal, and emotional questions.

Main revision priority: sharpen the thesis
Your current thesis is too broad and somewhat inconsistent. At times you argue euthanasia should not be allowed at all; elsewhere you argue it should not be practiced “as freely” as it is; elsewhere you call for more safeguards rather than elimination.

Pick one exact claim and stick to it. For example:
Option A: “Euthanasia should remain heavily restricted because patient competence is difficult to assess, legal safeguards are inadequate, and ethical uncertainty makes abuse too likely.”
Option B: “Rather than banning euthanasia outright, U.S. law should impose stricter safeguards involving families, counselors, and medical review.”

Right now, the paper moves between those positions, which weakens the argument.

Logic and argumentation
Several arguments need stronger reasoning.

A. Public opposition is not the same as moral wrongness
Your first body paragraph argues that because many Americans oppose killing, euthanasia should not be legalized. But public discomfort alone does not prove a law should be restricted. You need to explain why public opposition matters—does it show social instability, lack of democratic consensus, or danger of misuse?

Also, the examples about animals and monkeys do not strongly prove a point about human euthanasia. The leap from animal euthanasia to human euthanasia is too large. If you keep this paragraph, connect it much more carefully or replace it with evidence directly about human cases.

B. The abortion comparison is weak as currently written
You argue that because abortion and euthanasia both involve ending life, euthanasia may lead to similar violence. This is a speculative analogy. The comparison needs more support because abortion and euthanasia differ in legal, medical, and ethical contexts. If you use analogy, acknowledge the differences and explain precisely what lesson carries over.

C. Best argument: competence and safeguards
Your third argument is the strongest because it addresses policy rather than only emotion. Questions about depression, coercion, competence, and family involvement are relevant and persuasive. I would recommend making this the center of your essay and building the whole paper around whether current law adequately protects vulnerable patients.

Evidence and use of sources
A major issue is incomplete or vague citation. You include placeholders like “”, which means key evidence is missing. That seriously weakens credibility. Every outside source needs:
the author’s name,
the claim or quotation,
and correct citation format required by your class.

Also, some evidence is too general. For instance:
“Currently, euthanasia has little restrictions on its practice” needs factual support.
“The ruling on euthanasia took place in 1988” needs clarification—what ruling? In what court? What exactly did it decide?

Be precise. Define terms too: are you discussing active euthanasia, physician-assisted suicide, withdrawing life support, or all of them? Right now, the essay uses “euthanasia” very broadly, which causes confusion.

Organization
Your essay would be stronger with clearer paragraph focus.

Suggested structure:
Introduction: define euthanasia, provide context, state a precise thesis.
Body paragraph 1: patient competence and emotional instability.
Body paragraph 2: legal vagueness and inadequate safeguards.
Body paragraph 3: possible social consequences or ethical concerns.
Counterargument paragraph: explain why supporters emphasize autonomy, then respond.
Conclusion: restate claim and explain what legal changes you recommend.

Including a counterargument would make the essay more balanced and more persuasive.

Style and sentence-level issues
There are frequent grammar and wording problems that make ideas harder to follow. A few recurring issues:

Capitalization: “euthanasia” should not usually be capitalized mid-sentence.
Subject-verb agreement: “all American possess” → “all Americans possess.”
Word choice: “there own lives” → “their own lives”; “a live is worth living” → “a life is worth living.”
Singular/plural: “there is too much gray areas” → “there are too many gray areas.”
Clarity: “Simply because that there are few people…” is awkward and unclear.
Grammar: “there is no black or white answers” → “there are no black-and-white answers.”

Your sentences are often long and repetitive. Try to state ideas more directly.

Example revision:
Original: “There is no black and white area that says whether a life is worth living.”
Better: “No clear standard exists for deciding when a life is no longer worth living.”

Final advice
For revision, focus on these three goals:
Choose one precise thesis about restriction vs. prohibition.
Replace weak comparisons and unsupported claims with direct, credible evidence.

You have the basis for an argument here, but it needs tighter focus and more reliable support to become convincing.

You have a clear overall position: the argument for televising executions is weaker than the argument against it. That is a strong foundation. To revise effectively, focus on sharpening your thesis, integrating evidence, and improving organization and style.

Main strength: clear stance and awareness of argument types
Your essay consistently argues that KQED’s case is weak because it relies too heavily on a rights-based claim and does not address consequences in enough detail. You also attempt to distinguish between arguments of value and arguments of consequence, which is a thoughtful analytical move. Keep that idea, but make it clearer and more precise.

Biggest problem: missing and incomplete evidence
The most serious issue is that key quotations and source material are missing, marked as . Because of that, many paragraphs make claims about what sources say without actually showing the evidence. In an argumentative analysis essay, your reader needs to see the specific language or facts from the sources.

What to do:
Insert the missing quotations or paraphrases.
Introduce each source clearly.
Explain how each quotation supports your point.

For example, instead of:

Write something like:
> “KQED argues that preventing the broadcast would violate freedom of the press. However, this claim remains broad because it does not show why televising the execution is necessary for public understanding.”

That structure—source claim + your interpretation—will strengthen the paper immediately.

This is hard to follow, and it may not fully match what you later argue, since you often describe KQED’s case as an issue of value, not consequence.

States your judgment.
Gives the main reasons.

That version is clearer and better aligned with your body paragraphs.

Paragraph 2: Show why that claim is underdeveloped or unsupported.
Paragraph 3: Present the opponents’ strongest consequence-based argument.
Paragraph 4: Compare the quality of evidence on both sides.
Conclusion: Reaffirm why the anti-televising argument is stronger.

This will reduce repetition and make your reasoning easier to follow.

At times, the essay summarizes what each side believes, but it does not always explain *why* one side’s reasoning is better. For example, you say the opponents’ reasons are “concrete,” but you need to show exactly what makes them concrete.

Ask yourself after each example:

The essay becomes stronger when you explicitly compare the two arguments rather than discuss them separately.

“be drooms” / “ef fectively” → spacing/typing issues
“effect their ratings” → “affect their ratings”

Final revision priorities

Your essay has a clear position, a serious tone, and a recognizable argumentative structure. You aim to prove that abortion is morally wrong by arguing that the fetus is a living human being, a separate entity from the mother, and therefore protected by moral and legal principles. This gives the essay a strong sense of purpose. For revision, the biggest priorities are: clarifying your thesis, improving logical development, integrating evidence properly, and strengthening style and mechanics.

Thesis and overall argument
Your central claim appears to be: abortion is morally wrong because it kills an innocent human life. That is a strong, arguable thesis. However, the introduction spends a lot of time on emotionally charged framing before it states the argument directly. Revise so the thesis appears earlier and more precisely.

For example, instead of rhetorical questions, you could write something like:
> “Abortion is morally wrong, this essay argues, because the fetus is a living human organism, a distinct individual from the mother, and therefore entitled to moral protection.”

That version is clearer, more academic, and easier to follow.

Use of evidence
A major issue is that many quotations are missing or marked with placeholders (“”). As written, this makes the paper incomplete and weakens your credibility. In revision, make sure every source is fully included, accurately quoted, and cited in a consistent format.

Also, much of your evidence comes from assertion by authority (“Dr. X says…,” “Callahan states…”). Expert opinion can help, but it is not enough on its own. You need to explain:
what the evidence shows
why it matters
how it supports your moral conclusion

For example, even if biology shows fetal development, you still need to explain why “human organism” necessarily equals “person with full moral rights.” That is the key philosophical step in your argument, and right now it is assumed rather than defended.

Logic and counterargument
Your essay would be much stronger if it addressed opposing views more carefully. At present, pro-choice arguments are summarized somewhat simplistically and dismissed quickly. Strong argumentation requires engaging the best version of the opposing side.

Questions you should address:
Does biological life automatically establish full moral personhood?
How do you respond to arguments about bodily autonomy?
Are there circumstances (rape, incest, danger to the mother’s life) that complicate your claim?

By acknowledging these points and responding to them thoughtfully, you will sound more persuasive and less one-sided.

introduction to the debate  
fetus as human life  
fetus as separate from the mother  
role of doctors/Hippocratic Oath  
conclusion

This structure works, but some paragraphs are too long and repetitive. Try revising each body paragraph so it begins with a clear topic sentence and develops one main point only.

For example:
Paragraph 1: biological evidence of fetal life
Paragraph 2: argument that the fetus is genetically distinct
Paragraph 3: why distinct life deserves moral protection
Paragraph 4: medical ethics and the physician’s role

That third point is especially important because it connects biology to morality.

The essay often uses emotionally loaded wording: “senseless killing,” “innocent child,” “turn their backs,” “simple solution.” Such language makes your position clear, but too much of it can weaken academic credibility because it sounds more like advocacy than analysis.

Aim for a more formal, reasoned tone. Let evidence and logic do more of the work. You can still argue strongly without relying on charged phrasing.

Redundancy: “whatever decision the woman decides to make”

Feedback Report

Your essay takes on a difficult, provocative issue: whether modern medicine’s emphasis on preserving life may create long-term social and biological costs. You clearly aim to challenge assumptions, and that intellectual ambition is a strength. To revise successfully, however, you need to sharpen your thesis, improve logical fairness, and support claims with accurate evidence.

Clarify your central argument

Right now, the essay raises several related but distinct claims:

medicine focuses too much on preserving life at all costs,
symptom treatment is less valuable than prevention or cure,
genetic diseases may persist because medicine allows affected people to survive and reproduce,
antibiotic misuse contributes to resistant disease,
health-care spending is excessive.

These do connect, but the paper currently reads as if it is moving among different arguments without a clear hierarchy. Try to state one precise thesis near the end of the introduction. For example:

> Modern medicine should shift its emphasis from prolonging individual lives at any cost toward prevention, responsible use of treatment, and long-term public health consequences.

That thesis is still controversial, but it is more focused and easier to defend than the broader suggestion that preserving life itself may be a mistake.

Address major ethical and logical problems

Your essay makes very strong claims about people with genetic disease, “weak” immune systems, and the idea that some individuals should not reproduce. Because of that, readers may see the essay as advocating a eugenic argument, whether or not that is your intention. If you want readers to engage seriously, you must acknowledge the ethical stakes and avoid language that sounds dismissive of disabled or sick people.

For example, statements such as:

“I see no honor to life in this parent's selfish act.”
“We are presenting a continually weaker immune system…”
references to “defects” and minor conditions like “non 20/20 vision”

sound judgmental and overgeneralized. They also weaken your ethos because they seem to condemn people rather than analyze policy.

Instead, separate individual worth from public-health policy. You can argue for better counseling, prevention, and more responsible resource allocation without implying that some lives are less valuable.

Strengthen the use of evidence

Several paragraphs rely on unsupported assertions or oversimplified science.

PKD / evolution section
The Darwin argument is too simplistic. Many genetic diseases persist for complex reasons, and natural selection in humans is not as direct as you suggest. Also, if you argue that medicine increases disease prevalence, you need evidence from genetics or population studies—not speculation.

Genetic counseling section
You refer to Heimer and Bosk, but key quotations are missing (“”), so the paragraph cannot currently function as evidence. Also, you move from describing genetic counselors’ work to claiming they are “failing,” but you do not prove that conclusion. Add the missing source material and explain exactly how it supports your point.

Antibiotics section
This is one of the more persuasive examples because it shows how treatment practices can create resistance. However, there are factual problems:
antibiotics do not treat viral infections;
a finger infection from a cut would generally be bacterial, not viral;
“the bacteria may have become immune” is imprecise—better: “resistant.”

This section would be much stronger if you corrected the science and connected it explicitly to your thesis about short-term treatment versus long-term consequences.

AIDS / Hantavirus section
These examples feel underdeveloped. AIDS treatment is not just symptom management, and the Hantavirus paragraph contains a placeholder instead of evidence. If you cannot fully support these examples, cut them and focus on fewer, stronger cases.

Improve organization and paragraph coherence

A stronger structure might be:

Introduction: present the problem and thesis.
Body 1: medicine’s focus on prolonging life vs. prevention/cure.
Body 2: antibiotic resistance as a concrete example of short-term treatment causing long-term harm.
Body 3: genetic counseling and inherited disease, with careful ethical framing and solid evidence.
Body 4: economic/resource allocation consequences.
Conclusion: argue for a shift in priorities, not for devaluing life.

Make sure each paragraph begins with a clear claim and ends by linking back to the thesis.

Revise tone and style

Your voice is forceful, which can be effective in argumentative writing, but some phrasing is too absolute or emotionally charged. Academic argument works better when it is precise and measured.

Examples to revise:
“solely” → probably too absolute
“disgraceful” → evaluative without proof
“selfish act” → alienates readers
“all is well” → sarcastic rather than analytical

Also watch for mechanical issues:
“Aids” should be “AIDS”
“uniformed” should be “uninformed”
“highly advance” should be “highly advanced”
“Doctors” should not be capitalized mid-sentence
fix typos such as “t decide”

Most important revision priorities

Write a precise thesis.
Remove or rethink claims that imply some lives are less worthy.
Replace generalizations with credible evidence.
Correct scientific inaccuracies.
Complete all missing quotations and citations.
Adopt a more analytical, less accusatory tone.

You have the basis for a serious argument about prevention, public health, and the unintended consequences of medical practice. The key now is to make that argument ethically responsible, scientifically accurate, and logically focused.

or

A clearer thesis will help the body paragraphs feel less like separate summaries and more like parts of one argument.

Improve organization and paragraph focus
Several body paragraphs mix summary, evaluation, and new claims all at once. Try using a more consistent structure:

Topic sentence stating the argument you are discussing  
Evidence from a source  
Analysis explaining how that evidence works rhetorically or logically  
Evaluation of the argument’s strength/weakness  
Link back to thesis

For instance, your second paragraph on the “slippery slope” argument contains useful material, but it could be more controlled. Begin by naming the argument clearly, then explain why it persuades readers, and finally show its weakness—namely that some predictions may be exaggerated.

Insert the full quoted material
Introduce the speaker/source clearly
Explain the quote after presenting it
Cite it properly in the required format

Avoid dropping in a quote without analysis. Every quotation should be followed by explanation of *why it matters*.

What assumption does this argument depend on?

Does the argument rely on fear, compassion, trust, religion, or professional ethics?

This deeper analysis will make the essay feel more sophisticated.

The comparison between Kevorkian and Martin Luther King Jr. is striking, but it may weaken your credibility because the situations are so different. Some wording is emotionally loaded, such as “mercy killings,” “play God,” or “easy way out.” These phrases may reflect the language of one side, but if you use them without qualification, your own voice can sound biased. Since this is an argumentative analysis, aim for a more neutral academic tone:

Typos: “faith i doctors,” “af raid,” “i the care”

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central purpose: you argue that *opponents* of corporal punishment weaken their case by using unclear definitions and inconsistent positions. That is a valid argumentative angle, and your opening anecdote creates an immediate emotional and historical context. The paper shows that you are thinking critically not just about an issue, but about the quality of arguments made about that issue. That is a strong starting point.

What is working well

Engaging introduction
The Tommy scene is vivid and draws the reader in. It gives the topic immediacy and helps readers picture the issue before you move into analysis.

Clear overall claim
Your thesis is identifiable: the anti-corporal-punishment argument is ineffective because of “ambiguous terminology and a wavering position.” That gives the essay direction.

Logical line of development
The body generally follows the thesis: first definition, then inconsistency, then suggestions for strengthening the opposing argument. This organizational plan makes sense.

---

Main revision priorities

Clarify your argumentative position
At times, it is hard to tell whether you support corporal punishment, oppose it, or are only criticizing the weaknesses of anti-corporal-punishment writers. A reader needs that distinction clearly stated.

For example, if your claim is:
“Corporal punishment is acceptable because the opposition is weak,” that is one argument.
“I oppose corporal punishment, but current opponents argue poorly,” that is a different argument.
“I am not taking a side on corporal punishment; I am evaluating the rhetoric of one side,” that is another.

Choose one and state it explicitly in the introduction.

Replace placeholders with evidence and analyze it
The biggest weakness is that key quotations are missing (“”). Since your paper depends on proving that authors define the term differently or contradict themselves, you must include the actual language and then explain it.

A strong paragraph pattern would be:
Name the source
Quote or paraphrase the exact definition/claim
Show how it differs from another author’s definition/claim
Explain why that difference weakens the argument

Without this, the essay makes assertions but does not demonstrate them.

Make sure your standards are fair and precise
You argue that all opponents should “agree upon one definition.” That may be too absolute. In academic argument, writers do not always need one universal definition; they need to define their terms clearly and consistently for their own argument. Revising this point would make your reasoning more sophisticated.

Similarly, “they contradict themselves” is a strong accusation. Be certain that what you identify is a true contradiction, not a nuanced position. For example, saying corporal punishment sometimes stops behavior in the short term but is harmful in the long term is not necessarily inconsistent.

Strengthen the body paragraphs
Some paragraphs begin with strong claims but stay general. For instance, “The opponents also vacillate on the consequences…” needs concrete examples from texts. Try to build each paragraph around one specific point with evidence.

---

Style and language issues to revise

Tone
The essay sometimes shifts between formal analysis and conversational language:
“Were you a tommy?”
“How is it, then, that anyone would choose to believe...?”

These rhetorical questions can work sparingly, but too many make the essay sound less academic. Consider reducing them and replacing them with direct claims.

Word choice and correctness
There are several phrasing and grammar issues. A few examples:
“took he seat” → took his seat
“Even if definition” → Even if the definition
“unteeteringly” → probably unwaveringly
“The opponents to corporal punishment” → The opponents of corporal punishment
“subjected the paddle” → subjected to the paddle
“a member of the class which bought its new teacher her first paddle” is vivid, but the sentence is quite long and could be tightened.

Avoid overstatement
Claims such as “the argument…is rendered ineffective” may be too sweeping unless fully proved. Consider more measured wording:
“is weakened”
“loses some persuasive force”
“risks confusing readers”

This makes your argument sound more credible.

---

Organization suggestions

Consider revising into this structure:

Introduction
Brief anecdote (shortened)
Context
Clear thesis stating exactly what position you are taking

Body Paragraph 1: Definitions differ
Present two or three source definitions
Compare them directly
Explain why that matters rhetorically

Body Paragraph 2: Claims about effects are inconsistent
Present one or two examples of contradiction or hedging
Show how this affects persuasiveness

Body Paragraph 3: What stronger opposition would look like
Clear definition
Consistent claim
Better use of evidence

Conclusion
Return briefly to stakes of the debate
Emphasize why argumentative clarity matters

Your essay has an interesting critical angle, but it needs specific evidence, clearer positioning, and more precise academic language. As you revise, focus less on broad claims about “the opponents” and more on careful analysis of the exact texts you cite. If you do that, your argument will become much more convincing.

Your paper also shows effort to use sources, examples, and counterargument. Those are strong foundations for an argumentative essay. For revision, focus most on clarifying your thesis, improving organization, and correcting sentence-level problems so your ideas become more persuasive.

Strengthen the thesis and purpose
Right now, your essay often discusses “the advocates for censorship” rather than directly making your own argument. This creates distance between you and your claim.

Instead of writing about what “advocates” believe, state your position directly. For example:

Possible revised thesis:  
*Because children are highly impressionable, television networks should face stricter content regulation for violent and sexual material, especially on public television.*

This is clearer, more direct, and easier to support.

Improve organization
The essay repeats the same claim in multiple paragraphs: children are impressionable, networks are becoming more offensive, and society is growing numb. Try organizing by main reasons, not by repeating the same point.

A stronger structure might be:

Introduction
   - Define censorship more clearly
   - Present thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Children imitate televised violence
   - Use the Huston study
   - Explain how violent programming affects behavior

Body Paragraph 2: Sexual content on television shapes children’s understanding of sex
   - Use your sitcom/drama examples
   - Focus on one or two strong examples rather than many brief mentions

Body Paragraph 3: Counterargument and rebuttal
   - Address parental responsibility
   - Explain why parents alone may not be enough

Body Paragraph 4: Public policy and social consequences
   - Discuss government regulation, ratings, warnings, public opinion

Conclusion
   - Restate argument without repeating the whole essay

Use sources more carefully
You include studies, articles, and magazine references, which is good, but the essay often summarizes sources vaguely or uses them without enough explanation. After each source, ask:
What exactly does this evidence prove?
How does it support my thesis?
Is the source current and reliable?

For instance, the 1970 study may still be useful, but you should explain why older research still matters or pair it with a more recent example. Also, avoid assuming that one study proves all television causes harm; acknowledge limits and then argue why the evidence is still concerning.

Develop analysis instead of listing examples
At times, the essay names shows and then quickly moves on. Try to do more analysis of fewer examples. A paragraph is stronger when it:
introduces one claim,
gives one clear example,
explains how that example supports the claim.

For example, instead of listing several shows, choose one violent children’s show and one adult drama, then analyze the messages each sends and why they matter for children.

Improve the counterargument
You do include an opposing view—parents can turn the television off—but the rebuttal needs clearer logic and wording. Instead of rhetorical questions, use direct reasoning:

*Some argue that parents, not the government, should control what children watch. While parental supervision is important, it is not always sufficient. Many children watch television unsupervised, and public broadcasting reaches viewers of many ages. For that reason, baseline content standards remain necessary.*

That sounds more academic and persuasive.

Correct major sentence-level issues
This is the area that most affects readability. Main problems include:

a) Run-on sentences and sentence fragments
Many sentences are too long and contain several ideas without proper punctuation. Break them into shorter sentences.

Example:

This should be two sentences or joined with a semicolon.

b) Word choice errors
Some words are incorrect:
“effects their behavior” → affects their behavior
“effecting America’s youth” → affecting America’s youth
“suppose to” → supposed to
“peak his/her interest” → pique his or her interest
“rebuttal that statement” → rebut that statement

c) Grammar and usage
Watch for:
subject-verb agreement
apostrophes
plural forms
article usage

Examples:
“censorships” → usually censorship is uncountable
“mind’s of children” → minds of children
“seventy's” → seventies
“any body’s” → anybody’s

d) Tone
Phrases like “birds and the bees,” “back in the day,” and sarcastic comments such as “so they say” weaken the academic tone. Use formal language throughout.

Revise the conclusion
Your conclusion currently repeats earlier claims and again refers to “the advocates.” Instead, summarize your own argument concisely and confidently:

*Television shapes children’s behavior and values, especially when violent and sexual content is presented as normal or entertaining. Because parental supervision alone cannot fully protect young viewers, stronger standards for public television are justified.*

Final advice
For your next draft, I recommend this order:
Rewrite the thesis.
Reorganize paragraphs by main reason.
Cut repetition.

Your essay shows clear engagement with an important ethical issue, and you already have several strengths to build on. You identify major concerns about genetic research—eugenics, privacy, discrimination, and genetic manipulation—and you try to anticipate counterarguments, which is a key feature of argumentative writing. Instead, state your own position clearly and argue it throughout. For example:

This change alone will make the essay more authoritative.

Introduction and thesis
Historical warning: eugenics/Nazi misuse
Counterargument: medical benefits of genetic research
Response: benefits do not remove ethical risks
Major risk 1: privacy and discrimination
Major risk 2: designer babies/commodification
Problem of weak regulation
Conclusion

Each body paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that clearly connects to your thesis.

A major weakness is that several quotations are missing, marked only as . These gaps make the argument incomplete. You will need to insert the actual quotations or paraphrase the sources accurately. Also, be careful to explain how each source supports your point.

What exactly does the example prove?
Why does it challenge genetic elimination?
How does it support your broader claim?

Also, avoid relying too heavily on emotional association, especially with Nazi comparisons. The eugenics example is relevant, but it needs careful framing. Explain why it matters structurally: it shows that genetic science can be shaped by ideology and power.

Improve reasoning and avoid overstatement
Some claims are too broad or insufficiently qualified. For example:
“If the governments of the world cannot control the research than it should not be done.”
“Most readers would be doubtful of humanity being responsible…”

These statements assume too much. Academic argument is stronger when it acknowledges complexity. Instead of claiming research should not be done at all, you might argue that it should not proceed without enforceable ethical safeguards. That position will sound more credible and less absolute.

Revise for style and grammar
There are frequent sentence-level issues that weaken clarity. Some examples:

Spelling/word choice: “Mendal” should be Mendel.
Agreement: “One consequences” should be One consequence.
Pronoun form: “ones argument” should be one’s argument.
Comparative word choice: “more bad than good” is less formal than more harm than good.
Missing/awkward phrasing: “if genetic information be kept private” should be whether genetic information can be kept private.
Then/than: “control the research than it should not be done” should be then.
Run-on/wordy sentences: many sentences could be shortened for clarity.

You should also work on concision. For example:
> “The possible consequences of genetic research may be the strongest part of an argument opposing that research.”
could become:
> “The strongest case against genetic research lies in its potential consequences.”

Make the conclusion match the argument
Your conclusion still talks about what “would make for an effective argument,” rather than stating what your essay has shown. The conclusion should directly reinforce your thesis:
summarize your strongest points,
emphasize why they matter,
leave the reader with a final judgment.

Revision priorities
Replace the “how to argue” approach with a direct argument.
Write a clear thesis that reflects your actual position.
Insert and properly explain all missing source material.
Reorganize paragraphs around claims, not advice to writers.
Edit carefully for grammar, precision, and academic tone.

You have a strong topic and several promising ideas. The next step is to make the essay sound like your own focused, evidence-based argument rather than a blueprint for one.

Feedback Report

You have a clear and worthwhile goal: to compare how *Newsweek* and *JAMA* present the same issue differently because of audience and purpose. Your essay shows strong awareness of rhetoric, especially audience, tone, and presentation. The paper also has a clear overall structure, moving from introduction to comparison and then to conclusion. For revision, the main priorities are: sharpening your thesis, improving accuracy and fairness in your analysis, and strengthening paragraph-level organization.

What is working well

Strong topic choice and clear comparison. You identify an interesting difference between a popular magazine and a medical journal.
Good rhetorical focus. You consistently return to audience, context, and attitude.
Effective opening scene. The Saturday-night campus image creates interest and introduces the issue vividly.
Use of evidence. You include statistics and examples from both articles, which helps support your claims.

Main revision priorities

Make your thesis more precise and arguable
Your thesis currently suggests that *Newsweek* may “exploit” the topic to increase readership. That is a strong claim, but the essay does not fully prove exploitation; it more clearly proves that the two publications select and frame evidence differently for different audiences.

A revised thesis could be:
> Although both *Newsweek* and *JAMA* address college binge drinking, they differ in evidence, tone, and presentation because they write for different audiences and purposes.

This is more focused and easier to support consistently.

Separate description from judgment
At times, your essay moves from analysis into assumption, especially about *Newsweek*. For example, claims like “the problems… are really not of concern to them” or “people are for some reason drawn to look at the dark side” sound speculative unless you can prove them. In rhetorical analysis, it is stronger to focus on what the text does rather than what the writers secretly intend.

Instead of:
“Newsweek only looks at the negative side”
  
Try:
“Newsweek emphasizes dramatic consequences of binge drinking, using vivid examples and selective statistics to create urgency for a general readership.”

This keeps your point strong without overreaching.

Improve paragraph focus
Several body paragraphs cover too many ideas at once. For example, the paragraph beginning “Audience is one of the most important factors…” discusses audience, assumptions, emotional appeal, and rhetoric all together. Try giving each paragraph one main claim.

A possible structure:
Audience and purpose
Use of evidence/statistics
Physical presentation
Tone and attitude
Social context / implications

Then begin each paragraph with a clear topic sentence tied to the thesis.

Use more direct textual evidence
You refer to words such as  and mention examples from the articles, but some quotations are missing or incomplete. To make your analysis persuasive, include specific quotations or paraphrased details from both texts and explain them carefully.

For example:
Quote a loaded phrase from *Newsweek* and explain how it creates alarm.
Quote or paraphrase *JAMA*’s methods/results headings and explain how that structure builds credibility.

Be careful with key terms
You use “physical context” and “social context,” but these ideas are not always defined clearly. Sometimes “physical context” seems to mean layout/design; sometimes “social context” means tone, assumptions, or cultural meaning. Define these terms briefly when you first introduce them, and then use them consistently.

Also, “rhetorical site” in the conclusion seems to be the wrong term. You may mean “rhetorical situation.”

Style and sentence-level concerns

There are a number of grammar, word choice, and mechanics issues that make the essay sound less polished than your ideas deserve. Common patterns include:

Affect/effect confusion  
  “audience has an affect” → “audience has an effect”
Its/it’s confusion  
  “hook it audience” → “hook its audience”
Plural/possessive issues  
  “writers assumptions” → “writers’ assumptions”
Word form/typos  
  “any give time” → “any given time”  
  “todays college campuses” → “today’s college campuses”  
  “56% or men” → “56% of men”
Agreement problems  
  “this make for differences” → “this makes for differences”
Overuse of vague phrasing  
  Phrases like “looks at,” “good writer,” and “interesting” are too general. Try more precise verbs such as “emphasizes,” “frames,” “selects,” “positions,” or “appeals.”

You should also watch shifts between formal academic voice and casual opinion, such as “as any college student can tell you.” That line weakens your credibility because it substitutes personal opinion for analysis.

Final advice for revision

As you revise, focus on this question: How do these two publications frame the same issue differently because of audience and purpose? If you keep answering that question in each paragraph, your essay will become much more unified.

Revision checklist
Rewrite the thesis to make it precise and supportable.
Give each body paragraph one clear main point.
Add specific quotations/examples from both articles.
Reduce unsupported assumptions about writers’ motives.
Define key rhetorical terms clearly.
Edit carefully for grammar, punctuation, and word choice.

You have the foundation of a strong rhetorical analysis. With a more focused argument and cleaner organization, this essay could become much more convincing.

Biggest issues to revise

A. Missing quotations make the paper hard to evaluate

B. Thesis needs to be sharper
Your first paragraph circles around the topic, but the central claim is not stated directly enough. A strong thesis should tell the reader:
your position, and  
the main reasons for it.

For example, your thesis could be something like:

That would give the essay a much clearer structure.

C. Organization is repetitive

Then make sure each paragraph sticks to one claim.

“birthcontrol” → “birth control”

“prevelant” → “prevalent”

Some sentences try to do too much and become hard to follow. For example, your first paragraph contains multiple long sentences with several ideas packed together. Break them up so each sentence has one clear purpose.

You alternate among “proponents,” “supporters,” “followers,” and “advocates,” sometimes in ways that are confusing. Be precise about who is being discussed. Also, avoid putting quotation marks around terms every time (“abstinence-only education,” “comprehensive sex education”); define the terms once, then use them normally.

What is the evidence?
Why does this evidence matter?

Sometimes you move too quickly from quotation to conclusion. For instance, if a study is flawed, explain exactly why the flaw undermines the broader case for abstinence-only education.

Also be careful with statements like “can be easily refuted with common sense.” In academic argument, “common sense” is not enough; explain the logic directly.

For example:

Revision priorities

Final thought

Feedback Report

Your essay shows strong engagement with a difficult ethical and legal issue, and one of its biggest strengths is that it does more than summarize the debate: you try to evaluate the quality of each side’s arguments. That analytical move is ambitious and appropriate for an argumentative essay. You also return consistently to key criteria—values, consequences, legality, examples, and public opinion—which gives the paper a recognizable framework.

That said, your revision should focus on clarifying your thesis, tightening organization, and strengthening precision in style and evidence.

Clarify the central claim

Right now, your introduction suggests several different goals:

discussing who should have rights over a child,
evaluating how each side argues,
claiming there is “still no answer,”
criticizing both sides for weak use of values and consequences.

Because of this, the essay sometimes feels undecided about whether it is arguing a position on adoption disputes or analyzing the rhetoric of others.

A stronger thesis would state exactly what the essay is doing. For example:

*Although both biological-parent and adoptive-parent advocates raise valid concerns, public debate over adoption custody cases is weakened by emotional examples, inconsistent use of evidence, and overgeneralization.*
  
or, if you want to take a stronger position:

*Custody disputes in adoption cases should be decided case by case because both sides rely too heavily on simplified moral claims rather than careful evidence about the child’s welfare.*

That would give the essay a clearer purpose and help unify the body paragraphs.

Improve organization and paragraph focus

Many paragraphs contain several ideas at once: summary of one side’s argument, your critique of it, and then a suggestion for how that side could argue better. This makes the line of reasoning hard to follow.

Try revising so that each paragraph has:

a clear topic sentence,
a summary of one specific claim,
your analysis of its strengths/weaknesses,
a concluding sentence linking back to the thesis.

For example, the paragraphs on adoptive parents’ use of extreme cases could be grouped under one larger point: their argument often relies on fear rather than representative evidence.

You also repeat similar claims in multiple places, especially that both sides use rare cases and generalize too much. In revision, consolidate repetition and make each paragraph add something new.

Distinguish clearly between the debate and your own position

At times, the paper sounds neutral; at other times, you appear to side with one group briefly and then pull back. For instance, you write, “Therefore, it is only right that she be returned to her biological parents,” but elsewhere you criticize biological-parent advocates. That can confuse readers about whether you are endorsing the claim or simply explaining that side’s reasoning.

Use clearer signal phrases such as:

*Supporters of biological parents argue…*
*This reasoning is persuasive because…*
*However, this claim is weakened by…*

That way readers always know whose view is being presented.

Strengthen evidence and address incomplete citations

The essay refers to important cases and sources, but many references remain vague, and several placeholders (“”) are still present. Those must be filled in. As written, they seriously weaken your credibility because the reader cannot evaluate the evidence.

Also, some claims need more support or qualification. For example:

“The media portrayed the adoptive parents as saints…” is a strong claim. You need evidence from coverage or specific wording from articles.
“Young children are known for their resilience…” also needs support; otherwise it sounds like assumption rather than evidence.
“This point is useless” is too absolute unless you can prove the evidence is invalid.

Try to include more precise attribution and, if possible, quote or paraphrase specific sources.

Refine style and tone

Your writing is generally fluent, but some word choices are too informal, emotional, or imprecise for academic argument.

Examples:
“does nothing more than instill great fear” → could become *primarily appeals to fear rather than representative evidence*
“This point is useless” → *This point remains insufficiently supported*
“cut and dry” → *clear-cut*

Also watch for awkward or inconsistent phrasing:
“proponents for adoptive parents” → usually *proponents of adoptive parents’ rights* or *supporters of adoptive parents*
“opponents to biological parents” → *opponents of prioritizing biological parents’ rights*

Edit for sentence-level correctness

There are several grammar and mechanics issues to fix:

subject-verb agreement: “neither proponents nor opponents … attempt” should be checked for parallel structure and tense.
word form: “could possible happen” → *could possibly happen*
pronoun consistency: you shift among *him*, *his/her*, and *their*. Choose one consistent, inclusive strategy.
typos: “on could argue” → *one could argue*; “each cases” → *each case*.

Reading aloud during revision will help you catch these.

Strengthen the conclusion

Your conclusion effectively returns to the children’s welfare, which is compelling. However, it mostly restates earlier points. Consider ending with a sharper final claim: for example, that the debate becomes clearer only when courts and advocates move away from emotional absolutes and focus on the best interests of the child in each specific case.

Revision priorities

Replace the thesis with one clear, arguable claim.
Remove placeholders and complete all source references.
Reorganize body paragraphs by major analytical point.
Clarify when you are summarizing others versus giving your own judgment.
Revise tone for precision and academic style.

You have the foundation of a thoughtful analytical essay. With a clearer thesis and tighter structure, your argument will become much more persuasive.

Your essay has a clear topic, a definite position, and an attempt to weigh more than one solution. That gives you a strong starting point for an argumentative paper. Clarify the thesis and argument structure

state which one you support and why.

This gives the paper a clearer line of argument.

how it supports your position.

a quotation from a source,

or your own explanation.

Similarly, the University of Virginia example needs analysis: does the honor code work because students are educated about it early, because expectations are consistent, or because the community enforces it?

Feedback Report

Your essay shows clear effort to analyze *both sides* of a controversial legal issue rather than simply summarize the O.J. Simpson case. That balanced approach is a real strength. You also have a recognizable organizational pattern: introduction, background, argument for admissibility, argument against admissibility, and conclusion. For revision, though, you need to sharpen your thesis, improve accuracy and evidence use, and make your analysis more precise.

Clarify your central argument
Right now, the essay begins with a question—“How should the Fourth Amendment be interpreted?”—but it never fully answers it. Your conclusion also shifts away from taking a clear position and ends with a practical recommendation about search warrants. That makes the paper feel more descriptive than argumentative.

Revise by writing a thesis that makes a claim about the rhetoric of the two sides. For example:

*In debates over the search of O.J. Simpson’s home, supporters of admissibility rely mainly on appeals to emergency and judicial authority, while opponents focus on police credibility and procedural inconsistency; ultimately, the opposition’s argument is stronger because it raises more serious doubts about the reliability of the evidence.*

Or, if you want to stay neutral:

*The controversy over the search of Simpson’s home shows how disputes about the Fourth Amendment depend not only on legal interpretation but also on competing rhetorical appeals to safety, authority, and credibility.*

A strong thesis will help every paragraph stay focused.

Strengthen analytical focus
At several points, the essay slips into summary of what each side says instead of analyzing *how* the argument works. Since you repeatedly discuss what the proposition and opposition “attempt” to do, your paper seems to be about rhetoric. If so, make that explicit and consistently analyze persuasive strategies such as:

appeals to emotion (“another life was possibly in danger”)
appeals to authority (the judge’s ruling)
appeals to character/credibility (Fuhrman’s past conduct)
appeals to public memory (Rodney King/LAPD distrust)

When you revise, ask: *What is each side trying to make the audience feel, believe, or doubt? Why is that effective or ineffective?*

Use evidence more carefully
The biggest issue in the draft is incomplete evidence. You include placeholders like “,” which leaves key claims unsupported. You also refer to “one article” or “another article” without always integrating quotations or explaining them in detail.

For revision:
Replace every placeholder with the actual quotation or remove the reference.
Introduce sources clearly.
After quoting, explain how the quote supports your point.
Avoid overgeneralizations such as “there is no reason, then, that this crucial evidence is not fair game.” That sounds more like opinion than analysis.

A useful structure is: claim → evidence → explanation.

Improve legal and factual precision
Some wording is inaccurate or too broad:
The Fourth Amendment does not say “illegal search and seizure is unlawful, except for in ‘exigent circumstances.’” That is a simplification; “exigent circumstances” is a judicial doctrine, not a phrase from the Constitution itself.
Be careful with names and spelling: Los Angeles, Ronald Goldman, not “Goldwin.”
Distinguish between the constitutional issue (whether the search was lawful) and the evidentiary issue (whether the evidence should be admitted).

These corrections matter because your argument depends on legal credibility.

Work on tone and wording
Some sentences use informal or imprecise phrasing:
“they did a little homework”
“scratching to find anything”
“fair game”
“picking on the L.A.P.D.”

These phrases weaken your academic tone. Replace them with more neutral language, such as:
“investigated Fuhrman’s record”
“struggling to challenge admissibility”
“admissible evidence”
“criticizing LAPD conduct”

Also avoid unsupported claims like “After all, the detectives have earned their badge, and they deserve to have their decisions supported by the court.” That sounds emotionally loaded and doesn’t fit an analytical essay.

Refine paragraph structure and transitions
Most body paragraphs have a topic, but some become too long and cover several ideas at once. Try to give each paragraph one main point:
one paragraph on emotional appeals to danger
one on judicial authority
one on Fuhrman’s credibility
one on contradictions in police testimony

“More significantly”
“However”

subject-verb agreement: “the opposition … question” → “questions”

pronouns: avoid “he/she”; use “they” instead

Feedback Report

Your essay takes on a difficult and important topic, and your central idea is clear: arguments against suicide are often asserted without enough evidence. That is a focused and potentially strong claim. You also make a real effort to engage multiple sources and to evaluate the quality of their reasoning rather than simply summarize them. That analytical aim is a major strength.

What is working
Clear overall position  
   You consistently argue that opponents of suicide rely on claims about selfishness, family grief, religion, and “cries for help” without adequately supporting them.

Analytical intent  
   You are not just listing arguments; you are judging whether they are well supported. This is good argumentative writing.

Attention to counterargument  
   You acknowledge what opponents might say and then respond. That gives the essay a stronger critical frame.

Main revision priorities

Clarify your thesis and scope
Right now, the introduction moves between several ideas: suicide as drastic, “rational suicide,” arguments against suicide, and lack of evidence. Your actual thesis does not fully emerge until the end of the first paragraph.

Try revising the introduction so it leads directly to your main claim. For example:

> Although opponents of suicide often argue that suicide is selfish, harmful to families, and morally wrong, these claims are frequently presented without sufficient evidence. A convincing case against suicide requires stronger psychological, social, and ethical support.

Also, your essay says you will “only discuss the opponents of suicide,” but sometimes your phrasing suggests you are also defending rational suicide. Be precise about your purpose: are you arguing for rational suicide, or only criticizing weak anti-suicide arguments?

Integrate sources properly
The biggest problem in the current draft is the repeated use of “” where quotations or source material should appear. Without the actual evidence, the essay cannot persuade a reader. You must include:
the exact quotation or paraphrase,
the author’s full name,
enough context for the reader to understand the source,
and correct citation in the required style.

Also, sometimes you question authors’ evidence while providing none of your own. If your argument is that their claims are unsupported, show this by quoting them and then explaining exactly what is missing.

Strengthen logical precision
At times, the essay overstates or generalizes:
“Suicide means the end of all problems” is too broad and sounds insensitive.
“Those problems are usually solved easily” is an unsupported and troubling claim.
“The topic of suicide will somehow affect every person on earth” is exaggerated.

Use more careful academic language: *may*, *can*, *often*, *some opponents argue*, etc

Also, be careful not to substitute speculation for analysis. For example, “Maybe Mr Rauscher does not think about…” is informal and weak. Instead, write something like:

> Rauscher does not address cases in which terminal illness leads a person to believe that suicide might reduce suffering for both self and family.

Improve organization
The essay has a visible structure, but each paragraph can be made more unified. A stronger pattern would be:

Topic sentence naming the anti-suicide argument
Evidence from a source
Analysis of what the source claims
Evaluation of why the evidence is insufficient
Transition to the next point

For example, your paragraph on family grief begins well but becomes repetitive. Focus each paragraph on one claim only.

Distinguish lack of evidence from disagreement
Sometimes you criticize an argument because you disagree with its conclusion, not because it lacks support. Those are different criticisms. For instance, saying that a cancer patient might think about family suffering is a counterexample; saying Rauscher provides no data is a critique of evidence. Both can work, but separate them clearly.

There are several moments where the style becomes conversational or imprecise:
“Of course”
“I am sure”
“For all the reader’s know”
“car salespeople or accountants”

These phrases weaken your authority. Keep the tone analytical and respectful, especially on a topic like suicide.

Sentence-level issues to edit

“what does the person attempting suicide thinking?” → “what is the person attempting suicide thinking?”
“When there little safety” → “When there is little safety”
“the family’s helplessness in comforting the person does to their emotions” → awkward phrasing; simplify
“so lution” → “solution”
“For all the reader’s know” → “For all readers know”

Also check pronoun consistency and avoid repetitive phrases like “the person attempting suicide.”

Suggested next steps
Replace every “” with real quotations or paraphrases and citations.
Rewrite the introduction around one precise thesis.
Revise each body paragraph to focus on one anti-suicide argument.
Remove overgeneralized or unsupported claims.
Edit for tone, grammar, and concision.

This essay has a strong critical instinct and a meaningful central claim. Your revision should focus on evidence, precision, and organization. If you support your analysis with actual source material and sharpen the thesis, this could become a thoughtful and persuasive academic argument.

That is a strong direction for an argumentative paper. Your use of Durkheim and Marx gives the essay a scholarly frame, and the paper has a recognizable structure: introduction, theoretical discussion, and conclusion.

What is working well

Serious, focused topic
   - The essay stays centered on adolescent suicide rather than drifting too broadly into “problems facing youth.”

Use of theory
   - Bringing in Structural Functionalism/Durkheim and Conflict Theory/Marx is a good strategy. This gives the paper an analytical lens instead of only presenting statistics.

Attempt to synthesize sources
   - You do more than list facts; you try to connect research, case studies, and theory. That is an important academic skill.

Main revision priorities

Clarify your thesis
Right now, the paper suggests several possible claims:
adolescent suicide is the worst problem facing young people,
it gets too little attention,
Durkheim and Marx help explain it,
depression is the common cause.

Because there are multiple competing claims, the essay feels unfocused. Choose one main argument and make everything support it.

For example, a stronger thesis might be:
> Adolescent suicide should be understood not only as an individual psychological issue but also as a social problem shaped by weak social bonds, rapid social change, and structural pressures, as shown through Durkheim’s and Marx’s theories.

That thesis is arguable, specific, and matches the body paragraphs.

Strengthen paragraph organization
Many paragraphs begin broadly and then move in several directions. Try this structure for each body paragraph:

Topic sentence: state the paragraph’s main claim.
Explanation of theory: define the concept clearly.
Application to adolescent suicide: explain exactly how the theory helps us understand the issue.
Evidence: use statistics, examples, or case studies.
Link back to thesis.

For instance, your Durkheim section would be stronger if each suicide type had its own clear claim and evidence, rather than mixing summary and interpretation.

Be careful with argument vs. overstatement
Some claims are too absolute and weaken credibility. For example:
“the worst problem facing the young people today”
“There are no theories, theorists, researchers, or even case studies that argue...”

These sweeping statements are difficult to prove. Academic writing is usually stronger when it is precise and qualified:
“one of the most serious problems facing young people today”
“many researchers identify depression as a major contributing factor”

Improve source integration
You refer to sources with “<R>” and “,” but often the evidence is not introduced or explained fully. Avoid dropping in statistics or case-study references without context. When you use evidence, explain:
Who produced the data?
Why is it relevant?
What does it show in relation to your theory?

Also, some statistics appear dated. If this is a revised assignment, consider whether your evidence still supports claims about “today’s society.”

Style and sentence-level issues

Your draft has many grammar, word choice, and sentence clarity problems that make the argument harder to follow. Focus especially on these patterns:

Word choice and phrasing
Examples:
“the worst problem concerning are young people today” → “the worst problem concerning young people today”
“contraversal” → “controversial”
“publically” → “publicly”
“appopriatly” → “appropriately”
“programed” → “programmed”

Also avoid phrases like “Being that…” in formal academic writing. Use “Because” or “Since.”

Subject-verb agreement
Examples:
“These works and studies has looked” → “have looked”
“adolescents who has felt” → “adolescents who have felt”
“the victim no longer feel” → “the victim no longer feels”

Apostrophes and possessives
Examples:
“its” often should be it’s (“it is”)
“Rabkins book” → “Rabkin’s book”

Sentence structure
Some sentences are run-on or awkwardly connected. For example:
> “Thus, relating the social problem of adolescent suicide to theoretical perspectives...”
This is a fragment, not a complete sentence.

Try shorter, clearer sentences, especially when defining theory.

Content suggestions

Distinguish more carefully between psychological causes and sociological explanations. Right now, the paper sometimes shifts suddenly from theory to depression without explaining how they connect.
Your Marx/conflict section needs more precision. The connection between class conflict/dialectics and adolescent suicide is currently less convincing than the Durkheim section. Explain more specifically what structural inequalities, family stress, or social pressures conflict theory reveals.
The conclusion is too brief. Instead of ending with a vague statement, restate your thesis and show what the theoretical analysis reveals about prevention or public understanding.

Final advice for revision

When revising, focus in this order:
Rewrite the thesis so the paper has one clear argument.
Reorganize body paragraphs around that argument.
Clarify how each theory explains adolescent suicide.
Edit sentences carefully for grammar, spelling, and punctuation.
Strengthen the conclusion so it reflects your argument clearly.

You have a solid idea at the center of this essay. With a clearer thesis, stronger organization, and more polished language, this could become a thoughtful sociological analysis rather than just a general discussion of a serious issue.

Your essay addresses an important issue and attempts an ambitious task: connecting water pollution to sociological theory. You also use concrete examples and statistics to show why the topic matters. To revise successfully, focus on clarifying your argument, organizing ideas more logically, and improving sentence-level correctness.

Use of examples and evidence. References to contaminated water, beach closures, and illness help establish significance.
Effort to apply theory. You are not just describing pollution; you are trying to interpret it through Rational Choice Theory and Exchange Theory.

Main issues to revise

A. Clarify your thesis
Right now, the paper says it focuses on “water pollution and its relationship to the Exchange Theory and the Rational Choice Theory,” but that is a topic statement, not yet a strong argumentative thesis.

You need a thesis that makes a clear claim, for example:
Water pollution persists because individuals and companies often act according to short-term self-interest; however, Rational Choice Theory and Exchange Theory suggest that stronger education, incentives, and regulation can change this behavior.

This gives the paper a direction: not just “there is a relationship,” but what that relationship is and why it matters.

B. Strengthen organization
The essay currently moves between background on pollution, theory, law, companies, and health effects somewhat unpredictably. A clearer structure would help.

Consider this order:
Introduction + thesis
Background on water pollution  
   - types of pollutants  
   - point vs. non-point pollution
Rational Choice Theory  
   - define it clearly  
   - explain how it applies to individuals/companies  
   - use evidence
Exchange Theory  
   - define it clearly  
   - explain rewards/costs in pollution behavior  
   - use evidence
Conclusion  
   - bring the theories together  
   - state what should be done

Each body paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that directly supports your thesis.

C. Explain the theories more accurately and directly
Your discussion of both theories is promising, but sometimes the explanations are vague or repetitive.

For example:
When discussing Rational Choice Theory, emphasize that people make decisions by weighing costs and benefits according to what they know and value.
When discussing Exchange Theory, emphasize rewards, costs, reinforcement, and patterns of behavior.

Then apply each theory specifically:
Why might a company pollute? Lower disposal costs, convenience, weak enforcement.
What would change the behavior? Fines, public pressure, cleanup costs, education, incentives for proper disposal.

This will make the paper more analytical and less descriptive.

D. Integrate evidence better
You include several statistics, but often they appear as a list rather than as part of an argument. After each statistic, explain:
What does this prove?
How does it connect to Rational Choice or Exchange Theory?

For instance, if beaches are closed due to pollution, explain how that shows the social cost of decisions made for short-term benefit.

E. Address missing or unclear source material
Your draft contains several markers such as “ <R>” and “.” These make parts of the essay incomplete. Before submitting a revision, replace them with:
the missing quotation or source material, or
your own complete sentence if no quote is needed.

Without that, parts of the essay seem unfinished.

A. Word choice and precision
Some phrases are awkward or imprecise. Examples:
“Humans only get less than 3% of the planet’s fresh water supply” → awkward/inaccurate phrasing.
“water supply” should often be water supplies or freshwater resources, depending on meaning.
“war pollution threat” appears to be a typo for water pollution threat.

Aim for direct, precise academic language.

B. Grammar and mechanics
There are frequent errors with:
apostrophes:  
  - “its’ relationship” → its relationship  
  - “ones’ behavior” → one’s behavior
word form/agreement:

repetition/typos:

  - “this-water”
punctuation: comma splices and unnecessary commas appear throughout.

Revision priorities
