Feedback Report

Your essay takes a clear position in favor of marijuana legalization and raises several substantial arguments: medical use, prison overcrowding, industrial production, crime reduction, and tax revenue. You also attempt to apply sociological theories, which gives the paper ambition beyond a simple opinion essay. For revision, focus on clarifying your thesis, improving organization, strengthening evidence, and adopting a more academic style.

Clarify and narrow the thesis
Right now, the paper argues many things at once. The introduction promises discussion of medicine, fabric, personal use, prisons, violent crime, judges, and revenue, plus three different theories. That is too much for one essay, and the result is that the argument feels scattered.

Revise by choosing a sharper thesis, for example:
*Marijuana should be legalized because criminalization creates greater social harm than regulated legal use, especially in medicine, criminal justice, and public revenue.*

This would let you organize the body around three main claims instead of many loosely connected points.

Improve organization
The paper needs a stronger argumentative structure. At present, some paragraphs provide historical background, some argue policy, and some suddenly switch to sociological theory. Often the reader cannot see how one paragraph leads to the next.

A better structure would be:

Introduction
   - brief background
   - clear thesis

Medical legalization
   - evidence of medical benefits
   - why current classification is inconsistent
   - counterargument and response

Criminal justice effects
   - prison overcrowding
   - unequal enforcement
   - misdirected law-enforcement resources

Economic and industrial benefits
   - tax revenue
   - hemp/fabric/paper uses
   - reduced black market

Application of theory
   - either weave theory into each section clearly, or create one short section explaining how one or two theories illuminate the issue

Conclusion
   - restate thesis
   - emphasize strongest reasons

Use sociological theory more accurately
You repeatedly mention Exchange Theory, Conflict Theory, and Structural Functional Theory, but often the references feel added on rather than fully integrated. For example, sentences like “the rewards outweigh the costs” invoke exchange theory, but the analysis remains general. Likewise, conflict theory is sometimes reduced to “there will be tension.”

To improve this:
Define each theory briefly
Apply it directly to the issue
Use fewer theories but more depth

For instance, Conflict Theory is especially relevant to unequal enforcement by class and power. Structural Functionalism could be used to discuss how drug laws affect institutions such as medicine and criminal justice. You probably do not need all three theories.

Strengthen evidence and credibility
Your essay includes many claims, but some need better support or more cautious wording. A few examples:
“Not a single death has ever been credibly attributed…” — very strong claim; if you keep it, it needs excellent evidence.
“There are many thefts and breakins because people are trying to obtain pot” — this needs evidence and may oversimplify causes of crime.
“The government could make sure no other drugs were mixed in with the pot” — plausible, but explain how regulation would accomplish this.

Also, some of your information appears dated (1991, 1992, 1993). If this is a revision for a current class, update your sources. If the assignment uses older sources intentionally, you still need to explain their relevance.

Address counterarguments fairly
The essay mostly dismisses the opposing side. A stronger academic argument acknowledges serious concerns, such as:
impaired driving
adolescent use
dependency
public health regulation

Then respond to them by arguing that regulation is more effective than prohibition. This will make your paper more persuasive and balanced.

Adopt a more formal academic tone
The essay often uses informal language: “pot,” “getting high,” “crazed on pot,” etc Some of this may come from source material, but in your own voice you should prefer “marijuana” or “cannabis.”

Also avoid rhetorical questions such as:
“Would it not make sense to reduce crime by simply legalizing marijuana?”

Instead, make a direct claim:
*Legalization could reduce some forms of crime by weakening the black market and redirecting law-enforcement resources.*

Revise for sentence-level correctness
There are many mechanical problems that reduce readability and authority:
spelling: *cannavis* → *cannabis*; *heroine* → *heroin*
punctuation: missing periods and awkward hyphenation
capitalization inconsistencies
grammar: “Overcrowded prisons is” → “Overcrowded prisons are”
word choice: “good, intelligent judges” is vague; use “highly qualified judges”

Some sentences are also too long and overloaded. Break them into shorter, clearer units.

Introduction and conclusion
The opening list of slang terms grabs attention, but it feels more journalistic than academic. Consider beginning with the legal and social debate instead. Your conclusion does restate your position, but it should do more than repeat claims. End by emphasizing your strongest three reasons and why legalization better serves society than criminalization.

Revision Priorities

Reorganize around 3 main arguments
Use 1–2 sociological theories in depth

Use a more formal academic tone
Edit carefully for grammar, spelling, and punctuation

You have the basis of a strong argumentative essay, but it will improve significantly if you make it more focused, more analytical, and more polished.

Feedback Report

Your essay shows clear effort, strong concern for the topic, and familiarity with sociological approaches. You have an ambitious goal: to explain homelessness historically and then analyze it through conflict theory and symbolic interactionism. That is a solid foundation. For revision, the main priorities are focus, organization, evidence, and style.

Clarify your main argument
Right now, the essay often sounds informative rather than argumentative. You discuss history, causes, and two theories, but your central claim is not sharply defined.

Try to make your thesis more specific. For example:

*Homelessness in the United States is best understood as a structural problem caused by unequal access to housing, work, and power; while symbolic interactionism explains harmful stereotypes, conflict theory provides the stronger explanation for why homelessness persists.*

A thesis like this gives the paper a clear direction and helps you decide what to include.

Improve organization and reduce repetition
The essay is very long and sometimes repeats similar ideas. For example, several paragraphs restate that the powerful exploit the poor or that society labels homeless people negatively. Instead of repeating, group ideas more efficiently.

A stronger structure might be:

Introduction: define the issue and present thesis  
Brief background/history: only the most relevant moments  
Causes of homelessness: housing costs, deinstitutionalization, labor market changes, discrimination  
Conflict theory analysis  
Symbolic interactionist analysis  
Comparison of theories: which is more useful and why  
Conclusion

The historical section currently takes up too much space and sometimes feels disconnected from the later theory sections. Condense it so it supports your argument rather than becoming a separate paper.

Use evidence carefully and consistently
You make many strong claims, but several need support. Examples include:

“Real estate agencies have also conspired against the poor”
“The government… have tagged the homeless as lazy”
“Drug use has increased dramatically in the homeless community”

Statements like these require reliable evidence and precise wording. Avoid broad claims unless you can cite research. Also, you include an incomplete quotation: *“Homelessness: A Sourcebook... states that .”* That must either be completed and cited properly or removed.

In revision:

Add credible sources for major factual claims.
Use paraphrase and quotation accurately.
Distinguish between evidence and opinion.

Strengthen sociological analysis
Your essay improves when it explains theory, but sometimes the discussion becomes too general or oversimplified.

For example, conflict theory is presented almost as “powerful people are greedy,” which is too blunt for academic writing. Instead, frame it in terms of structural inequality, competition for scarce resources, and institutional power.

Similarly, symbolic interactionism should focus less on “people blindly follow” and more on how meanings, labels, and daily interactions shape social identity and public responses to homelessness.

The strongest part of your paper could be the comparison between the two theories. Push this further:
What does conflict theory explain well?
What does symbolic interactionism explain well?
Which theory better addresses causes, and which better addresses public attitudes?

Revise tone for academic credibility
Your passion is clear, which is a strength, but some phrasing is too emotional or absolute for an academic argument. For example:

“immensely greedy”
“mindlessly follow”
“atrocious things”
“constant thorn in the sides of all Americans”

These weaken your authority because they sound more rhetorical than analytical. Aim for precise, measured language.

For example:
Instead of “mindlessly follow,” say *“often adopt dominant social attitudes.”*
Instead of “immensely greedy,” say *“benefit from unequal distributions of power and resources.”*

Edit for grammar, wording, and sentence clarity
There are frequent sentence-level problems that make the essay harder to follow. A few patterns to watch:

Subject-verb agreement: “People living without shelter has been a problem” → “have been”
Word choice: “lobs” should be “jobs”; “mayor” should be “major”; “Contact theorists” should be “Conflict theorists”
Awkward phrasing: “A percentage of the population of the citizens of America” could simply be “Some Americans”
Run-on or unclear sentences: “Numerous factories developed, in these factories…” should be split into two sentences
Possessives: “sociologists choice” → “sociologist’s choice”
Effect/affect: “effects what they view” → “affects what they view”

Read aloud during revision; this will help you catch unclear wording and overly long sentences.

Make the conclusion more analytical
Your conclusion now mainly restates that homelessness is a serious problem. Instead, use it to answer the “so what?” question. Reaffirm which theory is more useful and why. You might also briefly suggest what this means for policy or public understanding.

Final revision priorities
Write a sharper thesis  
Cut and reorganize the history section  
Support claims with evidence  
Make the theory comparison more precise  
Adopt a more academic tone  
Proofread carefully for grammar and clarity

You have a strong topic and a clear investment in it. With a more focused argument and tighter organization, this essay could become much more persuasive and intellectually rigorous.

Feedback Report

Your essay has clear ambition: it tackles a complex policy issue, uses historical background, and compares two theoretical frameworks—Spencer and Merton—to organize the debate. That comparative structure is your strongest feature. You also end with a nuanced position rather than a simplistic “for or against” argument, which gives the paper intellectual maturity.

Clarify and strengthen your thesis
At present, the essay asks many questions early on, but your own main claim does not become fully clear until near the end. Readers need a precise thesis in the introduction that states your position and the reasons for it.

For example, your thesis could say that welfare should be reformed rather than abolished because long-term dependency is real, but structural barriers such as poor education, lack of childcare, and limited job opportunities make abolition both unrealistic and unjust.

This would help unify the paper. Right now, parts of the essay sound as if they support abolition, while others support reform; the final stance is more balanced, but it needs to guide the essay from the beginning.

Improve organization and paragraph control
The overall structure is logical—history, conservative/Spencer view, liberal/Merton view, then your conclusion—but several paragraphs are too long and contain multiple ideas. Revise so that each paragraph has:
a clear topic sentence,
evidence,
explanation linking the evidence to your argument.

In particular:
The historical section could be shorter and more focused on how welfare changed over time.
The Spencer section should explain not only what he believed, but why his framework is inadequate for modern policy.
The Merton section is stronger, but it also needs sharper transitions and less piling up of statistics.

You should also make sure each section clearly contributes to your own claim, not just summarizes other viewpoints.

Use evidence more critically
You include many statistics and case studies, which is good, but often they are presented without enough analysis. After a statistic, explain:
what it shows,
how reliable or limited it may be,
why it matters for your argument.

For example, when you describe individual welfare recipients, the paper sometimes generalizes from a few extreme cases. Be careful here. Anecdotes can illustrate a problem, but they do not prove that most recipients are “lazy” or representative of the whole population. In argumentative writing, broad claims need broad evidence.

Similarly, some of your phrasing suggests bias: “claim to be underprivileged,” “laziness,” “illegitimate kids,” and “not fit to survive.” Even when you are presenting conservative or Spencerian views, signal clearly that these are positions you are reporting, not necessarily endorsing. More neutral language will make your argument more persuasive and academically credible.

Distinguish summary from argument
A major revision goal is to move from reporting sources to analyzing them. Much of the essay summarizes Spencer, Merton, policy history, and examples from welfare programs. What is sometimes missing is your evaluation.

Ask yourself after each body paragraph:
What does this prove?
How does it support my thesis?
What is my interpretation?

For instance, after discussing Spencer, you might argue that his theory helps explain conservative frustration with dependency, but it fails because it ignores children, unequal opportunity, and the social costs of abandonment. That would keep your own voice at the center.

Address counterarguments more directly
You do some balancing already, which is good. To strengthen the essay, explicitly acknowledge the most serious conservative concern: that welfare can create dependency and reduce incentives to work. Then explain why abolition is still the wrong response. This would make your final position seem more reasoned and less reactive.

Style and tone
Your style is generally fluent, but the tone sometimes shifts from academic to judgmental or rhetorical. Questions like “what is wrong with society?” are effective for emphasis, but too many rhetorical questions can weaken an academic argument. In revision, replace some questions with clear claims.

Also, avoid emotionally loaded or outdated terms unless directly quoting a source. Prefer:
“children born outside marriage” instead of “illegitimate births”
“people receiving welfare” instead of “these people”
“work disincentives” instead of “laziness” unless carefully supported

Sentence-level editing
There are several proofreading issues:
typo: “Me United States” → “the United States”
typo: “deferent” → “different”
typo: “exploded in to” → “exploded into”
typo: “he no useful place” → “has no useful place”
punctuation errors such as “Thus, to Spencer. if…” and “Consider, for example. the father…”
some double punctuation, eg “<R>..”

There are also visible placeholders such as  and `<R>`, which must be removed or replaced. If these are citations or quotations, format them properly.

Final revision priorities
Write a sharper thesis in the introduction.
Reduce summary and increase your own analysis.
Use more neutral, precise academic language.
Treat anecdotes cautiously and avoid overgeneralizing.
Revise paragraph structure for unity and flow.
Proofread carefully and fix missing/placeholder material.

Overall, this is a thoughtful draft with strong potential. Your main task now is to make the essay sound less like a compilation of positions and more like a clear, well-supported argument in your own voice.

Feedback Report

Your essay shows strong effort and clear engagement with sociological theory. You bring in multiple perspectives—symbolic interactionism, functionalism, conflict theory, and exchange theory—and you consistently connect them to divorce as a social issue. The paper is informed, ambitious, and organized around recognizable conceptual sections. For revision, the main priorities are: (1) sharpening your argument, (2) improving organization and paragraph unity, (3) tightening style and grammar, and (4) handling evidence and claims more carefully.

Clarify your central argument
Right now, the essay often reads more like an informative survey of theories than an argumentative essay. You explain several causes of divorce, but your main claim is not precise enough.

For example, your conclusion suggests that divorce has increased because of “changes in the family,” but that idea is broad. Try revising your thesis so it makes a more debatable claim, such as:

*Although divorce has many individual causes, its rise is best understood as a social consequence of changing expectations of marriage, shifting gender roles, and the weakening of traditional family functions.*
  
This would give the paper a stronger argumentative focus and help you decide what to include.

Improve overall structure
The essay is very long and sometimes repetitive. Several sections explain similar points more than once, especially regarding changing expectations, gender roles, and family change. Consider restructuring the essay into clearer stages:

Introduction  
   - Introduce divorce as a social issue  
   - Present your thesis clearly

Why divorce rates rose: main causes  
   - Symbolic interactionism: expectations, meanings of love, changing stigma  
   - Functionalism: weakening of traditional family functions  
   - Conflict theory: gender inequality and power struggles  
   - Exchange theory: costs, rewards, and barriers to divorce

Effects of divorce  
   - Especially on children and post-divorce conflict

What might reduce divorce  
   - This section should follow directly from your argument, not feel like a separate paper

Conclusion  
   - Restate the argument, not just summarize everything

Also, make sure each paragraph has one clear main idea. Some paragraphs currently combine several ideas.

Strengthen transitions and paragraph unity
You often move abruptly from one point to another. Add topic sentences that connect each paragraph to your thesis. For example:

Instead of “Another view from which to look is the functionalist view,” try  
  *A functionalist perspective suggests that divorce rises when social changes weaken the family’s traditional roles and interdependence.*

This gives the paragraph a stronger argumentative purpose.

Be more precise and cautious with evidence
You use statistics and research references, but many claims are presented very strongly without explanation or citation context. Since the draft includes placeholder citations (“<R>”), make sure the final version properly documents all evidence.

Also, avoid overgeneralized or potentially problematic statements. For example:
“Marriage is a way men maintain their control…” is too absolute unless carefully framed as a conflict theorist interpretation.
“Other causes include… homosexuality” is especially problematic as written. If you keep this point, you would need strong evidence and much more careful phrasing; otherwise, it is better to remove it.

Similarly, the claim that “two-thirds of all first marriages will end in divorce” may be outdated or disputed, so verify your sources.

Revise style for concision and clarity
Many sentences are wordy or awkward. Aim for more direct prose. Examples:

“This leads us to say that widespread personal troubles indicate a social problem.”  
  → *Widespread personal troubles suggest a broader social problem.*

“All of these changes in people’s symbols have put excessive pressure on husband and wives.”  
  → *These changing meanings and expectations have placed greater pressure on husbands and wives.*

You should also avoid repeating sentence openings like “Another…” and “We have seen…”

Edit grammar, word choice, and proofreading errors
There are many sentence-level errors that distract from your ideas. A few examples:

“for the time” → for the moment / for now
“coning” → coming
“take for granted of what to expect” → take for granted what to expect
“then own job” → their own job
“it was once” → capitalize It
“other pasts of the social system” → other parts
“a fob” → a job
“must decided” → must decide
“moves forward divorce” → moves toward divorce
“Children many times” → Children often
“Society wilt always” → will always
“With me changing sex roles” → With the changing sex roles
“easier then working” → easier than working
“Piety needs to make…” seems to be the wrong word; perhaps Society or Policy
“it has involved through history” → it has evolved through history
“may possible lower” → may possibly lower
“there will stronger marriages” → there will be stronger marriages

You also sometimes shift awkwardly between he or she / they. Choose a consistent, natural approach; in most cases, singular they is acceptable in modern academic writing.

Final revision advice
As you revise, focus on these questions:
What is my exact argument about divorce?
Which three or four causes are most important?
Which examples or theories truly support that argument?
What can I cut because it repeats earlier points?
Are all claims accurate, current, and ethically framed?

Strengths to keep
Strong knowledge of sociological perspectives
Clear concern with divorce as a social issue, not just a private problem
Effective attempt to connect theory to real-life consequences
Ambitious scope and serious engagement with the topic

With a clearer thesis, tighter organization, and careful editing, this could become a much stronger argumentative essay.

“financial benefit”
“defeated decisively”

Also, avoid rhetorical questions too often. They can be effective once or twice, but here they sometimes replace analysis. Instead of asking, “what can a coach do?”, explain directly what the coach’s options are and why they are limited.

Strengthen evidence and reasoning
You rely heavily on the Florida/Steve Spurrier example. It’s a vivid example, but one case is not enough to prove a broad argument. If possible, add:
another example from another coach or team,
evidence about polls or rankings rewarding blowouts,
evidence about bowl revenue or Heisman voting.

Also, sometimes your reasoning jumps too quickly. For instance, when you say “No one knows,” that weakens your argument. As a writer, you should present a reasoned explanation, not step away from the issue.

Address the counterargument fairly
Your essay would be stronger if you admitted that critics have a legitimate concern: humiliating weaker opponents can look unsportsmanlike. Then show why that appearance does not always reveal the coach’s actual intention. This makes your argument more balanced and persuasive.

Fix sentence-level issues
There are many run-on sentences, comma splices, and punctuation errors. A few examples:

“It’s fourth down and just two yards to go with a forty-nine to seven lead, will they go for it?”  
  → This should be separated into two sentences or joined correctly.

“Yes they are going for it.”  
  → Add a comma: “Yes, they are going for it.”

“Florida was clearly the better team, so why did the NCAA, who discourages running the score up, give the better ranking to a team that did just that.”  
  → This should end with a question mark, and “that” may be clearer than “who” for the NCAA.

Watch for:
its/it’s (“it’s ranking” should be its ranking)
to/too (“do it to” should be do it too)
word choice (“unsurmountable” should be insurmountable)
extra punctuation (“Bowden,’”)
spacing before commas (“do , but pass”)

Final revision priorities
When you revise, focus on these in order:
Write a sharper thesis.
Reorganize paragraphs by reason.
Add at least one counterargument.
Replace slang with more formal language.
Edit for sentence boundaries, punctuation, and grammar.

You have a strong topic and a clear opinion. With better structure and a more polished academic style, this essay could become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a strong personal stake in the issue. You do several things well: you acknowledge opposing views, you distinguish between “heritage” and “hatred,” and you try to define key terms before arguing from them. The paper also has a clear overall claim: the Confederate battle flag should not fly over the South Carolina State Capitol. That focus gives your essay direction.

That said, your draft currently has a major thesis problem and several logic/evidence issues that weaken the argument.

Clarify your thesis and keep it consistent
At the start, your argument is:

the battle flag should come down.

But later, your argument becomes:

the battle flag should be replaced by the “real Confederate flag.”

This shift creates a contradiction. If your goal is to argue that the battle flag is too closely tied to racism and resistance to civil rights, replacing it with another Confederate symbol may not persuade readers who see the Confederacy itself as inseparable from slavery.

Ask yourself: What is your actual claim?
Possible options:
The battle flag should be removed and not replaced by any Confederate flag.
The battle flag should be removed and replaced by a different historical symbol.
The state should commemorate Southern heritage in a museum or monument, not on the Capitol.

Right now, the essay tries to separate the battle flag from the Confederacy more broadly, but many readers will not accept that distinction unless you provide strong historical evidence.

Strengthen the historical accuracy and evidence
Several claims need better support and, in some cases, correction.

For example:
“This was the year that the civil rights movement started” is inaccurate. The civil rights movement began well before 1964.
“the flag was set up the day after the civil rights movement started” is also inaccurate and too vague.
“In my mind that stands for the South fighting to keep slavery” signals opinion, but your essay needs historical evidence, not just personal interpretation.

To revise, use specific, credible sources:
Why was the battle flag raised over the State House in 1961/1962 (not 1964)?
What did lawmakers or public officials say at the time?
What do historians say about the symbolism of the battle flag versus other Confederate flags?

Your argument will be much stronger if you replace broad assertions with documented evidence.

Reconsider unsupported generalizations
Some statements are too sweeping and may alienate readers:
“The battle flag stands for nothing but racism and hatred.”
“true southerners”
“Many people not from the South do not understand the southern way of life”

These claims oversimplify a complex issue. In argumentative writing, absolute language often weakens credibility unless you can fully prove it. Try more precise phrasing:
“For many people, especially given its historical use during segregation, the battle flag has come to symbolize racism and resistance to civil rights.”
Avoid terms like “true southerners,” which suggest that some people are more authentic than others.

Develop the reasoning more fully
Your essay often makes a claim and then moves on before fully explaining it. For instance, you say the “real Confederate flag” stands for “a way of life,” but you do not define what values you mean. Since the Confederacy was historically tied to slavery, readers will question this phrase immediately.

If you want to argue for “heritage,” you need to specify what that heritage includes:
family ancestry?
regional identity?
military history?
cultural traditions such as music, food, religion, or local memory?

Without that explanation, “way of life” sounds vague and potentially evasive.

Improve organization
Your draft would benefit from a clearer structure:

Introduction: present the controversy and your thesis.
Body paragraph 1: explain why some people see the flag as heritage.
Body paragraph 2: explain why the battle flag is especially controversial.
Body paragraph 3: provide historical evidence about why it was raised at the Capitol.
Body paragraph 4: address what South Carolina should do instead.
Conclusion: restate your position and explain why it matters.

Right now, the essay repeats some points rather than building step by step.

Style and sentence-level revision
There are also some sentence issues:
Watch repetition of phrases like “way of life,” “real flag,” and “battle flag.”
Avoid informal phrasing such as “for all to see.”
Use more precise wording: “seceded” instead of “succeeded,” “Civil War” instead of “civil war,” “Capitol” if referring to the building.
You also have a paragraph break in the middle of a sentence (“certain way / of life”), which should be fixed.

Revision priorities
Decide on a single, consistent thesis.
Check and correct the historical facts.
Replace generalizations with specific evidence and careful language.
Explain exactly what you mean by heritage and way of life.
Reorganize the essay so each paragraph advances the argument.

You have the basis of a compelling argument here, especially because you write from personal connection rather than distance. The next step is to make the essay more historically grounded, logically consistent, and precise.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a recognizable argumentative structure: you state a claim, organize the body by categories of harm, and end with a firm conclusion. You also attempt to use outside sources, which is an important feature of academic argument. For revision, though, the essay needs major work in argument quality, tone, evidence, and sentence-level correctness.

Clarify and narrow your thesis
Your thesis is currently very broad and absolute: premarital sex is “overall destructive” because it causes disrespect for women, “illegitimate communities,” and STDs. This creates two problems:

It overclaims, making the essay easier to challenge.
It combines moral, social, medical, and religious claims without explaining how they connect.

Try revising to a more precise, arguable thesis. For example:  
“Although the sexual revolution increased personal freedom, it also contributed to changes in sexual behavior that some critics link to higher rates of STDs, nonmarital pregnancy, and the objectification of women.”  
This version is still argumentative, but it is more academically defensible.

Improve academic tone and avoid inflammatory language
A major weakness is the essay’s tone. Phrases such as “headed down the toilet,” “immoral slaughter of babies,” and “piece of ass” create a highly emotional, judgmental voice. In academic writing, strong opinions are more persuasive when expressed in measured language.

Also, terms such as “illegitimate family” or “illegitimate communities” are outdated and stigmatizing. Use neutral wording such as “single-parent households,” “nonmarital births,” or “children born outside marriage.”

Similarly, your conclusion shifts from social argument to explicitly religious prescription (“Christian values,” “God given”). That may reflect your beliefs, but in an academic essay you need to consider audience. Unless the assignment specifically invites a faith-based argument, ground your claims in evidence rather than doctrine.

Strengthen logic and avoid unsupported generalizations
Many of your claims assume causation without proving it. For instance:

Premarital sex leads to loss of respect for women.
Nontraditional families produce crime and weak education.
Sexual freedom causes moral decline.

These are large social claims, and they require strong, specific evidence. Right now, the essay often moves from a statistic to a broad conclusion without enough explanation. For example, statistics about number of partners do not automatically prove “long-term monogamous relationships are quite rare,” and preference for single parenthood does not prove family dysfunction.

When revising, ask:
What exactly does this evidence prove?
Does it show correlation or causation?
Are there other possible explanations?

A stronger essay would also acknowledge counterarguments: for example, some would argue that sex education, contraception, and changing gender roles have improved people’s lives. Addressing opposing views will make your argument more credible.

Use sources more carefully
You quote several sources, but the essay often drops quotations into paragraphs without integrating or analyzing them. A quotation should be followed by explanation of how it supports your point.

For example, after quoting Gardner on STDs, you should explain:
What trend Gardner identifies
Why that matters
How it connects specifically to your thesis

You should also check the reliability and age of your sources. The essay appears to rely heavily on older material and statistics. If you revise this for a current academic context, use recent peer-reviewed research or credible public health data.

Organize paragraphs more clearly
Your body paragraphs have identifiable topics, but they are often too long and internally scattered. A good revision strategy would be:

Paragraph 1: Sexual revolution and changing norms
Paragraph 2: Public health concerns
Paragraph 3: Pregnancy and family structure
Paragraph 4: Gender relations and objectification

Within each paragraph, use a clear topic sentence, present evidence, analyze it, and then link back to the thesis. Right now, some paragraphs jump between ideas too quickly.

Correct grammar, word choice, and proofreading errors
There are many sentence-level problems that distract from your argument. Some recurring issues:

Wrong word forms: “affects”/“effects,” “there”/“their,” “lost”/“loss,” “planed”/“planned,” “bare”/“bear”
Awkward phrasing: “cut down on have sex before marriage”
Sentence fragments: “The major change, of course, coming thirty-five years ago…”
Run-ons and comma splices
Spelling errors: “brazier” for “brassiere,” “din-finishing,” “tern” for “term”
Punctuation problems throughout

Example corrections:
“One the most potent solvents” → “One of the most potent solvents”
“have long ranging affects” → “have long-ranging effects”
“it leads to the lost of respect” → “it leads to the loss of respect”

Careful proofreading is essential. Reading the draft aloud can help you catch many of these errors.

Final revision priorities
If you revise this essay, focus on these steps in order:

Rewrite the thesis to make it more precise and less absolute.
Adopt a more neutral academic tone.
Cut unsupported claims or replace them with stronger evidence.
Reorganize paragraphs around one clear point each.
Edit carefully for grammar, spelling, and citation integration.

You have the basis of a structured argument, but to succeed in an academic setting, the essay needs to become more precise, evidence-based, and controlled in tone.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that women should have equal intellectual opportunities in the military but should not serve in combat because of physical and emotional differences. The paper shows conviction and a willingness to engage a controversial issue. For revision, the main priorities are strengthening the argument with evidence, improving organization, and revising language that relies on broad generalizations.

Clarify and sharpen your thesis
Right now, your thesis is somewhat spread across the first paragraph. Try to make it more precise and arguable in one sentence.

For example:
> Although women should have equal access to many military roles, combat positions should be determined by uniform physical and psychological standards because lowering those standards could harm military effectiveness.

This version is clearer because it identifies the issue, your claim, and your reasoning.

Replace repetition with focused development
The essay repeats the same core idea several times: men and women are different, and ignoring those differences is harmful. Instead of restating that point, develop two or three specific reasons in separate body paragraphs.

A stronger structure might be:

Introduction + thesis
Body paragraph 1: Combat requires specific physical standards; explain why uniform standards matter.
Body paragraph 2: If standards are lowered, military readiness may suffer; give evidence or examples.
Body paragraph 3: Address counterarguments—eg, some women do meet high standards, and the question should be about standards, not assumptions.
Conclusion: Restate your claim and explain why it matters.

This would make the essay more persuasive and less circular.

Use evidence, not assumptions
Your biggest weakness is that the argument depends mostly on opinion and general statements such as “the standards… are sometimes lowered” or women’s “emotional makeup” is different. These claims need support. In an argumentative essay, readers will expect:

facts
statistics
military policy examples
research on physical requirements
credible expert opinion

Without evidence, the essay risks sounding subjective rather than reasoned.

Also, some claims are too absolute:
“A woman's body… cannot function in every capacity as a man's does”
“The emotional makeup of a women is quite different from a man's”

These may reflect your view, but as written they are broad generalizations. If you want to make such claims academically, you need careful wording and evidence. For instance, you could discuss average physical differences relevant to combat tasks rather than making universal claims about all women.

Address counterarguments fairly
A strong argumentative essay acknowledges the other side. Right now, the paper assumes that admitting women to combat necessarily means standards are lowered. But an opposing reader might say:
some women can meet the same standards as men
combat eligibility should be based on ability, not sex
military roles vary, and not all combat jobs require the same physical tasks

If you address these points directly, your essay will seem more thoughtful and credible. You might concede that if identical standards are maintained and individuals meet them, the issue becomes more complex. Even if you still disagree, showing that complexity strengthens your ethos.

Revise tone and word choice
Some wording may alienate readers because it sounds dismissive or biased rather than analytical. Examples:
“the weaker sex”
“feminists that support this equality”
“distorts reality”

These phrases make the essay sound more emotional and less academic. Aim for neutral, precise language. For example:
“average physical differences between men and women”
“advocates of gender equality”
“may overlook relevant biological differences”

A more measured tone will make your argument stronger.

Improve paragraph-level coherence
Some sentences are hard to follow because they are long and repetitive. One sentence in the second paragraph appears accidentally duplicated. Clean this up carefully.

For example, this section:
> “For example, when a female enters the military…”

goes on too long and repeats itself. Break long sentences into shorter ones, and make sure each paragraph has:
a topic sentence
explanation
evidence/example
concluding link to your thesis

Edit grammar and mechanics
There are several sentence-level issues to correct:
agreement: “a males” → “a male’s”
capitalization: “and goes into the military” begins awkwardly after a period fragment
word form: “a women” → “a woman”; “women is” → “women are”
punctuation: comma splices and missing commas
parallelism: “physical and emotional makeup and man's…” should be “a woman’s… and a man’s…”

Also watch apostrophes and plural forms:
“woman’s” = singular possessive
“women” = plural
“male’s” = singular possessive

Strengthen the conclusion
Your conclusion currently returns to personal feeling (“I love having feminine characteristics…”). Personal voice is not wrong, but for an academic argument, end by reinforcing your central claim in broader terms. Focus on military effectiveness, standards, and fairness rather than personal preference.

Final advice
For your revision, focus on this question: Is your argument about sex differences, or is it really about maintaining consistent combat standards? If you shift toward the second, your essay will become more precise, more defensible, and more academically persuasive.

Your next draft should aim to be:
more specific
better organized
evidence-based
more neutral in tone

That will make your argument much stronger, even for readers who may disagree with you.

> Although traditional gender roles may offer some people a sense of structure, American culture should reject rigid gender stereotyping because it harms both men and women psychologically, restricts social development, and creates economic vulnerability.

That would better match the body of your essay and your conclusion.

Problems:

You often mention a source, then move on before explaining its significance.

What specific changes should American culture make?

fewer sweeping generalizations
careful phrasing when discussing “American culture”

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear, passionate position: residence-hall rules can be overly restrictive and may prevent students from developing independence. That strong sense of conviction is one of the paper’s biggest strengths. You also do a good job identifying specific policies—visitation, gender-based restrictions, and quiet hours—rather than speaking only in general terms. This gives your argument a concrete foundation.

Clarify and strengthen your thesis
Right now, your main claim is spread across the introduction rather than stated in one precise sentence. Try ending the introduction with a direct thesis that previews your points. For example:

Possible thesis:  
*Although residence-hall policies are intended to protect students, restrictive visitation rules, gender-based enforcement, and inflexible quiet hours often limit students’ independence and should be revised to better balance safety with personal responsibility.*

This would help readers immediately understand your argument and the structure of the essay.

Improve organization
The essay already has body paragraphs centered on different policies, which is good. To make the paper more persuasive, organize each paragraph around a single claim and then support it with evidence and explanation.

A stronger structure might be:

Introduction – context + thesis  
Visitation policies restrict independence  
Roommate agreements could regulate shared space more fairly  
Gender-based rules create inconsistencies or inequities  
Quiet hours should be more flexible, especially on weekends  
Conclusion – restate argument and propose change

Some paragraphs now move between emotional reaction, personal examples, and broad claims too quickly. Use clearer topic sentences and transitions so each paragraph develops one point fully.

Use a more academic tone
Your voice is energetic, but some phrases sound conversational or exaggerated for an argumentative essay. For example:

“mom and dad from downstairs”
“That’s ridiculous!”
“Heaven forbid”
“walking on egg shells”

These expressions show feeling, but they weaken the formal tone. Replace them with more measured language. For example:

Instead of “That’s ridiculous!”, write “This policy seems unnecessarily restrictive.”
Instead of “mom and dad from downstairs”, write “residence-hall staff” or “administrators.”

An academic tone will make your argument sound more credible.

Develop evidence more carefully
Much of the essay relies on personal opinion and hypothetical scenarios. Those can help, but they are not enough on their own in an argumentative essay. You need stronger support:

cite actual university policies accurately
include examples from student handbooks
refer to safety, liability, roommate rights, or student development research
distinguish between what you feel and what you can demonstrate

Also, some claims are too sweeping, such as “nine times out of ten when a male and female are together disturbance to others is the last thing on their minds.” This kind of statement sounds unsupported and may alienate readers. Be careful with generalizations.

Address counterarguments fairly
You do mention the other side—for example, safety and roommate comfort—but these points need fuller, fairer treatment. A strong argumentative essay shows why reasonable people support these rules and then explains why your alternative is better.

For example, you could acknowledge:
universities have legal and safety responsibilities
students share rooms and deserve sleep/privacy
unrestricted access might create security risks

Then respond by arguing for revised, not eliminated, policies. This would make your argument more balanced and convincing.

Be cautious with the gender/sexuality paragraph
This section raises an interesting issue about fairness, but the wording is sometimes imprecise and risks sounding insensitive or overly simplistic. Be especially careful when discussing rape, sexual orientation, and assumptions about who poses risk. Focus on the policy problem rather than making broad claims about groups of people.

For example, your core point seems to be: if rules are based only on opposite-sex visitation, they may be inconsistently enforced and fail to reflect the realities of students’ relationships. That is a strong argument. State it directly and calmly.

Revise sentence-level issues
There are frequent grammar and usage problems that distract from your ideas. Pay attention to:

agreement: “rules and regulations it has” → “rules and regulations they have”
word choice: “parenting rolls” → “parental roles”
possessives/plurals: “guest”/“guests,” “there opinions” → “their opinions”
spelling: “rapist” should often be “rapists”; “atleast” → “at least”
awkward phrasing: “You are supposed to feel at home in the resident halls” could be smoother as “Residence halls are intended to feel like a home environment.”
run-on sentences and comma splices: several sentences need to be divided or punctuated more carefully

Also maintain consistent point of view. The essay shifts between you, one, students, and I. Choose one main perspective—probably students—for a more polished style.

Conclusion
Your conclusion restates the main idea, but it could be stronger if it ended with a specific recommendation. For instance, you might propose:
extended weekend visitation hours
building-wide guest policies regardless of gender
quieter weekday hours but more flexible weekend rules
stronger use of roommate agreements

Final advice
You have a strong topic and a clear opinion. For revision, focus on turning this from a passionate personal response into a more polished academic argument: sharpen the thesis, organize each paragraph around one point, use more formal language, support claims with evidence, and address counterarguments fairly. The ideas are here—you now need clearer structure and a more careful style to make them persuasive.

Feedback Report

You have a clear, debatable thesis and strong investment in your topic. The essay’s main claim—that college football needs a playoff system because polls and bowls cannot reliably determine a true champion—is consistent throughout, and your use of historical seasons gives the paper substance. Your strongest asset is your sense of audience engagement: the opening questions are lively, and your examples show that you know the topic well.

That said, the draft needs revision in focus, organization, evidence use, and sentence-level correctness to become a stronger academic argument.

Strengthen the thesis and control the scope
Your thesis appears early and is arguable, which is good. However, the paper tries to do too much at once:
prove the current system is flawed,
show specific controversies,
argue playoffs would make more money,
preserve bowl games,
propose a 16-team model,
respond to objections.

That is a lot for one essay. Consider sharpening the thesis to something like:

Because polls and bowl tie-ins repeatedly failed to match the best teams against each other, college football should adopt a playoff system that preserves traditional bowls as neutral-site games.

This version is more precise and easier to support.

Improve organization: move from examples to argument
Right now, the essay reads somewhat like a long list of disputed seasons. The examples are useful, but they need a clearer structure. A stronger outline might be:

Introduction + thesis
Problem with polls and bowl tie-ins
Example 1: 1992–93
Example 2: 1993–94
Example 3: 1994
Why a playoff is better
   - determines a true champion
   - increases fan interest/revenue
   - allows dream matchups
Counterargument
   - season too long
   - bowls would lose importance
Your proposal
   - playoff format
   - bowls as neutral sites
Conclusion

Each body paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that explains the point of the paragraph, not just the chronology of events.

Use examples more analytically
Your historical cases are detailed, but often the essay describes controversy instead of analyzing what the controversy proves. After each season, explain the significance directly:
What flaw in the system does this example reveal?
Why could a playoff have solved that exact problem?
What broader conclusion should the reader draw?

For instance, after discussing Alabama, Miami, Florida State, and Texas A&M, you could say that the case shows how poll rankings and conference contracts prevented deserving teams from settling the issue on the field.

Develop the counterargument fairly
You do mention objections—tradition, bowls, season length, money—but these points are brief. A stronger argumentative essay should show that you understand the other side. For example:
Why do some fans value bowls as tradition?
Why might coaches worry about player fatigue?
Could academics be affected by a longer season?

Then answer those concerns with a reasoned rebuttal. This will make your argument more persuasive and balanced.

Work on evidence and citation
You include quotations from Rick Reilly, Tom Osborne, Joe Paterno, Steve Spurrier, and Bobby Bowden, which is helpful. But quotation formatting and citation are inconsistent. For example, the use of 11 instead of quotation marks appears to be an error. Make sure quotations are accurately punctuated and integrated.

Also, introduce and explain sources more clearly. Don’t let a quote stand alone; follow it with commentary explaining how it supports your claim.

Revise for clarity and correctness
There are many grammar, word choice, and proofreading issues that distract from your ideas. Common problems include:

Agreement and tense:  
  “there is always some debated” → “there is always some debate”
Missing words:  
  “couldn't really Florida State ahead of Alabama” → “couldn't really rank Florida State ahead of Alabama”
Wrong word forms:  
  “their was” → “there was”; “unjustly” → “unjust”
Awkward repetition:  
  “going to going to the Cotton Bowl”
Sentence fragments or punctuation issues:  
  “Both teams won their bowl games on.”
Pronoun/reference issues and informal phrasing:  
  “you've got a major dilemma” is conversational; in an academic essay, use more formal language.

You should also watch for:
comma splices,
overly long sentences,
repeated sentence openings (“But,” “Now,” “Again”).

Style and tone
Your voice is energetic, but sometimes too conversational for formal academic writing. Phrases like “absolutely ridiculous,” “whipped up on,” and “dream match-ups” create enthusiasm, but you should use a more measured tone. Strong arguments are often more convincing when they sound controlled and precise.

Final revision priorities
If you revise, focus on these in order:
Clarify the thesis
Reorganize around claims, not just seasons
Add analysis after each example
Strengthen the counterargument
Proofread carefully for grammar and mechanics

Overall
This draft shows strong knowledge of the topic and a real argument worth making. The core idea is solid: championships should be decided on the field, not left to polls and bowl arrangements. With clearer structure, stronger analysis, and careful editing, this could become a compelling argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear purpose: to persuade readers to follow go-cart safety rules. The strongest part of the draft is your personal experience. Because you work at the amusement park, you have credibility, and your examples of injuries, crashes, and worker risk make the topic feel real. You also keep your focus on one main claim throughout the essay, which is a good foundation for revision.

What is working well
Strong firsthand authority: Your job experience gives you ethos and makes readers more likely to trust you.
Clear central argument: The essay consistently argues that rules matter for safety.
Relevant examples: Specific details about seatbelts, brakes, bumping, and pit procedures help support your point.
Concern for multiple audiences: You explain how rules protect riders, children, workers, and the cars themselves.

Main areas to revise

Strengthen the thesis and overall structure
Right now, the introduction is conversational but not very focused. Try ending the first paragraph with a more direct thesis that previews your main reasons.

For example:
“Riders should follow all go-cart safety rules because these rules prevent injuries, protect workers and children, and reduce damage to the cars.”

This would help organize the body paragraphs. You already have logical categories:
seatbelts  
listening to instructions about gas and brake  
avoiding bumping and reckless driving  
following worker directions during accidents  
entering the pit safely and staying seated  

Consider grouping some of these into broader points so the essay feels less like a list and more like an argument.

Use a more formal academic tone
Your voice is energetic and sincere, but some phrases sound too informal for an argumentative essay.

Examples:
“Believe me”
“it doesn’t feel good”
“We hate throwing people off the track”
“you got to remember”

Replace these with more formal wording:
“My experience shows…”
“This can result in serious injury”
“Workers may need to remove riders who ignore the rules”
“Riders should remember…”

Also, avoid addressing the reader too often with “you” unless your instructor allows a direct second-person style. A more academic approach would refer to “riders” or “customers.”

Improve paragraph development
Many paragraphs contain good ideas, but some become repetitive or run on too long. Focus each paragraph on one main point and explain it clearly.

For example, your “no bumping” paragraph has several strong ideas, but it is crowded:
bumping hurts riders
children may be injured
cars may break down
workers should not be blamed
fights can happen
bumpers are not meant for intentional collisions

You could revise this into a more focused paragraph by beginning with a topic sentence and then selecting the strongest 2–3 supporting examples.

Edit sentence-level grammar and word choice
There are frequent grammar errors that distract from your argument. Pay special attention to:

Comma use: You often place commas between a subject and verb or where they are not needed.  
  Example: “I have seen a good bit of people get hurt, because…”  
  Better: “I have seen many people get hurt because…”

Word forms and spelling:  
  - “annoucements” → “announcements”  
  - “their” → “there”  
  - “When ever” → “Whenever”  
  - “taking off” → “taken off”  
  - “at anytime” → “at any time”

Subject-verb agreement:  
  - “The only reason the bumpers are… are…” → “The only reason the bumpers are… is…”  
  - “Having all these rules are…” → “Having all these rules is…”

Awkward phrasing:  
  - “The seatbelts shouldn't be taking off” → “Seatbelts should not be removed”  
  - “workers and I check to see that everybody has their seatbelt on” → “workers check that every rider is wearing a seatbelt”

Clarify and refine claims
A few statements feel exaggerated or imprecise, which can weaken your credibility. For instance, “going twenty-five miles per hour” may sound doubtful in context unless you explain it. Also, phrases like “a good bit of people” are vague. Use more precise wording:
“several riders”
“many incidents”
“serious bruising”
“rear-end collisions in the pit”

Precision will make your argument stronger.

Suggested revision priorities
Rewrite the introduction with a clear thesis.
Reorganize body paragraphs around 3–4 main reasons rather than many separate rules.
Make the tone more formal and less conversational.
Edit for grammar, punctuation, and spelling.
Revise the conclusion so it does more than repeat; it should reinforce why rule-following ensures a safe and enjoyable experience for everyone.

Final thought
You have the most important ingredient already: real experience and a meaningful point to make. With stronger organization, more formal language, and careful editing, this essay could become much more persuasive. Your examples show that this is not just about “following rules” in an abstract way—it is about preventing real injuries and protecting everyone at the park.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that Susan Smith should not receive the death penalty. The paper also shows strong engagement with the case and a sincere moral perspective. To revise effectively, focus on clarifying your argument, improving accuracy and organization, and strengthening your reasoning.

Clarify your main claim
Right now, your thesis is somewhat delayed and vague: “I would like to express my opinion on the justness of the sentence.” Instead, state your argument directly and specifically at the end of the introduction.

For example:
“Although Susan Smith committed a horrific crime, sentencing her to life imprisonment rather than death was the more just decision because of her history of mental illness, the lasting psychological punishment of living with her actions, and the moral problem of state execution.”

This gives your essay a roadmap and helps readers follow your reasoning.

Improve organization
The essay currently moves between biography, legal explanation, emotional description, and religious discussion without clear structure. Try organizing by reasons rather than by a stream of thoughts.

A possible structure:
Introduction: brief background + clear thesis  
Reason 1: her history of trauma and mental illness as mitigating factors  
Reason 2: life imprisonment is a severe punishment  
Reason 3: moral/religious objection to taking a life  
Conclusion: restate why life imprisonment was the better sentence

Also, use topic sentences to begin each body paragraph. For example:
“One reason the jury’s decision was justified is that Smith’s history suggests severe psychological instability.”
“A second reason is that life imprisonment can be a harsher punishment than death.”

Be careful with factual accuracy
There are several factual or word-choice problems that weaken credibility:
It was the jury, not “the Supreme Court,” that decided the sentence.
“prision” should be prison
“recieve” should be receive
“God�s” appears to be an encoding error; fix to God’s
“At the age of fifteen years old” is wordy; use “At age fifteen”
“endowed with logical mental coherence” sounds unnatural. Simpler is better: “was not mentally stable” or “was not thinking rationally.”

When writing an argumentative essay, accuracy matters because readers are more likely to trust your judgment if the details are correct.

Strengthen reasoning and avoid overstatement
Some claims are emotionally powerful but not fully reasoned. For instance:
“Death would have been quick, easy, and more economical.”
“She will hear the two small voices… pleading and crying for help.”
“Her greatest fear should be God’s wrath.”

These statements rely heavily on speculation. A stronger argument would focus on what can be supported:
life imprisonment is permanent and severe
trauma and depression may have influenced her actions
some people oppose the death penalty on moral or religious grounds

Try to distinguish between evidence, inference, and emotion. Emotional language can be effective, but too much of it can make the essay feel less analytical.

Handle religious appeals carefully
Your Christian perspective is clearly important to your argument, and it can be included. However, in an academic argumentative essay, religious reasoning works best when paired with broader ethical reasoning. If you rely only on “God will forgive her” or “she may go to heaven,” readers who do not share your beliefs may not be persuaded.

You might revise by presenting religion as one part of your reasoning:
morally, killing her would not undo the crime
legally, mitigating circumstances matter
spiritually, people can seek repentance and change

Also, if you quote the Bible, include the actual verse rather than “”.

Revise tone for academic credibility
Some phrasing is vivid but overly dramatic:
“cold-hearted murderer”
“scandalous life”
“same sick woman”
“punishment worse than… prison… eternity in hell”

These choices make the essay sound more like a sermon or personal reflection than a formal argument. Aim for a respectful but firm tone. You can still express moral seriousness without sensational language.

Sentence-level revision
You have several long or repetitive sentences. Shorten and combine ideas more effectively.

Example:
Original:
“Her boyfriend dumped her and gave her the reason that there were things in her life that did not suit him and they should end their relationship.”

Revised:
“Her boyfriend ended the relationship, saying that aspects of her life did not fit with his future plans.”

Final suggestion
Your strongest path forward is to turn this from an emotionally driven response into a well-structured argument about justice. Keep your core position, but support it with:
a precise thesis
clear paragraph structure
accurate facts
less repetition
more balanced and evidence-based reasoning

You have a clear viewpoint and strong commitment to the topic. With revision, this essay can become much more persuasive and academically effective.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0003.3.txt =====
Your essay presents a clear position and stays focused on it throughout: you argue that lowering the drinking age to eighteen would reduce crime, promote responsibility, and improve public health. That consistent thesis is a strength. You also organize the paper around three main claims, which gives the essay a recognizable argumentative structure.

For revision, the biggest task is to make the argument more credible, precise, and logically supported.

Strengthen the thesis and avoid overclaiming.  
Right now, your introduction makes a very broad claim: that lowering the drinking age would “easily” solve alcoholism and would improve the nation’s health overall. That is too sweeping, and it sets a standard the paper cannot prove. A stronger thesis would be more cautious and specific. For example:

> Lowering the drinking age from twenty-one to eighteen could reduce some illegal alcohol-related behavior among young adults, encourage more open education about drinking, and possibly lessen the culture of secretive binge drinking.

This kind of thesis is easier to defend.

Improve the logic of your body paragraphs.  
Several claims depend on assumptions rather than evidence. For instance, you argue that if drinking becomes legal at eighteen, young adults will automatically become more responsible. That is possible, but it is not self-evident. A reader may ask: why would legality produce responsibility? You need to explain the mechanism more clearly. Would legalization lead to supervised drinking? Less secrecy? More family involvement? Better campus education? Spell that out.

Similarly, saying crime would decrease because fewer young people would technically break alcohol laws is too narrow. A reader may respond that alcohol-related assaults, drunk driving, or disorderly conduct might not decrease. Distinguish between crimes of illegal possession/purchase and harmful alcohol-related crimes.

Use stronger evidence and integrate sources properly.  
Your paper refers to sources, but many citations are incomplete, outdated-looking, or not academically reliable (for example, email addresses, Netscape, or raw website links). Some sources are also dropped into the essay without explanation. Instead of listing a source in parentheses, introduce it and explain what it proves:

Who conducted the study?
What exactly did it find?
How does it support your point?

For example, rather than “A study taken by the Public Health Service showed that 40% of college students engage in binge drinking regularly,” add context: when was the study done, how large was the sample, and why does it matter?

Also, some factual claims are too broad and need evidence, such as:
“Alcoholism is not a problem in other countries”
“Binge drinking would be cured”
“Once these rights are given back, young adults will act responsibly”

These are especially vulnerable because they sound absolute. Replace them with more measured claims.

Address counterarguments.  
A strong argumentative essay should acknowledge the main objection: many people support the drinking age of twenty-one because of concerns about drunk driving, brain development, and public safety. If you address that view directly, your essay will seem much more thoughtful and persuasive. You might argue that the current law has not eliminated dangerous drinking, or that education and regulation matter more than age alone. But you should show readers you understand the other side.

Revise for paragraph focus and repetition.  
Many paragraphs repeat the same point in slightly different words. For example, the essay returns several times to the idea that alcohol is “glamorous” because it is forbidden. Instead of repeating, develop each paragraph with one clear sub-claim and supporting evidence.

A possible structure:
Paragraph 1: illegal possession/purchase vs. broader alcohol-related crime
Paragraph 2: secrecy, binge drinking, and campus culture
Paragraph 3: public-health/socialization argument, possibly with international comparison
Paragraph 4: counterargument and response

Edit sentence-level issues carefully.  
There are frequent grammar, capitalization, and word-choice problems that weaken your authority. A few examples:
“high school and college environment” → “high school and college environments”
“on most college campuses” → capitalize “On”
“effect” → “affect” in “would affect the United States”
“encourage” → “encouraged” in “would be encouraged”
“amoung” → “among”
“eighteen-year old’s” → “eighteen-year-olds” or “eighteen-year-olds’” depending on meaning

Also avoid phrases like “giving back drinking rights,” which sound emotional rather than analytical. Aim for a more formal tone.

Overall:  
You have a clear argument and a workable structure, but the essay needs more rigorous evidence, more careful reasoning, and a more academic tone. As you revise, focus less on repeating your opinion and more on proving each claim step by step. That will make your essay far more persuasive.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0003.4.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear argumentative purpose: you argue that swimming instruction should be required in school because it can prevent drowning and teach water safety. The paper also shows a strong sense of urgency and a real concern for public safety. To revise effectively, focus on three main areas: organization, development of evidence, and editing for grammar and correctness.

Strengths

Strong topic and clear position: Your thesis is understandable—schools should include swimming in P.E.
Effective sense of audience: The opening tries to make readers imagine a dangerous situation, which helps show why the topic matters.
Practical solutions: You do not just identify a problem; you suggest ways schools could implement swim instruction.

Main Revision Priorities

A. Sharpen the thesis
Right now, your main claim appears in the middle of the essay and is slightly buried. State it more directly at the end of the introduction.

For example:
> Because swimming is a life-saving skill and drowning deaths are preventable, schools should require swimming and water-safety instruction as part of physical education.

This version is clearer and more formal.

B. Improve organization
The essay has good ideas, but the structure can be more controlled. A stronger outline would be:

Introduction
   - Briefly introduce the drowning problem
   - End with a clear thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Why the issue matters
   - Explain drowning risk
   - Show why swimming and water-safety knowledge are important

Body Paragraph 2: Why schools are the right place
   - Schools already require P.E.
   - Universal access means all students can benefit

Body Paragraph 3: How the plan could work
   - Use school pools or local pools
   - Hire certified instructors

Body Paragraph 4: Address objections
   - Cost, access, scheduling, liability
   - Explain why these problems can be managed

Conclusion
   - Reinforce the importance and feasibility of the proposal

C. Use more evidence, not just opinion
At the moment, the essay relies mostly on emotional appeal and common-sense reasoning. That is a good start, but an argumentative essay becomes much stronger with facts, statistics, or expert support.

Consider adding:
drowning statistics
information from Red Cross, CDC, or similar organizations
evidence that swim lessons reduce drowning risk
examples of schools or communities that already offer swim programs

For instance, instead of saying “This is a common story,” you could support that claim with data.

D. Reduce overreliance on the dramatic scenario
Your opening is vivid, but it is also very long and written in second person (“you”). In an academic essay, this can feel too informal and fictional. Consider shortening it and moving more quickly to the real argument.

Also, some details in the scenario are unrealistic or exaggerated for an academic argument. A shorter, fact-based opening would sound more credible.

Style and Tone

Aim for a more formal academic tone.

Examples:
“turn on the News” → “watch the news”
“How often have you heard of dodgeball saving someone's life?” is memorable, but it sounds conversational. You could rephrase it more formally:
  > Unlike many traditional P.E. activities, swimming is a skill with direct life-saving value.

Also, avoid repeating words like “senseless deaths” too often. Vary your phrasing.

Paragraphing and clarity
Some of your sentences are run-ons or joined incorrectly with commas. For example:

> “This is not uncommon, most of your friends do not know how to swim.”

This is a comma splice. It should be revised into two sentences or joined properly.

You also have places where ideas run together without enough spacing or punctuation, such as:
> “...how to handle that situation.The topic...”

Be sure each paragraph develops one main point clearly.

Grammar, mechanics, and spelling
There are many sentence-level errors, and correcting them will significantly improve the essay’s credibility. Watch for:

Capitalization: “your” at the start of a sentence; “one class” after a period
Spelling:
  - panicing → panicking
  - drownding → drowning
  - cericulum → curriculum
  - evryone → everyone
  - instuctor → instructor
  - wuold → would
  - instuctors → instructors
  - thaech → teach
  - employent → employment
  - simlpe → simple
  - remidied → remedied
Spacing/punctuation: extra spaces before periods, missing spaces after periods
Sentence boundaries: avoid comma splices and fused sentences

Final advice for revision
When you revise, focus in this order:

Rewrite the introduction so it is shorter and ends with a strong thesis.
Reorganize body paragraphs around clear reasons.
Add evidence from reliable sources.
Include a counterargument about cost or logistics.
Proofread carefully for spelling, punctuation, and grammar.

Overall
You have the basis of a persuasive essay with a meaningful topic and a practical proposal. The next step is to make it more academic, evidence-based, and polished. With a clearer structure and better editing, this argument could become much more convincing.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0004.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a recognizable argumentative structure: you argue that rigid gender identities are harmful and should be abandoned. The paper also shows ambition by using examples from psychology, feminism, and family life. The main revision work now is to sharpen the thesis, improve logic and evidence, and edit for clarity and style.

Thesis and focus

Your central claim is understandable, but it needs to be more precise. Right now, the paper sometimes shifts between three ideas:

gender stereotypes are outdated,  
masculine/feminine traits can exist in anyone, and  
“distinct gender identities should be abolished.”

Those are related, but not identical. “Abolish gender identities” is a very broad and controversial claim, and in the body you mostly argue against rigid stereotypes, not against identity itself.

Suggestion: revise the thesis so it matches what you actually prove. For example:  
“Society should reject rigid gender stereotypes because they limit both men and women psychologically, socially, and within family life.”

That wording is clearer and easier to defend.

Development of argument

Your essay is organized by examples, which is good, but several paragraphs rely on assertion more than analysis.

Strongest points
The discussion of women being confined to wife/mother roles is relevant.
The paragraph on men facing pressure to be aggressive and successful also supports your argument well.
The single-parent example is practical and easy to understand.

What to improve
In several places, you make a claim and then move on too quickly. For instance, you say abolishing distinct gender identity would prevent psychological harm, but you do not fully explain how or why. Try to unpack the reasoning more carefully.

For each body paragraph, aim for this pattern:
topic sentence with a clear claim,
evidence or example,
explanation of how the evidence proves the claim,
link back to the thesis.

Use of evidence

You refer to sources (Masters, Johnson, Kolodny, Betty Friedan, Warren Farrell), which strengthens the essay. However, the evidence currently feels incomplete for two reasons:

Some quotations are missing or marked with placeholders (, `<R>`).
The sources are mentioned, but not always explained.

For example, instead of just naming Friedan, explain what her argument shows about the pressure placed on women. Likewise, if you cite researchers who separate masculinity and femininity, explain the significance: does this challenge the assumption that traits belong naturally to one sex?

Also be careful not to overstate. Claims such as “there would be no psychological impact” are too absolute. A better phrasing would be:  
“reducing rigid gender expectations could lessen psychological strain.”

Logic and nuance

Some of your wording unintentionally reproduces the stereotypes you are trying to criticize. For example, you describe traits as “strictly male” or “distinct female” characteristics. Since your argument is that these divisions are socially constructed and harmful, that language works against your point.

Instead, use phrasing like:
“traits traditionally coded as masculine”
“qualities often associated with femininity”

This gives your essay more nuance and academic credibility.

You should also avoid absolute claims such as “useless,” “obsolete,” or “should therefore be eliminated” unless you have fully demonstrated them. A more measured conclusion is often more persuasive.

Style and tone

The tone is generally formal, but there are moments where wording is awkward or repetitive.

Examples:
“ever changing society” → ever-changing society
“looked upon to be the aggressor” → expected to be the more aggressive sex
“provide for a more healthy male gender” → promote healthier expectations for men
“emanate these qualities” / “radiate these qualities” sound unnatural here; use display or develop

You also repeat phrases such as “distinct gender identity” and “male/female gender qualities” too often. Try varying your language.

Grammar and mechanics

There are a number of sentence-level issues to correct:

Missing space after quotation marks: `gender."Distinct` → `gender. “Distinct`
Capitalization: `if these traits` and `it would be ridiculous` should begin with capital letters.
Word choice: `dependant` should be independent if that is what you mean; “dependent” means relying on others, which contradicts your point.
Hyphenation: single-parent, well-adjusted, ever-changing
Comma use: some sentences are long and would benefit from being split for clarity.

Proofread carefully, especially for punctuation and sentence boundaries.

Revision priorities

If you revise in stages, I recommend this order:

Rewrite the thesis so it focuses on rejecting rigid gender stereotypes.  
Fill in and properly integrate all source material.  
Strengthen analysis in each body paragraph by explaining how the evidence supports the claim.  
Replace absolute or contradictory wording with more precise language.  
Edit for grammar, capitalization, and style.

Final thought

This essay has a strong foundation and an important argument. Its main strength is that it tries to show how gender expectations harm both women and men. If you make the thesis more precise, deepen the analysis, and tighten the language, the essay will become much more persuasive and academically effective.
