Feedback Report

You have a clear and passionate position: media and social beauty standards harm women’s physical and mental health. Your voice is strong, personal, and engaged, and that commitment gives the essay energy. You also use a wide range of examples—models, eating disorders, plastic surgery, advertising, and discrimination—to show the breadth of the problem. For revision, focus on sharpening your argument, improving organization, and checking accuracy and style.

Clarify and strengthen your thesis
Right now, your opening raises the topic effectively, but the main claim is somewhat scattered across the first two paragraphs. Try to state one precise thesis at the end of the introduction.

For example:
“Media and social beauty standards pressure women to pursue unrealistic ideals, damaging their self-image, encouraging dangerous physical practices, and contributing to discrimination against those who do not fit the ideal.”

This gives you three clear body-section ideas:
unrealistic ideals,
harmful consequences,
social discrimination.

Improve organization
The essay has many good points, but the paragraphs sometimes move too quickly from one idea to another. For example, the paragraph about anorexia, bulimia, plastic surgery, liposuction, and beauty products includes too many topics at once. Consider dividing it into separate paragraphs:
one on eating disorders,
one on cosmetic surgery and beauty products,
one on emotional or social consequences.

Also, use topic sentences that clearly connect to your thesis. For instance:
“One major way beauty standards harm women is by encouraging dangerous attempts to achieve unrealistic bodies.”
“These standards also affect how women are treated socially and economically.”

Distinguish emotional appeal from evidence
Your essay is persuasive partly because of your emotional investment, but some claims need stronger evidence or a more careful tone. Several statements are presented as fact but seem exaggerated or unsupported, such as:
“there is no cure” for anorexia,
implants are “a leading cause of Cancer,”
“everywhere you look, girls as young as fourteen are having breast implants.”

These claims weaken your credibility if readers question their accuracy. In an argumentative essay, be careful to use verified evidence and avoid overstatement. If you do not have a source, soften the wording:
“Eating disorders can be difficult to treat and may have long-term effects.”
“Some cosmetic procedures carry significant health risks.”

Develop analysis, not just examples
You include many examples, but sometimes the essay lists harms without fully explaining how they support your argument. After presenting an example, add analysis:
How does advertising create insecurity?
Why do unrealistic model measurements affect ordinary women?
How does discrimination against larger women show that beauty standards are social as well as personal?

Push each paragraph beyond description into explanation.

Use a more academic tone
Your personal voice is valuable, especially in lines like “I admit that I am the opposite,” but some expressions sound too informal for an academic argumentative essay:
“yeah right”
“top notch”
“I mean”
rhetorical strings of questions

You do not need to remove all personality, but try to balance personal engagement with formal style. For example:
Instead of “so can I (yeah right.)”
Write “Advertisements often imply that consumers can achieve the model’s appearance simply by purchasing a product, which creates unrealistic expectations.”

Sentence-level editing
There are frequent grammar, spelling, and usage errors that distract from your ideas. Some patterns to revise:

Common issues:
their/there/they’re: “they are doing their job”
woman/women: singular vs. plural
place/places: “society places on women”
adolescence spelling
Naomi Cambell should be Naomi Campbell
punctuation around quotations and dashes
unnecessary capitalization: “Cancer,” “Bulimia,” “Anorexia” usually lowercase unless part of a formal title

Example revisions:
“The 1990's base these standards...”  
  → “The 1990s promoted these standards as expectations for women.”
“The average women is 5'4" tall...”  
  → “The average woman is 5'4" tall...”
“it seems that everywhere you look”  
  → “it often seems that”

Read the essay aloud slowly, or review it sentence by sentence for proofreading.

Conclusion
Your conclusion has a strong message about inner beauty and self-respect. To make it even stronger, avoid repeating earlier points too generally. Instead, briefly restate the argument and end with a clear final claim:
If society continues to reward only one narrow version of beauty, women will continue to suffer physically, emotionally, and socially. Challenging those standards is therefore not just a personal issue, but a cultural necessity.

Revision priorities
Write a sharper thesis.
Reorganize body paragraphs by main idea.
Check factual claims and avoid unsupported exaggeration.
Add analysis after examples.
Revise for a more formal academic tone.
Proofread carefully for grammar and spelling.

You have a compelling topic and a strong sense of purpose. With clearer structure and more careful evidence, this could become a much more powerful argumentative essay.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0004.3.txt =====
Feedback Report

You have a clear, engaging topic and a strong sense that this issue has more than one side. Your essay shows energy, awareness of audience, and an attempt to move beyond a simple “talk shows are good/bad” argument. To revise effectively, focus on clarifying your main claim, improving organization, and correcting frequent sentence-level errors.

Clarify your thesis
Right now, the essay raises questions about talk shows and presents both benefits and harms, but your main argument is not fully clear until the final paragraph. An argumentative essay needs a direct thesis early in the introduction.

At the moment, you seem to argue something like:
talk shows can be vulgar and harmful,
but they can also help people,
and ultimately guests are responsible for choosing to appear.

That is a workable position, but you should state it plainly in one sentence at the end of paragraph 1.

For example:
> Although many daytime talk shows sensationalize conflict, they should not be dismissed entirely, because they can also provide exposure to real social problems; however, guests must take responsibility for the risks of appearing on them.

This would give the essay a clear direction.

Improve organization
Your essay has good material, but ideas sometimes run together. Try using a more controlled structure:

Introduction  
   Introduce the issue and state your thesis.

Body paragraph 1: Why people defend talk shows  
   Entertainment value, public discussion, practical advice, social awareness.

Body paragraph 2: Why critics object  
   Sensationalism, manipulation, staged conflict, audience encouragement of aggression.

Body paragraph 3: Example of real harm  
   Use the murder example carefully as evidence of possible consequences.

Conclusion/body paragraph 4: Your position  
   Talk shows should not necessarily be banned, but participants must recognize the risks.

This would make your argument easier to follow.

Develop analysis, not just description
Some of your body paragraphs describe what happens on talk shows in vivid detail, but the essay needs more explanation of how that supports your argument.

For instance, when you describe hosts digging for “deep, dark secrets,” ask:
Why is this ethically troubling?
Does it exploit guests for ratings?
Does it undermine the claim that talk shows help people?

Similarly, when you mention positive outcomes, explain:
How do these shows educate viewers?
Are they genuinely helping, or just packaging real problems as entertainment?

Make sure each paragraph does more than narrate; it should prove your point.

Tone and word choice
Your voice is lively, but sometimes the language becomes too dramatic or informal for an academic argumentative essay. Phrases like “flying fist,” “dark skeletons,” or “edge of their seats day in and day out” sound more emotional than analytical.

Aim for a tone that is engaged but controlled. You can still be interesting without sounding sensational. Since your essay critiques sensationalism, a more measured style will strengthen your credibility.

Sentence clarity and grammar
This draft has many errors in punctuation, spelling, agreement, and sentence structure. These distract from your ideas. Main patterns to fix:

A. Spelling
Examples:
audeinces → audiences
agressively → aggressively
thier → their
heath → health
fueding → feuding
thay → they
nieghbor → neighbor

B. Apostrophes and plurals
viewers lives → viewers’ lives
guest panelist → guest panelists
The guest are → The guests are

C. Subject-verb agreement
These differences in opinions leads → These differences in opinion lead
These guest appear → These guests appear

D. Punctuation
You often use periods where commas are needed, creating sentence fragments:
“Many faithful viewers. feel…”
“trash t.v.. is vulgar. and inappropriate…”

Read each sentence aloud and check whether it is complete and correctly punctuated.

E. Run-on sentences
Some sentences are very long and contain too many ideas. Break them into shorter, clearer units.

For example, this sentence:
> “The idea of each show is to build up the topic to a level where tempers may flare out of control and increasing the audiences' interest in the topic, making the discussions hotter.”

Could be revised to:
> The goal of many shows is to build tension until tempers flare. This increases audience interest and makes the discussion more heated.

Use evidence more carefully
The murder example is striking, but it needs context. If you use a real incident, name the show and explain the source clearly. Also explain what the example proves. One unusual case can illustrate risk, but it may not be enough to prove that talk shows as a whole are dangerous.

Strongest next steps for revision
Write a clear thesis at the end of the introduction.
Reorganize body paragraphs by argument, not by stream of thought.
Cut overly dramatic wording and replace it with analysis.
Proofread carefully for spelling, punctuation, and agreement.
Revise long sentences for clarity.

Final comment
This essay has a strong topic and genuine argumentative potential. Your best ideas appear when you acknowledge complexity: talk shows can be exploitative, but they can also expose real social issues. If you present that position more clearly and polish the language, this could become a much stronger academic argument.

Feedback Report

You have a clear position and a recognizable argumentative structure: your essay states a claim early, gives several reasons, and ends with a conclusion. That is a strong starting point. Your voice is direct, and readers can easily tell what you believe. For revision, focus on three main areas: clarity of argument, use of evidence, and sentence-level correctness.

Clarify and strengthen your thesis
Your main claim is: the legal drinking age should be lowered to eighteen in the United States. That is clear, but the essay would be stronger if your thesis also previewed your reasons.

For example:
> The legal drinking age in the United States should be lowered to eighteen because eighteen-year-olds already assume major adult responsibilities, the current law encourages unsafe hidden drinking, and a lower drinking age could reduce some alcohol-related offenses among young adults.

This gives your essay direction and helps each body paragraph connect back to the thesis.

Improve the logic of your argument
Several of your points are interesting, but some need more careful reasoning.

A. “If you can go to war, you should be able to drink”
This is your strongest point emotionally, but it needs tighter explanation. Right now, it relies mostly on opinion. Explain why military service should imply the right to drink. Is your point about fairness? citizenship? legal adulthood? Spell that out.

Also, be careful with word choice. Phrases such as “feel the affects of drinking alcohol” weaken the point because they make drinking sound irresponsible rather than mature.

B. Hidden drinking and parental supervision
You argue that people ages eighteen to twenty drink secretly because it is illegal, and that this leads to unsafe behavior. This could become a strong argument, but you need to explain it more fully. For example, you might argue that when alcohol use is legal, it can happen in more controlled settings rather than in cars, fields, or unsupervised parties.

C. High school diploma argument
This is currently your weakest paragraph. A diploma does not necessarily prove maturity, and making drinking rights depend on graduation raises fairness issues. Some responsible adults do not finish high school, so this point may distract from your main case. I recommend cutting this paragraph or replacing it with a stronger point, such as:
legal adulthood at eighteen,
consistency with voting, military service, and contracts,
or comparison with other countries.

D. Crime argument
Be careful here. You claim that lowering the drinking age would reduce teenage crime, especially drinking and driving. That may or may not be true, so you need evidence. Also, “most crime among teenagers is drinking and driving” is too broad and likely inaccurate. Avoid unsupported generalizations. Instead, use cautious wording:
> The current law may contribute to secretive drinking, which can increase risky behaviors such as drinking in cars or at unsupervised parties.

Use evidence instead of general statements
Right now, the essay depends almost entirely on personal belief. In argumentative writing, readers expect facts, statistics, studies, laws, or examples.

You could strengthen your essay by adding evidence such as:
the legal rights people gain at eighteen,
statistics on underage drinking patterns,
research about whether higher or lower drinking ages affect safety,
examples from other countries or U.S. states in the past.

Even one or two credible sources would make your argument much more persuasive.

Fix contradictions and wording
Your conclusion says:
> “Drinking should be prohibited to to kids eighteen or older.”

This directly contradicts your argument. You likely mean:
> “Drinking should not be prohibited for adults eighteen or older.”

Make sure your conclusion matches your thesis.

Also, avoid phrases that undermine your argument, such as:
“I would feel more like an adult”
This makes the issue sound personal rather than public and policy-based. Focus on broader reasoning.

Work on organization and paragraph unity
Each body paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that states one reason. Then give explanation and evidence. Right now, some paragraphs jump between ideas.

A clearer structure would be:
Introduction + thesis  
Eighteen-year-olds already have adult responsibilities  
The current law encourages hidden and unsafe drinking  
Lowering the age could reduce some minor alcohol-related offenses  
Conclusion

Edit grammar, spelling, and sentence correctness
There are many mechanical errors that distract from your ideas. Some examples:

“Every since” → “Ever since”
“Independance” → “Independence”
“dieing” → “dying”
“to young” → “too young”
“affects” → “effects”
“gonvernment” → “government”
“to to” → delete one “to”

Also, watch for comma splices and sentence fragments. Example:
> “People under the age of twenty one are considered to be minors, when it comes to drinking alcoholic beverages...”
The comma is unnecessary there.

Revision priorities
Rewrite the thesis to include your main reasons.  
Remove or replace the diploma paragraph.  
Add evidence to support your claims.  
Correct contradictions in the conclusion.  
Proofread carefully for spelling, grammar, and sentence structure.

You have a clear opinion and a solid foundation. With stronger evidence, cleaner logic, and careful editing, this essay can become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

You have a clear concern throughout this essay: children absorb restrictive ideas about gender early, and those ideas can damage both women and men later in life. Your essay also shows ambition because you try to synthesize several sources rather than discussing only one text. That is a strong foundation. For revision, the main priorities are clarifying your argument, improving organization, and correcting sentence-level errors so your ideas come across more convincingly.

Clarify your thesis
Right now, your position is not fully consistent. At some points you argue that there is a “core of behavior” that makes people boys or girls and that men and women are naturally different; at other points you argue that gender roles are socially imposed stereotypes that should be changed. These claims can conflict unless you define them very carefully.

Try to state a thesis that clearly explains:
what you mean by gender identity versus gender roles
which differences you think are socially constructed and which, if any, are natural
what exactly should change in children’s media and education

For example, your thesis might become something like:  
Children should be taught that gender does not limit their abilities or life choices, because rigid stereotypes in media and society harm both boys and girls in adulthood.

That would give the essay a more focused line of argument.

Improve organization and paragraph unity
Several paragraphs contain good ideas, but the essay often jumps too quickly from one point to another. A stronger structure would help.

A possible revision plan:
Introduction: define the issue and present your thesis.
Body paragraph 1: explain how children learn gender stereotypes early; use Pollitt as evidence.
Body paragraph 2: show effects on girls; connect media messages to later limitations.
Body paragraph 3: show effects on boys; use Ferrell’s “Ralph’s Story.”
Body paragraph 4: connect these pressures to women’s experiences in Friedan.
Body paragraph 5: evaluate possible solutions, including whether Gray actually supports your argument.
Conclusion: restate your argument and explain why it matters.

Also, make sure each paragraph begins with a topic sentence that states its main point. For example, your paragraph on Pollitt could begin:  
Pollitt demonstrates that children’s television teaches gender stereotypes at a very early age.

Use sources more carefully
You summarize several authors, but sometimes the discussion is too general or inaccurate. A few issues:

Be careful with names and titles: Katha Pollitt, Betty Friedan, Warren Farrell, John Gray, Men Are from Mars, Women Are from Venus.
Make sure each source is represented fairly. For example, saying all the writers support “separate gender identity” may oversimplify their positions.
Explain quotations and examples instead of only mentioning them. After citing a source, tell the reader how it supports your thesis.
Avoid overreliance on authority. Rather than “Masters and Johnson say…,” explain why that developmental point matters to your argument.

Strengthen reasoning
Some claims need more support or a more balanced tone. For example:
“Parents are not interested in justice, they just want quiet” is too sweeping and sounds unfair.
“Children need to know men and women walk, talk, drive, and grow in different ways” is an absolute claim that may weaken your argument because it sounds stereotypical itself.

Try to avoid generalizations and make your claims more precise. Ask yourself:
Is this always true?
Can I prove it?
Does this statement support my main argument, or does it contradict it?

Revise style and tone
Your voice is energetic, but some phrasing is too informal or emotionally loaded for an academic essay. For example:
“life’s roller coaster ride”
“wonderful start”
“scarry confession”
“bad message delivered at a bad time through a bad medium”

Aim for a more formal and analytical tone. Instead of dramatic wording, use precise language that shows critical thinking.

Correct grammar, spelling, and mechanics
There are many sentence-level errors that distract from your ideas. Please proofread carefully. Common issues include:

Spelling: “ammend” → *amend*; “relazed” → *realized?* or *relaxed?*; “scarry” → *scary*; “sponse” → *spouse*; “metophor” → *metaphor*
Punctuation: avoid commas after “but”; don’t place periods incorrectly after citations
Agreement: “there are other types of behavior that is open to change” → *are open to change*
Possessives: “boys lives” → *boys’ lives*; “their’s” → *theirs*
Consistency: don’t shift between “one,” “people,” and “your” in the same passage

Example revision:  
Original: “Children should be taught that there is a core of behavior that makes them boys or girls but, there are other types of behavior that is open to change.”  
Possible revision: Children should be taught that society often associates certain behaviors with gender, but those expectations should not limit individual choices.

Final advice
Your essay has a worthwhile topic and a real argumentative purpose. The next step is to make the argument more precise, more logically organized, and more polished at the sentence level. Focus first on revising the thesis and paragraph structure, then edit for style and correctness. That process will make your ideas much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

You have a clear position and a strong sense of urgency. Your essay argues consistently that legalizing at least some drugs—especially marijuana—could reduce crime, improve regulation, and better reflect personal freedom. The paper shows genuine engagement with the topic, and you raise several meaningful points: public health, prison overcrowding, black-market violence, age restrictions, tax revenue, and civil liberties. For revision, the main work is to make your argument more focused, better organized, and more carefully supported.

Clarify your thesis
Right now, your thesis is broad: “The legalization of some drugs like marijuana would improve many different aspects of society.” This is a good start, but your essay shifts between marijuana only, some drugs, and all drugs. That creates confusion.

Decide exactly what you are arguing:
legalize marijuana only?
legalize marijuana and some currently illegal drugs?
legalize all drugs under government regulation?

Choose one position and state it precisely in the introduction. For example:
> Legalizing and regulating marijuana would reduce black-market crime, allow better health oversight, and free law enforcement to focus on more serious threats.

A narrower claim will make the paper stronger.

Improve organization
Your essay currently reads more like a list of ideas than a structured argument. Try organizing by clear body paragraphs, each with one main claim.

A possible structure:
Introduction: background + clear thesis  
Public health argument: regulation, purity, safer access  
Crime and prisons: black market, violence, overcrowded jails  
Economic/social benefits: tax revenue, jobs, law enforcement priorities  
Counterargument and response: age limits, risks, abuse concerns  
Conclusion: restate argument and significance

Also, use topic sentences. For example:
> Legalization would improve public health by allowing the government to regulate drug purity and distribution.

That helps the reader follow your logic.

Develop reasoning more carefully
You make several interesting claims, but many need more explanation. For example:
“Hopefully, the number of drug users would decrease.” Why would legalization decrease use?
“Knowing that you can just buy drugs from a store, people will try to find other way to do illegal things.” This is unclear and needs evidence or revision.
“If people want to hurt themselves, let them…” This is a very strong claim and may alienate readers unless you explain it more thoughtfully.

Try to show how and why each point supports your thesis. Avoid jumping too quickly from one idea to the next.

Use evidence more effectively
At present, the essay relies mostly on personal opinion and general statements such as “people are going to use drugs anyway.” In an argumentative essay, readers will expect evidence:
statistics on marijuana arrests or prison overcrowding
research on regulated drug markets
studies comparing the harms of marijuana, alcohol, and tobacco
evidence about needle-exchange programs and disease prevention

You include one quotation, but it is not smoothly integrated and appears to have citation/quotation errors. Introduce sources clearly and explain their relevance.

Address counterarguments fairly
You mention health concerns and children’s access, which is good, but you should engage these objections more fully. For example:
Could legalization increase use?
How would the government prevent impaired driving?
Would legal access normalize drug use for teenagers?

A strong argument does not ignore these concerns; it answers them.

Revise tone and wording
Some sentences sound overly absolute or informal, such as:
“If people want to harm their bodies, why stop them?”
“If somebody does not want to live on this earth, why make them?”

These lines weaken your credibility and shift the paper into emotional territory that does not help your main argument. Aim for a more measured academic tone.

Also, be careful with wording such as “getting high.” In formal writing, use terms like intoxication, drug use, or substance use unless you are quoting someone directly.

Edit for grammar and style
There are frequent sentence fragments, agreement errors, and word-choice mistakes. A few examples:
“Issues such as permanent damage…” → fragment
“The side effects of marijuana is…” → should be are
“one of the lease harmful” → least harmful
“what anyone say” → says
“peoples body” → people’s bodies
“hann your body” → likely harm your body

You also have some run-on sentences and punctuation problems. Read the essay aloud slowly, or revise one sentence at a time for subject-verb agreement, sentence completeness, and comma use.

Final revision priorities
When you revise, focus on these steps:
Narrow the claim: marijuana only, or broader legalization?
Rewrite the introduction with a precise thesis.
Reorganize body paragraphs by topic.
Add evidence and examples from reliable sources.
Remove extreme or unclear claims.
Proofread carefully for grammar and sentence structure.

Your essay has a strong argumentative core. With a clearer thesis, better organization, and stronger evidence, it could become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position, a strong sense of audience, and an effective opening anecdote. The story about your friend immediately gives the issue urgency and helps readers see why language proficiency matters in the classroom. Your main claim is also easy to identify: professors at USC should be able to speak English well enough to teach effectively, and the university should require proof of that ability.

What is working well

Strong engagement with the topic  
You clearly care about this issue, and that conviction gives the essay energy. The paper does not feel detached or vague.

Effective use of anecdote  
The opening example is memorable and concrete. It gives the argument a human face rather than starting only with abstract claims.

Clear argumentative direction  
You move from problem to consequences to proposed solution. That structure is appropriate for an argumentative essay.

Main revision priorities

Refine the thesis
Your thesis is understandable, but it could be more precise and academically framed. Right now, some wording sounds emotional or absolute. For example, “before a professor should be allowed domain over a classroom” is dramatic but not very exact.

A stronger thesis would identify:
the problem: some instructors lack sufficient spoken English for teaching,
the consequence: this can limit student learning,
the solution: the university should assess instructional language proficiency before assigning professors to teach.

This would make your argument sound more reasoned and less reactive.

Strengthen logic and fairness
Your current argument sometimes overgeneralizes. For instance, “When professors can't speak English, they can't communicate with English speaking students; therefore they cannot teach them” is too absolute. A reader may object that some non-native speakers are excellent teachers, or that accent is different from lack of clarity.

To make the essay stronger, distinguish between:
having an accent
lacking enough English proficiency to explain course material clearly

That distinction is crucial. Without it, the essay risks sounding biased against international faculty rather than focused on instructional effectiveness. Your real argument is about communication, not nationality.

Use more evidence, not just personal experience
The anecdote is a good start, but one story is not enough to support a broad policy claim. Add other forms of support, such as:
university policies for graduate teaching assistants,
student learning needs in discussion-based or technical courses,
why communication matters in office hours, labs, and assessments,
possibly statistics or examples from similar universities.

Even if you do not have research yet, revise toward a more evidence-based style.

Address counterarguments
A strong argumentative essay should acknowledge objections. Consider addressing points such as:
universities also hire professors for research expertise,
many brilliant scholars are non-native English speakers,
students benefit from international faculty perspectives.

Then explain that these points are valid, but teaching assignments still require sufficient spoken English. Doing this will make your argument more credible and balanced.

Organization and development

Your essay generally follows a logical order, but some paragraphs repeat the same idea: students pay tuition and deserve instruction. Instead of repeating, deepen each paragraph’s purpose.

Possible structure:
Opening anecdote and thesis  
Why classroom communication matters for student learning  
Why the issue is especially serious in advanced or required courses  
Counterargument: research value and international hiring  
Policy solution: language assessment for instructors  
Conclusion

This will help each paragraph contribute something new.

Style and tone

At times, the tone becomes too forceful or simplistic for academic argument. Phrases like “the only solution” or “the only appropriate thing” shut down complexity. In revision, use more measured phrasing:
“a necessary solution”
“an effective policy response”
“should be required to demonstrate instructional English proficiency”

Also, avoid repeatedly using “he” or “his” as the generic professor/student pronoun. Use they/their for inclusivity and smoother style.

Sentence-level issues

There are many proofreading problems throughout the draft: punctuation, spacing, typos, sentence fragments, and awkward wording. These errors sometimes make your meaning unclear. Examples include:
“here at. USC”
“It s”
“b t U”
“began. To ask”
missing commas and run-on sentences

You should revise carefully for:
sentence boundaries
apostrophes
consistent verb tense
clearer word choice

A few wording choices also need attention:
“squander through complicated material alone” → probably struggle through
“ample amount of English” → sufficient English proficiency
“teacher’s aids” → teaching assistants or teaching aides

Final advice

Your essay has a solid core argument and a compelling real-world example. The biggest improvements will come from making the paper more precise, balanced, and evidence-based. Focus your revision on this question:

If you answer that carefully, your essay will become much more persuasive.

Your essay has a clear, passionate position: Art Modell’s decision to move the Browns was driven by greed and betrayed Cleveland fans. Clarify and strengthen your thesis
Right now, the essay asks several questions—“Is this right?” “Is business just business?”—but your central claim should be stated directly and early. At the end of the introduction, give one clear thesis that tells readers exactly what you will argue.

For example:
“Although team owners often defend relocation as a business decision, Art Modell’s move of the Cleveland Browns was unjust because it ignored fan loyalty, violated commitments made to the city, and prioritized private profit over a public investment.”

This kind of thesis is specific, arguable, and gives your body paragraphs a structure.

Improve organization
The essay includes useful points, but they are not always arranged logically. Try organizing around clear reasons rather than moving between examples and emotional reactions.

A stronger structure might be:

Introduction: background on NFL franchise moves + thesis  
Body paragraph 1: Cleveland’s investment in the city and in sports development  
Body paragraph 2: Modell’s previous statements and the city’s efforts to accommodate him  
Body paragraph 3: Why the move harms fans and public trust  
Conclusion: restate argument and broader implications

Use topic sentences to signal each paragraph’s main point. For example:
“Cleveland had already demonstrated its commitment to professional sports through major public investment.”

Your anger about the move is understandable, but in academic argument, readers need explanation as well as emotion. At times, statements such as “Arthur B. Modell is a liar” or “gave Cleveland the one finger salute” sound more like personal attacks than argument. Replace these with evidence-based analysis.

Instead of:
“Arthur B. Modell is a liar.”

Try:
“Modell’s decision contradicted his earlier public statements that he would keep the team in Cleveland, which undermined the trust of fans and city officials.”

That change keeps your point but makes it more persuasive.

Address opposing views fairly
You briefly mention that some people think the move is justified, but you do not really engage that argument. A strong argumentative essay should acknowledge the other side and respond to it.

For instance, you could say that owners have financial responsibilities and may seek more profitable stadium deals. Then explain why, even if that is true, Cleveland’s public support and Modell’s prior commitments make this case different. Doing this will make your argument seem more balanced and credible.

Use evidence more carefully
You include many details and numbers, which is good, but some need to be presented more clearly and, ideally, cited if this were a formal paper. Make sure each fact supports your claim and is explained.

For example, after listing stadium offers and tax figures, explain why they matter:

How do they prove Modell acted unfairly?
Why should readers care?

Also check factual accuracy and spelling of names/teams. A few errors weaken credibility.

Work on tone and word choice
You have a strong voice, but some phrasing is too informal or exaggerated for an academic essay. Examples include:
“dump city”
“suck it up and take it like a man”
“pure and simple”
“heart and soul of the city”

Some vivid language is fine, but too much can make the essay sound emotional rather than reasoned. Aim for a serious, controlled tone.

Edit sentence-level problems
The draft has many grammar, punctuation, and usage errors that distract from your ideas. Focus especially on:

Possessives/contradictions: “cities most beloved team” → “city’s most beloved team”
Its/it’s: “it’s tax subsidized teams” → “its tax-subsidized teams”
Capitalization: “march” → “March”
Spacing: “St.Louis” → “St. Louis”; “10years” → “10 years”
Word choice: “rapid” likely should be “rabid”
Run-ons/comma splices: several sentences combine too many ideas without proper punctuation
Typos: “inwhich” → “in which”; “to fast” → “too fast”

Conclusion

The main revision goal is to make the essay more structured, more analytical, and more polished in tone. stronger textual evidence from Friedan/Farrell, or
more careful phrasing.

“being ,pressured” → remove comma

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear, provocative position: you argue that men and women can value sex differences without abandoning legal equality. That central idea gives the paper energy and a recognizable voice. You also try to support the claim with examples from sports, advertising, entertainment, and business, which shows ambition. For revision, the main task is to make the argument more logically consistent, more academically persuasive, and more carefully phrased for a university audience.

Clarify and narrow the thesis
Right now, the paper makes several different claims at once:
men and women are naturally different,
society unfairly tries to erase those differences,
sexualized entertainment is healthy,
some sex-based business practices are not discriminatory,
equal treatment under the law should still exist.

These do not always fit together smoothly. A stronger thesis would define exactly what you mean by “enjoying differences” and where the limits are. For example:  
“Although men and women should have equal legal rights and opportunities, society should be able to acknowledge some average sex differences without treating either sex as inferior.”

That version is narrower, easier to defend, and more academically credible.

Strengthen logic and evidence
Many paragraphs rely on assertion rather than proof. In argumentative writing, examples need to do more than illustrate your opinion; they must demonstrate it.

Sports paragraph
The football/volleyball example is currently confusing and underdeveloped. You assume that unequal funding is justified because football generated its own support, but you do not address counterarguments such as historical inequality, access, or Title IX. The comparison to communism weakens the paragraph because it sounds exaggerated and distracts from your point.

Strip-club and Hooters paragraphs
These sections are the most likely to alienate readers because they move quickly from personal opinion to broad social claims without evidence. For example, saying strip clubs create “a new outlook and appreciation” in marriage is a major claim that would need research. Similarly, saying Hooters’ hiring practices are “not discrimination” ignores the legal and ethical complexity of the issue. You need to engage with opposing views fairly, not dismiss them.

Madonna/Charles Barkley example
This comparison could become useful if framed as a discussion of how society values male athletic bodies differently from female sexualized bodies. However, as written, it oversimplifies both figures and makes claims such as “Madonna would bring about a sense of love and affection,” which are subjective and unsupported.

Develop a more academic tone
The essay currently sounds conversational and sometimes provocative in ways that reduce credibility. Phrases like “Let us lust after the opposite sex,” “strip joint,” and “maybe open one called buttocks for women” may express personality, but they weaken the seriousness of the argument.

Try to replace emotionally charged or slang terms with more precise language:
“strip joint” → “strip club”
“lust after” → “feel sexual attraction toward”
“trashy” → “socially stigmatized”

Also avoid rhetorical overstatement such as “sacred differences,” “superhuman,” or “the obvious answer.” Academic arguments are usually stronger when they sound measured rather than absolute.

Address assumptions and inclusivity
Your essay assumes heterosexual attraction throughout (“opposite sex” as the only model). That limits the argument and may make readers question whether the paper has considered modern social realities. Even if your focus remains on male-female difference, you should acknowledge that not all attraction operates that way.

The essay also risks treating gender roles and sexual display as universally positive. Ask yourself:
Who benefits from these arrangements?
Who might feel excluded or objectified?
Can admiration of bodies coexist with respect and consent?

Engaging these questions will make your argument more thoughtful and persuasive.

Improve organization
The essay would benefit from a clearer structure:
Introduction: define your key terms and present a focused thesis.
Body paragraph 1: equality before the law vs. recognition of difference.
Body paragraph 2: sports as a case study.
Body paragraph 3: media/advertising and the celebration of physical difference.
Body paragraph 4: respond to objections about objectification/discrimination.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0006.3.txt =====
Your essay has a clear position and an identifiable structure: you argue that welfare reform should combine Democratic and Republican ideas, and you organize the paper around several specific policy changes. That gives the essay a solid argumentative foundation. To revise effectively, focus on three main areas: sharpening your thesis, strengthening your reasoning, and improving sentence-level correctness.

Clarify and narrow the thesis

Your thesis appears at the end of the introduction and is specific, which is good. However, it currently reads more like a list than a sharply argued claim. Consider making the central logic clearer: *why* does combining both parties’ ideas produce a better system? For example, your revised thesis could emphasize that welfare should provide temporary support while promoting long-term independence.

Also, be careful with claims such as “the welfare issue can be solved” or “a simple compromise would solve the problem.” These are too absolute. Welfare is a complex social issue, so a more credible claim would be that these reforms could *improve* the system or *reduce long-term dependency*.

Strengthen the argument, not just the summary

Much of the paper summarizes what Democrats, Republicans, and sources say. To make the essay more persuasive, add more of your own analysis after each example. Ask:
What does this evidence prove?
How exactly would this policy help?
Are there possible harms or limitations?

For instance, your section on training programs is one of the strongest because you use examples such as Project Match and Princess Rowell. But after presenting those examples, explain more fully why they matter. Do they show that education is more effective than simply cutting benefits? Do they suggest that support and accountability need to work together?

Similarly, in the section on time limits, you assume that time limits will automatically prevent dependency. That point needs more support. Some readers may ask what happens to recipients who cannot find stable work in time. Even if you still support time limits, acknowledging such concerns would make your argument more thoughtful and mature.

Watch for overgeneralization and biased phrasing

At times, the essay makes broad claims that may sound unfair or unsupported. For example:
“mothers on welfare are having more babies at the government’s expense”
“there would no chance for recipients to become dependent”

These statements are too sweeping and may weaken your credibility. Try using more precise, evidence-based phrasing. Instead of implying motives, focus on policy effects: for example, “Critics argue that some welfare structures may unintentionally encourage long-term reliance.”

Also, some arguments need stronger ethical consideration. For example, abolishing aid to mothers under eighteen or cutting payments for children is presented mainly as a cost-saving measure. You should address the human consequences of those cuts, especially for children, since readers may see this as a major omission.

Improve organization and paragraph development

Your body paragraphs generally stay on topic, but some paragraphs are very short and feel underdeveloped. Try using a clearer structure for each body paragraph:
Topic sentence stating the policy claim
Evidence from sources
Explanation of how the evidence supports your claim
Link back to your thesis

For example, the paragraph about Princess Rowell could be combined with the broader training-program paragraph so that the personal example supports the general policy argument.

Also, transitions between sections could be smoother. Right now, the essay sometimes moves abruptly from training, to time limits, to spending cuts. Add transition sentences that explain how each reform builds on the previous one.

Revise source integration and mechanics

This draft has many quotation, punctuation, capitalization, and spelling errors that need careful correction. A few examples:
“hot issue” is informal; use “controversial issue” or “major policy issue”
“heatedly argued” should be “hotly debated”
“highschool” should be “high school”
“Sliverstein” is misspelled; be consistent with source names
Several quotations have incorrect punctuation and quotation marks
Some sentences are fragments or have comma splices

Example:
> “In Chicago, a highly regarded training program, Project match, has given a mix of education, training, counseling, and job-placement to its clients and has gotten half of them off of welfare.”

This could be revised for correctness and clarity:
> In Chicago, a highly regarded training program called Project Match has provided education, counseling, job training, and placement services, helping about half of its participants leave welfare.

Use quotations more selectively. You rely heavily on quoted material, and sometimes your own voice disappears. Paraphrasing more often will help you sound more authoritative.

Final revision priorities
Rewrite the thesis to make the central argument more precise.
Add analysis after each piece of evidence.
Reduce absolute or unsupported claims.
Address counterarguments or consequences of proposed cuts.
Edit carefully for grammar, punctuation, spelling, and citation consistency.

Overall, you have a strong topic and a clear position. The next step is to turn this from a source-based summary into a more balanced, analytical argument.

Awareness of opposing concerns

These ideas are related, but they are not exactly the same. Choose one main claim and make it precise in your introduction.

Or, if you want a more limited claim:
“If the university will not remove the curfew entirely, it should at least allow overnight visitation on weekends in all dorms.”

Improve organization

Body paragraph 2: Practicality  
  The curfew disrupts studying, visiting with family, and normal social interaction.
Body paragraph 3: Safety  
  Forcing people to walk across campus at 2 a.m. may create risks.
Conclusion: restate your position and propose a solution.

This structure will help your reader follow your reasoning.

Your voice is strong, but some wording is too casual for an academic essay. For example:
“chill out with us”
“quality time”
“we’re not at home”
“this should be one thing that does”

Try replacing casual phrasing with more precise language:
“visit us”
“spend time together”

Also, avoid rhetorical overstatement where possible. Strong arguments are often more convincing when calmly explained.

When you argue that the curfew is dangerous, that is a strong point—but it would be stronger if you explained exactly how the rule creates risk.

You could connect the ideas more explicitly:

Edit sentence-level errors carefully

“sigu.her mate guest” → likely sign her male guest in
“We are In college” → in
“He* spent” → remove typo
“bad to go” → had to go
“can not he decided on” → cannot be decided on / cannot be agreed upon

You also have some run-on or unclear sentences. Read the draft aloud slowly, or review one sentence at a time for clarity.

Revision priorities

Decide on one exact thesis.
Reorganize the essay around 2–3 clear reasons.
Make each paragraph focus on one point.
Replace casual phrases with formal academic wording.
Proofread carefully for spelling, grammar, and sentence clarity.

Final thought

This essay has a strong foundation because it addresses a real policy problem and includes personal examples that matter. Your next step is to turn that passion into a more focused, organized argument. If you revise for clarity and structure, your essay will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

You raise an important issue—television violence and its effects on children—and you bring in outside sources to support your discussion. Your essay shows awareness of both sides of the debate: concerns about children’s exposure and concerns about censorship. For revision, the main priorities are clarifying your argument, improving organization, and using sources more effectively.

Clarify your thesis
Right now, your essay presents several ideas but does not clearly argue one consistent position. The sentence “some people disagree with its effects on children” is also vague.

What to do:  
State a precise thesis at the end of the introduction. For example:

If your position is anti-violence regulation:  
  *Although television violence is widespread and may influence some children, government regulation such as the V-chip and congressional bans are not the best solution; parental supervision is more effective.*
If your position supports regulation:  
  *Because violent programming is widespread and can negatively affect children, stronger controls such as content blocking and clearer rating systems are necessary.*

Once you choose a position, make sure every paragraph supports it.

Strengthen organization
Your essay currently reads more like a list of facts than a developed argument. Several paragraphs introduce ideas but do not explain how they support your point.

A stronger structure would be:

Body Paragraph 1: extent of TV violence  
Body Paragraph 2: effects on children  
Body Paragraph 3: policy responses (V-chip, Congress, report cards)  
Body Paragraph 4: counterargument and response  
Conclusion: restate argument and explain broader significance

Each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that links back to your thesis.

For instance, instead of “Some television shows show violence because it appeals to adult viewers,” you could write:  
*Supporters of violent programming argue that such content is sometimes necessary for realistic storytelling and is intended for adult audiences, not children.*

You include source material, but often you simply report what others said without explaining its significance. Academic argument requires you to interpret the evidence.

For example, after mentioning the Senate Commerce Committee’s proposals, explain:

Examples:

What to do:  
Introduce sources clearly and integrate them into your sentences. For example:

Also, avoid relying on only old or limited examples. If possible, use stronger evidence about effects on children, not just examples of violent shows.

“the infamous V-chip”

Your essay presents a clear pro-funding position and offers a wide range of reasons for supporting the arts: historical, social, economic, educational, and therapeutic. The breadth of your argument is a strength, and your tone is engaged and persuasive. To revise effectively, focus on clarifying your thesis, tightening organization, improving precision of evidence, and polishing style and grammar.

Thesis and overall argument

Right now, the essay lists many benefits, but the argument can feel diffuse. Try revising the thesis to forecast your main reasons:

Possible revision:  
“Government should continue to fund the arts because the arts preserve cultural memory, strengthen communities, contribute to local economies, and support education and mental well-being.”

This will help readers follow the structure of the essay and see how each paragraph contributes to the central claim.

Organization and paragraphing
The essay generally moves from cultural value to social value to economic and psychological benefits, which is logical. However, some paragraphs overlap or shift topics abruptly.

Suggestions:
Paragraph 2 and 3 both discuss art as communication. These could be combined or more clearly distinguished:
  - art across time/cultural memory
  - art in political or social communication
Paragraphs 6 and 7 both discuss psychological and therapeutic benefits. Consider merging them into one well-developed paragraph.
The paragraph about pornography feels disconnected from the rest of the essay. Unless your assignment specifically requires addressing controversies in arts funding, you may want to cut it or connect it much more clearly to public funding debates.
The conclusion is broad but vague. It should restate the thesis and synthesize the main reasons rather than ending in abstract language about “human needs.”

Use of evidence
You make many strong claims, but they are often presented in general terms (“researchers have found,” “psychologists have told us,” “continual research has been done”). In academic argument, these statements need specific support.

Strengthen evidence by:
naming specific studies, programs, or institutions
giving concrete examples of economic impact
using one or two well-developed examples rather than many brief assertions

For instance:
Instead of saying museums attract tourists, give an example of a city or museum whose arts sector contributes to local revenue.
Instead of saying prison art programs are successful, briefly explain one program and what results were observed.
Your personal example (“As somewhat of an artist…”) adds voice, but in a formal argumentative essay it should support—not replace—outside evidence.

Style and clarity
At times your language is thoughtful and vivid, but some sentences are wordy, awkward, or imprecise. Aim for clearer, more concise prose.

Examples:
“there are some areas, such as government support for the arts, which should keep”  
  → “there are some areas, such as support for the arts, that should be preserved”
“This is a classic model of how we of today can leave an impression”  
  → “This shows how contemporary societies can leave a record for future generations”
“it is interpretable by nearly everyone in a precise, pictorial form”  
  → “it can be understood by nearly everyone through visual form”

Also be careful with phrasing that sounds inflated or vague (“the very essence of early human thought,” “negative influences,” “productive measures”). Specific language is more persuasive than grand language.

Tone and audience awareness
You generally maintain a serious academic tone, but there are places where the voice becomes too conversational or overly subjective:
“Obviously”
“it’s interesting to note”
“As somewhat of an artist, I myself…”

Use these sparingly. Academic argument is usually stronger when claims are framed through reasoning and evidence rather than personal impression.

Grammar and mechanics

Watch for:
word choice/form
  - “which should keep” → “which should be kept”
  - “meant be valued” → “meant to be valued”
  - “Besides from being productive” → “Besides being productive”
articles and phrasing
  - “in a ever-more complex” → “in an ever more complex”
spelling/typos
  - “oftentiines” → “oftentimes”
  - “pschosanalysts” → “psychoanalysts”
hyphenation and consistency
  - “super-power” is usually “superpower”
historical reference

agreement and clarity
  - “friendships and bonds developed” → “friendships and bonds”

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and strong conviction: you argue consistently that professional football players’ salaries should be capped. The paper also has an identifiable structure, moving from moral concerns, to fan facilities, to player contracts. That gives you a foundation to revise effectively. The main work now is to strengthen your argument, improve fairness and precision, and polish style and correctness.

What is working
Clear thesis and purpose. The introduction states your position and previews reasons.
Paragraph-level organization. Most body paragraphs focus on one main point.
Engaged voice. Your writing sounds invested in the issue, which is valuable in argumentative writing.

Main revision priorities

Make the thesis more precise
Right now, your thesis says there should be a salary cap, but it does not explain what kind of cap or why that policy is the best solution. Try to frame the issue more specifically.

For example:
> Professional football should have a salary cap because it would promote competitive balance among teams, reduce salary inflation, and encourage franchises to invest more money in fan experience and long-term organizational needs.

This version is more focused and avoids broad moral judgments.

Replace emotional claims with evidence-based reasoning
A major weakness is that many claims are overstated or unsupported. For instance:
“A professional football player makes more money in one year than a doctor does in a lifetime.”
“All you have to be able to do is run and catch balls.”
“The whole reason why [O.J. Simpson] was let off was because he was a popular football player.”

These statements are either inaccurate, impossible to prove, or too simplified. In an argumentative essay, readers are more persuaded by facts, examples, and logic than by anger or exaggeration.

Revision strategy:
Use statistics on average NFL salaries, average medical salaries, franchise revenues, or stadium funding.
Explain how salary caps work in professional sports.
Show why a cap would improve league fairness or financial stability.

Avoid weak comparisons and unfair assumptions
Much of the essay compares football players to doctors and nurses. While this shows your concern about social values, it does not directly prove that a salary cap is the right policy. Salaries in entertainment and sports are determined differently from salaries in medicine.

Also, several statements generalize unfairly about athletes:
calling them “greedy”
suggesting they are unintelligent
implying they “do nothing but entertain”

These claims weaken your credibility because they sound biased. A stronger paper acknowledges complexity:
athletes generate enormous revenue
careers are short and physically risky
owners, networks, and leagues also profit heavily

Then you can still argue that a cap is necessary.

Strengthen logic within body paragraphs
Some paragraphs wander or rely on personal opinion rather than argument. For example, the stadium paragraph begins with worn-out facilities, which is potentially useful, but it needs clearer logic:
NFL teams earn substantial revenue.
A large portion goes to salaries.
A salary cap could free resources for maintenance, safety, and fan experience.

Right now, the paragraph mainly gives a personal anecdote about a broken seat and missing toilet paper. That can stay as an example, but it should not be the main evidence.

Address counterarguments
A strong argumentative essay should show awareness of the other side. Consider including a paragraph like this:
Some people argue that players deserve high salaries because they have rare talent, short careers, and generate billions in revenue.
However, even if elite athletes deserve strong compensation, a league-wide cap can still protect competitive balance and prevent excessive bidding wars.

This will make your argument more mature and persuasive.

Style and tone
Your tone is passionate, but at times it becomes judgmental or informal. Phrases like “do wee really need professional football players?” or “greedy football players” may alienate readers. Aim for a more academic tone:
Replace insults with analysis.
Use formal wording.
Reduce rhetorical questions; too many make the essay feel repetitive.

Sentence-level issues to edit
There are frequent grammar, spelling, and usage problems. A few recurring ones:
wee → *we*
other peoples lives → *other people’s lives*
affective → *effective*
use to be → *used to be*
ant other team → *any other team*
Forty-Niners is usually written *49ers*
Andre Risen may be misspelled (*Andre Rison*)

Also watch for:
comma splices and run-on sentences
repetition (“The one reason why…” appears more than once)
awkward phrasing (“important to live”)

Suggested revision plan
Rewrite the thesis to focus on policy, not outrage.
Cut exaggerated or insulting claims.
Add concrete evidence about salaries, league revenue, and salary caps.
Reorganize body paragraphs so each follows a clear claim-evidence-explanation pattern.
Include one counterargument and rebuttal.
Proofread carefully for grammar and spelling.

Final thought
You have a strong opinion and a clear topic, which is an excellent start. To make this essay persuasive at a college level, focus on accuracy, fairness, and evidence. If you revise with those goals in mind, your argument will become much more credible and effective.

Biggest issue: organization and thesis

Create a logical paragraph plan
Consider organizing the essay like this:
Introduction: problem + thesis  
Constitutional argument: why regulation is legal  
Evidence of harm: deaths, injuries, suicide, accidents  
Why current access is dangerous: impulse violence, domestic violence, theft, mental illness  
Policy solutions: Brady Bill, waiting periods, licensing, registration  
Counterargument and response: NRA / self-defense claims  
Conclusion

This structure will help you avoid repetition and make each paragraph do a distinct job.

Use sources more carefully
Your essay relies heavily on quotations and paraphrases, but sometimes the source material seems to drive the paper instead of your own reasoning.

What to improve
Introduce sources clearly: Explain why a source matters before quoting it.
Analyze after quoting: Don’t let a quotation stand alone.
Be consistent with citations: You cite “Cozic,” “Cole,” “CUF,” and “Close Up Foundation,” but there are errors and inconsistencies.

Example revision
Instead of:
> The NRA debates 11 the 2nd Amendment guarantees the right of any American to own anything from handguns to machineguns."

Write:
> The NRA argues that the Second Amendment protects broad private gun ownership, but this interpretation is disputed. As Cozic notes, the Supreme Court has historically linked the amendment to militia service rather than an unlimited individual right (84).

That version is clearer, more polished, and better integrated.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

You have a clear position, strong personal conviction, and a willingness to address exceptions rather than treating the issue too simply. Those are real strengths in argumentative writing. Your essay also uses a personal example, which gives the paper emotional force. For revision, focus on improving thesis clarity, organization, tone, evidence, and sentence-level correctness.

Clarify and sharpen your thesis
Right now, your position shifts somewhat. At first, you argue for “strict rules regarding abortions,” then later you allow exceptions for rape and danger to the mother’s life. That is not a problem in itself, but your thesis should state your position fully and precisely.

A stronger thesis might be:
“Although abortion should remain available in cases of rape and serious risk to the mother’s life, it should be more strictly regulated in cases of consensual sex because contraception, adoption, and public assistance provide alternatives.”

This kind of thesis helps the reader understand your full claim from the beginning.

Improve organization
The essay has several main ideas, but they are not always arranged in the clearest order. Consider organizing by argument rather than by emotion.

A possible structure:
Introduction: define your position and acknowledge complexity.
Body paragraph 1: consensual sex and personal responsibility.
Body paragraph 2: contraception and abstinence as prevention.
Body paragraph 3: alternatives such as adoption and government aid.
Body paragraph 4: exceptions—rape.
Body paragraph 5: exceptions—danger to the mother’s life.
Body paragraph 6: personal anecdote about your cousin.
Conclusion: restate claim in a balanced, persuasive way.

Also, use stronger topic sentences so each paragraph begins with a clear point.

Use a more academic and respectful tone
Your strongest weakness is tone. Some phrases are emotionally charged in ways that may alienate readers and weaken your credibility. Examples include:
“play the game”
“horseplay”
“kill it”
“wild maniac”
“vicious sperm”
“should be put into jail for murder”

These phrases sound more like emotional reaction than reasoned argument. In academic writing, persuasive power usually comes from measured, precise language. You can still express strong beliefs, but do so respectfully and analytically.

For example:
Instead of “play the game,” write “engage in consensual sexual activity.”
Instead of “kill it,” write “terminate the pregnancy.”
Instead of “wild maniac,” write “a rapist” or “an assailant.”

Strengthen your evidence
You mention effectiveness rates for contraception, but you do not cite sources. In an argumentative essay, statistics need credible support. Also, some claims are oversimplified, such as:
“a person has no excuse”
“The government gives aid”
“Welfare is there to help persons…”

These claims need evidence and nuance. Public assistance and adoption are not always simple or sufficient solutions. If you want to persuade skeptical readers, acknowledge complications rather than ignoring them.

Also, your cousin’s story is powerful, but it is an anecdote, not proof of what generally happens. Use it as a personal example, but support broader claims with research.

Develop reasoning more carefully
At times, your reasoning jumps too quickly from claim to conclusion. For example, saying women who have abortions after consensual sex should be jailed for murder is a very strong claim, but you do not fully explain the legal or ethical reasoning behind it. If you keep such a claim, you must defend it much more thoroughly. Otherwise, it may be better to argue for regulation rather than criminal punishment.

Similarly, your exception for rape is based mainly on emotional impact, but you should explain the principle behind the exception. Why does consent change your moral reasoning? Making that logic explicit will strengthen the essay.

Revise grammar and sentence clarity
There are many sentence-level errors involving grammar, word choice, punctuation, and agreement. A few patterns to address:

Subject-verb agreement: “women” not “women consents”
Missing words: “is said to be ninety-five percent effective”
Wrong word: “how are financially unable” should be “who are financially unable”
Run-on sentences: several sentences need to be divided or punctuated properly
Wordiness and repetition: some ideas are repeated without adding new support

Example revision:
Original:  
“Another case and point that would make me agree with an abortion is if the mother was unable to carry her child the full term without dying herself I does think the death of two people is a justified reason to prevent a woman from having an abortion.”

Revised:  
“Another case in which I would support abortion is when a woman cannot carry a pregnancy to term without a serious risk of death. In such a situation, abortion may be justified to save the mother’s life.”

End with a stronger conclusion
Your conclusion mostly repeats your belief. Try instead to synthesize your argument:
restate your position
acknowledge exceptions
leave the reader with a thoughtful final point

Final advice
Your essay shows passion and commitment, which are valuable in argumentative writing. The next step is to turn that passion into a more disciplined argument. Focus on:
a precise thesis
logical organization
credible evidence
respectful tone
careful editing

With revision, this could become a much more persuasive and mature argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear opinion and a strong sense of voice. You are clearly engaged with the topic, and your concern about how media representations of sex may influence teenagers gives the paper a real argumentative purpose. For revision, the main priorities are: sharpening your thesis, improving organization, developing evidence more carefully, and editing for grammar and style.

Strengthen the thesis
Right now, your main claim is understandable—sex is overused in the media and this can harm teenagers—but it appears in several slightly different forms throughout the essay. Try to state one precise thesis near the beginning and let the rest of the paper support it.

For example, you might revise the thesis into something like:

“The media overuses sexual content in television, music, and advertising, and by presenting sex as glamorous while ignoring responsibility, it encourages unhealthy attitudes about sex among teenagers.”

This version is more specific because it names:
the areas of media you will discuss,
the problem with the portrayal,
and the effect on teenagers.

Improve organization
Your essay already has a basic structure: television, radio, magazines, then effects on teens. That is a good foundation. However, some paragraphs drift or repeat ideas. Revise so that each paragraph has one clear focus.

A stronger structure could be:

Introduction: present the issue and thesis  
Television: explain how TV normalizes constant sexual activity  
Music/radio: show how lyrics and titles promote sex directly  
Advertising/magazines: explain how sex is used to sell unrelated products  
Effects on teenagers: argue that media glamorizes sex but rarely shows consequences or responsibility  
Conclusion: restate the argument and suggest what media should do differently

Use topic sentences to make this structure clear. For example:
“Television often portrays sexual activity as normal, casual, and consequence-free.”
“Music reinforces the same message by making sexual desire its central theme.”
“Advertising uses sexual imagery even when it has nothing to do with the product being sold.”

Develop evidence more carefully
You use examples, which is good, but many are based on personal observation (“every time I turn on the radio,” “almost every magazine”). In an academic argument, these broad claims can sound exaggerated unless you qualify them or support them more carefully.

Instead of absolute language, try phrases like:
“many popular shows”
“a large number of advertisements”
“some songs and music videos”
“media often presents”

Also, explain *how* your examples support your claim. For instance, don’t just say an ad shows a half-dressed model; explain why that matters: it shifts attention away from the product and uses sexual appeal to persuade consumers.

Make the reasoning more nuanced
Your argument will be more persuasive if you avoid sounding like all media is the same or that media alone causes teen pregnancy or STDs. Right now, some sentences suggest a direct cause-and-effect relationship that is too simple.

You could strengthen your credibility by acknowledging complexity:
parents, schools, and peers also influence teenagers;
not all sexual content is harmful;
the real issue is that media often shows sex without responsibility, discussion, or consequences.

This makes the paper sound more thoughtful and less like a general complaint.

Revise tone and word choice
Your voice is strong, but sometimes the wording becomes repetitive or conversational. Phrases like “Now,” “if I’m not mistaken,” and repeated uses of “everyone sleeps with everyone” can be tightened.

Aim for a more formal academic tone:
Replace “sex is used to do everything” with “sexual imagery is used to market and dramatize many forms of media.”
Replace “That’s not reality” with “This portrayal is unrealistic because it omits communication, contraception, and consequences.”

Also be careful with phrasing like “pregnant teenage females.” “Teenage girls” or “teenagers” would sound more natural.

Edit grammar and mechanics
There are many sentence-level errors that need attention. Major issues include:
unnecessary capitalization (“Sex Is”)
spelling errors (“Kellye,” “saids,” “your” for “you’re,” “Its” for “It’s,” “soak and wet” instead of “soaking wet”)
apostrophe and quotation mark problems
comma splices and awkward punctuation

A few examples:
“Sex Is used to do everything.” → “Sex is used to sell almost everything.”
“if you didn't look hard” is fine, but “your selling” should be “you’re selling.”
“You can't want to discuss sex openly without discussing taking precautions measures.” → “You cannot discuss sex openly without also discussing precautionary measures.”

Read the paper aloud during revision. That will help you catch repeated words, awkward sentences, and punctuation problems.

Conclusion
You have a strong topic and a clear point of view. The next step is to turn this into a more focused academic argument by:
stating one clear thesis,
organizing paragraphs more tightly,
using more precise and qualified language,
explaining your examples in greater depth,
and correcting grammar and style issues.

Your central insight is promising: the problem is not simply that sex appears in media, but that it is often presented without responsibility or consequences. If you make that idea the center of the essay, your revision will be much stronger.

Feedback Report

Your essay raises an interesting and important claim: clothing shapes both how others perceive us and how we perceive ourselves. You also show effort in using outside sources, which gives the paper an academic foundation. For revision, focus on three main areas: clarifying your argument, improving organization, and strengthening style/mechanics.

Clarify and sharpen your thesis

The paper seems to argue that fashion has psychological and social effects across different age groups because clothing influences identity, self-confidence, and social acceptance.

Try revising your introduction so it leads to a direct thesis, for example:

> Clothing is more than appearance: it influences how people are judged by others, how they understand themselves, and how they seek acceptance within society.

This gives the essay a clear line of argument and helps readers understand how each body paragraph fits.

Improve organization and paragraph unity
At present, the essay reads more like a series of notes from sources than a fully developed argument. Many paragraphs introduce a topic, add quotations or paraphrases, and then move on without enough explanation. To revise, give each paragraph one clear main idea and connect it back to your thesis.

A possible structure:

Introduction: clothing affects perception and identity  
Social judgment and first impressions  
Clothing and self-concept  
Adolescents and peer pressure  
Consumer behavior and fashion trends  
Older adults and appearance  
Modesty and cultural values  
Conclusion

This structure would help your essay feel intentional rather than scattered.

Analyze your evidence instead of only presenting it
You include many quotations and citations, but often they are not fully explained. For example, after the Herrick poem, the essay should explain how the poem supports your claim about clothing and psychology. Does it show that slight disorder in dress can seem more attractive than perfection? If so, say that directly.

A good rule: after every quotation or paraphrase, add 2–3 sentences of analysis answering:
What does this evidence mean?
Why does it matter?
How does it support my argument?

Without that explanation, the sources feel dropped into the essay.

Use a more academic tone
Some wording weakens your credibility because it sounds overly casual, judgmental, or unsupported. For instance, the opening sentence about judging someone to be “a slut” or “a pimp” is too blunt and risks alienating readers. It also introduces stereotypes without analysis.

Instead, use neutral academic language such as:
“people often make quick assumptions based on clothing”
“dress can trigger stereotypes about character, sexuality, or social status”

Similarly, phrases like “If she wears a mini-skirt, she probably feel sexy” are too sweeping and rely on assumptions. Be careful not to generalize about people’s motives without evidence.

Watch grammar, sentence structure, and word choice
There are many sentence-level errors that interfere with clarity. Some common issues:

Subject-verb agreement
“that person feel” → “that person feels”
“she probably feel sexy” → “she probably feels sexy”

Missing capitalization
“one doesn't know…” should be “One doesn't know…”
“older men and women…” should be “Older men and women…”

Incorrect word choice
“good indicator on how” → “good indicator of how”
“strong affect on adolescents” → “strong effect on adolescents”
“not as likely to fell self-pity” → “not as likely to feel self-pity”

Awkward or unclear phrasing
“The way of expressing the way one dresses…” is confusing.
“Designers make outfits that infer the instabilities people have…” is imprecise; perhaps you mean “exploit” or “respond to.”

Read the paper aloud during revision; this will help you catch unclear phrasing.

Integrate sources more smoothly
Right now, citations appear often, but the essay does not always introduce them clearly. Instead of dropping in a source, try signal phrases:

“According to Mary Shaw Ryan, …”
“Flugel argues that modesty…”
“Herrick’s poem suggests that…”

Also, be consistent in citation format. Make sure you are following the required style guide (MLA, APA, Chicago, etc). The poem citation and page numbers need especially careful formatting.

Strengthen the conclusion
Your conclusion restates some major ideas, but it becomes broad and repetitive. Instead, use the conclusion to reinforce your argument more powerfully: clothing matters not just because it covers the body, but because it communicates identity, status, conformity, and emotion.

Revision priorities
Write a clear thesis.
Reorganize body paragraphs around distinct claims.
Add analysis after each source.
Replace informal/judgmental language with academic phrasing.
Edit carefully for grammar and clarity.

You have a strong topic and a solid base of research. The next step is to turn this draft from a source-driven overview into a focused argument with clear, polished analysis.

This section seems unfinished. I think you want to discuss rape and perhaps exceptions in anti-abortion arguments, but the idea is not clearly expressed. In revision, each body paragraph should focus on one reason or one counterargument.

Body Paragraph 1: bodily autonomy / why the woman should decide.
Body Paragraph 2: difficult cases such as rape and unwanted pregnancy.
Body Paragraph 3: response to an opposing argument (for example, the genocide/Nazi comparison).
Conclusion: reinforce the central claim and explain why it matters.

Use topic sentences at the start of each paragraph so the reader can follow your logic.

You make several strong claims, but many need more support or more careful phrasing.

A. Avoid overstatement
For instance:
