This is too absolute. A reader might challenge it easily.
*“If a child is born unwanted, he is automatically psychologically damaged for life”*  
This is also too sweeping and not credible without evidence.

Instead, qualify your claims:
“the pregnant woman is usually in the best position to assess the personal, medical, and economic consequences of continuing the pregnancy.”
“children born into deeply unwanted or unsupported circumstances may face emotional and material hardships.”

B. Represent opposing views fairly
Your essay refers to “anti-abortionists” and “prolifers” in a dismissive way. In academic argument, you will sound stronger if you represent the other side accurately and respectfully before responding.

For example, instead of saying their arguments are “not very substantial,” explain why they are unconvincing:
Do they rely on faulty comparisons?
Do they ignore important exceptions?
Do they prioritize fetal rights over women’s autonomy without addressing conflicts between those rights?

C. Use evidence
Your current essay relies mostly on assertion. To become more persuasive, include evidence such as:
legal or ethical principles about bodily autonomy,
data on unsafe abortions where abortion is restricted,
sources on the effects of forced pregnancy,
definitions or scholarship if you discuss terms like genocide.

A. Run-on and comma splice errors
Example:

B. Word form and spelling
Examples:
*“this categories”* → “these categories”
*“pro-choiceor”* → “pro-choice”
*“its hard”* → “it’s hard”
*“there tendencies”* → “their tendencies”
*“woman women”* → likely a drafting error
*“proffers”* → probably “pro-lifers”

C. Awkward phrasing

a sharper thesis,
cleaner paragraph structure,

You have a clear position: television exposes children to sexual content, violence, and profanity too often, and adults/media should do more to protect them. Your concern is consistent throughout, and the essay shows commitment and urgency. To revise effectively, focus on sharpening your argument, improving organization, and strengthening correctness and evidence.

Clarify your main claim
Your essay argues several related points:
children watch adult programs unsupervised,
they imitate what they see,
they are too young to understand sexual content,
parents and television networks should provide more child-appropriate programming.

These ideas fit together, but the thesis should state them more directly. Right now, the essay opens with examples rather than a precise claim. Try a thesis such as:

> Because children often watch television without supervision and may imitate what they do not understand, broadcasters and parents should limit children’s exposure to adult content and provide more age-appropriate programming after school.

This gives the essay a stronger direction.

Improve organization
The essay currently repeats similar ideas across paragraphs: children are too young, parents are busy, television shows too much, and children imitate what they see. Revision will be stronger if each paragraph has one main point.

A possible structure:
Introduction: explain the issue and present thesis.
Body paragraph 1: children often have access to adult television when unsupervised.
Body paragraph 2: young children may misunderstand and imitate what they see.
Body paragraph 3: even programs assumed to be safe (such as cartoons) may model bad behavior.
Body paragraph 4: parents and broadcasters share responsibility; propose more child-friendly programming and better supervision.
Conclusion: restate the argument and why it matters.

This will help reduce repetition and make the essay easier to follow.

Use stronger evidence
A major weakness is that many claims are asserted without support. For example:
“Approximately ninety eight percent of television programs are not for children.”
“That only leaves a small two percent…”
“about ninety percent of the rated ‘R’ movies…”

These statistics sound doubtful because no source is given. In an argumentative essay, unsupported numbers can weaken credibility. If you do not have evidence, either:
find a reliable source, or
rephrase more cautiously, for example: “Much of daytime and evening television is aimed at adults rather than young children.”

Also, many examples come from personal experience. These can be useful, but they should not be your only support. Add broader reasoning or outside evidence if possible.

Watch tone and fairness
Your essay is passionate, but sometimes it overgeneralizes. For example, saying children will watch a show and then “want to experiment” is too absolute. Not every child responds the same way. Academic argument is usually stronger when it avoids exaggeration. Use wording like:
“may imitate”
“can influence”
“are at risk of misunderstanding”

Also be careful with sensitive topics. The paragraph on homosexuality sounds more reactive than analytical. If you include it, explain how young children may be confused by any relationship they do not yet understand, rather than singling out one topic in a way that may seem biased.

Sentence-level issues
There are frequent grammar, punctuation, and wording problems that make the essay harder to read. Main patterns:

Word choice / phrasing
“make the children to want” → “make children want”
“for an example” → “for example”
“suppose to explain” → “supposed to explain”
“some where else” → “somewhere else”

Agreement and form
“a man and women kiss” → “a man and a woman kiss”
“there was one thirty minute game show” → “there was only one thirty-minute game show”

Punctuation and spelling

“Opra” → “Oprah”
“nigh time” → “nighttime”
“everyday” (adjective) vs. “every day” (time expression)
“RI” → “R”

You should proofread carefully or read the essay aloud to catch awkward sentences.

Some sentences are very long and repetitive. For example, you repeat that parents are “cooking or cleaning,” and that children are “not educated enough” in several places. Say each point once clearly, then move on. This will make the argument sound more controlled and persuasive.

Feedback Report

You have a clear purpose: to persuade readers that recycling is necessary and practical. Your essay shows commitment to the topic, includes many concrete examples, and attempts to support claims with sources. The strongest feature is your range of evidence: you discuss plastics, glass, batteries, motor oil, appliances, and household toxics, which gives the paper breadth and shows real engagement with the issue.

To revise effectively, focus on argument, organization, and style.

Clarify your main argument
Right now, the essay sometimes sounds like an informational report on recyclable materials rather than a focused argument. Your thesis in the introduction should make a specific claim, not just announce the topic.

For example, your current introduction suggests that recycling should “become commonplace uniformly,” but it does not clearly explain why or what action readers should take. A stronger thesis might be:

“Because recycling reduces landfill waste, conserves natural resources, and prevents toxic pollution, individuals and communities should make recycling a routine part of daily life.”

This gives you three clear reasons to structure the essay around.

Improve organization
The essay currently moves from one material to another in a list-like way. That creates useful content, but it weakens the argumentative flow. Instead of organizing by item alone, consider organizing by major reasons:

Recycling conserves resources  
   - plastic, paper, glass, steel

Recycling reduces pollution and health risks  
   - refrigerators/Freon, batteries, motor oil, household toxics

Recycling is practical and achievable  
   - simple sorting, store take-back programs, cartridge refills, community bins

This would help the paper feel less like a catalog and more like an argument. Add topic sentences at the start of each paragraph so readers know how the example supports your larger claim.

Strengthen analysis
You include many facts and quotations, but often the essay moves on before explaining their significance. After presenting evidence, add 1–2 sentences of analysis that answer: Why does this matter? How does it support my argument?

For example, after citing the effect of recycling a glass bottle, explain that even a small household action can save energy at a large scale when multiplied across a population. That kind of commentary turns evidence into persuasion.

Be careful with claims and consistency
Some claims need qualification or better explanation. For instance, the section arguing that plastic bags are “much better” than paper bags is controversial and may distract from your broader argument unless you address complexity. If you keep this point, present it more carefully and acknowledge that both materials have environmental costs. Otherwise, readers may question your credibility.

Also, your essay shifts between several goals:
saving landfill space
protecting future generations
reducing pollution
conserving resources

These can all fit together, but you should consistently connect them back to your thesis.

Work on paragraph structure and transitions
Some paragraphs are underdeveloped or abruptly end. Others contain several ideas that should be separated. Use a consistent structure:

Topic sentence
Evidence/example
Explanation
Link back to thesis

Also add transitions between sections, such as:
“In addition to conserving space in landfills…”
“Recycling also protects public health…”
“Beyond large household items, everyday products…”

This will make the essay easier to follow.

Revise tone and word choice
Your tone is passionate, which suits an argumentative essay, but sometimes the wording becomes informal or imprecise. Examples:
“just tossing out the trash”
“a few more minutes”
“please make the effort”

Aim for a more consistently academic tone. Instead of directly telling the reader what to do in conversational language, frame claims more formally.

For example:
Less formal: “If paper must be used, please make the effort to recycle.”
More formal: “If paper products are used, they should be recycled to reduce unnecessary waste.”

Edit for grammar, punctuation, and mechanics
There are many sentence-level issues that need careful proofreading. Common problems include:

Comma splices and awkward phrasing
Quotation punctuation
Capitalization (“cadmium” should not be capitalized mid-sentence)
Typos (“fight bulb” for “light bulb,” “fun” for “full,” “m” for “in”)
Word choice errors (“hangars” should be “hangers”)
Sentence fragments (“The energy saved from recycling one glass bottle…” appears disconnected from the previous sentence)

Also, some quotations are dropped into the paragraph without enough introduction or integration. Introduce each source smoothly and cite it consistently.

Update and evaluate sources
Your essay relies heavily on a few sources, and some sound like advocacy materials rather than balanced evidence. In revision, consider whether the sources are credible, current, and sufficient. Try to use recent, authoritative evidence and cite it in a consistent format required by your course.

Final advice
Your essay has a strong foundation because it shows conviction and includes many relevant examples. The next step is to turn that material into a more focused argument. Prioritize these revisions:

Write a sharper thesis.
Reorganize around 2–3 main reasons.
Add analysis after evidence.
Use a more formal academic tone.
Proofread carefully for grammar and clarity.

With those changes, your essay will become much more persuasive and coherent.

Right now, the essay mostly describes stereotypes rather than arguing a specific point about them. Ask yourself: what do you want readers to believe?

For example, your thesis could be something like:
*American culture teaches people to associate pink with femininity and blue with masculinity through advertising, baby clothing, and consumer products.*
Or, if you want a stronger argumentative angle: *The color coding of pink for girls and blue for boys reinforces limiting gender stereotypes from infancy.*

A clear thesis should appear near the beginning and guide the whole essay.

Improve organization
Your essay repeats similar points several times: commercials use pink for women, baby girls wear pink, baby boys wear blue, and products are marketed by color. Group related ideas into separate paragraphs with topic sentences.

A possible structure:
Introduction: explain the pink/blue stereotype and state your thesis.
Advertising and media: shaving gel, deodorant, feminine products, diaper commercials.
Babies and clothing: hospital clothing, assumptions people make about babies.
Consumer products: bottles, dishes, brushes, diapers, packaging.
Conclusion: explain why this matters—how stereotypes shape expectations for boys and girls.

This will help the essay feel less repetitive and more purposeful.

Move from examples to analysis
You include many examples, but often you stop at description. After each example, explain why it matters.

For instance, instead of only saying that a deodorant commercial uses pink, you could add:
*This color choice suggests that femininity should be linked with softness and delicacy, reinforcing traditional ideas about women.*

That kind of sentence turns an observation into analysis, which is essential in an argumentative essay.

Be careful with overgeneralizations
Some claims are too absolute, such as “Commercials always show this stereotype” or “You would not catch a baby boy with pink on.” Words like always, automatically, and of course can weaken your credibility because readers can easily think of exceptions.

Use more precise phrasing:
*Many commercials reinforce this stereotype.*
*People often assume a baby in pink is a girl.*
*Baby boys are frequently dressed in blue.*

Academic writing is usually stronger when it avoids sweeping statements.

Strengthen tone and wording
Some phrases sound informal or conversational, such as “get away with it.” Try using a more academic tone:
Instead of *“Females can wear masculine colors and get away with it”*, write:
  *Girls and women are often given more freedom to wear colors associated with masculinity than boys and men are to wear pink.*

Also, “males” and “females” can sound overly clinical if used as nouns throughout. In many cases, men/women or boys/girls will sound more natural, depending on the age group.

Revise grammar, punctuation, and mechanics
There are frequent errors with capitalization, sentence structure, and word choice. A few important patterns:

Capitalize the first word of every sentence:
  - *On the Secret deodorant commercial...*
  - *Once a baby has developed its looks...*
  - *Of course...*

Do not begin a sentence with because unless the sentence is complete:
  - Incorrect: *Why? because it is a very soft color...*
  - Better: *Why is pink seen as feminine? One reason is that it is considered a soft color...*

Watch sentence fragments and awkward phrasing:
  - *Pink is almost always worn by baby girls* → *Baby girls are often dressed in pink.*
  - *Once a baby has developed its looks* is unclear. You may mean: *Once a baby is old enough for others to recognize its sex more easily...*

Correct typos:
  - *jam referring to babies* likely should be *I am referring to babies.*

Use transitions
Your ideas will flow better if you connect them with transitions:
*In addition*
*For example*
*Similarly*
*This pattern also appears in…*
*As a result*

These will help readers follow your logic.

End with a stronger conclusion
Your last paragraph introduces an interesting point—that girls are often allowed more flexibility in color choices than boys are—but it feels underdeveloped. This could become a strong conclusion if you explain what that reveals about gender expectations. For example:
*This difference suggests that society polices masculinity more strictly than femininity, especially in childhood.*

Final advice
In revision, focus first on:
Writing a clear thesis.
Reorganizing into distinct body paragraphs.
Adding analysis after each example.
Editing for grammar and sentence clarity.

You already have a strong base of examples. The next step is to shape them into a focused argument about how color reinforces gender stereotypes.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that drug legalization would benefit the United States. The paper also shows ambition because you try to address several dimensions of the issue—crime, prison overcrowding, public spending, and public health. That breadth gives you a strong starting point. To revise successfully, focus on clarity, development, organization, and credibility.

Clarify and narrow your thesis
Your introduction states your opinion strongly, but the thesis is still broad and somewhat informal:

> “personally I think it’s time we stop wasting money…”

Try to make the thesis more precise and academic. For example:

Possible revision:  
*The United States should legalize and regulate certain drugs because prohibition increases black-market crime, fills prisons with nonviolent offenders, wastes public resources, and creates avoidable public-health risks.*

This version does three things:
states a clear claim,
uses a more formal tone,
previews the main body points.

Strengthen paragraph development
Several body paragraphs introduce a good idea but do not fully explain it. Some even contain placeholders (“”), so your reasoning is incomplete. Each paragraph should follow a clear pattern:

topic sentence → explanation → evidence → analysis → concluding link to thesis

For example, in the paragraph about lower drug prices, you need to explain:
why legalization would reduce prices,
how lower prices would weaken illegal markets,
what evidence supports that claim.

Right now, the argument often jumps from claim to conclusion without enough support.

Use stronger evidence
You include one citation (“Duke 231”), which is a good start, but the paper needs much more evidence. Many claims are sweeping and currently unsupported, such as:
legalization would reduce crime,
legalization would reduce alcohol and tobacco use,
prison populations would drop significantly,
users would “pay their own debts.”

For revision:
add reliable academic or government sources,
use statistics carefully,
explain the source instead of simply dropping in a quotation.

Also be cautious about factual claims that sound uncertain or overstated. For example, the public-health paragraph contains claims that would need especially strong evidence, and some are not clearly explained.

Improve organization
Your essay would be easier to follow if each paragraph focused on one main reason. Right now, some paragraphs mix several ideas together.

A possible structure:
Introduction + thesis
Legalization would reduce black-market profits
Legalization would reduce some forms of crime
Legalization would reduce incarceration of nonviolent offenders
Legalization would save money and generate tax revenue
Legalization would improve public health through regulation
Conclusion

This structure will help your reader follow your logic.

Address counterarguments
An argumentative essay becomes stronger when it acknowledges objections. Right now, the paper only presents one side. Consider briefly addressing concerns such as:
Would legalization increase drug use?
Could addiction rates rise?
Are all drugs equally suitable for legalization?

You do not need to abandon your position. Instead, show that you understand the complexity of the issue and can respond thoughtfully.

Revise tone and word choice
Some wording is too emotional or conversational for academic argument. Examples include:
“come to reality”
“it’s time we stop wasting money”
“big money”
“hooked up in the drug business”

Aim for a more formal tone. For example:
“have recognized”
“public funds could be redirected”
“high profits”
“drawn into the illegal drug trade”

Also avoid broad statements about “Americans as a whole,” since they overgeneralize.

Sentence-level issues
There are many grammar and style problems that make parts of the essay hard to understand. Focus on these areas:

a. Sentence clarity
Some sentences are incomplete or awkward:
“Also in the cities, .”
“Similarly, when heroin, they become alcohol abusers.”

These need to be rewritten completely.

b. Word choice
Some words are incorrect:
“beneficiary to America” → beneficial to America
“imprisonmemt” → imprisonment

c. Punctuation and spacing
You have extra spaces and punctuation issues:
“that ,drug prohibition”
“it’ s”
unnecessary spacing before commas or after apostrophes

d. Parallel structure
In lists, keep the grammar consistent:
“save the nation money, reduce crime and make the users pay their own debts…”

This is close, but your earlier paragraphs should build those points in the same organized pattern.

Most important revision priorities
If you revise in stages, do this first:
Replace the placeholders and incomplete sentences
Rewrite the thesis to be precise and formal
Develop each body paragraph with evidence and explanation
Add at least one counterargument
Proofread for grammar, punctuation, and word choice

Final thought
Your essay has a clear purpose and several potentially strong arguments. The main challenge is that the paper currently asserts more than it proves. If you slow down, organize each paragraph around one reason, and support your claims with credible evidence, you can turn this into a much more persuasive academic argument.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: you believe South Carolina needs stricter gun control because it would reduce crime and save lives. You also use vivid local examples, which gives the paper emotional force and shows personal investment in the topic. That said, your revision should focus on strengthening the argument, organizing ideas more clearly, and improving source use and sentence-level correctness.

Main strengths
Clear stance: Your thesis consistently supports stronger gun control.
Relevant examples: The school shooting, bank robbery, Mr. Glover’s death, and your uncle’s death all show why the issue matters.
Emotional impact: Personal and local examples help readers understand the human cost of gun violence.

Biggest revision priorities

A. Make the thesis more precise
Your current thesis is understandable, but it is wordy and grammatically awkward:  
> “Gun Control needs to implemented because it would mainly reduce crime, save lives and lessen the fears of innocent ones.”

Revise it so it clearly states what kind of gun control you support and how it would help. For example:  
“South Carolina should adopt stricter gun control measures, including stronger background checks and limits on firearm access, because these policies could reduce violent crime, prevent unnecessary deaths, and improve public safety.”

This makes your argument more specific and easier to prove.

B. Improve organization
Right now, the essay reads like a series of incidents rather than a carefully structured argument. Try this structure:

Introduction
   - Define the issue
   - Give brief context
   - End with a clear thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Youth access to guns
   - Discuss the school shooting
   - Explain how access to firearms contributed

Body Paragraph 2: Guns and public crime
   - Discuss robbery in public places
   - Explain why stricter laws might deter or reduce such crimes

Body Paragraph 3: Personal/local impact
   - Use either Mr. Glover’s death or your uncle’s death
   - Focus on how gun violence damages families and communities

Body Paragraph 4: Counterargument
   - Address opposition such as the NRA or constitutional rights
   - Respond respectfully and logically

Conclusion
   - Restate your main claim
   - End with a strong final point or call for reform

C. Develop analysis, not just examples
Many paragraphs present a case but do not fully explain how the example proves your argument. After each example, add analysis:
What does this incident show about access to guns?
Would stricter laws realistically have prevented it?
What policy change follows from this example?

For instance, after the bank robbery example, you say stronger laws would make perpetrators “think twice.” That claim needs more support. Explain why: stricter purchase laws? better tracking? waiting periods? penalties for illegal possession?

Use evidence more carefully
You include quotations and references, but there are several problems:

Some sources are incomplete or inconsistently cited.
A few quotations are dropped into the paragraph without introduction or explanation.
Some statistics seem unclear or possibly inaccurate, especially the “hate crimes” section. Robbery and murder are not automatically “hate crimes,” so check the source carefully.

Suggestions:
Introduce each source: who is speaking, what publication it comes from, and why it matters.
Explain each quotation after presenting it.
Make sure your evidence directly supports your claim about gun control.

Example:
Instead of dropping in the quotation, write something like:  
According to *The Times and Democrat*, a 15-year-old student shot two teachers at Blackville-Hilda High School before killing himself. This case suggests that minors’ access to firearms can have deadly consequences and supports the need for stronger controls on who can obtain guns.

Strengthen logic and tone
Sometimes the essay makes broad claims without proof, such as “heinous crimes involving guns are at an all-time high.” If you make a factual claim like that, provide evidence. Also, avoid overstating points unless you can support them.

Your tone is passionate, which is good, but in academic writing, aim for a more formal style. For example:
“people killing others over nonsense” → “people using firearms in minor disputes that escalate into deadly violence”
“use their heads instead of the trigger” → vivid, but less formal; consider revising.

Sentence-level issues to edit
There are many grammar, spelling, capitalization, and punctuation errors. Examples include:
goverment → government
techers → teachers
memebers → members
misue → misuse
acquaintace → acquaintance

Also watch for:
unnecessary capitalization (“Gun Control”)
run-on sentences
awkward phrasing (“needs to implemented” → “needs to be implemented”)

Proofread carefully or read the essay aloud to catch errors.

Final advice
For revision, focus first on:
rewriting the thesis,
reorganizing the essay into clear argument paragraphs,
adding analysis after each example,
checking and clarifying sources,
editing for grammar and style.

You have a strong foundation because you care deeply about the issue and have meaningful examples. The next step is to turn that passion into a more structured, evidence-based academic argument.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0011.4.txt =====
Feedback Report

You have a clear position and a recognizable argumentative structure: introduction, three counterarguments, and conclusion. Your essay also shows strong engagement with the topic and a willingness to challenge opposing views. For revision, the main priorities are tone, evidence, clarity, and sentence-level correctness.

Strengthen your academic tone

Right now, the essay often sounds conversational or aggressive rather than academic. Phrases such as “ridiculous,” “obscene,” “pull their plug,” “let me throw a couple of statistics at you,” and rhetorical questions make the argument feel emotional rather than reasoned. In an argumentative essay, strong claims are more persuasive when they are stated calmly and supported with evidence.

Revise by:
Replacing loaded language with precise language.
Avoiding direct address (“I will show you,” “for you”).
Limiting rhetorical questions.

Example:
Instead of  
> “these reasons are as ridiculous as the source they are trying to endorse”  

try  
> “these arguments become less convincing when examined in light of cost, ethics, and deterrence data.”

Clarify and sharpen your thesis

Your introduction clearly opposes capital punishment, but the thesis is wordy and weakened by emotional phrasing. A stronger thesis should directly state your main claim and preview your reasons.

Possible revised thesis:
> Capital punishment should be abolished because it is more expensive than life imprisonment, relies on retribution rather than justice, and fails to deter violent crime.

This gives your essay a clear roadmap.

Improve use of evidence

You refer to “statistics” and “studies,” but you do not identify sources, dates, locations, or researchers. In academic writing, unsupported statistics are not persuasive. For example, the claim that executions cost “five times the amount” as life imprisonment needs a credible source.

Revise by:
Naming the source of each statistic or study.
Explaining how the evidence supports your point.
Avoiding vague references like “they have done studies.”

Example:
Instead of  
> “They have done studies in states where the death penalty has been employed.”  

try  
> “Studies comparing murder rates before and after reinstatement of the death penalty in several states have found no consistent deterrent effect.”

Even better would be to cite a specific study or organization.

Develop your reasoning more carefully

Your three body paragraphs are organized around cost, retribution, and deterrence, which is effective. However, some points need fuller explanation or more careful logic.

Cost paragraph:
This is one of your strongest points, but it would be stronger if you explained why death penalty cases cost more: longer trials, specialized attorneys, automatic appeals, and extended incarceration on death row.

Retribution paragraph:
This paragraph raises an important ethical issue, but it becomes unclear because of informal phrasing and broad moral claims. Distinguish between:
retribution as a legal principle,
revenge as a personal emotion,
and your argument that the death penalty blurs this distinction.

Deterrence paragraph:
You make a reasonable claim that many murders are impulsive or committed with the assumption of avoiding capture. This is promising, but you should present it as an argument supported by evidence, not just as personal opinion.

Organize sentences for clarity and coherence

Several sentences are long, repetitive, or awkwardly phrased. Try to keep each sentence focused on one main idea. Also, use stronger transitions between ideas within paragraphs.

Example:
> “How this statement can be valid is due to the reasons that when someone recieves the death penalty they are not killed the next day.”

This could become:
> “This higher cost results largely from the lengthy legal process required in death penalty cases.”

That revision is shorter, clearer, and more formal.

Correct grammar, spelling, and word choice

There are many mechanical errors that distract from your argument. These need careful proofreading. Some recurring issues include:

its / it’s:  
  “it’s” = “it is”; “its” = possessive  
until, not “untill”
received, not “recieves”
imprison, not “inprison”
medieval, not “midevil”
deterrent, not “deterent”
murders, not “murdeers”
states, not “statels”
pros and cons, not “prols and con’s”
detrimental, not “detramental”

Also watch for sentence fragments, comma spacing, and paragraph formatting.

Reduce first-person emphasis

Because this is an academic argument, you should reduce phrases like:
“I feel”
“I think”
“Correct me if I’m wrong”
“It seems very obvious to me”

These weaken your authority. Let the evidence speak.

Example:
Instead of  
> “I feel I have given significant reasons...”  

write  
> “The evidence suggests that the main arguments for capital punishment are unconvincing.”

Suggested revision priorities

Rewrite the introduction with a precise thesis.
Replace informal or emotional language with academic wording.
Add credible sources for every statistic and study.
Revise body paragraphs for clearer logic and explanation.
Proofread carefully for grammar and spelling errors.

Final comment

Your essay has a solid argumentative framework and strong conviction. The biggest step toward improvement is shifting from a passionate opinion piece to a well-supported academic argument. If you revise the tone, document your evidence, and edit carefully, this could become a much more persuasive essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay addresses an important ethical issue and shows clear engagement with the debate over euthanasia and physician-assisted suicide. You raise relevant questions, refer to Dr. Kevorkian, and bring in outside sources such as William F. May and Joseph Fletcher. For revision, focus on three main areas: clarifying your argument, improving organization, and correcting sentence-level problems.

Clarify your thesis and position
Your introduction sets up the controversy well, but your own argument is not fully clear. You ask whether doctors should be allowed to assist suicides or remain bound by the Hippocratic Oath, but the essay does not present a strong, direct thesis answering that question.

By the end, you seem to argue that:
doctors should not initiate or decide euthanasia,
but they may cooperate with a patient’s decision.

That is a nuanced position, but you should state it clearly near the end of the introduction. For example:

> Although terminally ill patients should have some control over end-of-life decisions, physicians should not be the ones to initiate death; their role should be limited by ethical obligations and patient autonomy.

A clear thesis will help the reader understand what each paragraph is trying to prove.

Strengthen organization and paragraph focus
Right now, the essay sometimes reads more like a collection of information than a sustained argument. Try to organize it so each paragraph supports your thesis.

A possible structure:
Introduction: define the issue and state your thesis.
Definitions and distinctions: explain different forms of euthanasia more carefully.
Arguments against physician-assisted suicide: religion, law, medical ethics, abuse concerns.
Arguments in favor: autonomy, relief of suffering, dignity.
Kevorkian case: use as evidence, not just as a timeline.
Hippocratic Oath and your conclusion: explain how your position resolves the conflict.

At present, the Kevorkian paragraph is long and mostly summarizes events. Instead of listing charges in sequence, explain why his case matters to your argument.

Be more precise with definitions
You use “euthanasia,” “assisted suicide,” refusal of treatment, withdrawal of life support, and suicide somewhat interchangeably. These are not identical. A stronger essay would distinguish among:
passive euthanasia / withdrawing treatment,
physician-assisted suicide / doctor provides means,
active euthanasia / direct act to end life.

Making these distinctions will improve the accuracy of your argument and prevent confusion.

Develop analysis, not just summary
You include sources, but often you summarize them without explaining their significance. For example, when you mention May’s “four major cultural and social forces,” you should analyze them:
Which of these forces do you find most persuasive?
Do they still apply today?
How do they support or challenge your thesis?

The same is true for the Hippocratic Oath. Don’t just mention it—explain how it functions as an ethical standard and whether it should be interpreted literally in modern medicine.

Address incomplete quotations and missing evidence
Several places contain placeholders such as “”, which makes the paper feel unfinished. These must be replaced with:
the actual quotation,
proper citation information,
and your explanation of the quote.

Also, be careful not to rely too heavily on one or two examples. Add evidence from legal cases, ethical scholarship, or medical practice if possible.

Revise tone and wording for formality
Some word choices are too casual or imprecise for an academic essay. For example:
“easy way out”
“vegetable”
“death by choice”

These phrases can sound biased or insensitive. Replace them with more formal, respectful language such as:
“an attempt to avoid prolonged suffering”
“persistent vegetative state” or “permanently unconscious state”
“physician-assisted dying” or “end-of-life choice,” depending on your intended meaning

Sentence-level issues to correct
There are frequent grammar and mechanics errors. Some common ones:

Apostrophes: “Those patient’s” should be “Those patients”
Word choice: “Christains” should be “Christians”; “break throughs” should be “breakthroughs”
Verb form: “would rather die than to continue” should be “would rather die than continue”
Agreement/pronouns: keep pronouns consistent; avoid shifting between “his,” “one,” “they”
Spacing/punctuation: several sentences need smoother punctuation and transitions

Example revision:
> “When the pain becomes unbearable and medication stops working, some patients would rather die than continue living in severe pain.”

Improve your conclusion
Your final paragraph introduces an important idea—doctors should not decide a patient’s fate, but may cooperate with patient choice. This should be expanded into a full conclusion that:
restates your thesis,
acknowledges the complexity of the issue,
and leaves the reader with a clear final claim.

Final priorities for revision
Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Distinguish carefully among types of euthanasia.
Replace summary with analysis tied to your argument.
Remove placeholders and complete all quotations.
Edit for grammar, tone, and formal academic style.

You have a strong topic and the basis for a thoughtful argument. The next step is to make your position clearer and support it with more precise, organized analysis.

For revision, focus on improving organization, clarity, and argumentation, and on correcting some sentence-level errors.

Strengthen the overall structure

Right now, the essay spends a long time explaining playoff mechanics before fully developing the argument. That makes the paper feel partly informative and only later argumentative.

What to revise
Shorten the background on old and new playoff formats.
Move more quickly to your main claims.
Organize the body around your three stated reasons:
  1. more teams and star players in the playoffs,
  2. more exciting pennant/wild-card races,
  3. more competition in postseason play.

A possible structure

Body 1: more teams/players/cities involved.
Body 2: more excitement at the end of the season.
Body 3: stronger postseason competition.
Conclusion: explain why this matters for baseball’s popularity.

This would make the essay feel more controlled and persuasive.

Make the thesis sharper

Your thesis is clear, but it could be more direct and concise. Right now, the first paragraph spends several sentences explaining realignment before getting to the main claim.

That version is argumentative, focused, and previews the essay’s structure.

For example, when you list many star players, the list is vivid, but the paragraph would be stronger if you analyzed more directly:

The section explaining playoff rules and home-field advantage could be condensed or split.
The paragraph on the wild-card race includes useful evidence, but the long bullet-point section interrupts the flow.

Suggestion
Rather than reproducing all the playoff possibilities, summarize them:
> On the final day of the season, several teams in the American League still had a path either to a division title or to the wild card, which kept fans in multiple cities invested until the very end.

That keeps the focus on your claim instead of on procedural detail.

Use sources more smoothly

You include quotations and references, which is good, but some source use feels dropped into the essay rather than integrated into your own reasoning.

Revise by
introducing each source clearly,
quoting only what is necessary,
explaining the quotation afterward.

Also, be consistent in citation style. Right now, references such as “(Major League Baseball)” and “(The Wildcard...)” are incomplete or uneven. Make sure your paper follows one format consistently.

“Baseball, unfortunately, feels the need to be different”
“big name stars”
“very boring”

“high-profile players”
“less compelling for fans”

“the team from each league with the best record the three Division winners”

“National Leagued Championship Series”

“who plays who”

“as made evident”  
  → as is evident

“Andres Galaragga”  
  → likely Andrés Galarraga

Final sentence: “losing popularity to football and baseball”  
  → probably football and basketball

Read carefully for spelling, word choice, punctuation, and repetition.

What is already working well

Clear central position
Strong knowledge of the topic
Effective use of concrete examples from the season

A conclusion that returns to the broader significance of the issue

Revision priorities
Cut and condense background explanation.
Reorganize body paragraphs around your three main reasons.

Edit for grammar, concision, and academic tone.

You have a solid argument with strong material; revision should focus on making that material more selective, better organized, and more explicitly persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear, passionate position: you argue that the media often abuses the public’s “right to know” and sacrifices privacy, fairness, and even safety for profit. The strongest feature of the draft is your sense of urgency. Readers can tell this matters to you, and that conviction gives the essay energy. You also provide several concrete examples—war coverage, political nominations, the O. J. Simpson trial, and public scrutiny of political figures—which helps move the paper beyond vague complaint.

For revision, the main task is to turn this strong opinion into a more focused and persuasive academic argument.

Clarify and narrow your thesis
Right now, your essay raises several related claims:
the media invades privacy,
the media sensationalizes events,
the media endangers lives,
the media prioritizes profit over truth,
the media unfairly destroys reputations.

All of these fit under a broad criticism of “new media,” but the paper would be stronger if you shaped them into one central claim. For example:

Possible thesis:  
*Although the press has an important duty to inform the public, modern media too often confuses the public’s right to know with a license to sensationalize private lives, influence public judgment, and prioritize profit over responsible reporting.*

A thesis like this gives you a clear structure: acknowledge the value of journalism, then argue that it often crosses ethical lines.

Improve organization
The essay currently reads somewhat like a series of examples added as they occur to you. To strengthen the argument, group examples by idea rather than by chronology.

A possible structure:
Introduction: define the problem and present thesis.
Body paragraph 1: media coverage that endangers public welfare or safety.
Body paragraph 2: media intrusion into private lives and reputations.
Body paragraph 3: sensationalism in major public events and trials.
Body paragraph 4: profit motive and its effect on journalistic ethics.
Conclusion: restate argument and call for more responsible journalism.

Add topic sentences so each paragraph clearly supports the thesis.

Use a more academic tone
Your voice is compelling, but some phrases sound more like conversation or a rant than an academic argument. For instance:
“How dare they…”
“Watch out, future warriors…”
“It was all to sell more newspapers…”
“The past is past. Let it be.”

These lines show emotion, but they may weaken credibility with an academic audience. Try replacing them with precise, analytical language. For example:

Instead of “How dare they endanger lives…”, write:  
*Such reporting raises serious ethical concerns when public access to information may put military personnel at risk.*

Instead of “It was all to sell more newspapers…”, write:  
*The intensity of coverage suggests that commercial incentives may have overshadowed concerns about justice.*

Strengthen evidence and explanation
You refer to many real events, which is good, but several examples need more explanation or support. In academic argument, readers need to know not just what happened, but how it proves your point. For instance:
In the Grenada, Desert Storm, and Bosnia examples, explain more specifically how reporting endangered troops.
In the Surgeon General example, identify the candidate and explain why the media coverage was unfair or disproportionate.
In the Colin Powell paragraph, be careful: “Perhaps the media is the reason…” is speculative. If you cannot support that claim, qualify it more carefully or omit it.

In revision, choose fewer examples and develop them more fully rather than listing many briefly.

Address complexity and counterargument
A strong argumentative essay usually acknowledges the other side. Right now, the paper assumes the media’s “right to know” is mostly harmful. But readers may object that investigative reporting can expose corruption, protect democracy, and hold powerful people accountable.

Consider adding a paragraph like:
*The press plays a necessary role in democratic life, especially when it uncovers corruption or informs citizens about public officials. However, that responsibility should not justify constant intrusion into private matters that have little relevance to public interest.*

This kind of concession makes your position sound balanced rather than absolute.

Revise for sentence-level correctness
There are many proofreading issues that distract from your ideas:
spelling: “successful,” “lives,” “immune,” “media’s,” “doesn’t”
punctuation with quotation marks and apostrophes
inconsistent capitalization
occasional word choice errors (“on this reason” should be “for this reason”)

There are also some sentence-level awkwardnesses. For example:
“Over the passing years, I have come to know a ‘new media.’”
“Nobody was inunune to these incessant facts.”

Read the essay slowly aloud, or print it and edit line by line. You may also want to check for comma splices and overly long sentences.

Final advice
Your paper has a strong core argument and a clear sense of purpose. The revision should focus on focus, structure, evidence, and tone. If you narrow the thesis, organize the examples by theme, and present your claims in a more measured academic voice, this could become a much more persuasive essay.

Revision priorities:
Write a clear, arguable thesis.
Reorganize paragraphs around main claims.
Develop fewer examples in more depth.
Add a brief counterargument.
Proofread carefully for grammar and spelling.

You have the raw material for a strong argument; now the goal is to shape that passion into a disciplined, credible essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you believe marijuana should be legalized. You also establish a strong personal voice, which makes the piece feel engaged and authentic. For revision, though, you need to turn this from a personal reflection into a stronger argumentative essay by improving your thesis, organization, evidence, and style.

Clarify and strengthen your thesis
Right now, your main claim is broad and somewhat inconsistent. You say marijuana should be legalized, but later you say “I don’t believe marijuana is a drug,” which is a definitional claim rather than a policy argument. Decide exactly what you are arguing.

A stronger thesis might be:
Marijuana should be legalized because it has medical uses, is less harmful than many illegal drugs, and criminalization mainly creates social and economic problems.

This gives you a roadmap for your body paragraphs.

Improve organization
At the moment, the essay moves from childhood memories, to politics, to Europe, to personal conversations, to celebrities, without a clear structure. A reader needs a logical progression.

A possible structure:
Introduction: Present the issue and your thesis.
Body Paragraph 1: Medical benefits of marijuana.
Body Paragraph 2: Problems with criminalization/enforcement.
Body Paragraph 3: Why marijuana differs from more dangerous drugs.
Counterargument Paragraph: Acknowledge concerns about abuse or health risks, then respond.
Conclusion: Reinforce why legalization is the better policy.

This will make your argument easier to follow and more persuasive.

Use stronger evidence
Your essay currently relies mostly on:
personal experience,
observations of people you know,
conversations with others,
celebrity examples.

These may help create interest, but they are not enough for an academic argument. For example, saying that classmates smoked before studying and then did well on tests does not prove marijuana helps academic performance. Similarly, mentioning celebrities or politicians does not prove legalization is justified.

You need reliable evidence such as:
medical research on marijuana’s therapeutic uses,
statistics about arrests and incarceration,
comparisons with alcohol or tobacco policy,
evidence from places where marijuana has been legalized.

Also be careful with unsupported claims such as:
“European countries you can buy weed like you buy a pack of cigarettes”
“government officials…keep weed illegal”
“there are nearly no long lasting side effects”

These are either inaccurate, too general, or need proof. If you include such points, support them with credible sources.

Avoid logical problems
Several parts of the essay make leaps in reasoning. For instance:
Because some officials may use drugs does not automatically mean marijuana should be legalized.
Because some dealers make money does not show that the law is wrong.
Because celebrities use marijuana does not prove it is safe or beneficial.

Make sure each paragraph answers this question: How does this evidence support my thesis?

Adjust tone for an academic audience
Your voice is lively, but some phrases are too informal for a college essay, such as:
“talking out of his head”
“mega bucks”
“weed was a really odd smelling cigarette”

You do not need to sound stiff, but you should sound more precise and formal. Also avoid rhetorical questions in excess (“Did you know…?” “Do you ever notice…?”). Instead, make direct claims and support them.

Sentence-level issues
There are many grammar, punctuation, and word-choice errors that weaken your credibility. Some recurring issues:

Comma splices/run-ons: Many sentences join multiple ideas incorrectly.
Word confusion: “their” should often be there or they’re; “it’s” should sometimes be its.
Capitalization: “European,” “Colombian,” “Bob Marley.”
Spelling: “critisize” → criticize; “c ession” appears to be session.
Quotation punctuation: Quotations need correct formatting.

Example revision:
Original: “Did you know that marijuana can be used to slow down certain eye ailments.”
Better: Marijuana has been used medically to treat certain conditions, including symptoms associated with eye disease.

Develop a stronger conclusion
Your conclusion currently ends with a Bob Marley quote. While memorable, a quote should not replace your own final reasoning. Use the conclusion to restate your main argument and explain why it matters socially, medically, or legally.

Revision priorities
Write a precise thesis.
Reorganize the essay into clear argumentative paragraphs.
Replace personal anecdotes with credible evidence.
Remove unsupported claims and overgeneralizations.
Edit carefully for grammar, punctuation, and formal tone.

Final thought
You have the beginning of a compelling essay because you clearly care about the topic and have a distinct perspective. The next step is to support that perspective with organized reasoning and trustworthy evidence so the essay persuades readers beyond those who already agree with you.

Feedback Report

Your essay takes a clear position in favor of shield laws and shows strong engagement with the issue. You also use several concrete examples, which is a real strength in argumentative writing. To revise effectively, focus on sharpening your thesis, improving organization, and making your argument more analytical and less repetitive.

Strengthen the thesis and central claim
Right now, your position is clear, but the essay does not present a sharply defined thesis early on. The first paragraphs introduce trust and secrecy, but they do not clearly state the main argumentative claim.

A stronger thesis would do three things:
state your position,
explain why it matters,
suggest the main reasons you will develop.

For example:
“Shield laws should strongly protect journalists from revealing confidential sources because source confidentiality is essential to investigative reporting, serves the public’s right to know, and prevents courts and prosecutors from using reporters as tools of investigation.”

This would give the essay a clearer direction.

Improve organization
The essay includes useful material, but the structure feels somewhat loose. You move from general ideas, to the history of Maryland’s law, to South Carolina, then to several national examples, and finally to emotional conclusions. Try organizing by argument, not just by examples.

A possible structure:
Introduction: define shield laws and present your thesis.
Body paragraph 1: why confidential sources matter to journalism and public trust.
Body paragraph 2: history/background of shield laws.
Body paragraph 3: limits of shield laws, using the Twila Decker case.
Body paragraph 4: dangers of weak protections, using Ohio/California examples.
Body paragraph 5: counterargument—courts may need evidence—but explain why reporter protection should still prevail except in rare cases.
Conclusion: restate the argument in a more formal, persuasive way.

This would help readers see your reasoning more clearly.

Move from summary to analysis
Much of the essay reports cases, but does not always explain how each case supports your argument. After each example, add 2–3 sentences of analysis.

For instance, after the Twila Decker case, you might explain:
what the case shows about the limits of state shield laws,
why judicial power can undermine press freedom,
how this supports the need for stronger legal protections.

The same applies to Watergate. You mention “Deep Throat,” but the point needs to be made explicit: anonymous sourcing can expose major wrongdoing that otherwise might remain hidden.

Address counterarguments fairly
A strong argumentative essay should acknowledge the opposing side. Judges and prosecutors may argue that source disclosure is necessary when:
a court order has been violated,
confidential records are leaked,
a criminal case depends on evidence.

At the moment, your essay tends to dismiss the justice system too quickly (“maybe the police could find the answers themselves”). That weakens your credibility. Instead, acknowledge that courts sometimes have legitimate concerns, then argue that compelling reporters should be a last resort, not a routine measure.

Revise tone and formality
Some sentences sound more emotional than academic, especially near the end:
“Protect the Shield Laws.”
“Don’t make people scared to talk.”
“Journalists do what they do for the people.”

These lines sound more like a speech or editorial than a university essay. Aim for a more formal style. For example:
“Weakening shield laws may discourage sources from sharing information of public importance, thereby reducing the press’s ability to inform citizens.”

Also avoid broad claims such as “A journalist’s job is to get the story no matter what,” which can sound absolute and ethically questionable.

Edit for clarity, grammar, and mechanics
There are many sentence-level issues that need attention:
extra punctuation: “example,,” “Swasy,,”
quotation mark errors: `deep Throat"` and `Boston"s`
awkward phrasing: “is varied greatly” should be “varies greatly”
missing commas: “especially in a courtroom setting” needs commas around interrupting phrases in some contexts
unnecessary repetition of “he/she”; consider revising into plural nouns like “reporters” and “their sources” for smoother style

Also be careful with formatting. The long block quotation of the Maryland law interrupts the flow. Unless your assignment specifically requires the full legal text, summarize it instead and quote only the most relevant phrase.

Strengthen the conclusion
Your conclusion currently becomes very brief and slogan-like. Instead of ending with commands, conclude by synthesizing your main argument:
source protection enables important reporting,
inconsistent laws leave journalists vulnerable,
society benefits when reporters can protect confidential sources.

Final revision priorities
Write a precise thesis in the introduction.
Reorganize paragraphs around reasons, not just examples.
Add analysis after every case.
Include and answer counterarguments.
Replace emotional ending with formal academic conclusion.
Proofread carefully for punctuation, wording, and citation consistency.

You have strong material here. With clearer structure and more analytical development, this could become a much more persuasive academic argument.

Your essay takes a clear position in favor of admitting women to The Citadel, and you offer several relevant reasons: equality, public funding, educational opportunity, and preparation for real life. Clarify your thesis

“Women should be allowed to attend The Citadel because it is a publicly funded institution, equal educational opportunities should be available to both sexes, and excluding women relies on unfair assumptions about their abilities.”

Improve organization
Your body paragraphs have good ideas, but they sometimes move too quickly from one point to another. Try organizing the essay so that each paragraph focuses on one main reason.

A possible structure:
Introduction with background and thesis  
Tradition is not a sufficient reason to exclude women  
Women are capable of meeting standards just as some men are and some are not  
Public funding means equal access under principles of fairness and law  
Coeducation does not create harmful distraction and better prepares students for society  
Conclusion that restates the argument

This will help your essay feel more controlled and persuasive.

Develop your reasoning more carefully
You make several strong claims, but some need more explanation or evidence.

A. Tradition argument
You argue that tradition alone should not decide policy. That is a good point. However, the comparison to slavery and women being housewives is very broad and may sound overstated. Instead, explain *why* tradition is not enough:

traditions can change when they exclude people unfairly
schools should reflect modern values of equal opportunity

That would sound more precise and persuasive.

B. Capability argument
You make one of your strongest points when you say not all women could succeed at The Citadel, but not all men could either. This is effective because it argues for individual evaluation rather than exclusion by gender. Expand this idea. For instance, you could say that admission and performance should depend on whether each applicant can meet the institution’s academic and physical standards.

C. Public funding argument

A publicly funded school serves the public.
Because women are part of that public, they should have equal access.

This will make the paragraph tighter and more convincing.

D. Distraction argument
Your rebuttal to the “distraction” claim is effective because you point to other coeducational colleges and to the workplace. To strengthen it further, explain that part of education is learning discipline and professionalism around others.

Your essay has conviction, which is good, but some phrases are too conversational for academic writing, such as:

“and so on”
“I especially know that is not true”

Try replacing personal or casual phrasing with more formal language. For example:
“Tradition alone does not justify exclusion.”
“Although society has made progress, full equality has not yet been achieved.”

Also, be careful with wording such as “women are less superior.” You likely mean “inferior.”

Work on grammar, spelling, and proofreading
There are many sentence-level errors that make your ideas harder to follow. These include:
subject-verb agreement: “there has been” → “there have been”
pronouns/determiners: “That questions is” → “That question is”
spelling: appeared, also, publicly, discriminate, Polish, shouldn’t, equal, learning
word choice: “excellence discipline” → “excellent discipline” or “discipline and an excellent education”
punctuation: several comma splices and missing commas

A few corrected examples:

Read your draft aloud slowly during revision. That can help you catch awkward wording and errors.

Your essay ends suddenly. Add a conclusion that restates your claim and leaves the reader with your main idea: women should not be excluded from a public institution because of outdated assumptions.

Final advice
Your essay already has a clear position and several valid arguments. The next step is to make those arguments more organized, more precise, and more polished. Focus especially on:
writing a clear thesis
giving each paragraph one central point
explaining your reasoning more fully
proofreading carefully for grammar and spelling

With revision, this could become a much stronger and more persuasive argument.

These are all relevant, but they need to be organized into one focused thesis. Try to state your main claim and your main reasons in one sentence.

That gives your essay a roadmap.

Domestic labor has real value

Society unfairly stereotypes stay-at-home wives
Conclusion

This would help readers follow your argument more easily.

or explain your existing examples more fully.

Your argument is clear, but parts of the language sound conversational or judgmental:
“If the guy didn’t like the idea...”
“watch the soaps”

In an argumentative essay, a more formal tone will make your position more persuasive. Also avoid quotation marks used for emphasis (“equal,” “support”), since they can make your meaning seem sarcastic or uncertain.

Your essay raises an important public-policy question and shows clear engagement with the topic. What to revise:

If argumentative, state your position early and directly.

For example:
Instead of saying the essay only presents facts, write something like:  
  “Marijuana should be legalized because criminalization is ineffective, its harms are often overstated compared with legal substances such as alcohol and tobacco, and legalization would allow regulation and taxation.”

A clear thesis will help the rest of the essay stay focused.

Improve organization and paragraph structure
Right now, the essay moves through several points, but the structure is loose. Some paragraphs begin with broad statements, and some ideas are not fully developed. Your reader needs a clearer roadmap.

Suggested structure:

History/legal context  
Medical/physical effects  
Comparison with alcohol and tobacco  
Social/economic arguments for legalization  
Counterargument + response  
Conclusion

Also, make sure each paragraph has:
a topic sentence
specific evidence
explanation of how that evidence supports your point

Strengthen evidence and check factual accuracy
This is the most important area for revision. Many claims are broad, unsupported, outdated, or possibly inaccurate. In academic argument, you need reliable, current sources.

Examples of issues:
“The FDA has also recently prescribed Marijuana cigarettes…” — this sounds doubtful and needs verification.
“NO ONE has ever died from marijuana” — this is too absolute. Avoid totalizing claims unless you can support them with strong evidence.
“Most of what people smoke nowadays is not just pure Marijuana, it is often laced with crack or PCP” — this is a major claim that requires strong evidence and may not be accurate.
“By legalizing Marijuana the United States government could elevate much of the crime in America” — likely you mean alleviate or reduce, not elevate.

What to do:
Replace unsupported claims with evidence from credible sources.
Use recent academic, medical, or government sources.
Avoid exaggeration and sweeping statements.

Instead of “any doctor will inform you,” cite a specific medical organization or study.

Address counterarguments fairly
A strong argumentative essay does not only present one side; it also acknowledges objections. For example, critics of legalization might argue:
marijuana can impair driving
heavy use may affect memory or mental health
legalization may increase access for minors

You do mention some harms, but mostly briefly. A stronger essay would present these concerns fairly and then explain why legalization is still the better policy.

Style and tone
Your tone is often energetic, which helps reader interest, but parts of the essay sound conversational rather than academic.

Examples:
“even the president admits to smoking it”
“look at what generation is office now”
“ask yourself…”

These phrases make the essay sound informal. In academic writing, aim for a more neutral and precise tone.

Better approach:
Replace rhetorical questions with statements.
Avoid appeals to the reader such as “ask yourself.”
Use formal wording and careful qualifications.

Sentence-level issues: grammar, word choice, and mechanics
There are many errors in spelling, punctuation, capitalization, and phrasing. These weaken your credibility.

Frequent problems:
Capitalization: “marijuana” should not usually be capitalized mid-sentence.
Word choice: “it’s purpose” should be its purpose; “govermnent” and “goverwnent” should be government; “suffers” should be sufferers or patients.
Comma use: you often insert commas where they are not needed.
Sentence fragments/run-ons:  
  Example: “The first thing to look at when deciding about a stance on Marijuana, the history of Marijuana in the United States.”  
  This needs a verb:  
  “The first thing to examine when deciding on a position is the history of marijuana in the United States.”

Read the essay aloud slowly.
Check each sentence for a subject and verb.
Use spellcheck, but also manually review homophones like its/it’s.
Standardize your terminology and capitalization.

Conclusion

a clear thesis
better organization
accurate, credible evidence
formal academic tone
careful proofreading

   - Beauty ideals

Progress and limitations
   - What feminism has achieved
   - What inequalities still remain

Conclusion
   - Reinforce the main claim
   - End with a thoughtful final statement

This structure will help readers follow your argument more easily.

Link back to the thesis

Use a more formal academic tone

“try to stop the world for turning”

“give it a chance”

Also avoid overgeneralizations such as implying that all parents, ministers, or school boards act the same way.

Try to replace emotionally charged statements with precise claims. For example:
> Censorship does not remove students’ exposure to difficult topics; it only removes structured opportunities to discuss them critically.

That sounds more persuasive in an academic context.

---

Revise grammar, spelling, and sentence clarity
This is the area needing the most attention. Frequent sentence-level errors weaken your credibility. Some recurring issues:

Spelling / word choice
“fireedom” → “freedom”
“Gutenburg” → “Gutenberg”
“bum” → “burn”
“openingly” → “openly”
“baimed” → “banned”
“curriculwn” → “curriculum”

Grammar
Subject-verb agreement: “The censorship of literary works ranges...”
Verb form: “was unable to provide...”
Articles: “under a previous agreement”
Possessives: “teachers’ First Amendment rights,” “students’ education,” “authors’ rights”

Punctuation
There are many extra commas, quotation/punctuation problems, and spacing errors. For example:
“In New Hampshire,, the magazine, Ms. , was considered...”
“However,’ since...”
“certain text?” not “certain text?”

Read the essay aloud slowly, sentence by sentence, to catch awkward phrasing and punctuation errors.

Be careful with names, titles, and references. For example:
*I Know Why the Caged Bird Sings* should be capitalized correctly.
“Ninety-Five Theses,” not “Thesis.”
Make sure organization names and statistics are accurate.
If possible, cite sources in a consistent format.

Accurate presentation makes your argument more trustworthy.

---

Suggested revision priorities

Write a clear thesis at the end of the introduction.  
Reorganize body paragraphs by main point.  
Add analysis after each example.  
Revise for formal tone and avoid overgeneralizations.  
Edit carefully for grammar, spelling, punctuation, and title formatting.

---

Final thought
This essay has a strong core argument: literature matters, and censorship limits education and expression. Your revision should focus on making that argument more precise, organized, and polished. You already have the passion and the material; now work on control and clarity so your reader is persuaded by both your ideas and your writing.

Feedback Report

Your essay takes on a difficult, emotionally charged issue and shows clear effort to research multiple viewpoints. You include historical background, public opinion, political debate, and a key primary source (Alexander Stephens), which gives you strong material to work with. For revision, the main goal is to turn this from a collection of quotations into a clear, well-argued essay with a consistent claim and stronger analysis.

Clarify your thesis

Right now, your position is somewhat unclear and shifts. At the end, you seem to argue that removing the Confederate flag is unnecessary and will create more controversy, but earlier you also include evidence that strongly links the Confederacy to slavery and white supremacy. A reader needs to know exactly what you are arguing.

Ask yourself:
Are you arguing that the flag should remain on public property?
That it should be preserved only in historical contexts?
That people should respect heritage claims even if the symbol is offensive?
Or that public spaces should not display it because of its origins?

Your conclusion suggests one claim, but your evidence sometimes points in another direction. Write a thesis that makes a precise argument and then make sure every paragraph supports it.

Example of a clearer thesis structure:
“Although some Americans view the Confederate flag as a symbol of regional heritage, it should not be displayed on government property because its historical meaning is inseparable from the Confederacy’s defense of slavery and racial hierarchy.”

If you want the opposite position, state that just as directly.

Strengthen organization

The essay currently reads like a sequence of sources rather than a line of reasoning. Try organizing by argument, not by article.

A possible structure:
Introduction: background + thesis  
Heritage argument: explain why some defend the flag  
Counterargument: explain why many see it as racist  
Historical evidence: use Stephens to show the Confederacy’s stated beliefs  
Public display vs. private remembrance: distinguish government use from personal heritage  
Conclusion: restate argument and explain why it matters now

Each body paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that connects back to your thesis.

Use sources more selectively and analyze them

You have many quotations, but often they are dropped into the essay without enough explanation. Avoid stacking quote after quote. Instead:
introduce the source,
quote only the key phrase,
explain what it proves,
connect it to your argument.

For example, the Stephens quotation is your strongest evidence because it comes from a Confederate leader explaining the Confederacy’s foundation. That source deserves more analysis than a list of public opinions from a survey.

Also, be careful: opinion polls and random interviews show what people think, but they do not prove historical meaning. Use them to illustrate debate, not as your main evidence.

Improve logic and handling of counterarguments

Some parts of the reasoning are weak or underdeveloped. For example, the point about the U.S. flag and violence against Native Americans is a comparison, but it does not fully answer whether the Confederate flag should fly over state buildings today. If you keep that point, explain its relevance more carefully.

Likewise, your final claim—“there is no reason to fight over something that was established so many years ago”—ignores the fact that symbols continue to shape public life in the present. A stronger argument would acknowledge why people still care.

Good argumentative writing does not dismiss the other side; it shows you understand it and then responds thoughtfully.

Work on style and tone

Several sentences are informal, vague, or awkwardly phrased. Examples:
“controversy has risen”
“There is always going to be controversy for since we live in this world”
“If we let these people have their way”

Aim for a more formal academic tone. Avoid phrases like “these people,” which sound dismissive and weaken your credibility. Be precise and respectful, especially on controversial issues.

Edit carefully for grammar, mechanics, and citation

There are frequent sentence-level errors that distract from your ideas:
spelling: “fory,” “siad,” “todl,” “unacceptabe”
capitalization: “united Daughter,” “united States”
punctuation and quotation formatting
inconsistent citation style
stray symbols and encoding errors
missing closing punctuation/parentheses

You should also check factual consistency. One statistic in the South Carolina paragraph appears contradictory and may be misquoted or misread. Recheck all numbers and sources.

Most important revision priorities

Write a clear, arguable thesis
Reorganize paragraphs around claims, not source summaries
Cut excess quotations and add analysis
Use the Stephens quotation as your central historical evidence
Revise for formal tone and mechanics

Final thought

You have a strong topic and enough source material for a persuasive essay, but right now the paper needs a clearer argument and more control over evidence. Focus on making your own position lead the essay, rather than letting the quotations lead it for you.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear argumentative purpose: you believe frivolous civil lawsuits waste public money and that lawmakers should reform the system. The paper is energetic and strongly opinionated, which gives it a clear voice. For revision, focus on three major areas: thesis and organization, use of evidence, and tone/style.

Strengthen the thesis
Right now, your main claim is present, but it is somewhat broad and delayed. The opening raises the topic of government spending, then moves into lawsuits, but the exact argument is not sharply stated until later.

Try to end the introduction with a precise thesis that states both your position and the reforms you will argue for. For example:

> Frivolous civil lawsuits burden taxpayers, delay legitimate cases, and raise costs for workers and consumers; therefore, lawmakers should expand judges’ authority to dismiss meritless claims, require penalties for unfounded suits, and strengthen fraud enforcement.

This gives the essay a clearer roadmap.

Improve organization and paragraph focus
The essay includes several different concerns:
taxpayer costs
inmate lawsuits
insurance fraud
workers’ compensation and malpractice claims
lawsuits against schools and police
court backlog
proposed reforms

These are all related, but right now they are presented in a somewhat list-like way. To improve coherence, give each body paragraph one main idea and connect it clearly to your thesis.

A stronger structure might be:
Introduction + thesis
Economic costs of frivolous lawsuits
Examples of abuse of the system (fraudulent accidents, inmate claims)
Consequences for society (court delays, higher insurance costs, effects on schools/workplaces)
Solutions/reforms
Conclusion

Also, use topic sentences to guide the reader. For example:
“One major consequence of frivolous lawsuits is the financial burden they place on taxpayers.”
“In addition to direct public expense, meritless claims delay justice for legitimate plaintiffs and defendants.”
“Because the problem affects both public institutions and private citizens, lawmakers should adopt targeted reforms.”

Use stronger evidence
This is the biggest area for improvement. Much of the essay relies on generalizations such as “Seems like everybody is suing everybody these days” or “I heard of a case last week.” These statements sound conversational, but in an academic argument they are not persuasive enough.

You need specific, credible evidence:
statistics on the number or cost of civil cases
evidence about court backlogs
data on fraudulent insurance claims
laws or policies that already exist in some states
reliable examples from news reports, court records, or studies

Be careful about isolated or unusual examples, especially inmate cases. These may grab attention, but one bizarre example does not prove a widespread problem. Ask yourself: Does this example represent a broader pattern, and can I prove that?

Make the tone more academic
Your strong feelings come through, but some wording weakens your credibility because it sounds emotional, sarcastic, or dismissive. Examples include:
“ridiculous cases”
“begging for people to call them”
“everybody is suing everybody”
“their time should be otherwise occupied”

In an academic essay, it is better to sound measured and fair, even when arguing strongly. For instance:
replace “ridiculous cases” with “meritless claims”
replace “begging” with “aggressively advertising”
replace “everybody is suing everybody” with “litigation appears increasingly common in public perception”

A more neutral tone will make your argument seem more thoughtful and credible.

Address complexity and counterarguments
The essay assumes many lawsuits are unnecessary, but it does not acknowledge that civil courts also protect injured people, workers, consumers, and prisoners whose rights may genuinely be violated. If you ignore this, readers may think your argument is one-sided.

Add a brief counterargument, such as:
some lawsuits are essential for justice and accountability
not all claims against employers, police, or prisons are frivolous
reforms must avoid blocking legitimate grievances

Then respond by explaining that your proposals target clearly unfounded claims, not valid ones. This will make your argument more balanced.

Edit carefully for grammar and mechanics
There are many spelling, typing, and punctuation errors, such as:
“1990’s” → 1990s
“govermnent” → government
“taxpayers dollars” → taxpayers’ dollars
“now a days” → nowadays
“then” → than
“payoff” → pay off or payout
“worker’s compensation” → usually workers’ compensation

There are also places where wording is unclear, likely because of typing or scanning errors. Proofread carefully and read the essay aloud to catch mistakes.

Final advice
Your essay has a strong central concern and clear passion. To revise successfully, focus on making it more precise, evidence-based, and academically balanced. If you combine your strong voice with better organization and credible support, your argument will become much more convincing.
