Feedback Report

Your essay tackles an important political issue and shows strong engagement with a real historical event. You clearly care about the effects of government shutdowns on ordinary people, and that concern gives the paper energy. For revision, focus on three main areas: accuracy and clarity of argument, organization, and sentence-level correctness.

Strengthen the thesis and argument
Your essay’s main claim seems to be that partisan conflict in government causes harmful shutdowns and hurts citizens. That is a workable argument, but your thesis needs to be stated more clearly and precisely in your own words.

Right now, the introduction opens with a quotation and then moves quickly into summary. Instead, give a direct thesis such as:

“The 1995 government shutdown shows how partisan conflict between Congress and the president can damage the economy, disrupt public services, and hurt the citizens government is supposed to serve.”

This would help unify the paper.

Also, be careful not to overstate your claim. For example, saying partisan conflict will cause “the downfall of the American government” is dramatic, but your evidence mainly shows that it causes gridlock, instability, and harm to workers. Make your conclusions match your evidence.

Improve historical and factual accuracy
Some parts of the essay need fact-checking. This is especially important in argumentative writing because inaccurate details weaken credibility.

Examples:
The 1995 shutdown did not occur on “Tuesday, November 13, 1995.” November 13, 1995 was a Monday.
The statement about “thirteen essential bills” may not be fully accurate as written; check how appropriations bills functioned at that time.
“Starting with the release of all nonessential personnel and ending with the president of the United States” is unclear and likely inaccurate. The president does not get “shut down.”
“October 1-1962 was the first time the United States government shutdown” should be verified.
Claims like “This could cause a depression” are too extreme unless you provide strong economic evidence.

When revising, verify all dates, numbers, and government procedures with reliable sources.

Organize the essay more logically
The essay currently repeats ideas and sometimes shifts abruptly. Try this structure:

Introduction
Brief context on the 1995 shutdown
Your thesis

Body Paragraph 1: What caused the shutdown
Explain the budget dispute between Congress and President Clinton
Define the role of partisan disagreement

Body Paragraph 2: Effects on workers and services
Discuss furloughed employees, closed services, and public disruption
Use specific examples

Body Paragraph 3: Why this matters to the public
Explain economic and civic consequences
Show why citizens should care

Conclusion
Return to your thesis
Explain what the event reveals about partisan politics

This structure will make the paper easier to follow.

Develop analysis, not just narration
Much of the essay retells events. To make it more argumentative, explain why each event matters.

For example, after describing the vetoes, add analysis like:
What does this reveal about divided government?
Why was compromise difficult?
How did political competition shape policy decisions?

Move beyond “what happened” to “what it means.”

Maintain a more formal academic tone
You use first person frequently (“I feel,” “I agree,” “I say no”). In academic argument, your position should come through in your reasoning rather than repeated personal statements.

For example:
Instead of “I feel the American people have been unfairly made to pay…”
Write: “The shutdown unfairly forced ordinary Americans to bear the consequences of political deadlock.”

Also avoid informal phrasing such as:
“like two kids fighting on a playground”

You can make the same point in a more formal way:
“Both parties acted with excessive stubbornness and failed to compromise.”

Revise sentence-level issues carefully
There are many grammar, punctuation, capitalization, and spelling errors that make the essay harder to read. Some common problems:

Sentence fragments:  
  “This statement that was made by Thomas Jefferson…”  
  This is not a complete sentence.

Run-ons/comma splices:  
  “Neither side is right.Both sides…”  
  Add proper punctuation and spacing.

Capitalization:  
  “united States,” “Should,” “congress,” “democrats”  
  Proper nouns and official titles need consistent capitalization.

Spelling/typing errors:  
  “govermnent,” “figthing,” “nonessentials,” “Strom Thurrnond”

Word choice:  
  “workers out of a job” is less precise than “furloughed federal employees.”

Read the essay aloud slowly, or review it sentence by sentence for correctness.

Integrate the Jefferson reference more effectively
The Jefferson quotation gives the paper an interesting starting point, but right now it feels loosely connected. Either:
use it to frame your argument throughout the paper, or
remove it and begin directly with the 1995 shutdown.

If you keep it, explain more clearly how the event supports or complicates Jefferson’s warning about partisanship.

Final advice
Your essay has a strong topic, clear concern for public impact, and a central idea with real argumentative potential. The next step is to make it more precise, better organized, and more formally written. Prioritize:
a clear thesis,
accurate facts,
stronger paragraph structure,
deeper analysis,
careful proofreading.

With revision, this could become a much more persuasive and credible argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: abortion should remain legal, and you try to support that claim with several reasons, including rape, poverty, personal liberty, and the danger of illegal abortions. You also show awareness that this is a controversial public issue. That gives you a strong starting point for an argumentative essay. To revise effectively, focus on three main areas: clarity of argument, quality of evidence, and sentence-level accuracy.

Strengthen your thesis and overall argument
Your introduction sets up the debate, but the thesis is not fully clear because one sentence appears corrupted: “Hapeo povertyg personal choice and illegal abortions are examples why people should maintain their right to decide.” I think you mean that rape, poverty, personal choice, and illegal abortions support abortion rights. Rewrite the thesis so it is precise and direct.

For example:
“Abortion should remain legal because banning it harms rape survivors, restricts women’s bodily autonomy, and leads to dangerous illegal abortions.”

This version is clearer and previews your strongest reasons.

Also, be careful about which arguments are ethically persuasive. Your paragraph about poverty argues that abortion helps “maintain a balance” between lower-income and wealthier families. This is likely to offend readers because it suggests reducing births among poor people is socially desirable. That sounds less like a rights-based argument and more like class control. If you want to discuss poverty, shift the focus to how financial hardship affects people’s ability to raise children, access healthcare, or make responsible reproductive choices.

Improve paragraph development
Several body paragraphs raise important points but do not fully explain them.

Rape paragraph
This is one of your strongest ideas emotionally, but it needs a more formal tone and better development. Instead of asking the reader, “Would [you] be able to love him or her?”, explain the point analytically: forced pregnancy can intensify trauma and remove agency from survivors. Then support this with evidence if possible.

Poverty paragraph
Right now this section contains broad claims such as:
“Most unwanted pregnancies occur in poverty stricken areas”
“Generally children born into impoverished environments remain in that socioeconomic level their entire life”

These need evidence and more careful wording. Avoid overgeneralizations. Also, the transition between poverty and family size is abrupt.

Legal rights paragraph
You mention “Rowe versus Way,” but the correct case is Roe v. Wade. This error affects your credibility. Also, simply stating women “won this right” is too basic. Explain why this case mattered and, if relevant, note that abortion rights have continued to be contested politically and legally.

Illegal abortion paragraph
This is another strong argument. Expand it with clearer logic: making abortion illegal does not necessarily stop abortions; it often makes them less safe. This point would become much more persuasive with a statistic or historical example.

Adoption/foster care paragraph
This paragraph raises an important issue, but it becomes too speculative. For instance, “Infant mortality rates would sky rocket” is a very large claim and needs evidence. Be cautious about making predictions unless you can support them.

Use evidence more effectively
Your essay relies mostly on assertion. In an academic argument, readers need sources, statistics, or examples. Right now, even when you use numbers (“One in four women…”; “five children on average”; “one hundred twenty-five thousand dollars”), you do not cite where they come from.

For revision:
Add 2–4 credible sources.
Use evidence from public health, law, or social policy.
Make sure each statistic is introduced and then explained.

Work on tone and audience
At times your tone is too conversational or emotional for academic writing. Rhetorical questions such as “Would [you] be able to love him or her?” can be effective in speeches, but in an essay they often weaken authority. Aim for a calm, reasoned tone.

Also, avoid wording that sounds judgmental or imprecise, such as “ignorant and uncaring parents” or “primitive tool like a coat hanger.” You can make the same point more professionally.

Edit carefully for grammar, spelling, and mechanics
There are many sentence-level errors that distract from your ideas. Some examples:

“life time” → lifetime
“being force to give birth” → being forced to give birth
“they would already be receiving” → should be revised for clarity
“they won this right in the case of Rowe versus Way” → Roe v. Wade
“if the remove this right” → if they remove this right
“No many women” → No; many women
“sky racket alsop” → skyrocket also
“eightteen” → eighteen
“they” / “the” / capitalization errors such as “they won…” and “Women deserve the right to have an abortion. they…”

You also need better punctuation between independent clauses. Several sentences are run-ons or fused sentences.

Suggested revision plan
Rewrite the thesis clearly.
Choose 3 strongest arguments instead of many loosely developed ones.
Remove or rethink the “balance” argument about poor vs. wealthy families.
Add credible evidence to each body paragraph.
Correct factual errors, especially Roe v. Wade.
Proofread carefully for grammar, spelling, and punctuation.

Overall, your essay shows a clear stance and genuine engagement with the issue. The next step is to make the argument more precise, better supported, and more academically credible.
